Unified synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds for Riemannian and sub-Riemannian structures
Davide Barilari, Andrea Mondino, Luca Rizzi

Abstract
Recent advances in the theory of metric measures spaces on the one hand, and of sub-Riemannian ones on the other hand, suggest the possibility of a “great unification” of Riemannian and sub-Riemannian geometries in a comprehensive framework of synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds, as put forth in [ 87 , Sec. 9] . With the aim of achieving such a unification program, in this paper we initiate the study of gauge metric measure spaces.



1
Introduction

The subject of this work is the synthetic treatment of curvature lower bounds. To illustrate our contribution, let us start by recalling the celebrated theory of Alexandrov spaces. These are metric spaces
(X,\mathsf{d})
where curvature bounds are defined via comparison of geodesic triangles in
X
with corresponding ones in model surfaces of constant curvature. The key observation is that Toponogov triangle comparison theorem gives a purely metric characterization of lower bounds for the sectional curvature on smooth Riemannian manifolds, which yields a synthetic definition for metric spaces.

A synthetic characterization of
Ricci
curvature lower bounds needs an additional structure, namely a reference measure
\mathfrak{m}
: following the seminal works by Gromov
[
50
]
, by Fukaya on spectral convergence
[
47
]
, by Cheeger-Colding on Ricci limit spaces
[
40
]
, it emerged that a natural framework for Ricci curvature lower bounds is the one of metric measure spaces.

A key fact is that, on a smooth Riemannian manifold, Ricci curvature lower bounds can be characterized in terms of suitable convexity inequalities of entropy functionals in the Wasserstein space
[
42
,
89
]
, after
[
67
,
74
]
.
This line of research culminated in the seminal work by Lott-Sturm-Villani in the 2000’s
[
65
,
83
,
84
]
, introducing the synthetic
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
conditions for metric measure spaces. The latter conditions depend on two parameters:
K\in\mathbb{R}
playing the role of a lower bound on the Ricci curvature, and
N\geq 1
playing the role of an upper bound on the dimension. Concretely,
K
and
N
enter into the theory via model
distortion coefficients

\beta_{K,N}
, encoding the effect of the Ricci curvature on the distortion of the measure along geodesics. These coefficients have the form:

(\beta_{K,N})_{t}(\theta):=t\,\frac{\sin\left(t\theta\sqrt{K/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}}{\sin\left(\theta\sqrt{K/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(1.1)

for
0<K\theta^{2}<(N-1)\pi^{2}
and with standard interpretation otherwise, see Section
3.3.1
.

A fundamental aspect of the
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
condition is that it enjoys compactness and stability properties under pmGH convergence. We recall that the class of
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
spaces includes Riemannian manifolds with Ricci curvature bounded from below, as well as Finsler ones
[
73
]
.

However, there is an important class of metric measure spaces which does not fit into this framework: sub-Riemannian structures. These are smooth manifolds endowed with a length metric obtained by considering only curves that are tangent to a bracket-generating vector distribution (see Appendix
A
for a self-contained account).

The simplest examples are the Heisenberg groups. In
[
54
]
, Juillet proved that the
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
condition fails for all values of
K\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\geq 1
on the Heisenberg groups, and indeed this is the case also all sub-Riemannian manifolds
[
15
,
55
,
66
,
80
]
that are not Riemannian. Moreover, in
[
54
]
, it was also proved that a weaker synthetic condition, known as measure contraction property
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
, holds in Heisenberg groups for suitable values of
K
and
N
. This property has been proved to hold in more general Carnot groups
[
18
,
75
]
.

Recently, in their seminal work
[
19
]
, Balogh-Kristály-Sipos showed that, despite the failure of the classical
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
condition, weaker entropy inequalities hold in the Heisenberg groups
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, with the following distortion coefficients:

\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{t}(\theta):=t\,\dfrac{\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)^{2d-1}\left[\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{t\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\right]}{\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2d-1}\left[\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right]},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(1.2)

for
0<\theta<2\pi
, and standard interpretation otherwise. See also
[
20
]
for similar inequalities for general co-rank
1
Carnot groups.

Two remarkable differences between the Riemannian distortion coefficients (
1.1
) and the Heisenberg ones (
1.2
) are:

•

as expected, the functional expressions (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) do not match;

•

more strikingly, when used to encode measure distortion, while in the Riemannian coefficients (
1.1
), the parameter
\theta
takes as value the
length
, in the Heisenberg ones (
1.2
),
\theta
takes as value the
curvature
of geodesics (as curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
).

A different approach to Ricci curvature lower bounds in sub-Riemannian manifolds was put forward by Baudoin-Garofalo
[
27
]
. Inspired by the Bakry-Émery semi-group techniques, they generalized curvature-dimension conditions for sub-Riemannian structures with symmetries, introducing a suitable generalization of Bochner’s identity and
\Gamma
-calculus. See the lecture notes
[
26
]
and references therein for an account of this theory. We also mention the recent work by Stefani
[
81
]
which, in the setting of Carnot groups, establishes a first link between the Lagrangian approach to Ricci curvature lower bounds (dealing with convexity-type properties of entropy along Wasserstein geodesics) and the Eulerian one (focused instead on the properties of the heat flow).

Motivated by the aforementioned contributions, in his 2017 Bourbaki Seminar, Villani envisioned the possibility of a “great unification” of Riemannian and sub-Riemannian geometries in a comprehensive theory of synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds. See
[
87
, Sec. 9, conclusions et perspectives]
, and
[
86
, Rmk. 14.23]
. Developing such a theory is the ambition of the present paper.

Gauge metric measure spaces

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space, m.m.s. for short. We add a supplementary structure to the metric measure one, namely a non-negative Borel function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
, that we call
gauge function
.
The quadruple
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
will be called
gauge metric measure space
.

The following analogy explains the role of the gauge function. A Riemannian manifold has Ricci curvature bounded from below by
K\in\mathbb{R}
if for any pair of points
x,y
and any geodesic
\gamma
between
x
and
y
it holds

\mathrm{Ric}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})\geq K\|\dot{\gamma}\|^{2}=K\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{2}.

(1.3)

The distance function
\mathsf{d}
is used in the right hand side as a
gauge
to measure the
extent
of the lower Ricci curvature bound, quantified by the constant
K
. The idea is to replace the distance
\mathsf{d}
with a general gauge function
\mathsf{G}
.

Gauge functions will be a key object in our extension of the synthetic theory of Ricci curvature bounds to the sub-Riemannian setting, where it is now well-understood that the effect of geometry on transport inequalities may not depend uniquely on the distance, but rather on other intrinsic sub-Riemannian quantities (see
[
19
]
,
[
24
, Sec. 8.1]
).

Distortion coefficients

Motivated by the comparison theory in sub-Riemannian geometry
[
8
,
11
,
91
,
63
,
52
,
5
,
62
,
22
,
25
]
(from the Hamiltonian viewpoint) and the discussion above, we now introduce general distortion coefficients.

Let
\mathsf{s}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function and
N\in[1,+\infty)
such that

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=c\,\theta^{N}+o(\theta^{N})\qquad\text{ as $\theta\to 0$},

(1.4)

for some
c>0
. The parameter
N
will be a sharp upper bound for a new notion of dimension, which is in general different from the Hausdorff one, see Section
4.5
.
Denote:

\mathcal{D}:=\inf\{\theta>0\mid\mathsf{s}(\theta)=0\}.

(1.5)

It is clear that
\mathcal{D}>0
. The latter will be a sharp upper bound on the gauge function, see Section
4.3
.
Define the
distortion coefficient

\beta_{(\cdot)}(\cdot):[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
as

\displaystyle(t,\theta)\in[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta):=\begin{cases}t^{N}&\theta=0,\\
\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}(\theta)}&0<\theta<\mathcal{D},\\
\displaystyle\liminf_{\phi\to\mathcal{D}^{-}}\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\phi)}{\mathsf{s}(\phi)}&\theta\geq\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.6)

Notice that, when properly understood, both the Riemannian (
1.1
) and the Heisenberg (
1.2
) distortion coefficients are obtained as in (
1.6
), for a suitable
\mathsf{s}
.

In applications to sub-Riemannian geometry, the function
\mathsf{s}
in (
1.4
) is chosen in a class of models characterized as solutions to suitable ODEs. We illustrate this characterization in Section
8
, and in particular we refer to Proposition
8.5
and Remark
8.6
, which provides a bridge between the synthetic viewpoint and sub-Riemannian geometry. Here we develop the theory in full generality, without further constraints on the function
\mathsf{s}
.

Entropy functionals

We consider the metric space
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X),W_{2})
of probability measures with finite second moment endowed with the Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein quadratic transportation distance, see Section
3.1.2
for the definitions.
A
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
can be equivalently represented by a probability measure
\nu
on the space of geodesics
\mathrm{Geo}(X)
, with
\mu_{t}=({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
, where
{\rm e}_{t}:\mathrm{Geo}(X)\to X
is the evaluation map at time
t
. Such a
\nu
is called
optimal dynamical plan
from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
, and the set of such measures is denoted by
{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
. Let also
\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})
be the space of probability measures that are absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
.

Recall that, for
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
, its relative (Boltzmann-Shannon) entropy is defined by

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=\int_{X}\rho\log\rho\,\mathfrak{m},\qquad\text{ if $\mu=\rho\,\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)\cap\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})$},

(1.7)

in case
\rho\log\rho\in L^{1}(X,\mathfrak{m})
, otherwise we set
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=+\infty
.

Let
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))
be the finiteness domain of the entropy and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X,\mathsf{d})\mid\text{ $\supp\,\mu$ is bounded and }\supp\,\mu\subseteq\supp\,\mathfrak{m}\},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\cap\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m}).

(1.9)

The subspaces
\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and
\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
will play a key role throughout the paper.

In order to formulate “dimensional” Ricci curvature lower bounds, it is convenient to introduce also the following dimensional entropy (cf.
[
44
]
):

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m})}{n}\right),\qquad n\in[1,+\infty),

(1.10)

with the understanding that
\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=0
if
\mu\notin\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))
.

\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces and
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
spaces

We introduce synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds on gauge m.m.s.: the Curvature-Dimension condition
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
and the Measure Contraction Property
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

Definition

(Definition
3.5
)
.

Let
n\in[1,+\infty)
, and
\beta
as in (
1.6
). A gauge metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies:

•

\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
if for all
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
, there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
connecting them, induced by
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, such that it holds

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{1-t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{0}|\mathfrak{m})\\
+\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(1.11)

with the convention that
\infty\cdot 0=0
.

•

\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
if for any
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\bar{x}\notin\supp\mu_{1}
there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
from
\mu_{0}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
to
\mu_{1}
such that

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x)\big)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(1.12)

for some (and then every)
n\geq 1
.

We anticipate here a few remarks.

1.

The
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition does not depend on the value of
n
. See Remark
3.6
.

2.

Let us stress that non-absolutely continuous
\mu_{0}
are allowed, which by construction gives the implication
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
. See Remarks
3.7
and
3.8
.

3.

Conversely, assuming that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
supports interpolation inequalities for densities with dimensional parameter
n
(in the sense of Definition
4.5
), we show that
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
. See Theorem
4.7
.

4.

The
\mathsf{CD}/\mathsf{MCP}
conditions imply that
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
(with metric
W_{2}
) and
\supp\mathfrak{m}
(with metric
\mathsf{d}
) are length spaces. See Remark
3.9
.

Compatibility with classical synthetic theories

The
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
and
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
conditions satisfy the following compatibility properties:

•

Compatibility with Lott-Sturm-Villani’s
\mathsf{CD}
: by choosing as distortion coefficients the Riemannian ones, i.e.
\beta=\beta_{K,N}
as in (
1.1
), and as gauge function the distance, i.e.
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
, we show that, for essentially non-branching m.m.s., the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
conditions are equivalent to the corresponding
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N},N)
. See Section
3.3.1
.

•

Compatibility with Ohta-Sturm’s
\mathsf{MCP}
: as above, we show that the Ohta-Sturm
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
are equivalent to the corresponding
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N})
. See Section
3.3.1
.

•

Compatibility with Balogh-Kristály-Sipos
: by choosing as distortion coefficients the Heisenberg ones, i.e.
\beta=\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}
as in (
1.2
), and as gauge function
\mathsf{G}(x,y)=\theta^{x,y}
where
\theta^{x,y}
is the curvature of the geodesic from
x
to
y
(as a curve in
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
), we show that the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
(endowed with the Carnot-Carathéodory distance, and the
2d+1
Lebesgue measure) satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}},2d+1)
condition. See Section
3.3.2
.

•

Compatibility with E. Milman’s
\mathsf{CGTD}
: the Curvature-Geodesic-Topological-Dimension conditions
\mathsf{CGTD}(K,N,n)
introduced by E. Milman in
[
69
, Sec. 7]
, for
K\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\geq 1
and
N\geq n
is equivalent to the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N},n)
condition, for gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
. See Remark
3.14
.

Geometric consequences

One of the novel features of the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
and
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
conditions above is the interplay of the gauge function
\mathsf{G}
(measuring the extent of the Ricci curvature lower bounds) and the distance
\mathsf{d}
(governing the geodesics and thus optimal transport). This leads to a decoupling between metric vs distortion aspects in the classical geometric consequences of the curvature-dimension conditions. In Section
4
we will obtain the following results:

•

Generalized Brunn-Minkowski inequality
: given two sets
A_{0},A_{1}
, we estimate from below the volume of the set
A_{t}
of
t
-intermediate points of geodesics from
A_{0}
to
A_{1}
, with a distortion which is quantified
purely in terms of the gauge function
\mathsf{G}
and the general distortion coefficients
\beta
. See Section
4.1
.

•

\mathsf{MCP}\Rightarrow\mathsf{CD}
for spaces supporting interpolation inequalities. See Section
4.2
.

•

Gauge-diameter estimates
: the parameter
\mathcal{D}
in (
1.5
) yields an upper bound on the essential supremum of the gauge function, called gauge diameter. A remarkable difference with respect to classical Bonnet-Myers Theorem is that the gauge diameter can be bounded also for non-compact metric measure spaces (e.g. the Heisenberg group, where the estimate we obtain is sharp) and, conversely, unbounded for compact ones. See Section
4.3
.

•

Local doubling inequalities for metric balls
: in contrast with the classical case when “locality” is measured in terms of the distance, here it is expressed in terms of the gauge function which also determines the doubling constant. See Section
4.4
.

•

Geodesic dimension estimates
: the parameter
N
occurring in (
1.4
) yields an upper bound for the Hausdorff dimension and for a notion of dimension for m.m.s. recently introduced in
[
3
,
78
]
, the so-called geodesic dimension. See Section
4.5
. The latter bound is sharp while the former is not, see Remark
4.21
.

•

Generalized Bishop-Gromov inequalities
: we obtain volume estimates on the (truncated) sub-level sets of the gauge function. Such sets are reminiscent of the “butterfly-shaped” sets appearing in
[
54
, Fig. 2]
. For the validity of the Bishop-Gromov inequality, we need an additional compatibility condition between the distance and the gauge function, which we name
meek property
. See Section
4.6
.

Stability and compactness

Key features of the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory are the stability and compactness properties with respect to the measured-Gromov-Hausdorff convergence (mGH for short).

For a sequence of gauge m.m.s. it is natural to consider the mGH convergence of the metric measure structures, coupled with an additional notion of convergence for the gauge functions.
A naive generalisation of the mGH convergence to gauge functions, see (
5.1
), would fail for natural sequences (e.g. convergence to the tangent cone for sub-Riemannian structures, cf. Section
10.4
). This is due to the low regularity of the gauge functions which in general are not even continuous, in sharp contrast with the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory where
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
is clearly
1
-Lipschitz.

For this reason, in Section
5
, we introduce a significantly weaker condition which provides a good balance yielding both
stability
and
compactness
properties for the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition and the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
. We ask for a sort of
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions in varying spaces, and a regularity condition on the limit gauge function outside of the diagonal. Compared to the classical Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory, the low regularity of the gauge functions and their weaker convergence introduce new challenges in the proof.

The vector-valued case

In Section
6
we extend the above theory to vector-valued gauge functions, namely

\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+},

(1.13)

where
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
is a projective semi-space. This generalization is necessary in order to obtain sharp estimates for important classes of sub-Riemannian examples, see Section
10.5
.

Natural gauge functions

In the Heisenberg group a natural gauge function is the quantity
\theta
mentioned earlier in the introduction (
\theta^{x,y}
is the curvature of the geodesic from
x
to
y
as a curve of
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
). A key observation is that
\theta
can be written in terms of the Carnot-Carathéodory distance and the norm of its gradient, computed with respect to a Riemannian extension.

This observation suggests a synthetic way to define natural gauge functions for any metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
equipped with a reference metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
(the subscript stands for
Reference
, or
Riemannian
). The construction is well-suited to sub-Riemannian geometry where, in several cases, one has natural Riemannian extensions serving as reference.

Natural gauge functions will be constructed out of two main building blocks: the distance function and the
\mathsf{D}
function
defined as follows

\mathsf{D}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty],\qquad\mathsf{D}(x,y):=\limsup_{z,w\to x}\frac{|\mathsf{d}^{2}(z,y)-\mathsf{d}^{2}(w,y)|}{2\mathsf{d}_{R}(z,w)},

(1.14)

with the convention that the limit is
0
if
x
is an isolated point.

Let us show how
\theta
is obtained via the above procedure, in the Heisenberg group
(\mathbb{H}^{d},\mathsf{d}_{cc},\mathscr{L}^{2d+1})
. As extension
\mathsf{d}_{R}
, we choose the left-invariant Riemannian metric obtained declaring the Reeb field
\partial_{z}
to be of unit length and orthogonal to the Heisenberg distribution. For
(x,y)
out of the cut locus, we prove in Proposition
7.28
that

\theta^{x,y}=\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}(x,y)-\mathsf{d}_{cc}^{2}(x,y)}.

(1.15)

We note that
\mathsf{D}\geq\mathsf{d}
, see Proposition
7.5
(i)
. Natural gauge functions are studied in Section
7
, in particular:

•

in Section
7.1
we define the
\mathsf{D}
function and we establish its basic properties;

•

in Section
7.2
we define
natural gauge functions
on metric measure spaces equipped with a reference metric, of which
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{d}
are particular cases;

•

in Section
7.3
we establish further properties of natural gauge functions, namely:

–

the meek condition used for the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem; see Section
7.3.1
;

–

the regularity and boundedness properties required for the stability and compactness results; see Sections
7.3.2
and
7.3.3
respectively.

We prove that all these properties follow from natural hypotheses in sub-Riemannian geometry, including: step
\leq
2, minimizing Sard property,
*
-minimizing Sard property, ideal, absence of non-trivial Goh geodesics, real-analyticity. For a glimpse of all the implications see Figure
3
;

•

in Section
7.4
we show how the natural gauge functions are related to the sub-Riemannian distance out of the cut locus.

Compatibility with the Hamiltonian theory of curvature

In Section
8
we establish the compatibility of the synthetic
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition with the Hamiltonian theory of Ricci curvature lower bounds for sub-Riemannian manifolds. The latter has its roots in the pioneering works by Agrachev and Gamkrelidze on the geometry of curves in Lagrange Grassmannians
[
8
,
11
]
, and was subsequently developed by Zelenko and Li
[
91
]
with the introduction of the so-called
canonical frame
. This technical tool, generalizing in a very broad sense the notion of parallel transport, has been pivotal in the development of the comparison theory for sub-Riemannian structures from the Hamiltonian point of view, started in
[
63
,
5
,
62
]
. Sub-Riemannian Ricci curvatures were finally introduced in
[
22
]
in full generality, as
partial traces
of the canonical curvatures. The corresponding comparison theory for distortion coefficients was finally set in
[
25
]
.

We illustrate such a theory in Section
8
, in a form adapted to our purposes. For the sake of self-consistency, in Appendix
B
, we include an account of the Agrachev-Zelenko-Li theory, that is used extensively in Sections
8
-
9
.

We depict here the main ideas. Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian metric measure space, i.e.
M
is a smooth manifold,
\mathsf{d}
is a sub-Riemannian distance, and
\mathfrak{m}
is a smooth measure, see Definition
7.9
. The paradigm of sub-Riemannian comparison is that, for the generic geodesic
\gamma
, one can decompose the tangent space along
\gamma
in a family of subspaces, depending on the Lie bracket structure. These are labeled by the boxes
\alpha
of a Young diagram. For each subspace we define a
canonical Ricci curvature

\mathfrak{Ric}^{\alpha}_{\gamma}
as the corresponding partial trace of the canonical curvature. We remark that in the Riemannian case there is only one such a box
\alpha
, and

\mathfrak{Ric}^{\alpha}_{\gamma}=\mathrm{Ric}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma}),

(1.16)

recovering the classical Ricci tensor along that geodesic.

For a general sub-Riemannian metric measure space
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, consider the “true” distortion coefficient of the m.m.s.:

\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y):=\limsup_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}(Z_{t}(\{x\},B_{r}(y)))}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(y))},\qquad\forall\,x,y\in M,\quad t\in[0,1],

(1.17)

see Definition
4.6
. For fixed
(x,y)
out of the cut locus, there is a unique geodesic
\gamma
joining
x
with
y
, and when
every
canonical Ricci curvature along
\gamma
is bounded from below,
\mathfrak{Ric}^{\alpha}_{\gamma}\geq\kappa_{\alpha}
, for some
\kappa_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
, and all
\alpha
, then the following comparison holds:

\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{mod}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

(1.18)

Here,
\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
is a
model
distortion coefficient. We illustrate its properties.

•

\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
is associated with a variational problem on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, of Linear-Quadratic type, coming from optimal control theory. This is a change of paradigm with respect to classical Riemannian comparison theory, where model distortion coefficients are the ones of Riemannian space forms, while the models in the present theory are not even metric spaces. See Section
8.1
.

•

\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
can be computed explicitly by solving a Hamiltonian ODE, which is identified by the values of the Ricci lower bounds
\kappa_{\alpha}
, and the structure of the Young diagram associated with the geodesic
\gamma
. See Section
8.2
.

•

\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
has the form (
1.6
), in other words it is a model distortion coefficient in the sense of the synthetic theory. This is not by chance, and in fact Proposition
8.5
, in particular item
6
, is the bridge between the curvature-dimension theory of Section
3
and the comparison theory of Section
8
.

•

When the Young diagram of
\gamma
is of Riemannian type (i.e. it consists of only one box),
\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
recovers the familiar Riemannian coefficients. See Section
8.2.1
.

•

The general class of
\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
contains
all
distortion coefficients observed so far in sub-Riemannian geometry: Heisenberg groups
[
19
]
, corank
1
Carnot groups
[
20
]
, Sasakian
[
5
,
62
]
and
3
-Sasakian manifolds
[
79
]
. See Sections
8.2.2
and
8.2.3
.

The comparison result in the sense of (
1.18
) is Theorem
8.9
, describing the distortion along a single geodesic. If the Ricci curvature bounds hold uniformly, in the precise technical sense of Definition
8.11
, then one obtains Theorem
8.12
.

Theorem

(Ideal sub-Riemannian structures with Ricci bounded below are
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an ideal sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with
n=\dim M
, equipped with a finite gauge function
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, with Ricci curvatures bounded from below in the sense of Definition
8.11
, and let
\beta
be the corresponding distortion coefficient. Then
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition.

This result establishes the compatibility of the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
theory with the one of sub-Riemannian Ricci bounds.

Compact fat structures satisfy the curvature-dimension condition

Clearly, any compact Riemannian manifold has Ricci curvature bounded below, and thus it verifies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition for suitable
\beta
(of course, in this case,
\beta=\beta_{\kappa,n}
where
\kappa
is a lower bound for the Ricci curvature and
n
is the topological dimension). In Section
9
we study the sub-Riemannian counterpart of such a statement, for the case of fat distributions (also called strong bracket generating, see
[
71
,
82
]
). We refer to (
9.1
) for the definition.

In order to apply Theorem
8.12
, one must prove uniform lower bounds on the Ricci curvatures of fat structures. In contrast with the Riemannian case, this requires a considerable effort and delicate estimates, which are the object of Section
9
. There, we prove that, when equipped with the natural gauge function
\mathsf{D}
of (
1.14
), compact fat sub-Riemannian metric measure spaces satisfy the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition, for an explicit
\beta
. See Theorem
9.23
for a precise statement, which we anticipate here in a simplified form.

Theorem

(Compact fat structures are
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a compact,
n
-dimensional, fat sub-Riemannian metric measure space. Then for the natural gauge function
\mathsf{D}:M\times M\to[0,+\infty)
of (
1.14
), there exists an explicit distortion coefficient
\beta
such that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{D})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition.

As a direct consequence of this result, we obtain the following, see Corollary
9.24
.

Corollary

(Classical
\mathsf{MCP}
for compact fat structures)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a compact
n
-dimensional sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with fat distribution of rank
k<n
. Then there exists
N^{\prime}\geq 3n-2k
such that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,N^{\prime})
.

The above result removes the real-analytic assumption of the analogous statement by Badreddine and Rifford in
[
18
, Thm. 1.3]
, in the case of fat distributions, obtaining it with a completely different strategy.

Examples and applications

Finally, in Section
10
we present examples and applications of the synthetic theory to sub-Riemannian spaces.

•

In Section
10.1
we illustrate in detail our constructions in the case of the three-dimensional Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
, including the computation of the model distortion coefficients via the comparison theory of Section
8
.

•

In Section
10.2
we show how the Grushin plane enters within the same
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
class of the Heisenberg group. This is natural, considering the fact that the Grushin plane is a quotient of the Heisenberg group (even though it is not a homogeneous space in the classical sense). This can be seen as a generalization of the by-now known fact that the Grushin plane satisfies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
.

•

In Section
10.3
we study a one-parameter family of Riemannian metric measure spaces, namely the canonical variations of the first Heisenberg group
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})
. We discuss in particular the sub-Riemannian limit (
\varepsilon\to 0
) and the adiabatic limit (
\varepsilon\to+\infty
), both occurring within a single
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
class.

•

In Section
10.4
we study the convergence to the tangent cone of sub-Riemannian structures with natural gauge functions, and how the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition behaves under the blow-up process.

•

In Section
10.5
we illustrate the vector-valued theory of Section
6
, in the setting of the three-dimensional left-invariant structures on Lie groups.

3
Synthetic Ricci curvature lower bounds for gauge spaces

3.1
Preliminaries and notation

3.1.1
Convergence of measures

Throughout all the paper,
(X,\mathsf{d})
is a complete and separable metric space. Denote with
\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
the set of Borel measures with values in
[0,+\infty]
which are finite on every bounded subset and with
\mathscr{M}(X)
the collection of all finite Borel measures. Notice that every measure in
\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
is
\sigma
-finite, by the exhaustion
X=\cup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}B_{j}(x)
, for some
x\in X
.

We endow
\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
with the (weak) topology induced by the duality with the space
C_{bs}(X)
of bounded and continuous functions on
X
with bounded support: a sequence
(\mu_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)

converges weakly
1
1

1

Such weak convergence of measures is also known in the literature as “convergence in vague topology”.
to
\mu_{\infty}\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
if

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{X}f\,\mu_{j}=\int_{X}f\,\mu_{\infty},\qquad\forall\,f\in C_{bs}(X).

(3.1)

Let
\mathcal{P}(X)
denote the set of Borel probability measures and
\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d})
the set of probability measures with bounded support.

When
(\mu_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
, the weak convergence (
3.1
) is equivalent to the
narrow
convergence, i.e. convergence in
\mathcal{P}(X)
in duality with the space
C_{b}(X)
of bounded and continuous functions on
X
:

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{X}f\,\mu_{j}=\int_{X}f\,\mu_{\infty},\qquad\forall\,f\in C_{b}(X).

Let us mention that
[
86
]
adopts the convention “weak convergence” for what we denote as “narrow convergence” in
\mathcal{P}(X)
; however, since our convention of “weak” and “narrow” give equivalent notions of convergence on
\mathcal{P}(X)
, there is no ambiguity.

Relative narrow compactness in
\mathcal{P}(X)
can be characterized by Prokhorov’s Theorem. In order to state it, recall that a subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
is said to be
tight
if, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a compact subset
K_{\varepsilon}\subset X
such that

\mu(X\setminus K_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathcal{K}.

(3.2)

Theorem 3.1

(Prokhorov)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be complete and separable, and let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
. Then
\mathcal{K}
is pre-compact in the narrow topology if and only if
\mathcal{K}
is tight.

3.1.2
Optimal transport and
W_{2}
-distance in metric spaces

Recall that if
f:X\to Y
is a Borel map between the separable metric spaces
(X,\mathsf{d}_{X})
,
(Y,\mathsf{d}_{Y})
, then any Borel (resp. probability) measure
\mu
can be
pushed forward
to a Borel (resp. probability) measure
f_{\sharp}\mu
defined as
(f_{\sharp}\mu)(B):=\mu(f^{-1}(B))
for every Borel subset
B\subset Y
. Call
P_{i}:X\times X\to X
,
i=1,2
, the projection on the
i^{th}
factor. Given
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(X)
,
i=1,2
, denote

\mathrm{Cpl}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):=\{\pi\in\mathcal{P}(X\times X)\mid(P_{i})_{\sharp}\pi=\mu_{i},\,i=1,2\}

the set of
admissible couplings
(also called “transference plans”) from
\mu_{1}
to
\mu_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
the subspace of probability measures with finite second moment, i.e.

\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d}):=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)\mid\int_{X}\mathsf{d}(x,\bar{x})^{2}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty\text{ for some (and thus for all) }\bar{x}\in X\right\}.

We endow the space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
with the quadratic (Kantorovich-Rubinstein-Wasserstein) transportation distance
W_{2}
defined as

W_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}:=\inf_{\pi\in\mathrm{Cpl}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{X\times X}\mathsf{d}^{2}(x,y)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y).

A coupling
\pi\in\mathrm{Cpl}(\mu,\nu)
achieving the infimum in the right hand side is called
optimal coupling
from
\mu
to
\nu
, and the set of such optimal couplings is denoted by
{\rm Opt}(\mu,\nu)
. Since
\mathrm{Cpl}(\mu,\nu)
is non-empty and compact in the weak topology, it is easily checked that
{\rm Opt}(\mu,\nu)\neq\emptyset
.

It is well-known that
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d}),W_{2})
is a complete and separable metric space (see e.g.
[
86
]
).
In order to discuss the relation between the narrow and
W_{2}
convergence, recall that a subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
is
2
-uniformly integrable
provided that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{K}}\int_{X\setminus B_{R}(\bar{x})}\mathsf{d}^{2}(x,\bar{x})\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)=0,\qquad\text{for some (and thus for every) }\bar{x}\in X.

For a proof of the following result see for instance
[
86
, Thm. 6.8]
.

Proposition 3.2

(Characterization of
W_{2}
convergence)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a complete and separable metric space and
(\mu_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

(\mu_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is
2
-uniformly integrable and converges narrowly to
\mu_{\infty}
;

•

W_{2}(\mu_{j},\mu_{\infty})\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

We next recall some basics about the geodesic structure of
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d}),W_{2})
, cf.
[
86
]
. A
geodesic
is a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to X
satisfying

\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t})=|s-t|\,\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}),\qquad\forall\,s,t\in[0,1].

(3.3)

In particular, geodesics are length-minimizing and parametrized with constant speed on the interval
[0,1]
.

The space of all geodesics on
(X,\mathsf{d})
is denoted by
\mathrm{Geo}(X)
, which is endowed with the complete and separable distance

\mathsf{d}_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}(\gamma,\eta):=\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{t},\eta_{t}).

(3.4)

Recall that
(X,\mathsf{d})
is said to be a
geodesic space
if every two points in
X
can be joined by a geodesic;
(X,\mathsf{d})
is a geodesic space if and only if
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d}),W_{2})
is so.

A useful procedure is to represent a geodesic in
(\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d}),W_{2})
by a single probability measure defined on
\mathrm{Geo}(X)
. More precisely: for every
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
, there exists
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Geo}(X))
such that
\mu_{t}=({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}(\nu)
for all
t\in[0,1]
, where

{\rm e}_{t}:\mathrm{Geo}(X)\to X,\qquad{\rm e}_{t}(\gamma):=\gamma_{t},

(3.5)

is the evaluation map. Such a measure
\nu
is called an
optimal dynamical plan
from
\mu_{0}
to
\mu_{1}
, the set of which is denoted by
{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.
Given
\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Geo}(X))
, it holds that
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
if and only if
({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu\in{\rm Opt}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
.

A set
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
is
a set of non-branching geodesics
if and only if for any
\gamma^{1},\gamma^{2}\in\Gamma
, it holds:

\exists\;\bar{t}\in(0,1)\text{ such that }\;\forall\,t\in[0,\bar{t}\,]\quad\gamma_{t}^{1}=\gamma_{t}^{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\gamma^{1}_{s}=\gamma^{2}_{s},\quad\forall\,s\in[0,1].

(3.6)

It is clear that if
(X,\mathsf{d})
is a smooth Riemannian manifold, then any subset
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
is a set of non branching geodesics. This is true, more generally, if
(X,\mathsf{d})
is an ideal sub-Riemannian manifold, i.e. admitting no non-trivial abnormal geodesics: in fact, a sub-Riemannian geodesic can branch only if it contains an abnormal segment, cf.
[
68
]
.

We endow the metric space
(X,\mathsf{d})
with a measure
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathscr{M}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X)
, i.e. non-negative and finite on bounded sets. The triple
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is called
metric measure space
(m.m.s. for short). We denote with

\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})\subset\mathcal{P}(X)
the subspace of probability measures which are absolutely continuous with respect to the reference measure
\mathfrak{m}
.

A metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is
essentially non-branching
if and only if for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})\cap\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})
, any
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is concentrated on a set
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
of non-branching geodesics.

3.1.3
Relative entropies

For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)
, we define its relative (Boltzmann-Shannon) entropy by

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=\int_{X}\rho\log(\rho)\,\mathfrak{m},\qquad\text{ if $\mu=\rho\,\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)\cap\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})$},

(3.7)

in case
\rho\log(\rho)\in L^{1}(X,\mathfrak{m})
, otherwise we set
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=+\infty
.

Since
(0,+\infty)\ni x\mapsto x\log x
is convex, Jensen’s inequality gives

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m})\geq-\log\mathfrak{m}(\supp\mu),\qquad\forall\,\mu\in\mathcal{P}(X)\text{ with }\mathfrak{m}(\supp\mu)<\infty.

(3.8)

We set
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m})):=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(X)\mid\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m})\in\mathbb{R}\}
to be the finiteness domain of the entropy and

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d})\mid\supp\,\mu\subseteq\supp\,\mathfrak{m}\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\cap\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m}).

In order to formulate “dimensional” Ricci curvature lower bounds, it is convenient to introduce also the following dimensional entropy (cf.
[
44
]
):

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=\exp\left(-\frac{\mathrm{Ent}(\mu|\mathfrak{m})}{n}\right),\qquad n\in[1,+\infty),

(3.9)

with the understanding that
\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu|\mathfrak{m}):=0
if
\mu\notin\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))
.

3.2
Gauge functions on metric spaces

In order to obtain a unified framework

•

embracing both sub-Riemannian structures and Lott-Sturm-Villani’s
\mathsf{CD}
m.m.s.,

•

yielding sharp geometric and functional inequalities,

we add an additional structure to a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, that is a non-negative Borel function, called
gauge function
:

\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty].

(3.10)

The following analogy explains the role of the gauge function. A Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
has Ricci curvature bounded from below if there exists
K\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for all
x,y\in M
and any geodesic
\gamma
between
x
and
y
it holds

\mathrm{Ric}(\dot{\gamma},\dot{\gamma})\geq K\|\dot{\gamma}\|^{2}=K\,\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{2}.

(3.11)

The distance function
\mathsf{d}
is used in the right hand side as a
gauge
to measure the extent of the lower Ricci curvature bound, quantified by the constant
K
. The idea is to replace the distance
\mathsf{d}
with a general gauge function
\mathsf{G}
for curvature bounds.

Gauge functions will be key in our extension of the synthetic theory of curvature bounds to the sub-Riemannian setting, where it is well-known that the effect of the curvature on transport inequalities is not expressed via the distance but rather via other intrinsically sub-Riemannian functions (a phenomenon observed in
[
19
]
,
[
24
, Sec. 8.1]
).

3.3
Curvature-dimension conditions for gauge spaces

Let
\mathsf{s}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function, with
N\in[1,+\infty)
such that

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=c\,\theta^{N}+o(\theta^{N})\qquad\text{ for some }c>0.

(3.12)

The parameter
N
will be the sharp upper bound for a new notion of dimension, which is in general different from the Hausdorff one, see Section
4.5
.
Denote:

\mathcal{D}:=\inf\{\theta>0\mid\mathsf{s}(\theta)=0\}.

(3.13)

From the assumptions on
\mathsf{s}
it is clear that
\mathcal{D}>0
.
The parameter
\mathcal{D}
will give a sharp upper bound on the gauge function, see Section
4.3
.
Define the
distortion coefficient

\beta_{(\cdot)}(\cdot):[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
as

\displaystyle(t,\theta)\in[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta):=\begin{cases}t^{N}&\theta=0,\\
\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}(\theta)}&0<\theta<\mathcal{D},\\
\displaystyle\liminf_{\phi\to\mathcal{D}^{-}}\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\phi)}{\mathsf{s}(\phi)}&\theta\geq\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(3.14)

Remark 3.3
.

In applications to sub-Riemannian geometry, the function
\mathsf{s}
is chosen in a class of models which are characterized as solutions to suitable ODEs, see Proposition
8.5
and Remark
8.6
. However, here we develop the theory in full generality.

We collect some elementary properties following from the definition.

Proposition 3.4
.

Any distortion coefficient satisfies the following:

(i)

\beta_{0}(\theta)=0
and
\beta_{1}(\theta)=1
for all
\theta\in[0,+\infty]
;

(ii)

if
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, then
\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
for all
\theta\geq\mathcal{D}
and
t\in(0,1)
;

(iii)

\beta_{t}(\theta)=0
for some
\theta<+\infty
if and only if
t=0
;

(iv)

for every
t\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{j},\theta\in[0,+\infty)
, if
\theta_{j}\to\theta
then
\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})\to\beta_{t}(\theta)
in
[0,+\infty]
;

(v)

\beta
is continuous and finite when restricted to
[0,1]\times[0,\mathcal{D})
;

(vi)

for all fixed
t\in(0,1]
, the distortion coefficient
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is bounded from below away from zero on any bounded set of
[0,+\infty)
;

(vii)

assume that
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, and that
\mathsf{s}\in C^{\omega}([0,\mathcal{D}])
. Then there exists
N^{\prime}\geq N
such that
\beta_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{N^{\prime}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,+\infty]
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
n\in[1,+\infty)
, and
\beta
as in (
3.14
). We say that a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with gauge
\mathsf{G}
satisfies:

•

\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
if for all
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
, there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
connecting them, induced by
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, such that it holds

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{1-t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}))\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{0}|\mathfrak{m})\\
+\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(3.15)

with the convention that
\infty\cdot 0=0
.
We say that
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
is satisfied
in the strong sense
if (
3.15
) is satisfied for all
W_{2}
-geodesics connecting
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
.

•

\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
if for any
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\bar{x}\notin\supp\mu_{1}
there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
from
\mu_{0}=\delta_{\bar{x}}
to
\mu_{1}
such that

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x)\big)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(3.16)

for some (and then every)
n\geq 1
.

Remark 3.6
.

Notice that (
3.16
) is equivalent to

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\leq\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m})-\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x)\big)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(3.17)

so that (
3.16
) does not depend on the value of
n
.

Remark 3.7

(
\mathsf{CD}
implies
\mathsf{MCP}
)
.

It is clear that, with our definitions,
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
for some
n\in[1,+\infty)
implies
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, since one can choose in (
3.15
)
\mu_{0}
equal to a Dirac mass.

Remark 3.8

(About the absolute continuity of
\mu_{0}
)
.

Our definition of
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
is not symmetric, in the sense that
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
while
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, and in particular
\mu_{1}\ll\mathfrak{m}
. When
\mathsf{G}
is not continuous, the flexibility to take a non-absolutely continuous
\mu_{0}
turns out to be technically convenient; for instance it makes neat the implication
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, implication which would not be clear otherwise.

On the other hand, if the gauge function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty)
is
continuous
, via a standard approximation argument it is possible to see that the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition of Definition
3.5
is equivalent to the following, symmetric, requirement:
for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
, there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
connecting them, induced by
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, such that (
3.15
) holds.
The latter formulation is closer in spirit to Lott-Sturm-Villani’s curvature-dimension conditions for metric measure spaces; note that in this case
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
is of course continuous.

Remark 3.9

(Geodesic property for
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
and length property for
\supp\mathfrak{m}
)
.

The fact that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
implies directly from the definition that
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
is a geodesic space (with metric
W_{2}
). Moreover if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
or
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, then
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is a length space (with metric
\mathsf{d}
); the proof follows verbatim
[
83
, Remark 4.6, (iii)]
and thus is omitted. We stress that these facts do not use the inequalities (
3.15
) or (
3.16
), but only the existence of
W_{2}
-geodesics between suitable pairs of measures. Moreover, as a consequence of
[
36
, Thm. 1.1]
it follows that: if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is an essentially non-branching
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
space, then
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
is a geodesic space (with metric
W_{2}
). Along the same lines, one can prove that
\mathrm{Dom}(\mathrm{Ent}(\cdot|\mathfrak{m}))\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
is a geodesic space under the assumption that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is an essentially non-branching
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
space.

Fix a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
. The next proposition contains interpolation and monotonicity properties of the
\mathsf{CD}
classes.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\beta_{0},\beta_{1}
distortion coefficients as in (
3.14
), let
n,n_{0},n_{1}\geq 1
, and
\theta\in[0,1]
. Then the following properties hold

(i)

\mathrm{CD}(\beta_{0},n_{0})\cap\mathrm{CD}(\beta_{1},n_{1})
with at least one of the two being satisfied in the strong sense
\Longrightarrow

\mathrm{CD}(\beta_{\theta},n_{\theta})
where we have set

\beta_{\theta}:=\beta_{0}^{1-\theta}\beta_{1}^{\theta},\qquad n_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)n_{0}+\theta n_{1};

(ii)

if
m\geq 0
. Then
\mathrm{CD}(\beta,n)

\Longrightarrow

\mathrm{CD}(t^{m}\beta,n+m)
;

(iii)

if
\beta\geq\alpha
, then
\mathrm{CD}(\beta,n)

\Longrightarrow

\mathrm{CD}(\alpha,n)
.

Proof.

Proof of
(i)
.
Observe that
\mathrm{\mathrm{U}}_{n}=(\mathrm{U}_{m})^{\frac{m}{n}}
for all
n,m\geq 1
. Hence

\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}=\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}^{(1-\theta)}\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}^{\theta}=\mathrm{U}_{n_{0}}^{(1-\theta)\frac{n_{0}}{n_{\theta}}}\mathrm{U}_{n_{1}}^{\theta\frac{n_{1}}{n_{\theta}}}.

(3.18)

Since at least one of the two
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{i},n_{i})
conditions is satisfied in the strong sense, we may assume that for any given
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that

\mathrm{U}_{n_{i}}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n_{i}}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{i,1-t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n_{i}}(\mu_{0}|\mathfrak{m})\\
+\exp\left(\frac{1}{n_{i}}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{i,t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n_{i}}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),\;i=0,1.

(3.19)

For
i=0,1
and
t\in(0,1)
, set

\displaystyle a_{i,t}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{1}{n_{i}}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{i,1-t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right),

\displaystyle b_{i,t}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\frac{1}{n_{i}}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{i,t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right).

Omitting
\mathfrak{m}
from the notation, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}(\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{U}_{n_{0}}(\mu_{t})^{(1-\theta)\tfrac{n_{0}}{n_{\theta}}}\mathrm{U}_{n_{1}}(\mu_{t})^{\theta\tfrac{n_{1}}{n_{\theta}}}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\geq\left[a_{0,t}\mathrm{U}_{n_{0}}(\mu_{0})+b_{0,t}\mathrm{U}_{n_{0}}(\mu_{1})\right]^{(1-\theta)\tfrac{n_{0}}{n_{\theta}}}\left[a_{1,t}\mathrm{U}_{n_{1}}(\mu_{0})+b_{1,t}\mathrm{U}_{n_{1}}(\mu_{1})\right]^{\theta\tfrac{n_{1}}{n_{\theta}}}.

(3.21)

Now we use the Hölder inequality: for
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
we have for positive
x_{j},y_{j}

\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}\right)^{1/p}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{j}\right)^{1/q}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}^{1/p}y_{j}^{1/q}.

(3.22)

Hence we obtain

\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}(\mu_{t})\geq a_{0,t}^{(1-\theta)\tfrac{n_{0}}{n_{\theta}}}a_{1,t}^{\theta\tfrac{n_{1}}{n_{\theta}}}\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}(\mu_{0})+b_{0,t}^{(1-\theta)\tfrac{n_{0}}{n_{\theta}}}b_{1,t}^{\theta\tfrac{n_{1}}{n_{\theta}}}\mathrm{U}_{n_{\theta}}(\mu_{1}).

(3.23)

This corresponds to the desired inequality for the condition
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{\theta},n_{\theta})
.

Proof of
(ii)
.
We observe first that the map
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\log(e^{x}+e^{y})
is convex, and thus for
n\geq 1
,
m\geq 0
, all
t\in(0,1)
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{n}{n+m}\log(e^{x}+e^{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{n+m}\log(e^{x}+e^{y})+\frac{m}{n+m}\log\left(e^{\log(1-t)}+e^{\log t}\right)

(3.24)

\displaystyle\geq\log\left(e^{\tfrac{n}{n+m}x+\tfrac{m}{m+n}\log(1-t)}+e^{\tfrac{n}{n+m}y+\tfrac{m}{n+m}\log t}\right).

(3.25)

Fix a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
for which the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality holds true. Observe that:

\displaystyle\log\mathrm{U}_{n+m}(\mu_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{n+m}\log\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

(3.26)

Using the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality in the previous identity and applying (
3.25
), we immediately obtain the validity of the
\mathsf{CD}(t^{m}\beta,n+m)
condition.

Proof of
(iii)
.
Obvious.
∎

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be essentially non-branching. Assume that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with gauge
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, for
\beta
defined as in (
3.14
) in terms of a function
\mathsf{s}
as in (
3.12
), with order
N
at zero. If
\mathcal{D}
is finite and
\mathsf{s}\in C^{\omega}([0,\mathcal{D}])
, then there exists
N^{\prime}\geq N
such that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(t^{N^{\prime}})
with gauge
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
and thus
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the classical measure contraction property
\mathsf{MCP}(0,N^{\prime})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4
(vii)
, there exists
N^{\prime}\geq N
such that
\beta_{t}\geq t^{N^{\prime}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Hence
\mathsf{MCP}(t^{N^{\prime}})
holds and we conclude by Theorem
3.12
(ii)
.
∎

3.3.1
Recovering the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory

In this section we explain how our general theory of curvature bounds for gauge spaces contains the classical curvature-dimension theory for m.m.s. à la Lott-Sturm-Villani. In this section we consider
K\in\mathbb{R}
and
N\in[1,+\infty)
. Set

[0,+\infty]\ni\theta\mapsto\mathsf{s}_{K,N}(\theta)=\begin{cases}\sin\left(\theta\sqrt{K/N}\right)^{N}&K>0,\\
\theta^{N}&K=0,\\
\sinh\left(\theta\sqrt{-K/N}\right)^{N}&K<0.\end{cases}

(3.27)

Notice that
\mathsf{s}_{K,N}(\theta)
has order
N
as
\theta\to 0
for all
K\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover,

\mathcal{D}_{K,N}=\inf\{\theta>0\mid\mathsf{s}_{K,N}(\theta)=0\}=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{K/N}}&K>0,\\
+\infty&K\leq 0.\end{cases}

(3.28)

The corresponding distortion coefficient, following the recipe of (
3.14
), is then

[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\ni(t,\theta)\mapsto(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(\theta)=\begin{cases}+\infty&K\theta^{2}\geq N\pi^{2},\\
\frac{\sin\left(t\theta\sqrt{K/N}\right)^{N}}{\sin\left(\theta\sqrt{K/N}\right)^{N}}&0<K\theta^{2}<N\pi^{2},\\
t^{N}&K\theta^{2}=0,\\
\frac{\sinh\left(t\theta\sqrt{|K|/N}\right)^{N}}{\sinh\left(\theta\sqrt{|K|/N}\right)^{N}}&K\theta^{2}<0.\end{cases}

(3.29)

The notation is motivated by the fact that

(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(\theta)=\left(\sigma_{K,N}^{(t)}(\theta)\right)^{N},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,+\infty),

(3.30)

where
\sigma_{K,N}^{(\cdot)}(\cdot)
are the functions considered in
[
17
,
44
]
(note that
[
44
]
used the notation
\sigma_{K/N}^{(\cdot)}(\cdot)
). Consider also

[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\ni(t,\theta)\mapsto(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})_{t}(\theta)=\begin{cases}+\infty&K\theta^{2}\geq(N-1)\pi^{2},\\
t\frac{\sin\left(t\theta\sqrt{K/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}}{\sin\left(\theta\sqrt{K/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}}&0<K\theta^{2}<(N-1)\pi^{2},\\
t^{N}&K\theta^{2}=0,\\
t\frac{\sinh\left(t\theta\sqrt{|K|/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}}{\sinh\left(\theta\sqrt{|K|/(N-1)}\right)^{N-1}}&K\theta^{2}<0.\end{cases}

(3.31)

Again, the notation is motivated by the fact that

(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})_{t}(\theta)=\big(\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\theta)\big)^{N},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,+\infty),

(3.32)

where
\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\theta):=t^{1/N}\sigma^{(t)}_{K,N-1}(\theta)^{1-1/N}
are the functions considered in
[
84
]
.

Theorem 3.12
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an essentially non-branching m.m.s. equipped with the gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
. The following hold:

(i)

(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the entropic curvature-dimension condition
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
of Erbar-Kuwada-Sturm
[
44
]
if and only if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)
in the sense of Definition
3.5
;

(ii)

(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the measure contraction property
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
in the formulation of Ohta
[
72
]
if and only if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})
in the sense of Definition
3.5
.

Proof.

Proof of
(i)
,
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)\,\Rightarrow\,\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
.

The map
\theta\mapsto\log\left[(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(\sqrt{\theta})\right]
is convex, as one can check by a direct computation. Using Jensen’s inequality, it follows that for any
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}))\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{N}\log(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}\left(\sqrt{\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)}\right)

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\log\left[(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))\right]

(3.34)

\displaystyle=\log\sigma_{K/N}^{(t)}(W_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}))\,.

(3.35)

Thus, if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is a metric measure space satisfying the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)
condition for the Gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
, then the entropic
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
convexity inequality of
[
44
]
is satisfied for measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
. A straightforward approximation argument, yields the validity of the
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
convexity inequality for measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\mathfrak{m}(\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1})=0
. At this point, one can follow verbatim the proof of
[
44
, Thm. 3.12, (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)]
(noticing that there the
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
convexity inequality is used only for measures whose supports have
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible intersection) and infer that for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
(without any condition on the intersection of the supports) there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
t\in(0,1)
and

\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{-\frac{1}{N}}\geq\sigma_{K/N}^{(1-t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{-\frac{1}{N}}+\sigma_{K/N}^{(t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{-\frac{1}{N}},

(3.36)

for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X)
, where
\rho_{t}
is the density of
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{m}
. Using now
[
44
, Thm. 3.12, (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii)]
we infer that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the entropic curvature-dimension condition
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
.

Proof of
(i)
,
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)\,\Rightarrow\,\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)
.

Conversely, if
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is essentially non-branching and it satisfies
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
, then from
[
44
, Thm. 3.12, (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)]
we know that for any
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
t\in(0,1)
and (
3.36
) hold for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X)
, where
\rho_{t}
is the density of
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{m}
. With the same argument one can prove that (
3.36
) holds more generally when
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, up to removing the first term in the right hand side (we will assume this convention throughout the proof).
We rewrite (
3.36
) as

-\frac{1}{N}\log\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})\geq\log\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{N}\log\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})+\log a_{1-t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\right)\right.\\
\left.+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{N}\log\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})+\log a_{t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\right)\right],

(3.37)

where we set
a_{t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})=\sigma_{K/N}^{(t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))
. Recalling that the map
(x,y)\mapsto\log(e^{x}+e^{y})
is convex, integrating (
3.37
) w.r.t.
\nu
and using Jensen’s inequality, we obtain

-\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\log\left[\exp\left(-\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{0}|\mathfrak{m})+\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log a_{1-t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\right.\\
\left.+\exp\left(-\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m})+\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log a_{t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\right].

(3.38)

Taking the exponential of (
3.38
) and recalling (
3.30
), we obtain that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
endowed with the gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)
condition (
3.15
).

Proof of
(ii)
,
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
.
Using (
3.32
) and arguing along the lines of the proof of
[
44
, Thm. 3.12, (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)]
, one uses the essential non-branching assumption to localize the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})
integral estimate to a pointwise estimate along geodesics. More precisely, one can show that for every
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and every
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\supp\mu_{1}
there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{x_{0}},\mu_{1})
such that
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
t\in(0,1)
and

\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})\leq\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))^{-N}\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1}),\qquad\nu\text{-a.e. }\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X),

(3.39)

where
\rho_{t}
denotes the density of
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
w.r.t.
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
A
be a bounded Borel set with positive measure and let
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\bar{A}
, where
\bar{A}
is the closure of
A
. Letting
\mu_{1}:=\mathfrak{m}(A)^{-1}\,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner_{A}
and applying (
3.39
), one gets

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})}\geq\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))^{N}\;\mathfrak{m}(A),\qquad\nu\text{-a.e. }\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X).

(3.40)

Let now
E\subset X
be an arbitrary Borel set. Integrating the last inequality over
{\rm e}_{t}^{-1}(E)
and recalling that
({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu=\rho_{t}\,\mathfrak{m}
, yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(E)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\rm e}_{t}^{-1}(E)}\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})}\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{{\rm e}_{t}^{-1}(E)}\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))^{N}\;\mathfrak{m}(A)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma).

One thus obtains Ohta’s
[
72
]
formulation of
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)

\mathfrak{m}\geq({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\left(\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}\big(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)^{N}\mathfrak{m}(A)\,\nu\right),

(3.41)

with the caveat
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\bar{A}
and
A
bounded. The general case follows by a standard approximation argument, that we sketch below.

Observing that the distance is a
meek
gauge function in the sense of Definition
4.23
, the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
holds. We infer that
\mathfrak{m}(\{x\})=0
for every
x\in X
and that
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is a proper metric space (see Corollary
4.16
).

Let
A
be a bounded Borel set with positive measure and let
x_{0}\in\bar{A}\,\cap\,\supp\mathfrak{m}
. For
\varepsilon>0
small enough we have that
\mathfrak{m}(A\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}))>0
. Set

\mu_{1,\varepsilon}:=(\mathfrak{m}(A\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})))^{-1}\,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner_{A\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})}.

(3.42)

From the discussion above, we know that there exists
\nu_{\varepsilon}\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{x_{0}},\mu_{1,\varepsilon})
such that

\mathfrak{m}\geq({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\left(\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}\big(\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)^{N}\mathfrak{m}\big(A\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\big)\nu_{\varepsilon}\right).

(3.43)

By letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, by stability of optimal transport
[
86
, Thm. 28.9]
and using that
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is a proper metric space, one obtains that there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{x_{0}},\mu_{1})
such that (
3.41
) holds. For a general Borel set
A
of finite positive measure: intersect
A
with the metric ball
B_{R}(x_{0})
, obtain (
3.41
) and take the limit as
R\uparrow+\infty
to conclude.

Proof of
(ii)
,
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})
.
Conversely, it is standard to check that (
3.41
) implies (
3.39
)
(see for instance
[
36
, App. A]
). Arguing along the lines of
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)\,\Rightarrow\,\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma},N)
, we obtain that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
endowed with the gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})
condition (
3.16
).
∎

Remark 3.13

(Connection with other synthetic formulations)
.

Let
K\in\mathbb{R},N\in[1,+\infty)
. In case
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is essentially non-branching:

•

It was proved in
[
44
, Thm. 3.12]
that the reduced
\mathsf{CD}^{*}(K,N)
condition (see
[
17
]
) is equivalent to the entropic
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
.

•

The
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
condition (as in
[
84
]
) is equivalent to
\mathsf{CD}^{*}(K,N)
. This is a deep result establishing the local-to-global property for
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
; it was proved in
[
35
]
in case
\mathfrak{m}(X)<\infty
.
Their approach is based on the
needle decomposition
of
[
58
]
and the
localization technique
developed in
[
37
]
.

•

Ohta’s
[
72
]
and Sturm’s
[
84
]
formulations of
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
are equivalent, see for instance
[
36
, App. A]
.

•

Our theory is modeled on the convexity inequalities for the entropic curvature-dimension condition à la Erbar-Kuwada-Sturm
[
44
]
, that involves the functional
\mathrm{U}_{N}
. One could have developed the theory studying instead the Renyi entropy functional, as in
[
84
,
17
]
, or general functionals in the McCann class
\mathcal{DC}(N)
as in
[
65
,
64
]
, with analogous outcomes. For a discussion of connections between the approach in
[
65
,
64
]
and the
\mathsf{CD}^{*}(K,N)
condition see for instance
[
14
, Sec. 9]
.

Remark 3.14

(Connection with E. Milman’s theory)
.

In
[
69
]
, E. Milman introduced a quasi-convex relaxation of the Lott–Sturm–Villani’s
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
, called the Quasi Curvature-Dimension condition
\mathsf{QCD}(Q,K,N)
, see
[
69
, Def. 2.3]
.
Q\geq 1
is an auxiliary parameter so that for
Q=1
the
\mathsf{QCD}(1,K,N)
is equivalent to the
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
, and it is strictly weaker than the latter for
Q>1
. With the same arguments used in the proof of Theorem
3.12
, for essentially non-branching m.m.s., the
\mathsf{QCD}(Q,K,N)
for
N=n
corresponds to the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality (
3.16
) with gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
, and

\beta_{t}(\theta)=\frac{1}{Q}(\beta^{\tau}_{K,n})_{t}(\theta),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,+\infty),

(3.44)

where
(\beta^{\tau}_{K,n})_{t}
is the same coefficient (
3.32
) of the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory. Formally the coefficient (
3.44
) is not of the form (
3.14
) due to the extra factor
1/Q
. However, all results of Section
4
hold with the same proofs for this more general class of coefficients, up to keeping track of this extra factor in the statements.

On the other hand the finer Curvature-Geodesic-Topological-Dimension condition
\mathsf{CGTD}(K,N,n)
introduced by E.  Milman in
[
69
, Sec. 7]
, for
K\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\geq 1
and
N\geq n
corresponds to the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau},n)
for gauge function
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
.

Notice that for
Q=1
and
N=n
,
\mathsf{CGTD}(K,N,n)
and
\mathsf{QCD}(Q,K,N)
are equivalent, and in turn equivalent to the classical
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
.

3.3.2
Recovering the Balogh-Kristály-Sipos’ theory

In this section we show how the general theory introduced above also contains the one developed in
[
19
]
for Heisenberg groups.

Let
d\geq 1
. The Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
is the non-commutative group structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}
given, in coordinates
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d},y_{1},\ldots,y_{d},z)
, by the law

(x,y,z)\star(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{2}(x_{i}y_{i}^{\prime}-y_{i}x_{i}^{\prime})\right).

(3.45)

Consider the
2d
left-invariant vector fields

X_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}-\frac{y_{i}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\qquad Y_{i}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}}+\frac{x_{i}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\qquad i=1,\ldots,d,

(3.46)

and the left-invariant metric making
\{X_{i},Y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
a global orthonormal frame. We equip
\mathbb{H}^{d}
with the associated sub-Riemannian (Carnot-Carathéodory) distance, denoted by
\mathsf{d}_{cc}
(see Appendix
A
), and the Lebesgue measure
\mathscr{L}^{2d+1}
, which is a Haar measure.

We set

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=\theta\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2d-1}\left[\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right],\qquad\theta\in[0,+\infty).

(3.47)

Notice that
\mathsf{s}(\theta)
has order
N=2d+3
as
\theta\to 0
. Moreover, it holds

\mathcal{D}=\inf\{\theta>0\mid\mathsf{s}_{d}(\theta)=0\}=2\pi.

(3.48)

The corresponding distortion coefficient, following the recipe of (
3.14
), is then

[0,1]\times[0,+\infty]\ni(t,\theta)\mapsto\\
\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{t}(\theta)=\begin{cases}t^{2d+3}&\theta=0,\\
t\dfrac{\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)^{2d-1}\left[\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{t\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\right]}{\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)^{2d-1}\left[\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right]}&0<\theta<2\pi,\\
+\infty&\theta\geq 2\pi,t\neq 0,\\
0&\theta\geq 2\pi,t=0.\\
\end{cases}

(3.49)

We highlight in particular the following identity

\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{t}(\theta)=(\tau_{t}^{d}(\theta))^{2d+1},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,2\pi],

(3.50)

where
\tau_{s}^{d}(\theta)
is the function considered in
[
19
, Eq. (1.6)]
.

The main result
[
19
, Thm. 1.1]
is a Jacobian determinant inequality which permits by standard manipulations to obtain an interpolation inequality for optimal transport, see
[
19
, Eq. (3.17), p. 61]
. More precisely, for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(\mathbb{H}^{d},\mathsf{d}_{cc},\mathscr{L}^{2d+1})
, there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
associated with a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
\mu_{t}\ll\mathscr{L}^{2d+1}
for all
t\in(0,1]
, for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma
it holds
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
for all
t\in(0,1]
, and

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/(2d+1)}}\geq\frac{\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{1-t}(\theta^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}})^{1/(2d+1)}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/(2d+1)}}+\frac{\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{t}(\theta^{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}})^{1/(2d+1)}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/(2d+1)}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(3.51)

where
\rho_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{L}^{2d+1}}
, and
\theta^{x,y}
is the vertical norm of the covector associated with the geodesic joining
x
and
y
, for
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{d})
. In (
3.51
) we use the convention that if
\mu_{0}
is not absolutely continuous then the first term in the right hand side is omitted.

Using the definition of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
, the convexity of the map
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\log(e^{x}+e^{y})
and Jensen’s inequality, one establishes the validity of the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,2d+1)
, with
\beta=\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}
.

We stress that the argument in the r.h.s. of (
3.51
) does not depend on the distance. It is then natural to set as a gauge function any map
\mathsf{G}:\mathbb{H}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}^{d}\to[0,+\infty]
such that

\mathsf{G}(x,y):=\theta^{x,y},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{d}).

(3.52)

Then the results of
[
19
]
can be restated within our framework as follows.

Theorem 3.15
.

The gauge m.m.s.
(\mathbb{H}^{d},\mathsf{d}_{cc},\mathscr{L}^{2d+1},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}},2d+1)
.

It is not hard to check that
\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}}_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{2d+3}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
. Therefore, by Proposition
3.10
(iii)
, the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{d}},2d+1)
implies the
\mathsf{CD}(t^{2d+3},2d+1)
. In turn, the latter implies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,2d+3)
, originally established for
\mathbb{H}^{d}
by Juillet in
[
54
]
.

4
Geometric consequences

In this section we establish some geometric properties implied by the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition (compare with
[
44
,
84
,
65
]
for the Lott-Sturm-Villani’s
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
spaces).

4.1
Generalized Brunn-Minkowski inequality

Given two Borel sets
A_{0},A_{1}\subset X
, denote with
A_{t}
the set of
t
-intermediate points, i.e.

A_{t}:=Z_{t}(A_{0},A_{1})=\{\gamma_{t}\mid\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X),\,\gamma_{0}\in A_{0},\,\gamma_{1}\in A_{1}\}.

(4.1)

It is well-known that
A_{t}
is a Suslin set. For non-empty Borel sets
A_{0},A_{1}\subset X
, denote

\beta_{t}(A_{0},A_{1}):=\sup_{\tilde{A}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}}\inf\{\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x_{1}))\mid x_{i}\in\tilde{A}_{i}\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}\},

(4.2)

where the sup is taken over all full-measure non-empty subsets
\tilde{A}_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
, for
i=0,1
.
It is clear from the definition (
4.2
) that

\mathfrak{m}(A_{0}\triangle\tilde{A}_{0})=\mathfrak{m}(A_{1}\triangle\tilde{A}_{1})=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\beta_{t}(A_{0},A_{1})=\beta_{t}(\tilde{A}_{0},\tilde{A}_{1})\,,

(4.3)

where
A_{0},\tilde{A}_{0},A_{1},\tilde{A}_{1}\subset X
are Borel sets and
\triangle
denotes the symmetric difference of sets.

Theorem 4.1

(Generalized Brunn-Minkowski inequality)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition, with
n\in[1,+\infty)
and
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Let
A_{0},A_{1}\subset X
be Borel sets with
\mathfrak{m}(A_{0}),\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})>0
and
\mathfrak{m}(A_{0}\cap A_{1})=0
. Then

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})^{1/n}\geq\beta_{1-t}(A_{1},A_{0})^{1/n}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(A_{0})^{1/n}+\beta_{t}(A_{0},A_{1})^{1/n}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})^{1/n},\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(4.4)

where
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
is the completion of
\mathfrak{m}
,
A_{t}
is the set of
t
-intermediate points (
4.1
), and with the convention that
0\cdot\infty=0
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathfrak{m}(A_{i})<+\infty
for
i=0,1
. We first reduce the proof to the bounded and disjoint case. Since
\mathfrak{m}(A_{i})<+\infty
and
\mathfrak{m}(A_{0}\cap A_{1})=0
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
A_{i,\varepsilon}\subset A_{i}
which is closed and bounded,
A_{0,\varepsilon}\cap A_{1,\varepsilon}=\emptyset
, and
\mathfrak{m}(A_{i,\varepsilon})\to\mathfrak{m}(A_{i})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Furthermore, setting
A_{t,\varepsilon}:=Z_{t}(A_{0,\varepsilon},A_{1,\varepsilon})
the set of
t
-intermediate points from
A_{0,\varepsilon}
to
A_{1,\varepsilon}
, it holds

\beta_{t}(A_{0,\varepsilon},A_{1,\varepsilon})\geq\beta_{t}(A_{0},A_{1}),\qquad\beta_{t}(A_{1,\varepsilon},A_{0,\varepsilon})\geq\beta_{t}(A_{1},A_{0}),\qquad A_{t}\supset A_{t,\varepsilon}.

(4.5)

Thus it is sufficient to prove (
4.4
) for closed and bounded sets
A_{0},A_{1}
with
A_{0}\cap A_{1}=\emptyset
.
Let
\mu_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{i})}\mathfrak{m}\llcorner_{A_{i}}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
for
i=0,1
and note that
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
. Observe that
\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{i})=\mathfrak{m}(A_{i})^{1/n}
. By assumption, there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
joining them, and satisfying the inequality (
3.15
). We remark that
\mu_{t}
is concentrated on
A_{t}
, which is a Suslin set. Using Jensen’s inequality twice, we obtain

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t})=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{n}\int_{X}\log\,\rho_{t}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}\right)\leq\int_{X}\rho_{t}^{-1/n}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}=\int_{A_{t}}\rho_{t}^{1-\frac{1}{n}}\mathrm{d}\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\leq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})^{1/n}.

(4.6)

Then (
4.4
) for sets of finite measure follows directly from the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality (
3.15
), and from optimizing with respect to full-measure subsets of
A_{i}
.

Since
\mathfrak{m}
is
\sigma
-finite, we can prove the result for sets of possibly infinite measure by approximating them with a monotone sequence of sets with finite measure.
∎

With analogous arguments one obtains the next generalized half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, which will play a pivotal role in geometric applications.

Theorem 4.2

(Generalized half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition, with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Let
A
be a Borel set with
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
, and
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathfrak{m}(\{\bar{x}\}\cap A)=0
. Then

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})\geq\beta_{t}(\bar{x},A)\cdot\mathfrak{m}(A),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(4.7)

where
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
is the completion of
\mathfrak{m}
,
A_{t}
denotes the set of
t
-intermediate points (
4.1
) between
A_{0}=\{\bar{x}\}
and
A_{1}=A
, and with the convention that
0\cdot\infty=0
.

Remark 4.3
.

In case
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is not a singleton, the condition
\mathfrak{m}(\{x\})=0
for every
x\in X
is very natural: indeed it is satisfied for all smooth measures typically employed in sub-Riemannian geometry, and it is implied by the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
(ii)
under the assumption that
\mathsf{G}
is meek (see Definition
4.23
) and that the natural assumption (
4.54
) holds (see also Remark
4.26
). Recall that
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
spaces (for
N\in[1,+\infty)
,
K\in\mathbb{R}
), thus including all
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
ones, satisfy this condition.

Remark 4.4
.

Let
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a
W_{2}
-geodesic between the uniform distributions on
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
, for which the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality (
3.15
) holds. Then in the l.h.s. of (
4.4
) one can replace
A_{t}
with any (possibly smaller) measurable set on which
\mu_{t}
is concentrated. An analogous remark applies for the l.h.s. of (
4.7
).

If the distortion coefficients are non-increasing or non-decreasing, then the above inequalities can be expressed in terms of the minimal/maximal value of the gauge function
\mathsf{G}
on an appropriate set. More precisely, if
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing or non-increasing for all
t\in(0,1)
, then letting
