\Theta:=\begin{cases}\inf\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x_{1})\mid x_{i}\in A_{i}\cap\supp\mathfrak{m},\;i=1,2\}&\text{if $\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)$ is non-decreasing},\\
\sup\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x_{1})\mid x_{i}\in A_{i}\cap\supp\mathfrak{m},\;i=1,2\}&\text{if $\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)$ is non-increasing},\end{cases}

(4.8)

it holds

\beta_{t}(A_{0},A_{1})\geq\beta_{t}(\Theta).

(4.9)

This observation, together with the fact that the classical distortion coefficients

(\beta_{K,N}^{\tau})_{t}(\theta)=\tau_{K,N}^{(t)}(\theta)^{N},\qquad\text{resp.}\qquad(\beta_{K,N}^{\sigma})_{t}(\theta)=\sigma_{K/N}^{(t)}(\theta)^{N},

(4.10)

are non-increasing or non-decreasing (according to the sign of
K
), yields the familiar forms of the generalized Brunn-Minkowski inequalities for classical
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
spaces
[
84
]
(resp. for classical
\mathsf{CD}^{*}(K,N)
or
\mathsf{CD}^{e}(K,N)
spaces
[
17
,
44
]
; see also Theorem
3.12
and Remark
3.13
).

4.2
MCP implies CD for spaces supporting interpolation inequalities

The implication
\mathsf{CD}\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCP}
is trivial with our definitions. Here we discuss the converse implication. The result of this section can be applied to ideal sub-Riemannian manifolds of topological dimension
n
, that support interpolation inequalities for densities as in
[
24
]
. This includes, of course, all
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds
[
42
]
.

Definition 4.5
.

We say that a m.m.s.
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})

supports interpolation inequalities for densities, with dimensional parameter
n\in[1,+\infty)
, if for any
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
there exists a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, induced by
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that
\mu_{t}\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
t\in(0,1]
, and letting
\rho_{t}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}
be the corresponding density, it holds for all
t\in(0,1)
:

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/n}}\geq\frac{\beta_{1-t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})^{1/n}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/n}}+\frac{\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{1/n}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/n}},\qquad\nu\text{-a.e.}\ \gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X),

(4.11)

with the understanding that the first term in the right hand side of (
4.11
) is omitted if
\mu_{0}\notin\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})
.

In (
4.11
),
\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}
denotes the “true” distortion coefficient of the metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, of which we recall the definition.

Definition 4.6
.

The
distortion coefficient
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is the map
\beta_{(\cdot)}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\cdot,\cdot):[0,1]\times X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
defined by

\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y):=\limsup_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(Z_{t}(\{x\},B_{r}(y)))}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(y))},\qquad\forall\,x,y\in X,\quad t\in[0,1],

(4.12)

where
Z_{t}(A,B)
denotes the set of
t
-midpoints between two Borel sets as in (
4.1
), and
\bar{\mathfrak{m}}
denotes the completion of
\mathfrak{m}
.

Theorem 4.7

(
\mathsf{MCP}
to
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. that supports interpolation inequalities for densities, with dimensional parameter
n\in[1,+\infty)
. Let
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
be a gauge function. Assume that:

•

each
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
occurring in Definition
4.5
is concentrated on a set of geodesics whose endpoints
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
and
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})
are continuity points for
\mathsf{G}
;

•

(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, with
\beta
as in (
3.14
), and with
N\geq n
.

Then
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
also satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.

Proof.

Let
x\neq y\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
with
(x,y)
a continuity point of
\mathsf{G}
, and let
r<\mathsf{d}(x,y)
.
We use the half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
spaces of Proposition
4.2
, for
\bar{x}=x
,
A=B_{r}(y)
. It holds, for all
t\in[0,1]
,

\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(Z_{t}(\{x\},B_{r}(y)))}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(y))}\geq\beta_{t}(x,B_{r}(y))\geq\inf\{\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x,z))\mid z\in B_{r}(y)\}.

(4.13)

Taking the
\limsup
for
r\to 0^{+}
, and using the continuity of
\mathsf{G}
at
(x,y)
, we find a sequence
\theta_{j}\to\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in[0,+\infty]
such that

\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq\lim_{j}\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})=\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

(4.14)

Let
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be as in Definition
4.5
. Assume for simplicity that
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})
(the argument works verbatim if
\mu_{0}\notin\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X,\mathfrak{m})
by omitting the corresponding term in all inequalities). By our assumption,
\nu
is concentrated on a set of geodesics
\gamma
such that
\mathsf{G}
is continuous at
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
and
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})
.

For
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma
we can plug (
4.14
) in (
4.11
), finding that for all
t\in(0,1)
it holds

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/n}}\geq\frac{\beta_{1-t}(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}))^{1/n}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/n}}+\frac{\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))^{1/n}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/n}},\qquad\nu\text{-a.e.}\ \gamma.

(4.15)

Using the definition of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
, the convexity of the map
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\log(e^{x}+e^{y})
and Jensen’s inequality, we obtain the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

For Riemannian manifolds, the classical
\mathsf{CD}(K,n)
(resp.
\mathsf{MCP}(K,n)
) corresponds to the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\tau}_{K,n},n)
(resp.
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta^{\tau}_{K,n})
), see Remark
3.13
. Thus Theorem
4.7
is consistent with the well-known fact that, on
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifolds,
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
implies
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
if
N=n
.

4.3
Gauge diameter estimate

Definition 4.9

(
\mathsf{G}
-diameter)
.

The
\mathsf{G}
-diameter
of a non-empty set
S
is defined by:

\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)=\sup_{x\in S}\left(\mathfrak{m}\shortminus\esssup_{y\in S,\,y\neq x}\mathsf{G}(x,y)\right).

(4.16)

We say that
S\subset X
is
\mathsf{G}
-bounded
if it is non-empty and it has finite
\mathsf{G}
-diameter.

Recall that the parameter
\mathcal{D}
in (
3.13
), when finite, characterizes the blow-up of
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
, for
t\in(0,1)
.

Proposition 4.10

(
\mathsf{G}
-diameter estimate)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
. Let
\beta
be as in (
3.14
) and assume that
\beta_{t}(+\infty)=+\infty
for all
t\in(0,1)
. Note that this is in particular the case if
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
.

•

If
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, then for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds

\mathsf{G}(x,y)<\mathcal{D},\qquad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e. }y\in X,\quad y\neq x.

(4.17)

In particular,
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})\leq\mathcal{D}
.

•

If
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
for some
n\in[1,+\infty)
, then

\mathsf{G}(x,y)<\mathcal{D},\qquad\pi\text{-a.e. }(x,y)\in X\times X.

(4.18)

for all
\pi=({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu
, where
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is such that inequality (
3.15
) holds.

Remark 4.11
.

We remark that the case
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
is included in the statement of Proposition
4.10
. In this case,
\mathfrak{m}(\{y\in X\mid y\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,y)=+\infty\})=0
for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
. This implies the non-triviality condition (
4.41
) considered below.

Proof.

Fix
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
. We show that the set
A=\{y\in X\mid y\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq\mathcal{D}\}\cap B_{R}(x)
has zero measure for all
R>0
. By contradiction, assume that
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
. Then we apply the half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality of Theorem
4.2
to the set
A
. Notice that
\beta_{t}(x,A)=+\infty
for all
t\in(0,1)
, and that
A_{t}\subset(B_{R}(x))_{t}\subset B_{tR}(x)
. We obtain hence

\mathfrak{m}(B_{tR}(x))\geq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})=+\infty,\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(4.19)

contradicting the local finiteness of
\mathfrak{m}
.

To prove the second part of the proposition, we argue in a similar way: let
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
as in the statement (in particular
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
have bounded support) such that
\pi(\{(x,y)\in X\times X\mid\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq\mathcal{D}\})>0
, and using (
3.15
) we obtain that, for the corresponding
W_{2}
-geodesic
\mu_{t}=({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
it holds

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})=+\infty,\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(4.20)

This implies, by (
3.8
), that
\mathfrak{m}(\supp\mu_{t})=+\infty
. However, since
\supp\mu_{0}
and
\supp\mu_{1}
are bounded, then also
\supp\mu_{t}
is bounded, yielding a contradiction.
∎

For some interesting cases (cf. Section
7
), it holds
\mathsf{G}\geq\mathsf{d}
. In this case, Proposition
4.10
admits the following Bonnet-Myers type result.

Corollary 4.12

(Bonnet-Myers)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Assume that
\mathsf{G}\geq\mathsf{d}
. Then
\diam(\supp\mathfrak{m})\leq\mathcal{D}
, and if
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
then
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is compact.

Proof.

The estimate on the diameter is immediate by continuity of
\mathsf{d}
. If
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, then
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is bounded, and by Proposition
4.10
it is also
\mathsf{G}
-bounded. By Corollary
4.16
,
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is totally bounded, complete, and thus compact.
∎

4.4
Doubling inequalities for balls

We first prove a local doubling inequality for metric balls under minimal assumptions. We stress that “locality” in Proposition
4.13
is measured with respect to the gauge function, which also determines the doubling constant. For this reason, and without additional relations between
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{d}
, the general form of the statement is subtly different with respect to the classical one.

Proposition 4.13

(Local doubling)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
3.14
).
Then any
\mathsf{G}
-bounded Borel subset
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
satisfies the following inequality: for all
t\in(0,1)
there exists
C_{S,t}>0
such that

\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\leq C_{S,t}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(B_{tr}(x_{0})),\qquad\forall\,r\geq 0,\;\forall\,x_{0}\in S.

(4.21)

The constant
C_{S,t}
can be estimated in terms of
\beta
and
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)
:

\frac{1}{C_{S,t}}=\inf\{\beta_{t}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)]\}\in(0,t^{N}].

(4.22)

Proof.

Fix
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
and let
\mathsf{G}_{0}:=\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)<\infty
. We let

\frac{1}{C_{S,t}}:=\inf\{\beta_{t}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,\mathsf{G}_{0}]\}\in(0,t^{N}].

(4.23)

Here, the upper bound is a consequence of the value of
\beta_{t}(0)=t^{N}
in (
3.14
), while the lower bound follows from Proposition
3.4
(vi)
. The case
r=0
of (
4.21
) is trivial, so let
r>0
.

Let
x_{0}\in S
, and let
\check{B}_{r}(x_{0}):=B_{r}(x_{0})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
. Let
A=\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S
. If
\mathfrak{m}(A)=0
there is nothing to prove. Assume then that
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
, and note that
\mathfrak{m}(A\cap\{x_{0}\})=0
. Thus we can apply the half-Brunn-Minkowski estimate of Theorem
4.2
with
\bar{x}=x_{0}
and
A=\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S
. Since
A_{t}\subseteq\check{B}_{tr}(x_{0})
, we have for all
t\in(0,1)
:

\mathfrak{m}(\check{B}_{tr}(x_{0}))\geq\beta_{t}(x_{0},\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\cdot\mathfrak{m}(\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\geq\tfrac{1}{C_{S,t}}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S).

(4.24)

We justify the bound
\beta_{t}(x_{0},\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\geq 1/C_{S,t}
. To do it, for any
x\in S
and any non-empty Borel set
U\subseteq S
, recall the definition (
4.2
), which for our case reduces to

\beta_{t}(x,U)=\sup_{\tilde{U}\subseteq U}\inf\{\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))\mid y\in\tilde{U}\},

(4.25)

where
\tilde{U}
ranges over all full-measure and non-empty subsets of
U
. Assume that
x\in S\setminus U
. Since
U\subseteq S
and the latter has bounded
\mathsf{G}
-diameter (see Definition
4.9
), there exists a full-measure set
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
such that
\mathsf{G}(x,y)\leq\mathsf{G}_{0}
for all
y\in U^{\prime}
, and in particular it holds

\beta_{t}(x,U)\geq\inf\{\beta_{t}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,\mathsf{G}_{0}]\}=\frac{1}{C_{S,t}}.

(4.26)

In particular the above inequality holds for
x=x_{0}
and
U=\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S
as required.

Thus we have proved (
4.21
) for
\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})
in place of
B_{r}(x_{0})
, that is

\mathfrak{m}(\check{B}_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\leq C_{S,t}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(\check{B}_{tr}(x_{0})),\quad\forall\,r\geq 0,\;\forall\,x_{0}\in S.

(4.27)

Since
C_{S,t}\geq 1/t^{N}\geq 1
, we add
\mathfrak{m}(\{x_{0}\}\cap S)
to both sides to obtain (
4.21
).
∎

In some natural applications, bounded sets are
\mathsf{G}
-bounded, cf. Section
7
. In this case Proposition
4.13
can be restated as a classical doubling inequality.

Corollary 4.14

(Classical local doubling)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Assume that bounded subsets of
\supp\mathfrak{m}
are
\mathsf{G}
-bounded. Then for any bounded set
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
R>0
there exists
C=C(S,R)>0
such that

\mathfrak{m}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))\leq C\cdot\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(x_{0})),\qquad\forall\,r\in[0,R],\;\forall\,x_{0}\in S.

(4.28)

Proof.

Let
R_{0}
be the diameter of
S
, and let
S^{\prime}:=B_{2R+R_{0}}(\bar{x})
be a ball centered in any point
\bar{x}\in S
. Of course any ball
B_{2r}(x_{0})
with
r\in[0,R]
and
x_{0}\in S
is contained in
S^{\prime}
. By our additional hypothesis,
S^{\prime}
is also
\mathsf{G}
-bounded. We apply then Proposition
4.13
to the set
S^{\prime}
with
t=1/2
and get the claim with
C=C_{S^{\prime},1/2}
.
∎

Finally, if
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})
is finite, or if
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing, then we have a classical global doubling inequality.

Corollary 4.15

(Classical global doubling)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Assume that
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})
is finite, or that
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathfrak{m}(B_{2r}(x_{0}))\leq C\cdot\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(x_{0})),\qquad\forall\,r\in[0,+\infty),\;\forall\,x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(4.29)

Proof.

In both cases, using the same argument of the proof of Proposition
4.13
, taking
S=\supp\mathfrak{m}
, we have that

\beta_{t}(x_{0},B_{r}(x_{0}))\geq\frac{1}{C(t)}>0,

(4.30)

for a constant
C(t)>0
that does not depend on
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
or
r\in[0,+\infty)
: if
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})
is finite (
4.30
) follows from the definition of
\beta_{t}(x_{0},B_{r}(x_{0}))
and Proposition
3.4
(vi)
, while if
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing the lower bound is given by
\beta_{t}(0)=t^{N}
.
∎

We also record a standard consequence that will be useful in the following.

Corollary 4.16

(Proper geodesic space)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Assume that bounded subsets of
\supp\mathfrak{m}
are
\mathsf{G}
-bounded, or
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing. Then
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is a proper, geodesic space.

Proof.

The proof follows the classical strategy. Assume first that bounded subsets of
\supp\mathfrak{m}
are
\mathsf{G}
-bounded. Let
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
L>0
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
be a collection of
k\in\mathbb{N}
points in
B_{L}(x_{0})
, and
\varepsilon>0
. If
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})\subseteq B_{L}(x_{0})
for all
i=1,\dots,k
are pairwise disjoint, it holds

\mathfrak{m}(B_{L}(x_{0}))\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{m}(B_{\varepsilon}(x_{i}))\geq\frac{1}{C_{S,t}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{m}(B_{2L}(x_{i})\cap S)\geq\frac{k}{C_{S,t}}\mathfrak{m}(B_{L}(x_{0})),

(4.31)

where we applied Proposition
4.13
with
S=B_{3L}(x_{0})
and
t=\varepsilon/2L
. Thus, the maximal number of pairwise disjoint balls of radius
\varepsilon>0
contained in
B_{L}(x_{0})
is bounded by

k\leq C_{S,t}<+\infty,\qquad\text{with}\quad S=B_{3L}(x_{0}),\quad t=\varepsilon/2L,

(4.32)

where
C_{S,t}
is the constant of (
4.22
). As a consequence, the ball
B_{L}(x_{0})
can be covered by
C_{S,t}
metric balls of radius
2\varepsilon
. Since
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
L>0
were arbitrary, we obtain that balls in
\supp\mathfrak{m}
are totally bounded, hence their closure is compact. In other words the metric space
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is proper. Since
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is a length space by the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition (cf. Remark
3.9
), it is also geodesic.

If
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-decreasing, the result is obtained by the same argument but using, instead, the classical global doubling inequality of Corollary
4.15
.
∎

Remark 4.17
.

We notice that as a by-product of the proof of Corollary
4.16
, any
\mathsf{G}
-bounded metric ball
B_{L}(x_{0})
with
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
can be covered by a finite number of balls of radius
\varepsilon/L
, and such a number depends only on
\varepsilon
and the
\mathsf{G}
-diameter of
B_{3L}(x_{0})
.

4.5
Geodesic dimension estimates

The parameter
N\in[1,+\infty)
occurring in the definition of
\beta
as in (
3.12
) turns out to be a sharp upper bound for a different notion of dimension, called
geodesic dimension
. The latter was introduced in
[
3
]
for sub-Riemannian manifolds, and extended to metric measure spaces in
[
78
]
. We recall its definition.

Definition 4.18

(Geodesic dimension)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space. For any
x\in X
and
s>0
, define

C_{s}(x):=\sup\left\{\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{s}}\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}\mid\text{$A_{1}$ Borel,\, bounded, $x\notin A_{1}$,\, $\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})\in(0,+\infty)$}\right\},

(4.33)

where
A_{t}
is the set of
t
-intermediate points between
A_{0}=\{x\}
and
A_{1}
. We define the
geodesic dimension
at
x\in X
of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
as the non-negative real number

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x):=\inf\{s>0\mid C_{s}(x)=+\infty\}=\sup\{s>0\mid C_{s}(x)=0\}\,,

(4.34)

with the convention
\inf\emptyset=+\infty
and
\sup\emptyset=0
. Finally, for any subset
S\subset X
, we denote

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(S):=\sup\{\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)\mid x\in S\}.

(4.35)

We remark that if
x\notin\supp\mathfrak{m}
we have
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)=+\infty
so it makes sense to consider only the geodesic dimension at points in the support of
\mathfrak{m}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. Then for any Borel set
S\subset X
it holds

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(S)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S).

(4.36)

Proof.

We first observe that if there exists
z\in S
with
z\notin\supp\mathfrak{m}
, we have
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(z)=+\infty
, so that there is nothing to prove. Thus, we assume
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
.

Let
S_{0}\subseteq S
be any Borel subset of
S
with
\mathfrak{m}(S_{0})<+\infty
. We claim that there exists
x\in S_{0}
such that
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0})
. If
\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0})=0
the claim is true, so assume that
\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0})>0
. If
k<\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0})
, then for the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure it holds
\mathcal{H}^{k}(S_{0})=+\infty
, so that by using
[
16
, Thm. 2.4.3]
there exists
x\in S_{0}
such that

\limsup_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(x))}{r^{k}}<+\infty.

(4.37)

Let
A_{1}
be a bounded Borel set not containing
x
with
\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})\subset(0,+\infty)
, and consider the corresponding set of
t
-midpoints between
A_{1}
and
A_{0}=\{x\}
. We have
A_{1}\subseteq B_{R}(x)
for some large
R>0
, so that
A_{t}\subseteq B_{tR}(x)
. Hence for all
\varepsilon>0
it holds

\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{k-\varepsilon}}\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}\leq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{k-\varepsilon}}\frac{\mathfrak{m}(B_{tR}(x))}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}=0,\qquad\forall\,\varepsilon>0.

(4.38)

We deduce that
C_{k-\varepsilon}(x)=0
for all
\varepsilon>0
(cf. Definition
4.18
), and hence
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0})
. In particular, for any Borel subset
S_{0}\subseteq S
with
\mathfrak{m}(S_{0})<+\infty
it holds

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(S)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{0}).

(4.39)

Next, by
\sigma
-finiteness,
X=\cup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}B_{j}
for a countable family of Borel sets
(B_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mathfrak{m}(B_{j})<+\infty
. Hence, setting
S_{j}:=S\cap B_{j}
, we have
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(S)\geq\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{j})
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Finally, we obtain

\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S)=\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}\left(\bigcup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}S_{j}\right)=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(S_{j})\leq\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(S),

(4.40)

concluding the proof.
∎

The next result establishes a (sharp) geodesic dimension estimate. We will assume the following
non-triviality condition
:

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid z\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,z)<+\infty\})>0,\qquad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(4.41)

In other words,
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is not a singleton and
\mathsf{G}(x,\cdot)
does not take the value
+\infty
almost everywhere, for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
. Notice that (
4.41
) is implied by the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
provided that
\beta_{t}(+\infty)=+\infty
, see Remark
4.11
.

Theorem 4.20

(Geodesic dimension estimate)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
satisfying the non-triviality condition (
4.41
). Assume that
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition, with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Recall that
N\in[1,+\infty)
is the parameter given in (
3.12
). Then the geodesic dimension of
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)\leq N,\qquad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(4.42)

In particular it holds for the Hausdorff dimension:

\dim_{\mathrm{Haus}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})\leq\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})\leq N.

(4.43)

Proof.

Thanks to Lemma
4.19
, estimate (
4.42
) implies (
4.43
), so it is sufficient to prove the former. We use the half Brunn-Minkowski inequality (Theorem
4.2
) implied by the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.
Fix
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
. Using that
\mathfrak{m}
is locally finite and the non triviality (
4.41
) of the gauge function, it is immediate to see that there exists a bounded Borel set
A_{1}\subset X
, with
\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})\in(0,+\infty)
, and disjoint from
x
, satisfying

\sup_{y\in A_{1}}\mathsf{G}(x,y)<\infty.

(4.44)

By the half Brunn-Minkowski inequality (Theorem
4.2
), it holds

\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}\geq\mathfrak{m}\shortminus\essinf_{y\in A_{1}}\,\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(4.45)

Let
(\theta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\theta_{j}\in[0,\sup_{y\in A_{1}}\mathsf{G}(x,y)]
be such that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})=\mathfrak{m}\shortminus\essinf_{y\in A_{1}}\,\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)).

(4.46)

Assume first that
\theta_{j}
is definitely bounded away from zero, so that we can extract a subsequence such that
\lim_{j\to\infty}\theta_{j}=\theta_{\infty}\in(0,+\infty)
, where the finiteness of
\theta_{\infty}
follows from (
4.44
). Since
\beta_{t}
is continuous in this range (cf. Proposition
3.4
), the essential infimum above is achieved at
\beta_{t}(\theta_{\infty})
. If
\theta_{\infty}\in(0,\mathcal{D})
then, by the properties (
3.12
) and (
3.14
), we infer that

\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}\geq\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\beta_{t}(\theta_{\infty})=\limsup_{t\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\frac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta_{\infty})}{\mathsf{s}(\theta_{\infty})}=+\infty.

(4.47)

If
\theta_{\infty}\in[\mathcal{D},+\infty)
, then
\beta_{t}(\theta_{\infty})=+\infty
by (
3.14
), so we have a similar conclusion.

The remaining case is when
(\theta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
admits a subsequence converging to zero. So we assume that
\lim_{j\to\infty}\theta_{j}=0
. In this case, by the properties (
3.12
) and (
3.14
), we have

\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\frac{\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})}{\mathfrak{m}(A_{1})}\geq\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{t^{N+\varepsilon}}\frac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta_{j})}{\mathsf{s}(\theta_{j})}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{(t\theta_{j})^{N}+o(t\theta_{j})^{N}}{t^{N+\varepsilon}\left(\theta_{j}^{N}+o(\theta_{j})^{N}\right)}=\frac{1}{t^{\varepsilon}}.

(4.48)

Taking the
\limsup
for
t\to 0^{+}
gives
+\infty
. Combining all the cases, we deduce that
C_{N+\varepsilon}(x)=+\infty
for all
\varepsilon>0
(cf. Definition
4.18
), that implies (
4.42
).
∎

Remark 4.21
.

The Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{d}
, equipped with its Carnot-Carathéodory distance and a Haar measure, is an example where the geodesic dimension is strictly larger than the Hausdorff dimension (the former being equal to
2d+3
, while the latter is equal to
2d+2
, while the topological dimension is
2d+1
). This is true, more in general, for all sub-Riemannian structures that are not Riemannian. Furthermore, when equipped with a suitable gauge function (see Section
3.3.2
), the estimate provided by Theorem
4.20
is attained.

4.6
Generalized Bishop-Gromov inequality

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
. Observe that the sublevel sets of a gauge function
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\leq r
may be unbounded. This occurs with the natural gauge in the Heisenberg group (here, even
\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)=0\}
is unbounded). For this reason we work with the intersection with standard balls.

Definition 4.22
.

For a point
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and for
r,\rho\geq 0
, we set:

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\}\right),

(4.49)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\}\right),\quad r\neq 0.

(4.50)

Consider also the following measure of “gauge balls” and “gauge spheres”:

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq r\}\right),

(4.51)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\delta}\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta)\right],\quad r\neq 0.

(4.52)

Notice also that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)=\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,r)
but
\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)\neq\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,r)
.

In Figure
1
we sketch these sets for a natural gauge function in the Heisenberg group.

Figure 1:
Typical gauge sets in the Heisenberg group for the natural choice of gauge function described in Section
3.3.2
. The green region corresponds to the intersection of the ball
\{\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)\leq r\}
and the yellow one
\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\leq r\}
. Notice the red line which represents the set
\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)=0\}
.

Definition 4.23

(Meek)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. The gauge function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
is
meek
if for all
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
with
\bar{x}\notin\supp\,\mu_{1}
, and any optimal dynamical plan
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu_{1})
, there exists a Borel set
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
with
\nu(\Gamma)=1
such that it holds:

\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})=t\,\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1],\quad\forall\,\gamma\in\Gamma.

(4.53)

Remark 4.24
.

The meek property is used in the proof of Theorem
4.25
below for gauge m.m.s. satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
. There, it can be replaced by a weaker assumption where condition (
4.53
) is required to hold for one
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu_{1})
for which the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
inequality (
3.16
) holds, instead than for
every

\nu
. This variant is stable under the convergence of gauge metric measure spaces studied in Section
5
, see proof of item
(iii)
of Theorem
5.17
. We refrain from stating Definition
4.23
in this weaker sense since it would make it dependent on the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition.

The meek property is satisfied by a large class of natural gauge functions in sub-Riemannian geometry, see Section
7.3.1
(in particular, Theorem
7.16
and Remark
7.17
)

Theorem 4.25

(Generalized Bishop-Gromov)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. endowed with a gauge function
\mathsf{G}
satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition, with
\beta
as in (
3.14
). Assume that

•

\supp\mathfrak{m}
is not a singleton;

•

\mathsf{G}
is meek; (see Remark
4.24
for a slightly weaker condition)

•

for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds (see Remark
4.26
for a slightly weaker condition)

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid z\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,z)=+\infty\})=0.

(4.54)

Then for every
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
the following properties hold:

(i)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\})=0
, for every
r>0
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x_{0}\})=0
; in particular, the half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality (
4.7
) holds for every
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and Borel set
A\subset X
with
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
;

(iii)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=r\})=0
, for every
r>0
;

(iv)

For every
\rho>0
, the functions

(0,\mathcal{D})\ni r\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)}{\mathsf{s}(r)/r}\qquad\text{and}\\
(0,\mathcal{D}]\cap(0,+\infty)\ni r\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)}{\int_{0}^{r}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t},

(4.55)

are monotone non-increasing. Here
\mathsf{s}
is the function defining
\beta
as in (
3.14
);

(v)

Assume that
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is monotone non-increasing for all
t\in[0,1]
, or that for all
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.. Then the functions

(0,\mathcal{D})\ni r\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)}{\mathsf{s}(r)/r}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(0,\mathcal{D}]\cap(0,+\infty)\ni r\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)}{\int_{0}^{r}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t}

(4.56)

are monotone non-increasing.

(vi)

For every
R_{0}>0
there exists a constant

\frac{1}{C_{r/R,0}}:=\inf\{\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,R_{0}]\}\in(0,(r/R)^{N}],

(4.57)

such that it holds

\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)\leq\frac{r}{R}C_{r/R,0}\cdot\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r),\qquad\forall\,0<r\leq R\leq R_{0}.

(4.58)

As a consequence, the following doubling estimate for gauge balls holds: there exists a constant
C_{0}=C_{1/2,0}>0
such that

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},2r)\leq C_{0}\cdot\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r),\qquad\forall\,r\in[0,R_{0}/2].

(4.59)

Remark 4.26
.

For the proof of Thorem
4.25
condition (
4.54
) can be weakened as follows: for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and all
r>0
it holds

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid\mathsf{d}(x,z)=r,\,\mathsf{G}(x,z)=+\infty\})=0.

(4.60)

Both conditions (
4.54
) and (
4.60
) follow from the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, provided that
\beta_{t}(+\infty)=+\infty
, see Remark
4.11
.

Remark 4.27
.

Item
(iv)
can be seen as a parametric version of the Bishop-Gromov inequality (the parameter being the “scale”
\rho
). The Bishop-Gromov estimate for “gauge balls” and “spheres” as in item
(v)
holds under some additional assumptions.

Remark 4.28
.

We are not aware of any situation in which
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)
does not vanish, but it does not seem to be a consequence of the
\mathsf{MCP}
condition. Thus it seems that the extra term in item
(iv)
cannot be in general removed.

Proof.

Fix
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
throughout the proof.

Proof of
(i)
.
Let
\delta,\rho>0
, and
0<r-\delta r<r<R-\delta R<R<+\infty
. Let

E:=\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(R-R\delta,R]\},

(4.61)

and note that
E
is Borel. Assume
\mathfrak{m}(E)>0
. Let
\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{0}}
and
\mu_{1}=\mathfrak{m}(E)^{-1}\mathfrak{m}\llcorner_{E}
. Let
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be such that the
W_{2}
-geodesic
\mu_{t}=({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
inequality. Let
\Gamma\subseteq\mathrm{Geo}(X)
be the corresponding set of geodesics in the meek assumption, for which
\nu(\Gamma)=1
. Observe that
\supp\nu\subseteq\Gamma_{E}:={\rm e}_{1}^{-1}(E)\cap{\rm e}_{0}^{-1}(x_{0})
so that, letting
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\cap\Gamma_{E}
, it holds that
\nu(\Gamma^{\prime})=1
and the measure
\mu_{t}
is concentrated on
{\rm e}_{t}(\Gamma^{\prime})
. Since
\Gamma^{\prime}
is Borel,
{\rm e}_{t}(\Gamma^{\prime})
is Suslin. Then, by Theorem
4.2
and Remark
4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{m}}({\rm e}_{r/R}(\Gamma^{\prime}))\geq\beta_{r/R}(x_{0},E)\cdot\mathfrak{m}(E)\geq C_{r/R,\rho}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(E),

(4.62)

where we set

C_{r/R,\rho}:=\inf\{\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,\rho]\}>0.

(4.63)

The same inequality holds true trivially also if
\mathfrak{m}(E)=0
. An important remark is that any
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is supported on a set of non-trivial geodesics, since by construction
x_{0}\notin E
and
({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu=\delta_{x_{0}}\otimes\mu_{1}
, so that all geodesics in
\Gamma^{\prime}
are non-trivial. Then, by the meek assumption, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}
it holds

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\gamma_{r/R})

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{R}\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in[0,r\rho/R],

(4.64)

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\gamma_{r/R})

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{R}\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in(r-\delta r,r].

(4.65)

In other words, we have

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\left({\rm e}_{r/R}(\Gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r\rho/R,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r]\}\right)=0.

(4.66)

Since
\rho>r\rho/R
, for the given range of parameters, we have:

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r]\}\right)\geq\\
C_{r/R,\rho}\cdot\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R]\}\right),

(4.67)

where we noted that the set in the left hand side of (
4.67
) is
\mathfrak{m}
-measurable. Thus for fixed
\rho\in(0,+\infty)
the function

(0,+\infty)\ni r\mapsto\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,r)=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq r\}\right),

(4.68)

by (
4.67
) satisfies

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,r-\delta r)\geq C_{r/R,\rho}\cdot\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,R)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,R-\delta R)\right].

(4.69)

Recall that, by standing assumption,
\mathfrak{m}
is finite on bounded sets, thus

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,r)<+\infty,\quad\text{ for any $r,\rho>0$.}

(4.70)

Furthermore, for fixed
\rho\in(0,+\infty)
, the function
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,\cdot)
is right-continuous, non-decreasing, with at most countably many discontinuities. In particular there exists arbitrarily small
r_{0}>0
for which
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,\cdot)
is continuous at
r_{0}
. Choosing
r=r_{0}
and sending
\delta\downarrow 0
in (
4.69
), we obtain that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\rho,\cdot)
is also left-continuous on
(0,+\infty)
and thus continuous. In particular,

\mathfrak{m}\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\}=0,\qquad\text{for any $r,\rho>0$}.

(4.71)

It follows that for any
r>0

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\}\right)=\mathfrak{m}\left(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq n,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\}\right)\\
+\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=\infty,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\}\right)=0.

(4.72)

The first term vanishes by (
4.71
), while the second one vanishes by (
4.54
).

Proof of
(ii)
.
By the previous item, and since
\supp\mathfrak{m}
is not a singleton, any
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
is on a sphere of positive radius of another point in
\supp\mathfrak{m}
, hence
\mathfrak{m}(\{x_{0}\})=0
.

Proof of
(iii)
.
Let
\varepsilon,\delta,\rho>0
and
0<r(1-\delta)<r<R<R(1+\delta)<\mathcal{D}
.

The argument is similar to
(i)
exchanging the roles of
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{d}
, with the important difference that while the
metric
annulus
\{\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)\in(r,R)\}
does not contain
x_{0}
, the
gauge
annulus
\{\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\in(r,R)\}
may contain
x_{0}
(see the typical situation pictured in Figure
1
); for this reason we will argue by approximation by cutting off a small metric ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
out of the gauge annulus, perform the transport argument using the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
and the meek conditions, and then finally let
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
Consider the measurable set

C_{\varepsilon}:=\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in[\varepsilon,\rho]\}.

(4.73)

Assume
\mathfrak{m}(C_{\varepsilon})>0
, and set
\mu_{1}=\mathfrak{m}(C_{\varepsilon})^{-1}\mathfrak{m}\llcorner_{C_{\varepsilon}}
,
\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{0}}
. As in the proof of
(i)
, we find a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
for which the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
inequality holds, and a Borel set
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
of non-trivial geodesics verifying the meek condition (
4.53
),
({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})(\Gamma)=\{x_{0}\}\times C_{\varepsilon}
, and
\mu_{t}
is concentrated on
{\rm e}_{t}(\Gamma)
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Then by Theorem
4.2
, and Remark
4.4
we have

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{m}}({\rm e}_{r/R}(\Gamma))

\displaystyle\geq\beta_{r/R}(x_{0},C_{\varepsilon})\cdot\mathfrak{m}(C_{\varepsilon}),

(4.74)

and the same inequality holds trivially if
\mathfrak{m}(C_{\varepsilon})=0
.

By the meek condition, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\gamma_{r/R})=\frac{r}{R}\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in(r-\delta r,r],\qquad\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\gamma_{r/R})=\frac{r}{R}\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in[\varepsilon r/R,\rho r/R].

(4.75)

In other words, we have

\bar{\mathfrak{m}}\left({\rm e}_{r/R}(\Gamma)\setminus\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in[\varepsilon r/R,\rho r/R]\}\right)=0.

(4.76)

So that, for all
0<r<R<\mathcal{D}
and
\varepsilon,\delta>0
sufficiently small, we have

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in[\varepsilon r/R,\rho r/R]\}\right)\geq\\
\beta_{r/R}(x_{0},C_{\varepsilon})\cdot\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in[\varepsilon,\rho]\}\right),

(4.77)

where we noted that the set
\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r]\}
is measurable. We also remark that by definition of
\beta
and
C_{\varepsilon}
, for all
0<r<R<\mathcal{D}
it holds:

\displaystyle\beta_{r/R}(x_{0},C_{\varepsilon})\geq\inf\{\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\mid\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]\}\in(0,+\infty).

(4.78)

Letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
in (
4.77
), using (
4.78
) and that
\mathfrak{m}(\{x_{0}\})=0
by
(ii)
, we infer that

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho r/R\}\right)\geq\\
\inf_{\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\cdot\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\}\right).

(4.79)

Finally, since
\rho>\rho r/R
, we obtain from (
4.79
)

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r,\rho)\geq\\
\inf_{\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\cdot\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-\delta R,\rho)\right].

(4.80)

Since
\mathfrak{m}
is finite on bounded sets, it holds
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)<+\infty
for any
\rho>0
and
R\in(0,\mathcal{D})
. Furthermore, by construction,
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\cdot,\rho)
is right-continuous, non-decreasing, with at most countably many discontinuities. In particular, there exists arbitrarily small
r\in(0,\mathcal{D})
for which
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\cdot,\rho)
is continuous at
r
. Since also
\beta_{r/R}(\cdot)
is continuous on
(0,\mathcal{D})
, we obtain from (
4.80
) that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\cdot,\rho)
is left continuous and thus continuous on
(0,\mathcal{D})
. Note that the argument requires
R>0
.

It follows that
\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=R,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\}\right)=0
for every
R\in(0,\mathcal{D})
and
\rho>0
, and thus
\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=R\})=0
. If
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
, this concludes the proof of
(iii)
. If
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, then we invoke Proposition
4.10
, so that also in this case the equality keeps holding also for
R\geq\mathcal{D}
.

Proof of
(iv)
.
From (
4.80
), we obtain:

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r,\rho)}{\delta r}\\
\geq\frac{R}{r}\left(\inf_{\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\right)\cdot\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-\delta R,\rho)}{\delta R}.

(4.81)

Taking the
\limsup
for
\delta\downarrow 0
, and using the continuity of
\beta_{r/R}(\cdot)
on
(0,\mathcal{D})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r,\rho)}{\delta r}

(4.82)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{R}{r}\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\left(\inf_{\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\right)\cdot\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)

(4.83)

\displaystyle=\frac{R}{r}\beta_{r/R}(R)\cdot\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho).

(4.84)

Using the fact that
\beta_{t}(\theta)=\frac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}(\theta)}
for
\theta\in(0,\mathcal{D})
and
t\in[0,1]
, from (
3.14
) we infer the left inequality in (
4.55
).

To prove its integrated counterpart, we claim that the map
(0,\mathcal{D})\ni r\mapsto\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)
is locally Lipschitz, for fixed
\rho>0
. If by contradiction it were not locally Lipschitz, then there would exist
R>0
and a sequence
h_{n}\downarrow 0
such that at least one of the two conditions hold

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-h_{n},\rho)\geq nh_{n},\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\text{ or }

(4.85)

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R+h_{n},\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)\geq nh_{n},\qquad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N}.

We assume that (
4.85
) holds. The argument for the second case is analogous.

For every fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, observing that
c:=\inf\{\beta_{t}(\theta)\mid t\in[1/2,1],\,\theta\in[0,R]\}>0
, the combination of (
4.85
) and (
4.81
) gives that

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-(j-1)h_{n},\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-jh_{n},\rho)\geq cnh_{n},\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\;\forall\,j=1,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{R}{2h_{n}}\right\rfloor.

(4.86)

Summing up over
j
for every fixed
n
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}\left(x_{0},R-\left\lfloor\frac{R}{2h_{n}}\right\rfloor h_{n},\rho\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{R}{2h_{n}}\right\rfloor}\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-(j-1)h_{n},\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-jh_{n},\rho)

\displaystyle\geq cnh_{n}\left\lfloor\frac{R}{2h_{n}}\right\rfloor.

The right hand side diverges to
+\infty
as
n\to\infty
, contradicting that bounded sets have finite measure. Thus the map
(0,\mathcal{D})\ni r\mapsto\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)
is locally Lipschitz and finite.

It follows that the function
r\mapsto\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)
is differentiable a.e. on
(0,\mathcal{D})
, and it coincides with the integral of its a.e. derivative
r\mapsto\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)
. We can thus apply the classical Gromov’s Lemma (see
[
39
, Lemma III.4.1]
) to infer that the first claim in (
4.55
) implies the integrated version given in the second of (
4.55
) for the open interval
(0,\mathcal{D})
.

We finally show that the second in (
4.55
) holds on
(0,\mathcal{D}]
, for
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
. Since
\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=\mathcal{D}\})=0
from Proposition
4.10
, by monotone convergence we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},\mathcal{D},\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\uparrow\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)\right]\lim_{R\uparrow\mathcal{D}}\frac{\int_{0}^{R}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int_{0}^{r}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t}

\displaystyle=\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)\right]\frac{\int_{0}^{\mathcal{D}}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int_{0}^{r}(\mathsf{s}(t)/t)\,\mathrm{d}t},\quad\forall\,r\in(0,\mathcal{D}).

This concludes the proof of
(iv)
.

Proof of
(v)
.
Recall that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)
,
\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)
were defined in (
4.51
) and (
4.52
), for
r\geq 0
.
Observe that, by item
(ii)
, it holds
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0)=0
. Moreover,

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r)=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq r\}\right)\\
+\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r-\delta r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r]\}\right).

(4.87)

Arguing as above we obtain for the two addends, for
0<r<R<\mathcal{D}
and sufficiently small
\delta>0

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq r\}\right)\geq\left(\inf_{\theta\in(R-\delta R,R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\right)\\
\cdot\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq R\}\right),

(4.88)

\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r-\delta r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta r,r]\}\right)\geq\left(\inf_{\theta\in[0,R-\delta R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\right)\\
\cdot\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq R-\delta R,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\in(R-\delta R,R]\}\right).

(4.89)

If
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is non-increasing, then
\inf_{\theta\in[0,R-\delta R]}\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\geq\beta_{r/R}(R)
. In case
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)
for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
, then both sides of (
4.89
) vanish. In both cases we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r)}{\delta r}\geq\frac{R}{r}\beta_{r/R}(R)\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-\delta R)}{\delta R}.

(4.90)

We now can conclude arguing exactly as in item
(iv)
, concluding the proof of
(v)
.

Proof of
(vi)
.
We argue as in the proof of
(v)
, but in (
4.88
) and (
4.89
) we bound from below the factors depending on the distortion coefficient by the minimum on
[0,R_{0}]
:

\frac{1}{C_{r/R,0}}:=\inf\{\beta_{r/R}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,R_{0}]\}\in(0,(r/R)^{N}],

(4.91)

which is positive by Proposition
3.4
and satisfies the above upper bound by inspection of the explicit expression (
3.14
). Therefore we obtain for
0<r\leq R\leq R_{0}
and
\delta>0
:

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta r)}{\delta r}\geq\frac{R}{r}\frac{1}{C_{r/R,0}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R-\delta R)}{\delta R}.

(4.92)

Taking the
\limsup
for
\delta\downarrow 0
, we obtain the first inequality of
(vi)
:

\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)\leq\frac{r}{R}C_{r/R,0}\cdot\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r),\qquad\forall\,0<r\leq R\leq R_{0}.

(4.93)

As in the previous steps, we remark that
r\mapsto\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)
is locally Lipschitz continuous for
r\in(0,\mathcal{D})
and its a.e. derivative is
r\mapsto\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)
. Integrating the above inequality, and since
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0)=0
, we obtain

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)\leq C_{r/R,0}\cdot\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r),\qquad\forall\,0<r\leq R\leq R_{0},

(4.94)

concluding the proof.
∎

We can estimate the maximal number of pairwise disjoint gauge balls of radius
\varepsilon>0
whose centres are contained in a bounded and
\mathsf{G}
-bounded set.

Proposition 4.29

(Local total boundedness for gauge balls)
.

With the same assumptions of Theorem
4.25
, let
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
be bounded and
\mathsf{G}
-bounded with
\mathfrak{m}(S)>0
. Then the maximal number of pairwise disjoint gauge balls of radius
\varepsilon>0
(i.e. sets
\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq\varepsilon,\,\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\varepsilon\}
) contained in
S
is bounded above by

k_{0}=C_{0}\left(\frac{\max\{\diam(S),\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)\}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\log_{2}C_{0}},

(4.95)

where
C_{0}>0
can be estimated in terms of
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)
and
\beta
defining the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
class.

Proof.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
and let
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in S\subset\supp\mathfrak{m}
be such that

\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{i},x)\leq\varepsilon,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{i},x)\leq\varepsilon\}\subseteq S

(4.96)

are pairwise disjoint. Since
S
is bounded,
\mathfrak{m}(S)<+\infty
. Let

R:=\max\{\diam(S),\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)\}.

(4.97)

Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be the smallest integer such that
2^{M}\varepsilon\geq R
. By the doubling property for gauge balls (cf. Theorem
4.25
item
(vi)
), we have

\mathfrak{m}(S)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{i},\varepsilon)\geq C_{0}^{-M}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{i},R),

(4.98)

where

\frac{1}{C_{0}}=\inf\{\beta_{1/2}(\theta)\mid\theta\in[0,R]\}\in(0,1/2^{N}].

(4.99)

By construction,
\mathfrak{m}(S\setminus\{x\in X\,:\,\mathsf{d}(x_{i},x)\leq R,\,\mathsf{G}(x_{i},x)\leq R\})=0
, so that the last sum in (
4.98
) can be estimated from below by
k\mathfrak{m}(S)>0
. Continuing the chain of inequalities, and since
\mathfrak{m}(S)<+\infty
, we have

k\leq k_{0}:=C_{0}^{M}\leq C_{0}^{\log_{2}(R/\varepsilon)+1}=C_{0}\left(\frac{R}{\varepsilon}\right)^{\log_{2}C_{0}},

(4.100)

proving the claim.
∎

Remark 4.30
.

If
\mathsf{G}
is symmetric and there exists
\alpha\geq 1
such that

\mathsf{G}(x,z)\leq\alpha(\mathsf{G}(x,y)+\mathsf{G}(y,z)),\qquad\forall\,x,y,z\in X,

(4.101)

so in particular if
\mathsf{G}
is a quasi-metric, then we can estimate the number of gauge balls needed to cover a bounded and
\mathsf{G}
-bounded set. Indeed the number of gauge balls of radius
2\alpha\varepsilon
covering
S
is bounded above by
k_{0}
. This is the case when
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
. However notice that (
4.101
) is not satisfied by natural gauge functions in sub-Riemannian geometry.

4.7
Parametric doubling for gauge balls

We close this section with the following doubling property, for intersection of sublevel sets of
\mathsf{G}
with arbitrarily large balls of radius
\rho
.

Proposition 4.31

(Parametric doubling)
.

With the same assumptions of Theorem
4.25
, for every
R>0
there exists
C_{R}=C_{R}(\beta)\geq 1
such that

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}\leq 2^{N}C_{R},\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,R),\quad\forall\,\rho>0,\quad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m},

(4.102)

where
N>1
is given by (
3.12
). Furthermore,
C_{R}\downarrow 1
as
R\downarrow 0
.

If
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is monotone non-increasing for all
t\in[0,1]
, or if for all
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e., then the previous inequality holds true for gauge balls, with the same constants, that is

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r)}{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,r)}\leq 2^{N}C_{R},\qquad\forall\,r\in(0,R),\quad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(4.103)

Proof.

Fix
\rho>0
. Assume first that
0<r<\mathcal{D}/2
. The second claim in (
4.55
) combined with (
3.12
) gives that

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}\leq\frac{\int_{0}^{2r}\mathsf{s}(t)/t\,\mathrm{d}t}{\int_{0}^{r}\mathsf{s}(t)/t\,\mathrm{d}t}=\frac{(2r)^{N}(1+o(1))}{r^{N}(1+o(1))}\leq 2^{N}C_{R}.

The constant
C_{R}
depends only on the values of
\mathsf{s}
on the interval
[0,R]
, so that it does not depend on
\rho
.
If
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
, there is nothing left to prove since we covered already the whole range of
r\in(0,R)
. If
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, notice that by Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
4.25
(ii)
,
\mathsf{G}(x,\cdot)<\mathcal{D}(x,\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-almost everywhere, so that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r,\rho)
is constant in the range
r\geq\mathcal{D}/2
.

If
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is monotone non-increasing for all
t\in[0,1]
, or if for all
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e., then we perform the same proof but starting from (
4.56
) that holds in this case, instead of (
4.55
).
∎

5
Stability and compactness

The aim of this section is to prove stability results for curvature-dimension conditions for sequences of m.m.s. converging in the pmGH sense. First, we recall the definition of pointed
measured Gromov Hausdorff convergence (pmGH for short; we follow the convention from
[
49
, Def. 4.1]
adapted from
[
86
, Def. 8.1.1]
).

Definition 5.1

(pmGH convergence)
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
, be pointed metric measure spaces.
We say that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})
converges to

(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense if for any
\varepsilon,R>0
there exists
N({\varepsilon,R})\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq N({\varepsilon,R})
there exists a Borel map
f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k}:B_{R}(\star_{k})\to X_{\infty}
such that

(i)
′

f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k}(\star_{k})=\star_{\infty}
;

(ii)
′

\sup_{x,y\in B_{R}(\star_{k})}|\mathsf{d}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k}(x),f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k}(y))|\leq\varepsilon
;

(iii)
′

the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k}(B_{R}(\star_{k}))
contains
B_{R-\varepsilon}(\star_{\infty})
;

(iv)
′

(f^{R,\varepsilon}_{k})_{\sharp}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}\llcorner{B_{R}(\star_{k})})\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\llcorner{B_{R}(\star_{\infty})}
as
k\to\infty
, for a.e.
R>0
(weak convergence against bounded continuous functions with bounded support).

Remark 5.2
.

It is not hard to check (cf.
[
49
, Prop. 3.28]
) that the pmGH convergence of
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})
to
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
is equivalent to the following condition: there are sequences
R_{k}\uparrow+\infty
,
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
and Borel maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
such that

(i)

f_{k}(\star_{k})=\star_{\infty}
;

(ii)

\sup_{x,y\in B_{R_{k}}(\star_{k})}|\mathsf{d}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(y))|\leq\varepsilon_{k}
;

(iii)

the
\varepsilon_{k}
-neighborhood of
f_{k}(B_{R_{k}}(\star_{k}))
contains
B_{R_{k}-\varepsilon_{k}}(\star_{\infty})
;

(iv)

(f_{k})_{\sharp}(\mathfrak{m}_{k})\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
as
k\to\infty
(weak convergence against bounded continuous functions with bounded support).

Having globally defined approximation maps slightly simplifies the notation; thus, in the following, we will make use of this formulation of pmGH convergence.

For sequences of gauge m.m.s. it is natural to add extra assumptions to ensure that “
\mathsf{G}_{k}\to\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
”. The natural extension of (ii) to gauge functions would be:

|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(y))|\leq\varepsilon_{k},\qquad\forall\,x,y\in B_{R_{k}}(\star_{k}).

(5.1)

However, due to the a priori low regularity of the gauge function (which in general is not Lipschitz continuous), this would be a too strong requirement in geometric situations (e.g. convergence to the tangent cone for sub-Riemannian structures; cf. Section
10.4
). It turns out that a significantly weaker condition is sufficient for the stability of our curvature-dimension inequalities.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be gauge metric measure spaces such that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense, with approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
as in Remark
5.2
. We introduce the following conditions:

•

L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions:
if for all sequences
x_{k}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
such that
f_{k}(x_{k})
is convergent in
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
it holds

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(\star_{k}){\setminus\{x_{k}\}}}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x_{k}),f_{k}(z))|\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)=0,\qquad\forall\,R>0.

(5.2)

•

regularity condition:
if for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
-a.e.
y\in X_{\infty}
,
y\neq x
, with exceptional set depending on
x
,
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
is continuous at
(x,y)
.

Remark 5.4
.

The removal of
\{x_{k}\}
in the domain of integration in (
5.2
) and the condition
y\neq x
in the regularity condition can be omitted if
\mathfrak{m}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{m}
do not give mass to points.

In Section
7.3.2
we show that the regularity condition above is satisfied in natural classes of examples.

Lemma 5.5

(Lower semi-continuity of
\mathsf{G}
-diameter)
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be gauge metric measure spaces such that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense. Assume the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions and the regularity condition as in Definition
5.3
. Then it holds:

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k})\geq\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}).

(5.3)

For a counter-example to upper semi-continuity, take the trivial sequence given by the Euclidean structure and the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Choose a sequence of gauge functions
\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,y)=\mathsf{G}_{k}(y)
, and its pointwise limit
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(y)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathsf{G}_{k}(y)
as in the picture.

The difference
|\mathsf{G}_{k}(y)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(y)|
is a small a.e. continuous bump localized in a shrinking neighborhood of the origin, that decreases monotonically to zero pointwise a.e. One can check in particular that the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence in Definition
5.3
is satisfied by dominated convergence. In this situation, we have for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
:
2=\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k})>\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})=1
.

Proof.

We drop the subscript
\infty
in the notation for the limit objects. Let
D:=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k})
, and suppose that
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})=D+3\eta
for
\eta>0
. Recalling Definition
4.9
of
\mathsf{G}
-diameter, there exists
x_{o}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and a Borel set
P\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
with
\mathfrak{m}(P)>0
and
x_{o}\notin P
such that

\mathsf{G}(x_{o},y)\geq D+2\eta,\qquad\forall\,y\in P.

(5.4)

By the regularity condition, removing a zero
\mathfrak{m}
-measure set from
P
, we can assume that
\mathsf{G}
is continuous at all points
(x_{o},y)
for
y\in P
. In particular there is
(x_{o},y_{o})\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\times\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
\varepsilon>0
such that
B_{2\varepsilon}(x_{o})\cap B_{2\varepsilon}(y_{o})=\emptyset
and

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq D+\eta,\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\in B_{\varepsilon}(x_{o})\times B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o}).

(5.5)

Recall the approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X
from Remark
5.2
.
Since
(f_{k})_{\sharp}(\mathfrak{m}_{k})\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}
, we can find a sequence
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
f_{k}(x_{k})\to x_{o}
. We can assume that
\mathsf{d}(f_{k}(x_{k}),x_{o})\leq\varepsilon
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and thus

\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x_{k}),y)\geq D+\eta,\qquad\forall\,y\in B_{\varepsilon}(y_{0}),\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.6)

Let
h:X\to[0,1]
be a continuous function such that
h\equiv 1
on
\overline{B_{\varepsilon/2}(y_{o})}
and
h\equiv 0
on
X\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o})
. Consider the sequence
\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{ac}(X_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
given by

\mu_{k}:=\frac{1}{\int_{X_{k}}h(f_{k}(z))\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)}h\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k},\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.7)

The support of
\mu_{k}
is contained in the Borel set
f_{k}^{-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o}))
.
Note also that
x_{k}\notin\supp\,\mu_{k}
, for
k
sufficiently large. Since
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}
we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}}h(f_{k}(z))\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)=\int_{X}h(z)\,\mathfrak{m}(\mathrm{d}z).

(5.8)

Furthermore, by local boundedness of
\mathfrak{m}
and since
y_{o}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
, it holds

0<\mathfrak{m}(B_{\varepsilon/2}(y_{o}))\leq\int_{X}h(z)\,\mathfrak{m}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq\mathfrak{m}(B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o}))<+\infty.

(5.9)

Thus, we have:

\int_{X_{k}}\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)=\int_{X_{k}}\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x_{k}),f_{k}(z))\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)\\
+\int_{X_{k}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)-\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x_{k}),f_{k}(z))\right]\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z).

(5.10)

We claim that the second integral in the r.h.s. of (
5.10
) converges to
0
by the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence condition. Indeed recall that
x_{k}\notin\supp\mu_{k}
and furthermore
\supp\mu_{k}\subseteq f_{k}^{-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o}))
. Using the properties of
f_{k}
, there exists
R>0
such that
\supp\mu_{k}\subseteq B_{R}(\star_{k})
for all sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, using the construction of
\mu_{k}
, we obtain:

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}}\left[\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)-\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x_{k}),f_{k}(z))\right]\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq\\
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{m}(B_{\varepsilon/2}(y_{o}))}\int_{B_{R}(\star_{k})\setminus\{x_{k}\}}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)-\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x_{k}),f_{k}(z))|\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)=0,

(5.11)

proving our claim.

Since
\supp\mu_{k}\subseteq f_{k}^{-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(y_{o}))
, by (
5.6
) the first integral in the right hand side of (
5.10
) is
\geq D+\eta
. Therefore

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}}\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)\geq D+\eta>D.

(5.12)

On the other hand, by definition of
\mathsf{G}
-diameter, and since
x_{k}\notin\supp\mu_{k}
it holds:

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}}\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},z)\,\mu_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k})=D,

(5.13)

yielding a contradiction.
∎

5.1
Stability of
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)

Theorem 5.6

(Stability of
\mathsf{MCP}
)
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be gauge m.m.s. with
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense. Let
\beta
be distortion coefficients as in (
3.14
). Assume that:

(i)

(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
is locally compact and geodesic for all
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
;

(ii)

the supports are locally uniformly
\mathsf{G}
-bounded: for any
R>0
there exists
D=D(R)>0
such that
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}\cap B_{R}(\star_{k}))\leq D
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
(and thus for all
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
by Lemma
5.5
);

(iii)

the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence and regularity conditions hold (cf. Definition
5.3
);

(iv)

for all
t\in(0,1)
the function
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to[0,\infty)
is locally Lipschitz;

(v)

(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Then, also
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

Remark 5.7
.

Assumption
(i)
for
finite

k\in\mathbb{N}
is implied by the other ones. More precisely, by
(ii)
, bounded subsets of
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
are
\mathsf{G}
-bounded, and we can apply Corollary
4.16
.

Remark 5.8
.

The local Lipschitz assumption on the function
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is not restrictive. In all cases of interest,
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is real-analytic on
[0,\mathcal{D})
.

Proof.

The first part of the proof follows the blueprint of the classical one (cf.
[
86
]
), however, later in the argument, we will need to deal with additional difficulties caused by the non-continuity of
\mathsf{G}
and the general distortion coefficients.

We drop the
\infty
from the notation for the limit so that
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
will be denoted by
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G},\star)
.
Let
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X
be the approximations as in Remark
5.2
.
Let
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and fix
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\supp\mu_{1}
. Since
\supp\mu_{1}
is compact (recall that
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is assumed to be locally compact), the distance between
\bar{x}
and
\supp\mu_{1}
is positive. Up to extraction and relabeling, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we find
\bar{x}_{k}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
such that
f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k})\to\bar{x}
as
k\to\infty
.

Furthermore, letting
\mu_{1}=\rho_{1}\,\mathfrak{m}
for
\rho_{1}\in L^{1}(X,\mathfrak{m})
with bounded support, we define

\mu_{1,k}:=\frac{\rho_{1}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}}{\int_{X_{k}}\rho_{1}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}}.

(5.14)

We assume from now on that
\rho_{1}
is continuous, and we will deal with the discontinuous case at the end of the proof. Notice that

Z_{k}:=\int_{X_{k}}\rho_{1}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}=\int_{X}\rho_{1}\,(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}\to 1,

(5.15)

so that,
\mu_{1,k}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
and
\bar{x}_{k}\notin\supp\mu_{1,k}
, for sufficiently large
k
.

By the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
we find a
W_{2}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
-geodesic
(\mu_{t,k})_{t\in[0,1]}
from
\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}}
to
\mu_{1,k}
such that

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})\leq\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})-\int_{X_{k}}\log\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x))\,\mu_{1,k}(\mathrm{d}x).

(5.16)

By construction,
\supp\mu_{1}
and
\supp(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{1,k}
are contained in a common bounded set, say
B_{R}(\star)\subset X
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and some
R>0
. Thus, using the properties of the approximating maps
f_{k}
and the fact that
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
are locally compact and geodesic, one can show that the supports of the family
\{(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},t\in[0,1]}
are contained in the common compact set
\bar{B}_{2R}(\star)
. By stability of optimal transport
[
86
, Thm. 28.9]
, up to extraction, there exists a
W_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
with

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{2}((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k},\mu_{t})\to 0.

(5.17)

In particular by Proposition
3.2
we also have
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{t}
weakly. We can now pass to the limit in the l.h.s. of (
5.16
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{Ent}((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}|(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k})\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k}),

(5.18)

where in the first inequality we use the joint lower semi-continuity of the Boltzmann-Shannon entropy, and in the second one we used the fact that the push-forward via a Borel map does not increase the entropy (cf.
[
86
, Thm. 29.20 (i), (ii)]
).

We now study the r.h.s. of (
5.16
) as
k\to\infty
. We first observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})=\frac{1}{Z_{k}}\int_{X}\rho_{1}\log\rho_{1}\,(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}-\log Z_{k}.

(5.19)

Since
\rho_{1}
is continuous and with bounded support, then
\rho_{1}\log\rho_{1}
is bounded, continuous, and with bounded support. Furthermore, since
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}
and
Z_{k}\to 1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})=\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m}).

(5.20)

This settles the first term in (
5.16
).

The limit of the second term in (
5.16
) is quite delicate as a consequence of the low regularity of
\mathsf{G}
and the almost-everywhere nature of the
\mathsf{G}
-diameter bound, here is where the proof departs from the classical one.

As a first remark notice that
\bar{x}_{k}
(resp.
\bar{x}
) and
\supp\mu_{1,k}
(resp.
\supp\mu_{1}
) are contained in a common bounded set of
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
(resp.
\supp\mathfrak{m}
), say
B_{\bar{R}}(\star_{k})\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
(resp.
B_{\bar{R}}(\star)\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}
) for some
\bar{R}>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In the rest of the proof, only the values of
\mathsf{G}_{k}
(resp.
\mathsf{G}
) on these sets play a role. Therefore without loss of generality we can strengthen hypothesis
(ii)
by assuming, instead, that
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
is
\mathsf{G}
-bounded, uniformly w.r.t.
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
. In other words there exists
D>0
such that

\mathfrak{m}(\{y\mid y\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq D+\varepsilon\})=0,\qquad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m},\quad\forall\,\varepsilon>0.

(5.21)

On the one hand, we have no information for
x\notin\supp\mathfrak{m}
. On the other hand, for fixed
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
, it may happen that
\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq D+\varepsilon
on a set of zero
\mathfrak{m}
-measure which may have positive
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}
measure. For this reason, fix
\varepsilon>0
once for all, and introduce the following modification of the limit gauge function
\mathsf{G}
:

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y):=\min\{\mathsf{G}(x,y),D+\varepsilon\}.

(5.22)

The modified gauge function has the following properties:

1.

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)\leq D+\varepsilon
for all
x,y\in X
;

2.

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\mathsf{G}(x,y)
for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
y\in X
,
y\neq x
;

3.

The
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of the gauge functions of Definition
5.3
remains true replacing
\mathsf{G}
with
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
. In particular it holds:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}(\star_{k}){\setminus\{\bar{x}_{k}\}}}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},z)-\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),f_{k}(z))|\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)=0,\qquad\forall\,R>0.

(5.23)

Item (a) is trivial, while (b) follows from the condition
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(X)\leq D
that is (
5.21
). Item (c) follows from the observation that, since
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(X_{k})\leq D
, the integrand in (
5.2
) decreases when replacing
\mathsf{G}
with
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
, for all fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We can now study the second term in (
5.16
) as
k\to\infty
. We have:

\begin{multlined}\int_{X_{k}}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x)\big)\,\mu_{1,k}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),x)\big)\,((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{1,k})(\mathrm{d}x)\\
+\int_{X_{k}}\Big[\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x)\big)-\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),f_{k}(x))\big)\Big]\,\mu_{1,k}(\mathrm{d}x).\end{multlined}

(5.24)

To proceed with the proof, assume first that
\beta_{t}:[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
is bounded on bounded intervals. This is the case precisely when
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
.

We claim first that the second integral in the r.h.s. of (
5.24
) converges to zero for any
t\in(0,1)
as
k\to\infty
. First observe that
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
, restricted to
[0,D+\varepsilon]
, is bounded from below away from zero (cf. Proposition
3.4
(vi)
), bounded from above, and Lipschitz continuous on
[0,D+\varepsilon]
. Thus
\log\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz continuous on
[0,D+\varepsilon]
, with Lipschitz constant
C>0
(the constant depends on
\beta_{t}
and
D+\varepsilon
only). Furthermore, the arguments of
\log\beta_{t}(\cdot)
in the second integral of (
5.24
) take values on
[0,D+\varepsilon]

\mu_{1,k}
-almost everywhere: the first one since
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(X_{k})\leq D
(recall that
\bar{x}_{k}\notin\supp\mu_{1,k}
), while the second one by construction of
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
. Hence for the second integral in the r.h.s. of (
5.24
) we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x)\big)-\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),f_{k}(x))\big)\right\rvert\,\mu_{1,k}(\mathrm{d}x)

(5.25)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}C\int_{X_{k}}\left\lvert\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x)-\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),f_{k}(x))\right\rvert\,\mu_{1,k}(\mathrm{d}x)

(5.26)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{C\|\rho_{1}\|_{\infty}}{Z_{k}}\int_{B_{\bar{R}}(\star_{k})\setminus\{\bar{x}_{k}\}}\left\lvert\mathsf{G}_{k}(\bar{x}_{k},x)-\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),f_{k}(x))\right\rvert\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}x)=0,

(5.27)

where
\bar{R}>0
is such that
\supp\mu_{1,k}\subset B_{\bar{R}}(\star_{k})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

It remains to discuss the limit of the first term in the r.h.s. of (
5.24
). Notice that
(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}(\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}}\otimes\mu_{1,k})\rightharpoonup\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes\mu_{1}
. Thus we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),x)\big)\,((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{1,k})(\mathrm{d}x)\\
=\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)\big)\,(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\left(\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}}\otimes\mu_{1,k}\right)(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y)\\
=\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x)\big)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x).

(5.28)

In (
5.28
), we used the fact that
\log\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}=\log\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}
and is continuous
\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes\mu_{1}
-a.e., bounded by construction, so that we can apply
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.10]
.

This concludes the proof in the case when
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is finite on bounded intervals (that is when
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
), and
\rho_{1}=\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}/\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}
is continuous.

We deal now with the case
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
. In this case what fails is (
5.26
), as the arguments of the
\log
can attain infinite value. We will argue by approximation. For
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
we let

\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}(\theta):=\min\{\beta_{t}(\theta),\lambda\},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{N},\,t\in[0,1],\,\theta\in[0,+\infty].

(5.29)

Since
\beta_{t}\geq\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}
, then
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta^{(\lambda)})
inequality (
3.17
).

Furthermore, the functions
\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}:[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
are bounded from below away from zero by a positive constant, bounded from above, and Lipschitz continuous. To see the global Lipschitz continuity, recall that
\beta_{t}
is locally Lipschitz on
[0,\mathcal{D})
and
\liminf_{\theta\to\mathcal{D}^{-}}\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
, so that
\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}
can be written as the minimum of two globally Lipschitz functions. It follows that
\log\beta^{(\lambda)}_{t}
is also Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant that depends on
t
,
\lambda
,
\mathcal{D}
, but not on
k
.

Thus we can argue as in the previous part of the proof, and we obtain that for any
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with continuous density and any
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\supp\mu_{1}
there exists a
W_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
between
\delta_{\bar{x}}
and
\mu_{1}
such, that for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{N}
:

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\leq\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1}|\mathfrak{m})-\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x))\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(5.30)

By construction, the sequence of functions
\log\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},\cdot))
is monotone w.r.t.
\lambda
, measurable, uniformly bounded from below, and
\log\beta_{t}^{(\lambda)}(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x))\uparrow\log\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x))
as
\lambda\to\infty
for all
x\in X
. We conclude by the monotone convergence theorem.

This ends the proof in the case in which the density
\rho_{1}=\mathrm{d}\mu_{1}/\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}
is continuous. The strategy to reduce the general case to the continuous case is the same as
[
86
]
: we find a regularized measure
\mu_{1}^{\varepsilon}
with continuous density
\rho_{1}^{\varepsilon}
and bounded support using
[
86
, Ch. 29, First Appendix]
, and we prove the desired
\mathsf{MCP}
inequality between
\bar{x}
and
\mu_{1}^{\varepsilon}
:

\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon}|\mathfrak{m})\leq\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{1}^{\varepsilon}|\mathfrak{m})-\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x))\mu_{1}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}x)\,,

(5.31)

where
(\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a
W_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
-geodesic between
\delta_{\bar{x}}
and
\mu_{1}^{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon>0
. The sequence
(\mu_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}
converges in the
W_{2}
distance to a
W_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
-geodesic between
\delta_{\bar{x}}
and
\mu_{1}
. Then we pass to the limit for
\varepsilon\to 0
using similar arguments as before: the lower semi-continuity of the Boltzmann entropy in the l.h.s., its upper semi-continuity of the first term in the r.h.s. along this particular regularizing sequence, and again the regularity property of
\mathsf{G}
and
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.10]
for the second term in the r.h.s.
∎

Remark 5.9

(More general coefficients)
.

The proof works for any family of non-negative functions
\beta_{t}:[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
, not necessarily defined as in (
3.14
), but such that, for all
t\in(0,1)
:

•

are bounded from below away from zero on any compact set;

•

are finite on
[0,\mathcal{D})
for some
\mathcal{D}\in(0,+\infty]
and, if
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
, it holds
\liminf_{\theta\to\mathcal{D}^{-}}\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
, and
\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
for
\theta\geq\mathcal{D}
;

•

\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is locally Lipschitz on
[0,\mathcal{D})
.

Remark 5.10

(Variable coefficients)
.

Theorem
5.6
can be further extended to the case in which each space
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k},\star_{k})
satisfies a
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta^{k})
condition, with coefficients
\beta^{k}
as in (
3.14
) for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and with the following properties:

•

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathcal{D}_{k}=+\infty
(for simplicity);

•

for all fixed
t\in(0,1)
, the family
(\beta_{t}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is definitely bounded below by a positive constant on any compact set, uniformly w.r.t.
k
;

•

for all fixed
t\in(0,1)
, the family
(\beta_{t}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, restricted on any compact interval, is definitely Lipschitz, uniformly w.r.t.
k
;

In fact, up to extraction, and by Arzelà-Ascoli’s Theorem, we can assume that there exists
\beta_{t}^{\infty}:[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
such that
\beta_{t}^{k}\to\beta_{t}^{\infty}
uniformly on compact sets, and for all fixed
t\in(0,1)
. Then under the same assumptions of Theorem
5.6
the limit space satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta^{\infty})
. To prove it, take the function
\mathfrak{B}_{t}^{m}(\theta):=\inf_{k\geq m}\beta_{t}^{k}(\theta)
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\theta\in[0,+\infty]
,
t\in[0,1]
. By construction,
\mathfrak{B}_{t}^{m}(\cdot)
is finite on
[0,+\infty)
, locally Lipschitz, and bounded from below away from zero. We then use Remark
