5.9
, so that the limit space satisfies
\mathsf{MCP}(\mathfrak{B}^{m})
. Then take the limit for
m\to\infty
, using Fatou’s lemma.

Remark 5.11

(A semi-continuous variant)
.

Instead of assuming
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
to satisfy the “
\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
-a.e. continuity” in the sense of the
regularity condition
(cf. Definition
5.3
), one could alternatively assume that
\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
is lower semi-continuous on
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\times\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, for all fixed
t\in(0,1)
. In the proof, (
5.28
) would be replaced by the inequality

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(\bar{x}_{k}),x)\big)\,((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{1,k})(\mathrm{d}x)\\
=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)\big)\,(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\left(\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}}\otimes\mu_{1,k}\right)(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y)\\
\geq\int_{X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}(\bar{x},x)\big)\,\mu_{1}(\mathrm{d}x).

(5.32)

which would follow from
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.6]
. Note that (
5.32
) is sufficient for the proof.

5.2
Stability of
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)

The analogous stability statement for the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition requires slightly stronger assumptions with respect to the ones in Definition
5.3
.

The main difference is that the optimal transport plans used in the
\mathsf{MCP}
condition are of the form
\pi_{k}=\delta_{x_{k}}\otimes\mu_{1,k}
with
\mu_{1,k}\ll\mathfrak{m}_{k}
, and thus one can reduce integrals over
\pi_{k}
to integrals with respect to
\mathfrak{m}_{k}
. In the corresponding construction for the
\mathsf{CD}
case, the sequences of optimal plans
\pi_{k}
are in general not absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathfrak{m}_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}
. Thus, it is convenient to strengthen the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions and regularity conditions in the following way, that involves the optimal transport plans.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be gauge metric measure spaces such that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense, with approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
as in Remark
5.2
. We introduce the following conditions:

•

L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions over plans:

for all sequences
\mu_{0,k}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
,
\mu_{1,k}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
with
\supp\mu_{0,k}\cap\supp\mu_{1,k}=\emptyset
, and all
\nu_{k}\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0,k},\mu_{1,k})
such that (
3.15
) holds, for the corresponding sequence of optimal plans
\pi_{k}=({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu_{k}
such that
(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\pi_{k}
is weakly convergent in
\mathcal{P}(X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty})
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}((\star_{k},\star_{k}))}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))|\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)=0,\qquad\forall\,R>0.

(5.33)

•

regularity condition for
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
over plans:
for all
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
,
\pi\in{\rm Opt}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, it holds that
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
is continuous
\pi
-a.e.

Remark 5.13
.

Since
\pi_{k}
and
\pi
are supported out of the diagonal, the conditions above can be written in a fashion similar to Definition
5.3
, by removing the diagonal.

In Section
7.3.2
we show that the regularity condition above is satisfied in natural classes of examples.

Theorem 5.14

(Stability of
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
,
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
be gauge m.m.s. with
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH sense. Let
\beta
be distortion coefficients as in (
3.14
), and
n\in[1,+\infty
). Assume that:

(i)

(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
is locally compact and geodesic for all
k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}
;

(ii)

for any
R>0
there exists
D=D(R)>0
such that
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D
on
B_{R}(\star_{k})\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}\times B_{R}(\star_{k})\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
;

(iii)

the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence and regularity conditions over plans hold (cf. Definition
5.12
);

(iv)

for all
t\in(0,1)
the function
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to[0,+\infty)
is locally Lipschitz;

(v)

(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
;

Then, also
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.

Remark 5.15
.

The local boundedness of
\mathsf{G}_{k}
(i.e.
(ii)
) can be weakened by asking that
\mathsf{G}_{k}
is locally
\pi_{k}
-essentially bounded, uniformly with respect to
k\in\mathbb{N}
and all optimal plans
\pi_{k}
for which the
\mathsf{CD}
inequality (
3.15
) holds. More precisely, for all
R>0
there exists
D\in(0,+\infty)
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for all
\pi_{k}
for which the
\mathsf{CD}
inequality is required to hold on
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
, we have
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D

\pi_{k}
-a.e. on
B_{R}(\star_{k})\times B_{R}(\star_{k})
.

Proof.

We drop the
\infty
from the notation for the limit so that
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
will be denoted by
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G},\star)
.
Let
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X
be the approximations as in Remark
5.2
.
Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, and
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
. Since
\supp\mu_{i}
,
i=0,1
, is compact (recall that
(\supp\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{d})
is assumed to be locally compact), the distance between
\supp\mu_{0}
and
\supp\mu_{1}
is positive. We start by assuming that
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\ll\mathfrak{m}
, with continuous density
\mu_{i}=\rho_{i}\,\mathfrak{m}
, and bounded support. Define

\mu_{i,k}:=\frac{\rho_{i}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}}{\int_{X_{k}}\rho_{i}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}},\qquad i=0,1.

(5.34)

Notice that

Z_{i,k}:=\int_{X_{k}}\rho_{i}\circ f_{k}\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}=\int_{X}\rho_{i}\,(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}\to 1,\qquad i=0,1,

(5.35)

so that
\mu_{i,k}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
and
\supp\mu_{0,k}\cap\supp\mu_{1,k}=\emptyset
, for sufficiently large
k
.

By the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
we find a
W_{2}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
-geodesic
(\mu_{t,k})_{t\in[0,1]}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
, induced by
\nu_{k}\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0,k},\mu_{1,k})
such that

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})\geq\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X_{k})}\log\beta_{1-t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}))\,\nu_{k}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{0,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})\\
+\exp\left(\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X_{k})}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu_{k}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)\right)\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{1,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k}),\quad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(5.36)

By construction,
\supp\mu_{i}
and
\supp(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{i,k}
,
i=0,1
, are contained in a common bounded set, say
B_{R}(\star)\subset X
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and some
R>0
. Thus, using the properties of the approximating maps
f_{k}
and the fact that
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
are locally compact and geodesic, one can show that the supports of the family
\{(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},t\in[0,1]}
are contained in the common compact set
\bar{B}_{2R}(\star)
. By stability of optimal transport
[
86
, Thm. 28.9]
, up to extraction, there exists a
W_{2}(X,\mathsf{d})
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, and corresponding optimal plan
\pi\in{\rm Opt}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
with

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}W_{2}((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k},\mu_{t})\to 0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\pi_{k}\rightharpoonup\pi.

(5.37)

In particular by Proposition
3.2
we also have
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}\rightharpoonup\mu_{t}
weakly.

We can now pass to the limit in the l.h.s. of (
5.36
):

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{U}_{n}((f_{k})_{\sharp}\mu_{t,k}|(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k})\geq\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k}),

(5.38)

where in the first inequality we use the joint upper semi-continuity of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
, and in the second one we used the fact that the push-forward via a Borel map does not increase the entropy (cf.
[
86
, Thm. 29.20 (i), (ii)]
).

We now study the r.h.s. of (
5.36
) as
k\to\infty
. We first observe that, for
i=0,1

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{i,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})=\frac{1}{Z_{i,k}}\int_{X}\rho_{i}\log\rho_{i}\,(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}-\log Z_{i,k}.

(5.39)

Since
\rho_{i}
is continuous and with bounded support, then
\rho_{i}\log\rho_{i}
is bounded, continuous, and with bounded support. Furthermore, since
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}\rightharpoonup\mathfrak{m}
and
Z_{i,k}\to 1
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{i,k}|\mathfrak{m}_{k})=\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{i}|\mathfrak{m}),\qquad i=0,1.

(5.40)

This settles the entropic factors in the right hand side of (
5.36
).

To deal with the remaining factors in the right hand side of (
5.36
) we first claim that

\pi\shortminus\esssup\mathsf{G}\leq D.

(5.41)

To prove it, notice that
\supp\pi_{k}\subseteq B_{\bar{R}}((\star_{k},\star_{k}))\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}
for sufficiently large
\bar{R}>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore by assumption
(ii)
there exists
D>0
such that
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D
on
\supp\pi_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If there exists set
A\subset X\times X
with
\pi(A)>0
such that
\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq D+\varepsilon
for all
(x,y)\in A
, then, letting
A_{k}:=(f_{k},f_{k})^{-1}(A)
, we obtain

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{A_{k}}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)-\mathsf{G}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))|\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)\geq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\varepsilon\int_{A_{k}}\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)>0,

(5.42)

contradicting the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence over plans of Definition
5.12
.

In particular it holds

\pi(\{(x,y)\mid\mathsf{G}(x,y)\geq D+\varepsilon\})=0,\qquad\forall\,\varepsilon>0,

(5.43)

proving (
5.41
). Fix
\varepsilon>0
once for all, and let:

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y):=\min\{\mathsf{G}(x,y),D+\varepsilon\},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\in X\times X.

(5.44)

The modified gauge function has the following properties:

1.

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)\leq D+\varepsilon
for all
x,y\in X
;

2.

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y)=\mathsf{G}(x,y)
for
\pi
-a.e.
(x,y)\in X\times X
;

3.

The
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of the gauge functions for plans of Definition
5.12
remains true replacing
\mathsf{G}
with
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
. In particular it holds:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{B_{R}((\star_{k},\star_{k}))}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)-\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))|\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)=0,\qquad\forall\,R>0.

(5.45)

Item (a) is trivial, while (b) follows from (
5.41
). Item (c) follows from the observation that, since
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D
on
\supp\pi_{k}
for large
k
, the integrand in (
5.33
) decreases when replacing
\mathsf{G}
with
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
, for all fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We now study the second factor in the right hand side of (
5.36
) (the other one is similar). We have:

\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X_{k})}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu_{k}(\mathrm{d}\gamma)=\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,((f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\pi_{k})(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)\\
+\int_{X_{k}\times X_{k}}\Big[\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)\big)-\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))\big)\Big]\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z).

(5.46)

To proceed with the proof, assume that
\beta_{t}:[0,+\infty]\to[0,+\infty]
is bounded on bounded intervals. This is the case precisely when
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
.

We claim first that the second integral in the r.h.s. of (
5.46
) converges to zero for any
t\in(0,1)
as
k\to\infty
. First observe that
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
, restricted to
[0,D+\varepsilon]
, is bounded from below away from zero (cf. Proposition
3.4
(vi)
), bounded from above, and Lipschitz continuous on
[0,D+\varepsilon]
. Thus
\log\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is Lipschitz continuous on
[0,D+\varepsilon]
, with Lipschitz constant
C>0
(the constant depends on
\beta_{t}
and
D+\varepsilon
only). Furthermore, the arguments of
\log\beta_{t}(\cdot)
take values on
[0,D+\varepsilon]
everywhere: the first one since
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D
on
\supp\pi_{k}
for large
k
, while the second one by construction of
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
. Hence for the second integral in the r.h.s. of (
5.46
) we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}\times X_{k}}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)\big)-\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))\big)\right\rvert\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\leq C\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X_{k}\times X_{k}}\left\lvert\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,z)-\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(z))\right\rvert\,\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)=0.

(5.47)

It remains to discuss the limit of the first term in the r.h.s. of (
5.46
). We have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)=\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z).

(5.48)

In (
5.48
), we used the fact that
\log\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}=\log\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}
and is continuous
\pi
-a.e., bounded by construction, so that we can apply
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.10]
.

Furthermore we observe that, if
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is the dynamic optimal plan representing the
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, we have

\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)=\int_{\mathrm{Geo}(X)}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\big)\,\nu(\mathrm{d}\gamma).

(5.49)

This concludes the proof in the case when
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is finite on bounded intervals (that is when
\mathcal{D}=+\infty
), and both
\rho_{i}=\mathrm{d}\mu_{i}/\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}
are continuous. The extension to the case
\mathcal{D}<+\infty
is done verbatim as in the proof of Theorem
5.6
.

The strategy to reduce the general case (i.e.
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
) to the previous one is also along the same lines. As in the proof of Theorem
5.6
, we regularize
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
to obtain a.c. measures with continuous density
[
86
, Ch. 29, First Appendix]
. The only difference in the argument is that, when passing to the limit, one uses (
5.41
) in order to apply
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.10]
.
∎

Remark 5.16

(A semi-continuous variant)
.

Instead of assuming
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
to satisfy the
regularity condition over plans
(cf. Definition
5.12
), one could alternatively assume that
\beta_{t}\circ\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
is lower semi-continuous on
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}\times\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
, for all fixed
t\in(0,1)
. In the proof, (
5.48
) would be replaced by the inequality

\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,(f_{k},f_{k})_{\sharp}\pi_{k}(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z)\geq\int_{X\times X}\log\beta_{t}\big(\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,z)\big)\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}z).

(5.50)

which would follow from
[
34
, Cor. 2.2.6]
. Note that (
5.50
) is sufficient for the proof.

5.3
Compactness for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
and
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces

The goal of this section is to establish some compactness results for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
and
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces. First, let us recall some notation. Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space.
Given an open subset
\Omega\subset X
, we denote with
\mathrm{LIP}(\Omega)
the set of Lipschitz functions
u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
. The space
\mathrm{LIP}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
is the set of functions
u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that
u|_{A}\in\mathrm{LIP}(A)
for every open set
A\Subset\Omega
.

For
u\in\mathrm{LIP}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, we define the
modulus of the gradient
of
u
at
x\in\Omega
as

\|\nabla u\|(x):=\liminf_{r\to 0}\frac{1}{r}\sup_{y\in\overline{B_{r}(x)}}|u(y)-u(x)|.

(5.51)

Following
[
70
, Sec. 3]
, for
u\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner\Omega)
, we define the
total variation
of
u
on
\Omega
as

\|Du\|(\Omega):=\inf\left\{\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\|\nabla u_{k}\|\mathfrak{m}\mid(u_{k})_{k}\subset\mathrm{LIP}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega),\;u_{k}\to u\text{ in }L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner\Omega)\right\}.

(5.52)

A function
u\in L^{1}_{loc}(\Omega,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner\Omega)
is said to have bounded total variation on
\Omega
provided
\|Du\|(\Omega)<\infty
. For
u\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner\Omega)
with bounded total variation on
\Omega
, we denote

\|u\|_{BV(\Omega)}:=\|u\|_{L^{1}(\Omega,\mathfrak{m}\llcorner\Omega)}+\|Du\|(\Omega).

(5.53)

The next result gives pre-compactness criteria for gauge metric measure spaces satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}
, that generalizes the well-known one for metric measures spaces.

Theorem 5.17

(Pre-compactness for
\mathsf{MCP}
)
.

Let
\beta
be distortion coefficients as in (
3.14
). Let
\{(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k},\star_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of pointed gauge metric measure spaces, with
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}=X_{k}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
. Assume that for all
L>0
there exists
D=D(L)>0
such that

\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(B_{L}(\star_{k}))\leq D(L),\qquad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{N}.

(5.54)

Then, the following hold:

(i)

If there exists
M>0
such that
\tfrac{1}{M}\leq\mathfrak{m}_{k}(B_{1}(\star_{k}))\leq M
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then the family
\{(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is pre-compact in the pmGH topology.

Moreover, if
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
is any pmGH limit space, then
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty})
is proper and geodesic.

(ii)

Assume that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH topology, by means of approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
. Assume that the family
\{\mathsf{G}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is locally asymptotically equi-continuous, in the sense that for all
L>0
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,L)>0
and
\bar{k}=\bar{k}(\varepsilon,L)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq\bar{k}
it holds:

\mathsf{d}_{k}(x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime})\leq\delta,\quad\mathsf{d}_{k}(y_{k},y_{k}^{\prime})\leq\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})-\mathsf{G}_{k}(x^{\prime}_{k},y^{\prime}_{k})|\leq\varepsilon,

(5.55)

for all
x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime},y_{k},y_{k}^{\prime}\in B_{L}(\star_{k})
.

Then, up to extraction of a subsequence, there exists a continuous map
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}:X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty)
, and
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
, such that

|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(y))|\leq\varepsilon_{k},\qquad\forall\,x,y\in B_{L}(\star_{k}).

(5.56)

In particular the conditions of Definition
5.3
hold, and if the functions
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz, then
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

(iii)

With the same assumption as in item
(ii)
, if the
\mathsf{G}_{k}
are meek (cf. Definition
4.23
), then the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
and Propositions
4.29
–
4.31
hold for
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
.

(iv)

Assume that
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH topology, by means of approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
. Assume that

•

for every
x\in X_{\infty}
there exist
x_{k}\in X_{k}
such that
f_{k}(x_{k})\to x
in
X_{\infty}
and

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},\cdot)\|_{{\rm BV}(B_{L}(\star_{k}))}<\infty,\qquad\forall\,L>0;

(5.57)

•

the spaces
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
satisfy a weak
L^{1}
-Poincaré inequality, with uniform constants in
k
: there exist
c\geq 1,C>0,R>0
such that for every
f\in\mathrm{LIP}_{\mathrm{loc}}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
, every
r\in(0,R]
, and every
x\in X_{k}
, it holds

\frac{1}{\mathfrak{m}_{k}(B^{X_{k}}_{r}(x))}\int_{B^{X_{k}}_{r}(x)}\int_{B^{X_{k}}_{r}(x)}|f(y)-f(z)|\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}y)\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}z)\\
\leq Cr\int_{B^{X_{k}}_{cr}(x)}\|\nabla f\|(y)\,\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\mathrm{d}y).

(5.58)

Then, up to extraction of a subsequence, there exists a map
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}:X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty]
with
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(x,\cdot)\in{\rm BV_{loc}}(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
for every
x\in X_{\infty}
such that
\mathsf{G}_{k}\to\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense of Definition
5.3
. If in addition
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
satisfies the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
and the functions
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz, then
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

Proof.

From
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}=X_{k}
, the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition, and the local
\mathsf{G}
-boundedness assumption, each
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k})
is a locally compact geodesic space (see Corollary
4.16
). We adopt the convention of Remark
5.2
for the pmGH convergence.

Proof of
(i)
.
Thanks to our assumptions on the
\mathsf{G}
-diameters, we have that the family of metric spaces
\{(B_{L}(\star_{k}),\mathsf{d}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are totally bounded for any fixed
L>0
, uniformly with respect to
k
(cf. Corollary
4.16
and Remark
4.17
); hence, the pre-compactness in the pGH topology follows from the classical characterization in terms of nets (cf. for example
[
86
, Thm. 27.10, Def. 27.13]
). Using the local doubling inequality of Corollary
4.14
, we have that the assumption
\mathfrak{m}_{k}(B_{1}(\star_{k}))\leq M
propagates to any scale, that is there exists
M=M(R)>0
such that
\mathfrak{m}_{k}(B_{R}(\star_{k}))\leq M(R)
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus we obtain via a diagonal argument that
(f_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}_{k}
is pre-compact in the weak topology. The assumption
\mathfrak{m}_{k}(B_{1}(\star_{k}))\geq\tfrac{1}{M}
prevents the limit measures to vanish identically.

Now, let
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
be any pmGH limit space. Then
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
satisfies a local doubling inequality with the same (uniform) constants as the approximating sequence, and thus
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty})
is proper. Moreover,
\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty}
is a length space, as GH-limit of length spaces. Since any proper length space is geodesic, we conclude that
(\supp\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty})
is a geodesic space.

Proof of
(ii)
. We can apply Arzelà-Ascoli theorem for pmGH converging sequences (see for instance
[
86
, Prop. 27.20]
). Letting
f^{\prime}_{k}:X_{\infty}\to X_{k}
be the approximate inverses of
f_{k}
(i.e.
\mathsf{d}_{\infty}(f_{k}\circ f^{\prime}_{k}(y),y)\to 0
and
\mathsf{d}_{k}(f^{\prime}_{k}\circ f_{k}(x),x)\to 0
as
k\to\infty
), there exists a continuous
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}:X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty)
such that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{x,y\in B_{L}(\star_{\infty})}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(f_{k}^{\prime}(x),f_{k}^{\prime}(y))-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(x,y)|=0,\qquad\forall\,L>0.

(5.59)

Using the approximate inverse property and the equi-continuity of
\{\mathsf{G}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we obtain

\lim_{k\to\infty}\sup_{x,y\in B_{L}(\star_{k})}|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(y))|=0,\qquad\forall\,L>0.

(5.60)

Clearly, (
5.60
) implies the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of Definition
5.3
.

Furthermore, by construction,
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
is continuous. All the assumptions of Theorem
5.6
are then met, and thus the limit
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

Proof of
(iii)
.
Fix
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
and
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}\setminus\supp\mu_{1}
. We claim that there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu_{1})
satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}
inequality (
3.16
) and there exists a Borel set
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
with
\nu(\Gamma)=1
, such that it holds

\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})=t\,\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1],\quad\forall\,\gamma\in\Gamma.

(5.61)

Note that (
5.61
) is weaker than the meek property for
\mathsf{G}
, but it is sufficient for the proof of the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
, as explained in Remark
4.24
.

We prove now the claim. By the argument used in the proof of Theorem
5.6
, we know that there exist
\mu_{1,k}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})
,
\bar{x}_{k}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
with
\bar{x}_{k}\notin\supp\mu_{1,k}
,
\nu_{k}\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}},\mu_{1,k})
, and
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu_{1})
such that
\nu_{k}
and
\nu
satisfy the
\mathsf{MCP}
condition and moreover (up to a extraction of a subsequence)

(f_{k})_{\sharp}\big(({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu_{k}\big)\rightharpoonup({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

(5.62)

Since each
\mathsf{G}_{k}
is meek, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
\Gamma_{k}\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X_{k})
with
\nu_{k}(\Gamma_{k})=1
, such that for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{k}
it holds

\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})=t\,\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1].

(5.63)

From (
5.62
) we infer that for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma
, there exist
\gamma_{k}\in\Gamma_{k}
such that

f_{k}(\gamma_{k,t})\to\gamma_{t},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}.

(5.64)

Since
\mu_{1}
has bounded support, one can choose the

\nu_{k}\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}_{k}},\mu_{1,k})
with the following property: there exists
R>0
such that
\cup_{t\in[0,1]}\supp\big(({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu_{k}\big)\subset B_{R}(\bar{x}_{k})
. It follows that the geodesics
\gamma_{k}
have a uniform bound on the length and thus are equi-Lipschitz. Hence, by the Arzelà-Ascoli Theorem for varying spaces converging in the GH sense (see for instance
[
86
, Prop. 27.20]
), we can promote (
5.64
) to

f_{k}(\gamma_{k,t})\to\gamma_{t}\;\text{ for every $t\in[0,1]$}.

(5.65)

Combining (
5.63
) and (
5.65
) with the asymptotic equi-continuity of the Gauge functions (
5.55
), we obtain for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(X)

\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{k,0},\gamma_{k,t})=t\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathsf{G}_{k}(\gamma_{k,0},\gamma_{1,t})=t\,\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t}),\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1],

(5.66)

as claimed.

Proof of
(iv)
.
By assumption, the spaces
\{(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy local doubling (see Corollary
4.14
) and weak
L^{1}
-Poincaré inequalities, with constants uniform in
k\in\mathbb{N}
; moreover they converge in pmGH sense to
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
. Under such conditions, it is well-known that BV compactness in varying spaces holds (see for instance
[
59
, Thm. 4.15]
). It follows that there exists a map
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}:X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty)
with
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(x,\cdot)\in{\rm BV_{loc}}(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty})
for every
x\in X_{\infty}
such that
\mathsf{G}_{k}\to\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense of Definition
5.3
. If in addition
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}
satisfies the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
and the functions
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz, then the assumptions of Theorem
5.6
are met; thus we can conclude that the limit
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.
∎

We next comment on the assumptions of Theorem
5.17
.

Remark 5.18
.

The weak
L^{1}
-Poincaré inequality (
5.58
) is a very natural assumption in the framework of this paper. Indeed it was proved to hold with
c=2
, any
R>0
, for an explicit
C=C(K,N,R)>0
in the class of
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
spaces
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfying the following mild “a.e. non-branching” assumption
[
88
, Cor. p. 28]
: the set

\{y\in X\mid\text{ there exist }\gamma^{1}\neq\gamma^{2}\in{\rm Geo}(X)\text{ such that }x=\gamma_{0}^{1}=\gamma_{0}^{2},\,y=\gamma_{1}^{1}=\gamma_{1}^{2}\}

(5.67)

has
\mathfrak{m}
-measure zero for
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in X
. It is well-known that such a property holds for essentially non-branching
\mathsf{MCP}(K,N)
spaces (see for instance
[
38
, Rmk. 2.6]
).

Remark 5.19
.

The classical framework of Lott-Sturm-Villani’s theory corresponds to the choice of gauge function as
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot):=\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)
, for
x_{0}\in X
. In this case,
\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)
is (trivially) 1-Lipschitz; thus the asymptotic equi-continuity assumed in item
(ii)
holds trivially, and the uniform local BV bounds (
5.57
) assumed in item
(iv)
hold as a consequence of the uniform upper bound on the volume of metric balls.

Since
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
implies
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, it is clear that
(i)
in Theorem
5.17
implies a corresponding pre-compactness result for the class of
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces. Also a compactness result analogous to item
(ii)
can be proved along the same lines, so we only state it.

Theorem 5.20

(Pre-compactness for
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
\beta
be the distortion coefficients as (
3.14
), and
n\in[1,+\infty)
. Let
\{(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k},\star_{k})\}_{k\in\bar{\mathbb{N}}}
be a family of pointed gauge metric measure spaces, with
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\star_{k})\to(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\star_{\infty})
in the pmGH topology, by means of approximating maps
f_{k}:X_{k}\to X_{\infty}
as in Remark
5.2
. Assume that

•

for any
L>0
there exists
D=D(L)>0
such that
\mathsf{G}_{k}\leq D
on
B_{L}(\star_{k})\times B_{L}(\star_{k})\cap\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}\times\supp\mathfrak{m}_{k}
;

•

the family
\{\mathsf{G}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is locally asymptotically equi-continuous, in the sense that for all
L>0
and
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,L)>0
and
\bar{k}=\bar{k}(\varepsilon,L)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq\bar{k}
it holds:

\mathsf{d}_{k}(x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime})\leq\delta,\quad\mathsf{d}_{k}(y_{k},y_{k}^{\prime})\leq\delta\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x_{k},y_{k})-\mathsf{G}_{k}(x^{\prime}_{k},y^{\prime}_{k})|\leq\varepsilon,

(5.68)

for all
x_{k},x_{k}^{\prime},y_{k},y_{k}^{\prime}\in B_{L}(\star_{k})
.

Then, up to extraction of a subsequence, there exists a continuous map
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}:X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}\to[0,+\infty)
, and
\varepsilon_{k}\downarrow 0
, such that

|\mathsf{G}_{k}(x,y)-\mathsf{G}_{\infty}(f_{k}(x),f_{k}(y))|\leq\varepsilon_{k},\qquad\forall\,x,y\in B_{L}(\star_{k}).

(5.69)

In particular the conditions of Definition
5.12
hold. If the functions
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{R}
are locally Lipschitz, and
(X_{k},\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},\mathsf{G}_{k})
satisfy the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
, then also
(X_{\infty},\mathsf{d}_{\infty},\mathfrak{m}_{\infty},\mathsf{G}_{\infty})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.

Remark 5.21
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a Carnot group, equipped with its Haar measure. It is well-known that they support Poincaré inequalities (see e.g.
[
51
, Prop. 11.17]
and references within). If the Carnot group is ideal (which means, in the special case of Carnot groups, that it is also fat and thus of step
\leq 2
) and it is equipped with a natural gauge function (see Section
7
), then
\mathsf{G}
is locally bounded (see Figure
3
). Furthermore
\mathsf{d}
is locally semiconcave in charts outside of the diagonal
[
76
]
. As a consequence one can prove that natural gauge functions satisfy

\sup_{x\in\Omega}\|\mathsf{G}(x,\cdot)\|_{BV(\Omega)}<+\infty,

(5.70)

for any bounded set
\Omega
. This means that one can apply the compactness Theorem
5.17
(iv)
to sequences of ideal Carnot groups equipped with natural gauge functions provided that the Poincaré inequality, the
\mathrm{BV}
bound on
\mathsf{G}
, and the local
\mathsf{G}
-diameter bound hold with uniform constants w.r.t. to the sequence.

6
Vector-valued gauge functions

In this section we extend the previous theory to the case of a vector-valued gauge function. Let
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}:=[0,+\infty)^{m}
. We consider the real projective “positive” space:

\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}:=\Big(\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{+}\setminus\{0\}\Big)/\sim\,,

(6.1)

where
x\sim y
if
x=\lambda y
for
\lambda>0
. To convey the correct intuition, in this section
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
shall be thought as a compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, adding one point at infinity for every direction, rather than space of directions in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}_{+}
.

The map
x\mapsto[x:1]
embeds
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
in
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
. For
\theta\in\mathbb{RP}_{+}^{m}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, we set
|\theta|=+\infty
; otherwise, if
\theta=[x:1]
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
|\theta|
is the standard norm of the vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
.

Finally, dilations on
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
extend to
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
: if
\theta=[x:a]
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
, and
t>0
, we denote
t\theta=[tx:a]
.

A vector valued gauge function on a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is a Borel function

\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}\,.

(6.2)

Let
N\in[1,+\infty)
, and
\mathsf{s}:[0,+\infty)^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function such that

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=c|\theta|^{N}+o(|\theta|^{N})\qquad\text{ for some }c>0.

(6.3)

The
positivity domain
of
\mathsf{s}
is the largest open and star-shaped (with respect to the origin
0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
) subset
{\rm{DOM}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
such that
\mathsf{s}>0
on
{\rm{DOM}}\setminus\{0\}
. Since
{\rm{DOM}}
is star-shaped then, for every
\theta\neq 0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, there is a unique non-zero limit point of
{\rm{DOM}}
in
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
, along the half-line defined by
\theta
, which we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
(it can be either in the affine part
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
or at infinity, see Figure
2
).

Figure 2:
An example of positivity domain
{\rm{DOM}}
and the corresponding
\mathcal{D}_{\theta}\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
.

Define the
distortion coefficient

\beta_{(\cdot)}(\cdot):[0,1]\times\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}\to[0,+\infty]
as

\displaystyle\beta_{t}(\theta):=\begin{cases}t^{N}\quad&|\theta|=0,\\
\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}(\theta)}&|\theta|\neq 0\text{ and }\theta\in\mathrm{DOM},\\
\displaystyle\liminf_{\phi\to\mathcal{D}_{\theta}^{-}}\dfrac{\mathsf{s}(t\phi)}{\mathsf{s}(\phi)}&\theta\notin{\rm{DOM}},\end{cases}

(6.4)

where the liminf is a directional limit, in the direction of
\theta
, from the inside of
{\rm{DOM}}
.

For a scalar gauge function, in some statements it was useful to consider the case when
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is monotone. When the gauge function is vector-valued, the appropriate counterpart is the
radial monotonicity
: we say that
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is
radially non-increasing
(resp.
radially non-decreasing
) if, for every fixed
\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}\setminus\{0\}
and
t\in(0,1)
, the function
\lambda\mapsto\beta_{t}(\lambda\theta)
is non-increasing (resp. non-decreasing). Likewise, we say that
\mathsf{s}(\cdot)
is
radially real-analytic on
\overline{{\rm{DOM}}}
if for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
the function
[0,|\mathcal{D}_{\theta}|]\ni t\mapsto\mathsf{s}(t\hat{\theta})
is real-analytic, where
\hat{\theta}:=\theta/|\theta|
.

Upon a suitable reformulation, we have the following elementary properties that generalize the ones of Proposition
3.4
to the vector case.

Proposition 6.1
.

Any distortion coefficient satisfies the following:

(i)

\beta_{0}(\theta)=0
and
\beta_{1}(\theta)=1
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
;

(ii)

if
|\mathcal{D}_{\theta}|<+\infty
, then
\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
for all
\theta\notin{\rm{DOM}}
and
t\in(0,1)
;

(iii)

\beta_{t}(\theta)=0
for some
|\theta|<+\infty
if and only if
t=0
;

(iv)

for every
t\in[0,1]
,
\theta_{j},\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, if
\theta_{j}\to\theta
along the direction of
\theta
, then
\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})\to\beta_{t}(\theta)
in
[0,+\infty]
; if moreover
\theta,\theta_{j}\in{\rm{DOM}}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\theta_{j}\to\theta
, then
\beta_{t}(\theta_{j})\to\beta_{t}(\theta)
in
[0,+\infty)
, for every
t\in[0,1]
;

(v)

\beta
is continuous and finite when restricted to
[0,1]\times{\rm{DOM}}
;

(vi)

for all fixed
t\in(0,1]
, the distortion coefficient
\beta_{t}(\cdot)
is bounded from below away from zero on any bounded set of
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
;

(vii)

assume that
{\rm{DOM}}
is a bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, that
\mathsf{s}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{{\rm{DOM}}})
and
\mathsf{s}
is radially real analytic on
\overline{{\rm{DOM}}}
. Then there exists
N^{\prime}\geq N
such that
\beta_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{N^{\prime}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
.

Proof.

The only item requiring a proof is
(vii)
. Denote by
\mathsf{s}^{(k)}_{rad}(x):=\left.\frac{d^{k}}{dt^{k}}\right|_{t=1}\mathsf{s}(tx)
the
k^{th}
-order derivative of
\mathsf{s}
in the radial direction. Assume by contradiction that there exists a sequence
(x_{k})\subset\partial{\rm{DOM}}
such that
\mathsf{s}^{(j)}_{rad}(x_{k})=0
for all
j\leq k
. Since by assumption
{\rm{DOM}}
is bounded and
\mathsf{s}
satisfies (
6.3
) then, up to a non-relabeled subsequence, there exists
x_{\infty}\in\partial{\rm{DOM}}{\setminus\{0\}}
such that
x_{k}\to x_{\infty}
. Then, by the smoothness of
\mathsf{s}
, we infer that
\mathsf{s}^{(k)}_{rad}(x_{\infty})=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. But then, since
\mathsf{s}
is radially real-analytic on
\overline{{\rm{DOM}}}
, it must be that
\mathsf{s}\equiv 0
on the half line
\{tx_{\infty}\mid t>0\}\cap\overline{{\rm{DOM}}}
, contradicting (
6.3
).
∎

The definitions of
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
and
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
for a metric measure space
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
endowed with a vector-valued gauge
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
are verbatim as in Definition
3.5
, once the distortion coefficients are as in (
6.4
).

6.1
Geometric consequences

Generalized Brunn-Minkowski

The generalized Brunn-Minkowski inequality for
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces (Theorem
4.1
) and the generalized half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
spaces (Theorem
4.2
) hold with verbatim the same statements and proofs in case of a vector-valued gauge function.

Gauge diameter estimate

The following extends Definition
4.9
to the vector-valued case.

Definition 6.2
.

The
\mathsf{G}
-diameter for a non-empty set
S\subset X
is defined as

\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)=\sup_{x\in S}\left(\mathfrak{m}\shortminus\esssup_{y\in S,\,y\neq x}|\mathsf{G}(x,y)|\right).

(6.5)

A non-empty set
S\subset X
is
\mathsf{G}
-bounded if it has finite
\mathsf{G}
-diameter.

Recall that
{\rm{DOM}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
is defined as the largest open and star-shaped (with respect to the origin) subset such that
\mathsf{s}>0
on
{\rm{DOM}}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proposition 6.3

(
\mathsf{G}
-diameter estimate)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
. Let
\beta
as in (
6.4
), and assume that
\beta_{t}(\theta)=+\infty
for all
\theta\notin{\rm{DOM}}
,
t\in(0,1)
. Note that this is in particular the case if
{\rm{DOM}}
is bounded.

•

If
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, then for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in{\rm{DOM}},\qquad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e. $y\in X$,\, $y\neq x$}.

(6.6)

In particular,
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\supp\mathfrak{m})\leq\diam_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}({\rm{DOM}})
.

•

If
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
for some
n\in[1,+\infty)
, then

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in{\rm{DOM}},\qquad\pi\text{-a.e. }(x,y)\in X\times X,

(6.7)

for all
\pi=({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu
, where
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
is such that inequality (
3.15
) holds.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction. Fix
R>0
,
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and let

A=\{y\mid y\neq x,\,\mathsf{G}(x,y)\notin{\rm{DOM}}\}\cap B_{R}(x).

(6.8)

Assume by contradiction that
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
. Then we apply the half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality to the set
A
. Notice that
\beta_{t}(x,A)=+\infty
for all
t\in(0,1)
, and that
A_{t}\subset(B_{R}(x))_{t}\subset B_{tR}(x)
. We obtain hence

\mathfrak{m}(B_{tR}(x))\geq\bar{\mathfrak{m}}(A_{t})=+\infty,\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1),

(6.9)

contradicting the local finiteness of
\mathfrak{m}
.

To prove the second part of the proposition, we argue in a similar way: assume that there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
as in the statement (in particular
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
have bounded and disjoint supports) such that

\pi(\{(x,y)\in X\times X\mid\mathsf{G}(x,y)\notin{\rm{DOM}}\})>0.

(6.10)

Using (
3.15
) we obtain that, for the corresponding
W_{2}
-geodesic
\mu_{t}=({\rm e}_{t})_{\sharp}\nu
it holds

\mathrm{U}_{n}(\mu_{t}|\mathfrak{m})=+\infty,\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(6.11)

This implies that
\mathfrak{m}(\supp\mu_{t})=+\infty
. However, since
\supp\mu_{0}
and
\supp\mu_{1}
are bounded, then also
\supp\mu_{t}
is bounded, yielding a contradiction.
∎

Local doubling

The following proposition can be proved along the same lines of Proposition
4.13
.

Proposition 6.4

(Local doubling)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. with gauge function
\mathsf{G}
, satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
with
\beta
as in (
6.4
). Then any
\mathsf{G}
-bounded Borel subset
S\subseteq\supp\mathfrak{m}
satisfies the following inequality: for all
t\in(0,1)
there exists
C_{S,t}>0
such that

\mathfrak{m}(B_{r}(x_{0})\cap S)\leq C_{S,t}\cdot\mathfrak{m}(B_{tr}(x_{0})),\qquad\forall\,r\geq 0,\;\forall\,x_{0}\in S.

(6.12)

The constant
C_{S,t}
can be estimated in terms of
\beta
and
\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)
:

\frac{1}{C_{S,t}}=\inf\{\beta_{t}(\theta)\,:\,|\theta|\in[0,\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(S)]\}\in(0,t^{N}].

(6.13)

Corollaries
4.14
,
4.15
and
4.16
hold with verbatim the same statements and proofs replacing, when necessary, the monotonicity of
\beta_{t}
with the radial monotonicity.

Geodesic dimension estimates

Recall the role of the parameter
N\in[1,+\infty)
in (
6.3
) and the Definition
4.18
of geodesic dimension
\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)
at a point
x
of a metric measure space. Since the proof of the geodesic dimension estimate (see Theorem
4.20
)

\dim_{\mathrm{geo}}(x)\leq N,\qquad\text{for all }x\in\supp\mathfrak{m},

(6.14)

built on top of the half-Brunn Minkowski inequality, which holds in the case of a vector-valued gauge function, the same result holds with analogous proof, provided that we assume the following non-triviality condition

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid z\neq x,\,|\mathsf{G}(x,z)|<+\infty\})>0,\qquad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(6.15)

which replaces (
4.41
) in the vector case.

Generalized Bishop-Gromov inequality

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space with gauge function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
. In the following, for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+},\mathsf{G}\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
, the notation

\mathsf{G}\leq r,\qquad\text{(resp.{} $\mathsf{G}\geq r$)},

(6.16)

means that
\mathsf{G}=[\mathsf{G}_{1}:\dots:\mathsf{G}_{m}:1]
is in the affine part
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\subset\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
and
\mathsf{G}_{i}\leq r_{i}
(resp.
\mathsf{G}_{i}\geq r_{i}
) for all
i=1,\dots,m
. In particular, the notation
\mathsf{G}\in(r,R)
for
r,R\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
means that
\mathsf{G}\leq R
and
r\leq\mathsf{G}
. In other words, the inequalities for vector-valued functions are meant component-wise. Furthermore, for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\setminus 0
, we set
\hat{r}:=\tfrac{r}{|r|}
; if
r=0
then
\hat{r}=0
.

Definition 6.5
.

For a point
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and for
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
\rho\geq 0
, we set:

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\}\right),

(6.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\delta}\mathfrak{m}\left(\left\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\in(r-\delta\hat{r},r],\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq\rho\right\}\right),\quad r\neq 0.

(6.18)

Consider also the following measure of “gauge balls” and “gauge spheres”:

\displaystyle\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{m}\left(\{x\in X\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)\leq r,\;\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)\leq|r|\}\right),

(6.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\delta\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{\delta}\left[\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r-\delta\hat{r})\right],\quad r\neq 0.

(6.20)

Notice that
\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)=\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,|r|)
but
\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)\neq\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,|r|)
.

The definition of the meek property is formulated verbatim for vector-valued gauge functions, see Definition
4.23
.

The proof of the generalized Bishop-Gromov inequality for a vector-valued gauge function can be adapted from the scalar case (Theorem
4.25
), by arguing along rays.

Theorem 6.6

(Generalized Bishop-Gromov)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. endowed with a gauge function
\mathsf{G}
satisfying the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
condition, with
\beta
as in (
6.4
).
Assume that

•

\supp\mathfrak{m}
is not a singleton;

•

\mathsf{G}
is meek; (see Remark
4.24
for a slightly weaker condition)

•

for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds (see Remark
6.7
for a slightly weaker condition)

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid x\neq z,\,|\mathsf{G}(x,z)|=+\infty\})=0.

(6.21)

Then for every
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
the following properties hold:

(i)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{d}(x_{0},x)=r\})=0
, for every
r>0
;

(ii)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x_{0}\})=0
; in particular, the half-Brunn-Minkowski inequality (
4.7
) holds for every
\bar{x}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and Borel set
A\subset X
with
\mathfrak{m}(A)>0
;

(iii)

\mathfrak{m}(\{x\mid\mathsf{G}(x_{0},x)=r\})=0
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
r\neq 0
;

(iv)

For every
\rho>0
, and
r,R\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
lying on the same direction
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}
with
0<|r|\leq|R|<\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)}{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)}\geq\frac{|R|}{|r|}\frac{\mathsf{s}(r)}{\mathsf{s}(R)}.

(6.22)

Furthermore, it holds

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)}{\int_{0}^{|r|}\mathsf{s}(t\theta)/t\,\mathrm{d}t}\geq\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},0,\rho)}{\int_{0}^{|R|}\mathsf{s}(t\theta)/t\,\mathrm{d}t};

(6.23)

(v)

Assume that
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is radially non-increasing for all
t\in[0,1]
, or that for all
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds
|\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)|\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.. Then, for every
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
,
r,R\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
lying on the same direction
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{m-1}_{+}
with
0<|r|\leq|R|<\mathcal{D}_{\theta}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)}{\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)}\geq\frac{|R|}{|r|}\frac{\mathsf{s}(r)}{\mathsf{s}(R)}.

(6.24)

Furthermore, it holds:

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r)}{\int_{0}^{|r|}\mathsf{s}(t\theta)/t\,\mathrm{d}t}\geq\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)}{\int_{0}^{|R|}\mathsf{s}(t\theta)/t\,\mathrm{d}t};

(6.25)

(vi)

For every compact set
K_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
, and
r,R\in K_{0}
there exists a constant

\frac{1}{C_{|r|/|R|,0}}:=\inf\{\beta_{|r|/|R|}(\theta)\mid\theta\in K_{0}\}\in(0,(|r|/|R|)^{N}],

(6.26)

such that, if
r,R\in K_{0}
lie along the same direction, it holds

\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},R)\leq\frac{|r|}{|R|}C_{|r|/|R|,0}\cdot\mathrm{s}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r).

(6.27)

As a consequence, the following doubling estimate for gauge balls holds: there exists a constant
C_{0}=C_{1/2,0}>0
such that

\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},2r)\leq C_{0}\cdot\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x_{0},r),\qquad\forall\,r\in K_{0}/2.

(6.28)

The estimate on the maximal number of disjoint gauge balls (Proposition
4.29
) holds with verbatim the same statement and proof in case of a vector-valued gauge function (replacing
\mathsf{G}
by
|\mathsf{G}|
in its statement and its proof).

Remark 6.7
.

For the proof of Thorem
6.6
condition (
6.21
) can be weakened as follows: for all
x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
and all
r>0
it holds

\mathfrak{m}(\{z\in X\mid\mathsf{d}(x,z)=r,\,|\mathsf{G}(x,z)|=+\infty\})=0.

(6.29)

Both conditions (
6.21
) and (
6.29
) follow from the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
, provided that
\beta_{t}(+\infty)=+\infty
, see Remark
4.11
.

Parametric doubling for gauge balls

Finally, the parametric doubling property (Proposition
4.31
) holds also in the case of vector-valued gauge functions. The proof follows verbatim making use of the appropriate vector-valued versions of the Bishop-Gromov theorem (Theorem
6.6
) and gauge diameter estimates (Proposition
6.3
).

Proposition 6.8

(Parametric doubling property)
.

With the same assumptions of Theorem
6.6
, for every bounded set
K\subseteq{\rm{DOM}}
there exists
C_{K}=C_{K}(\beta)\geq 1
such that

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,r,\rho)-\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,0,\rho)}\leq 2^{N}C_{K},\qquad\forall\,r\in K,\quad\forall\,\rho>0,\quad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m},

(6.30)

where
N>1
is given by (
6.3
). Furthermore,
C_{K}\downarrow 1
as
\diam_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}(K)\downarrow 0
.

If
\theta\mapsto\beta_{t}(\theta)
is radially monotone non-increasing for all
t\in[0,1]
, or if for all
x_{0}\in\supp\mathfrak{m}
it holds
|\mathsf{G}(x_{0},\cdot)|\geq\mathsf{d}(x_{0},\cdot)

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e., then the previous inequality holds true for gauge balls, with the same constants, that is

\frac{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,2r)}{\mathrm{v}_{\mathsf{G}}(x,r)}\leq 2^{N}C_{K},\qquad\forall\,r\in K,\quad\forall\,x\in\supp\mathfrak{m}.

(6.31)

6.2
Stability and compactness

We adopt verbatim the same notion of
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of gauge functions and regularity condition for the limit gauge function (resp. over plans) as in Definition
5.3
(resp. as in Definition
5.12
), in case of vector-valued gauge functions.
Under this convention, verbatim the same statements for the stability of
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
(i.e. Theorem
5.6
) and of
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
(i.e. Theorem
5.14
) hold. The proofs hold verbatim, with the only difference being the definition of the truncated gauge function
\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}
, which is now vector-valued; however, in order to follow the arguments, it is enough to replace (
5.22
) with

\mathsf{G}^{\varepsilon}(x,y):=\begin{cases}\mathsf{G}&\text{ if }|\mathsf{G}|\leq D+\varepsilon,\\
\frac{D+\varepsilon}{|\mathsf{G}|}\cdot\mathsf{G}&\text{ if }|\mathsf{G}|>D+\varepsilon.\end{cases}

(6.32)

Also the statements and proofs of the pre-compactness criteria for
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
(i.e. Theorem
5.17
) and
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
(i.e. Theorem
5.20
) hold verbatim in case of vector-valued gauge functions.

7
Natural gauge functions

The purpose of this section is to introduce natural gauge functions for metric spaces equipped with an additional reference metric. This construction is inspired by sub-Riemannian geometry, where the sub-Riemannian distance can always be seen as the restriction of a Riemannian length structure on a subset of admissible curves.

Definition 7.1

(Reference and extension)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a complete metric space. We say that a complete length metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
on
X
is a
reference
for
\mathsf{d}
if
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
, and
\mathsf{d},\mathsf{d}_{R}
induce the same topology on
X
. Furthermore, we say that the reference metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is an
extension
of
\mathsf{d}
if for all
\mathsf{d}
-rectifiable curves
\gamma
it holds

L_{\mathsf{d}_{R}}(\gamma)=L_{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma).

(7.1)

Remark 7.2
.

The inequality
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
implies that each rectifiable curve in
(X,\mathsf{d})
is rectifiable also in
(X,\mathsf{d}_{R})
. The fact that
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is complete is not implied by the other requirements. For example take the standard Carnot-Carathéodory metric on the Heisenberg group. Extend it to a Riemannian metric by setting
\|\partial_{z}\|=1/(1+z^{2})
. Any point on the
z
-axis is at extended distance
\leq\pi/2
from the origin, and the extension is not complete.

Definition 7.3

(Asymptotic Lipschitz number)
.

Let
(X,\rho)
be a metric space. For a Borel function
f:X\to[0,+\infty]
we define the asymptotic Lipschitz number with respect to
\rho
at
x\in X
as

\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\rho}[f](x):=\limsup_{z,w\to x}\frac{|f(z)-f(w)|}{\rho(z,w)},

(7.2)

with the convention that
\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\rho}[f](x)=0
if
x
is an isolated point.

7.1
The
\mathsf{D}
function

We introduce the building block for natural gauge functions.

Definition 7.4

(The
\mathsf{D}
function)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a metric space with reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Let
c:=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}^{2}
. We define:

\mathsf{D}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty],\qquad\mathsf{D}(x,y):=\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\mathsf{d}_{R}}[c(\cdot,y)](x).

(7.3)

For the next proposition we say that
f,g:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
are locally equivalent if for any
o\in X
there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
of
o
and a constant
C=C_{\mathcal{O}}>0
such that

C^{-1}g(x,y)\leq f(x,y)\leq Cg(x,y),\qquad\forall\,x,y\in\mathcal{O}.

(7.4)

Proposition 7.5

(Properties of the
\mathsf{D}
function)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d})
be a length metric space with reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. The
\mathsf{D}
function has the following properties:

(i)

it holds
\mathsf{d}\leq\mathsf{D}
;

(ii)

\mathsf{d}_{R}
and
\mathsf{d}
are locally equivalent if and only if
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{d}
are locally equivalent;

(iii)

\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
if and only if the reference is the trivial one, that is
\mathsf{d}_{R}=\mathsf{d}
;

(iv)

assume that
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is an extension of
\mathsf{d}
; then
\mathsf{d}
is locally equivalent to
\mathsf{d}_{R}
if and only if
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
;

(v)

For every fixed
y\in X
, the map
\mathsf{D}(\cdot,y):X\to[0,+\infty]
is upper semi-continuous.

Proof.

Proof of
(i)
.
By definition
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
. Therefore for all
x,y\in X
it holds

\mathsf{D}(x,y)=\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\mathsf{d}_{R}}[c(\cdot,y)](x)\geq\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\mathsf{d}}[c(\cdot,y)](x)=\mathsf{d}(x,y),

(7.5)

where we used the assumption that
(X,\mathsf{d})
is a length metric space in the last identity.

Proof of
(ii)
.
Observe first that for any
z,w,y\in X
it holds

\displaystyle\frac{|c(z,y)-c(w,y)|}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(z,w)}

\displaystyle=\frac{|\mathsf{d}(z,y)+\mathsf{d}(w,y)||\mathsf{d}(z,y)-\mathsf{d}(w,y)|}{2\mathsf{d}_{R}(z,w)}

(7.6)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\mathsf{d}(z,y)+\mathsf{d}(w,y)}{2}\right)\frac{\mathsf{d}(z,w)}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(z,w)}.

(7.7)

We prove first the
\Rightarrow
implication. By assumption, for
o\in X
there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
where it holds
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}\leq C\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Plugging the upper bound in (
7.7
) and taking the limsup for
z,w\to x
we obtain
\mathsf{D}(x,y)\leq C\mathsf{d}(x,y)
, for all
y\in X
and
x\in\mathcal{O}
. Together with the global inequality of
(i)
we obtain that
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{d}
are locally equivalent.

To prove the
\Leftarrow
implication, assume that
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{d}
are locally equivalent. Thus for
o\in X
there exists
r>0
and
C=C_{r}>0
such that
\mathsf{D}\leq C\mathsf{d}
on
B_{3r}(o)
. By dividing (
7.6
) by
\mathsf{d}(x,y)
, and taking the limsup, we obtain:

\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\mathsf{d}_{R}}[\mathsf{d}(\cdot,y)](x)=\limsup_{z,w\to x}\frac{|\mathsf{d}(z,y)-\mathsf{d}(w,y)|}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(w,z)}\leq C,\qquad\forall\,x,y\in B_{3r}(o),\quad x\neq y.

(7.8)

Let now
\mathcal{O}=B_{r}(o)
. For
a,b\in\mathcal{O}\setminus\{y\}
, and for any
\varepsilon>0
take a
\mathsf{d}_{R}
-unit-speed curve
\gamma:[0,\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon]\to X
joining
a
and
b
. Observe that
\gamma
is contained in
B_{3r}(o)
, that is the region where (
7.8
) holds. If
\gamma
does not cross
y
it holds

|\mathsf{d}(a,y)-\mathsf{d}(b,y)|\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{0}^{\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon}\mathrm{Lip}_{a}^{\mathsf{d}_{R}}[\mathsf{d}(\cdot,y)](\gamma_{t})dt\leq C\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b),\quad\forall\,a,b\in\mathcal{O}\setminus\{y\},

(7.9)

where in the second equality we used (
7.8
) since
\gamma
does not intersect
y
.

If, instead,
\gamma
intersect
y
, we can first assume without loss of generality that the intersection occurs at a single time
t\in(0,\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon)
, where
\gamma_{t}=y
. Then, define
y_{\pm}=\gamma_{t\pm\eta}
for sufficiently small
\eta>0
. Consider the two
\mathsf{d}_{R}
-unit-speed curves
\gamma_{-}=\gamma|_{[0,t-\eta]}
joining
a
with
y_{-}
and
\gamma_{+}=\gamma|_{[t+\eta,\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon]}
joining
y_{+}
with
b
. Neither of them intersects
y
and we can use the previous estimate for the endpoints of
\gamma_{\pm}
. Hence

\displaystyle|\mathsf{d}(a,y)-\mathsf{d}(b,y)|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathsf{d}(a,y)-\mathsf{d}(y_{-},y)|+|\mathsf{d}(y_{+},y)-\mathsf{d}(b,y)|+\mathsf{d}(y_{-},y_{+})

(7.10)

\displaystyle\leq C(t-\eta)+C(\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon-t-\eta)+\mathsf{d}(y_{-},y_{+})

(7.11)

\displaystyle=C(\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)+\varepsilon-2\eta)+\mathsf{d}(y_{-},y_{+}).

(7.12)

Letting
\varepsilon,\eta\to 0
we have
y_{-},y_{+}\to y
and we obtain that
|\mathsf{d}(a,y)-\mathsf{d}(b,y)|\leq C\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{O}\setminus\{y\}
. By continuity such an inequality holds for all
a,b\in\mathcal{O}
. Taking for example
b=y
we obtain
\mathsf{d}(a,b)\leq C\mathsf{d}_{R}(a,b)
for all
a,b\in\mathcal{O}
. Since
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is a reference, we also have the opposite inequality
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
, and thus
\mathsf{d}
,
\mathsf{d}_{R}
are locally equivalent.

Proof of
(iii)
.
If
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}_{R}
then clearly
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
. Conversely, if
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
, one can follow the same steps of the proof of point
(ii)
with
C=1
and
\mathcal{O}=X
, yielding that
\mathsf{d}\leq\mathsf{d}_{R}
on
X
. Since
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is a reference, it holds
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
so that
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}_{R}
.

Proof of
(iv)
.
Observe that if
\mathsf{d}_{R}
and
\mathsf{d}
are locally equivalent, then they have the same class of rectifiable curves; moreover, if
(X,\mathsf{d}_{R})
is an extension of
(X,\mathsf{d})
, on the class of rectifiable curves the length functionals associated with
\mathsf{d}
and
\mathsf{d}_{R}
coincide. Since
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is length it follows that
\mathsf{d}\leq\mathsf{d}_{R}
and since
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is an extension
\mathsf{d}_{R}\leq\mathsf{d}
. Thus
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}_{R}
and so
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
. On the other hand if
\mathsf{D}=\mathsf{d}
, then
\mathsf{d}_{R}=\mathsf{d}
by item
(iii)
.

Proof of
(v)
.
This is a consequence of the upper semi-continuity of the asymptotic Lipschitz number of a Borel function with respect to a length metric (cf.
[
13
]
).
∎

7.2
Definition of natural gauge functions

The functions
\mathsf{d}
,
\mathsf{D}
, and
\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}}
appear as gauge functions for a large class of sub-Riemannian metric spaces, depending on the geometric structure under consideration:

•

\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
for Riemannian manifolds, see Section
3.3.1
;

•

\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
for qualitative bounds on fat sub-Riemannian manifolds, see Section
9
;

•

\mathsf{G}=\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}}
for the Heisenberg group, see Section
10.1
. This quantity corresponds to the “absolute value of the vertical part of the covector”, appearing in the study of interpolation inequalities in the Heisenberg group performed in
[
19
]
, see Section
3.3.2
(there, such a function was called
\theta
);

•

\mathsf{G}=(\mathsf{G}_{1},\mathsf{G}_{2})
, with
\mathsf{G}_{1}=\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}}
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}=\mathsf{d}
(this is a vector-valued gauge function, see Section
6
), for left-invariant structures on three-dimensional Lie groups, see Section
10.5
.

This motivates the following definition of natural gauge functions.

Definition 7.6

(Natural gauge functions)
.

We call a
natural gauge function
any function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
, such that
\mathsf{G}(x,y)=f(\mathsf{d}(x,y),\mathsf{D}(x,y))
for all
x,y\in X
, where

f:\Omega:=\{(a,b)\in[0,+\infty)\times[0,+\infty]\mid a\leq b\}\to[0,+\infty],

(7.13)

is continuous and homogeneous on
\Omega
, i.e.
f(\lambda a,\lambda b)=\lambda f(a,b)
for all
\lambda>0
,
(a,b)\in\Omega
with
f(a,b)<+\infty
. Similarly, natural vector-valued gauge functions are those
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, induced by a continuous function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
, such that
f(\lambda a,\lambda b)=\lambda f(a,b)
for all
\lambda>0
and
a,b\in\Omega
with
|f(a,b)|<+\infty
(see Section
6
for the definition of dilations on
\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
).

To avoid trivialities, we assume that
|f(a,b)|<+\infty
for
(a,b)\in\Omega
if
a,b<+\infty
.

Remark 7.7
.

The
1
-homogeneity property in Definition
7.6
is required to preserve the meek assumption of Definition
4.23
, provided that
\mathsf{D}
is meek as well.

Remark 7.8
.

The function
\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}}
is well-defined for general metric spaces equipped with a reference metric, by Proposition
7.5
(i)
.

7.3
Natural gauge functions in sub-Riemannian metric spaces

We assume a basic knowledge of sub-Riemannian geometry. For the benefit of the reader, we collect in Appendix
A
a summary tailored to our purposes.

Definition 7.9

(Sub-Riemannian m.m.s.)
.

A sub-Riemannian metric measure space
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
is a smooth manifold
M
, equipped with a complete Carnot-Carathéodory distance
\mathsf{d}
, and a smooth measure
\mathfrak{m}
, i.e. with smooth positive density in local charts.

Under standard assumptions in sub-Riemannian geometry, natural gauge functions satisfy the conditions required for the validity of the results of Sections
4
and
5
. We discuss, in particular:

•

the meek condition for
\mathsf{G}
, see Definition
4.23
, used for the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
;

•

the regularity properties for
\mathsf{G}
, see Definitions
5.3
and
5.12
, used for the pre-compactness Theorems
5.17
and
5.20
;

•

the
\mathsf{G}-
boundedness properties, used for the stability Theorems
5.6
and
5.14
.

For simplicity we focus on the case of scalar-valued gauge functions, but all the statements of this section hold in general for the vector-valued case, with no modifications, using the corresponding definitions and statements that can be found in Section
6
.

7.3.1
Meek condition

We start with a sufficient condition for the validity of the meek assumption for
\mathsf{D}
, in terms of the local regularity of
\mathsf{d}
. We remark that it is valid for general m.m.s. with an underlying smooth structure.

Proposition 7.10

(Criterion for meekness)
.

Let
(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a m.m.s. equipped with a reference metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Assume that:

(a)

X
is a smooth manifold and
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is the distance induced by a Riemannian metric;

(b)

for any
x\in X
there exists a Borel set
C_{x}
with
\mathfrak{m}(C_{x})=0
, such that for all
y\notin C_{x}
and geodesic
\gamma
with
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})=(x,y)
the function
z\mapsto\mathsf{d}(z,\gamma_{t})
is continuously differentiable in charts in a neighborhood of
x
for any
t\in(0,1]
.

Then any natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}:X\times X\to[0,+\infty]
is meek.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the statement for
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
. Fix
\bar{x}\in X
and
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\bar{x}\notin\supp\mu_{1}
. Let
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{Geo}(X)
be the set of geodesics starting at
\bar{x}
and ending out of
C_{\bar{x}}
:

\Gamma:=({\rm e}_{0}\times{\rm e}_{1})^{-1}(\{\bar{x}\}\times X\setminus C_{\bar{x}}),

(7.14)

so that
\Gamma
is Borel.

Let
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\delta_{\bar{x}},\mu_{1})
. It holds
({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu=\pi=(\bar{x},{\rm id})_{\sharp}\mu_{1}
, which is the (unique) optimal plan between
\delta_{\bar{x}}
and
\mu_{1}
. We have

1=\mu_{1}(X)=\mu_{1}(X\setminus C_{\bar{x}})=\pi(\{\bar{x}\}\times X\setminus C_{\bar{x}})=\nu(({\rm e}_{0}\times{\rm e}_{1})^{-1}(\{\bar{x}\}\times X\setminus C_{\bar{x}}))=\nu(\Gamma),

(7.15)

where we used
\mu_{1}\ll\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{m}(C_{\bar{x}})=0
.

We now show that any
\gamma\in\Gamma
satisfies the meek equality (
4.53
). Fix an arbitrary
\gamma\in\Gamma
. We will use the following general inequality, which follows from squaring the triangle inequality (recall that
c=\mathsf{d}^{2}/2
):

c(p,q)\leq(1+\varepsilon^{-1})c(p,r)+(1+\varepsilon)c(r,q),\qquad\forall\,p,q,r\in X,\;\forall\,\varepsilon>0.

(7.16)

Now take
p=z
,
q=y
,
r=\gamma_{t}
,
t\in(0,1)
, and
\varepsilon=t/(1-t)
, so that (
7.16
) reads

c(z,y)\leq\frac{1}{t}c(z,\gamma_{t})+\frac{1}{1-t}c(\gamma_{t},y),\qquad\forall\,z\in X,

(7.17)

with equality at
z=\bar{x}
. It follows that for all
t\in(0,1)
the function

f:z\mapsto c(z,\gamma_{t})-tc(z,y),\qquad z\in X,

(7.18)

has a minimum at
z=\bar{x}
. By assumption
(b)
, for all
t\in(0,1)
it holds

\nabla_{\bar{x}}^{R}c(\cdot,\gamma_{t})=t\nabla_{\bar{x}}^{R}c(\cdot,y),

(7.19)

where
\nabla^{R}_{\bar{x}}
denotes the Riemannian gradient at
\bar{x}
. Furthermore, we observe that since
c(\cdot,\gamma_{t})
is continuously differentiable in a neighborhood of
\bar{x}
, the asymptotic
\mathsf{d}_{R}
-Lipschitz constant is the norm of the Riemannian gradient, that is

\mathsf{D}(\bar{x},\gamma_{t})=\|\nabla^{R}_{\bar{x}}c(\cdot,\gamma_{t})\|_{R},\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1).

(7.20)

Using (
7.19
) we obtain that
\mathsf{D}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})=t\mathsf{D}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
for all
t\in(0,1]
.
∎

We use Proposition
7.10
to prove that natural gauge functions satisfy the meek property. We first recall the definition of sub-Riemannian cut locus.

Definition 7.11

(Smooth points and cut locus)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a sub-Riemannian metric space.
We say that
y\in M
is a
smooth point
, with respect to
x\in M
, if there exists a unique geodesic joining
x
with
y
, which is not abnormal, and with non-conjugate endpoints. The
cut locus

\mathrm{Cut}(x)
is the complement of the set of smooth points with respect to
x
. The
global cut locus
of
M
is

\mathrm{Cut}(M):=\{(x,y)\in M\times M\mid y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)\}.

(7.21)

We have the following fundamental result
[
9
,
77
]
.

Theorem 7.12

(Agrachev, Rifford, Trélat)
.

The set of smooth points is open and dense in
M
, and the squared sub-Riemannian distance is smooth on
M\times M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
.

It is an open problem to determine whether
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
has zero measure. Even in this case, the complement of
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
may not be geodesically star-shaped: more precisely, if
y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
and
\gamma
is the geodesic joining
x
with
y
, it may happen that
\gamma_{t}\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
for
t\in(0,1)
. This is related with the presence of abnormal non-trivial segments on strictly normal geodesics, and the branching phenomenon in sub-Riemannian geometry
[
68
]
. Therefore we cannot apply Proposition
7.10
simply with
C_{x}=\mathrm{Cut}(x)
.

To proceed, we recall the classical minimizing Sard property, and we introduce its strengthening that we call “
*
-minimizing Sard property”.

Definition 7.13

(Sard properties)
.

A sub-Riemannian metric space
(M,\mathsf{d})
satisfies

•

the
minimizing Sard property
if for any
x\in M
the set of final points of abnormal geodesics has zero measure in
M
;

•

the
*
-minimizing Sard property
if for any
x\in M
the set of final points of geodesics from
x
containing non-trivial abnormal segments has zero measure in
M
.

It is well-known that, if the minimizing Sard property holds, then
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
has zero measure for all
x\in M
. We now show that, if the
*
-minimizing Sard property holds, then there exists a set, larger than
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, but still with zero measure, whose complement is star-shaped. We do not know if such a set is closed, but it is Borel, which is what we need in Proposition
7.10
.

Lemma 7.14

(Star-shaped set of smooth points)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a sub-Riemannian metric space. Then for any
x\in M
there exists a set
\mathcal{U}_{x}\subseteq M
such that:

(i)

\mathcal{U}_{x}
is the countable intersection of open sets, and in particular it is Borel;

(ii)

\mathsf{d}^{2}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
(x,y)
for any
y\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
, i.e.
\mathcal{U}_{x}\subseteq M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(x)
;

(iii)

\mathcal{U}_{x}
is geodesically star-shaped at
x
: for any
y\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma
joining
x
with
y
, and
\gamma_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
for all
t\in(0,1]
(
t=0
is excluded);

(iv)

if the
*
-minimizing Sard property holds,
\mathcal{U}_{x}
has full measure and it is dense.

Remark 7.15
.

If the sub-Riemannian structure is real-analytic, geodesics are either abnormal, or do not contain non-trivial abnormal segments, and then the
*
-minimizing Sard property coincides with the classical minimizing Sard property. For real-analytic sub-Riemannian structures satisfying the minimizing Sard property, one can take
\mathcal{U}_{x}:=M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, and thus
\mathcal{U}_{x}
is open, see
[
18
, Prop. 6]
.

Proof.

Fix
x\in M
. Let
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
be the sub-Riemannian cut locus, as in Definition
7.11
, and recall Theorem
7.12
. We also recall that if
\gamma
is a normal geodesic that is also abnormal, then any pair of distinct points along
\gamma
are conjugate.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let us define the following monotone family of sets:

\mathcal{U}^{n}_{x}:=\{\gamma_{1}\mid\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(M),\;\gamma_{0}=x,\;\gamma_{1}\in M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(x),\;\text{the distance between}\\
\text{two distinct conjugate points along $\gamma$ is strictly smaller than $1/n$}\}.

(7.22)

By construction, for any
y\in\mathcal{U}^{n}_{x}
there is a unique geodesic
\gamma
between
x
and
y
,
\gamma
is not abnormal, but
\gamma
can contain abnormal segments of length strictly smaller than
1/n
.

We claim that the set
\mathcal{U}^{n}_{x}
is open. Let
y\in\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n}
, and let
\gamma
be the unique non-abnormal geodesic between
x
and
y
, that is
\gamma_{t}=\exp_{x}(t\lambda)
for a unique
\lambda\in T_{x}^{*}M
. For any
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[0,1]
, with
|\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}|\geq 1/n
the map

d_{e^{\tau_{1}\vec{H}}(\lambda)}\exp_{\gamma_{\tau_{1}}}((\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})\;\cdot\;):T_{\gamma_{\tau_{1}}}^{*}M\to T_{\gamma_{\tau_{2}}}M,

(7.23)

must be invertible, otherwise
\gamma(\tau_{1})
and
\gamma(\tau_{2})
would be conjugate. Notice also that since
y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, we have a neighborhood
\mathcal{W}_{y}
of
y
and a neighborhood
\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}
of
\lambda
such that
\exp_{x}:\mathcal{V}_{\lambda}\to\mathcal{W}_{y}
is a diffeomorphism. It follows by compactness that there exists an open neighborhood
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{y}
of
y
such that for all
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{y}
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma^{\prime}
joining
x
with
y^{\prime}
, that is not abnormal, and all pairs of points along
\gamma^{\prime}
with distance
\geq 1/n
are not conjugate (and in particular,
\gamma^{\prime}
cannot contain abnormal segments of length
\geq 1/n
). In other words,
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}_{y}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{n}_{x}
, proving that
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{x}
is open. Define

\mathcal{U}_{x}:=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n},

(7.24)

which satisfies
(i)
. By construction
\mathcal{U}_{x}\subseteq M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, proving
(ii)
. Furthermore, for all
y\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma
joining
x
with
y
, which does not contain non-trivial segments with conjugate endpoints (and in particular, it does not contain non-trivial abnormal segments). Thus
\gamma_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{x}
for all
t\in(0,1]
, proving
(iii)
.

Claim.
We claim that
M\setminus\mathcal{U}_{x}
is equal to the union of
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
with the set of endpoints of geodesics starting from
x
that contain a non-trivial abnormal segment. Notice that the latter set has zero measure if the
*
-minimizing Sard property holds. Furthermore also
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
has zero measure
2
2

2

This is proved for example in
[
18
, Prop. 6]
, and the minimizing Sard property is sufficient. A self-contained argument will be provided at the end of this proof.
. Thus, the claim implies that
\mathcal{U}_{x}
has full measure. Furthermore, each open set
\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n}
must have full measure, and in particular it must be dense in
M
. By the Baire theorem
\mathcal{U}_{x}
is dense, concluding the proof of
(iv)
.

It only remains to prove the claim:

\supseteq:
If
y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
clearly it does not belong to any of the
\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n}
. Let then
y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, such that there exists a geodesic
\gamma
with
\gamma_{0}=x
that contains a non-trivial abnormal segment. Observe that
\gamma
is not abnormal and is the unique geodesic joining
x
with
y
. Let
\gamma|_{[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]}
with
0<|\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}|<1
be the non-trivial abnormal segment. Any pair of distinct points on
\gamma|_{[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]}
are conjugate, and thus
y\notin\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n}
for
n
sufficiently large.

\subseteq:
Let
y\notin\mathcal{U}_{x}^{n}
for some
n
. It follows that either
y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, or if not there exist two distinct conjugate points along the unique geodesic joining
x
with
y
. This can happen only if
\gamma
contains non-trivial abnormal segments (otherwise it would cease to be a geodesic after meeting the first conjugate point
[
24
, Thm. 72]
).

To make our proof self-contained, we show that
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
has zero measure if the minimizing Sard conjecture holds, that is when the set
\mathrm{Abn}(x)
of endpoints of abnormal geodesics from
x
has zero measure. By completeness, the image of
\exp_{x}:T_{x}^{*}M\to M
covers
M\setminus\mathrm{Abn}(x)
. The set of conjugate points to
x
is contained in the set
S(x)
of singular values of
\exp_{x}
, which has zero measure by the Sard theorem. Furthermore, one can show that the subset
R_{2}(x)
of points
z
where
z
is not conjugate to
x
and there exist at least two normal geodesics joining
x
with
z
has zero measure, cf.
[
77
, Lemma 4.8]
. Hence, by construction, for any
y\in M
which is not in
R_{2}(x)\cup\mathrm{Abn}(x)\cup S(x)
there exists a unique strictly normal geodesic
\gamma
joining
x
with
y
, with non-conjugate endpoints. In other words
\mathrm{Cut}(x)\subseteq R_{2}(x)\cup\mathrm{Abn}(x)\cup S(x)
, which is a union of zero measure sets.
∎

We can now prove that, for a large class of sub-Riemannian structures, the
\mathsf{D}
function (cf. Definition
7.4
) satisfies the meek assumption (cf. Definition
4.23
).

Theorem 7.16

(Natural gauge functions are meek)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian m.m.s., equipped with a Riemannian reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Assume that
(M,\mathsf{d})
satisfies the
*
-minimizing Sard property. Then any natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}
is meek.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the property for
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
. In this case, apply Proposition
7.10
with
C_{x}:=M\setminus\mathcal{U}_{x}
, with the set
\mathcal{U}_{x}
of Lemma
7.14
.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

The
*
-minimizing Sard property is true for:

•

ideal structures, i.e. those not admitting non-trivial abnormal geodesics, which is generically true for constant rank
\geq 3
distributions, cf.
[
41
, Thm. 2.8]
;

•

real-analytic structures satisfying the minimizing Sard conjecture. This includes Carnot groups of step
2
, or more general Carnot groups, cf.
[
60
, Thm. 1.2]
.

Thus, for all those structures, then all natural gauge functions are meek.

7.3.2
Regularity condition

We first discuss the regularity condition required in the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
stability Theorem
5.6
.

Theorem 7.18

(Natural gauge functions are regular I)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian m.m.s., equipped with a Riemannian reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Then any natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}
is continuous and finite on
M\times M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
. In particular, if
(M,\mathsf{d})
satisfies the minimizing Sard property, any such
\mathsf{G}
satisfies the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the statement for the case
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
. We observe that
\mathsf{d}
is smooth in charts on
M\times M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
by Theorem
7.12
. At all those points,
\mathsf{D}
coincides with the norm of the gradient of
c=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}^{2}
computed with respect to the Riemannian reference, cf. Proposition
7.28
. Then
\mathsf{D}
is finite and continuous on
M\times M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
. Furthermore, if the minimizing Sard property holds, then
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
is a closed set with zero measure for all
x\in M
. Thus
\mathsf{D}
is finite and continuous at all points
(x,y)
with
y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, which is the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
.
∎

We turn now to the regularity condition for plans occurring in
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
stability in Theorem
5.14
. We recall the following definition, introduced by Rifford in
[
75
,
76
]
.

Definition 7.19

(Ideal structures)
.

A sub-Riemannian metric space
(M,\mathsf{d})
is ideal if it has no non-trivial abnormal geodesics.

Generic sub-Riemannian structures are ideal, when the rank of the distribution is constant and at least 3, see
[
41
, Thm. 2.8]
. For ideal structures, a sub-Riemannian version of the McCann-Brenier theorem holds
[
46
,
76
]
. Furthermore, the non-static part of the transport map between suitable measures avoids almost surely the cut locus
[
24
]
, so that
\mathsf{d}
is smooth
\pi
-a.e. for any optimal plan whose marginals have disjoint support.

Theorem 7.20

(Natural gauge functions are regular II)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian m.m.s., equipped with a Riemannian reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Assume that
(M,\mathsf{d})
is ideal. Then for all
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(M,\mathsf{d})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, with
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
there is a unique
\pi\in{\rm Opt}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, and any natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}
is continuous and finite
\pi
-a.e. In particular, the regularity condition of Definition
5.12
is verified.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the result for the case
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
. If
(M,\mathsf{d})
is ideal, there exists a unique
\pi\in{\rm Opt}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, and it is induced by a transport map
T:M\to M
. Furthermore, for
\mu_{0}
-a.e.
x\in M
, either
T(x)=x
, or
T(x)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, cf.
[
24
, Cor. 38]
. Since
\supp\mu_{0}
and
\supp\mu_{1}
