are disjoint, the first case never occurs. It follows that
\mathsf{D}
is continuous at
(x,y)
for
\pi
-a.e.
(x,y)\in M\times M
.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

One can appreciate in the above proof the importance of the restriction to measures
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}
with disjoint support. In general (and more precisely, whenever the sub-Riemannian structure is not Riemannian), the diagonal
\Delta\subset M\times M
is always part of the cut locus, and
\mathsf{D}
is not continuous there. Thus, the regularity condition of Definition
5.12
would not be verified without that restriction.

Remark 7.22
.

The ideal assumption cannot be removed. In fact, in
[
20
, Fig. 2]
, the authors built an explicit example, on corank 1 Carnot groups, and measures
\mu_{i}\in\mathcal{P}^{*}_{bs}(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
with disjoint support where any point
x\in\supp\mu_{0}
is sent to
T(x)\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
, and more precisely
T(x)
is the endpoint of an abnormal geodesic from
x
. Here, it turns out that
\mathsf{D}
is not continuous at every
(x,y)\in\supp\pi
.

7.3.3
G-boundedness condition

We discuss here the boundedness properties of natural gauge functions. We recall the following definition, see
[
2
, Sec. 3.1]
for further details.

Definition 7.23

(Step)
.

A sub-Riemannian metric space has step
k\in\mathbb{N}
at
x\in M
if Lie brackets of length
k
are sufficient to satisfy the bracket-generating condition at
x
, and
k=k(x)
is the smallest number with this property. The step of a sub-Riemannian metric space is the supremum of the step at all its points.

The next result is a reformulation of well-known facts about the regularity of the sub-Riemannian distance.

Theorem 7.24

(Boundedness properties of natural gauge functions)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian m.m.s. Let
\mathsf{G}
be a natural gauge function. Then the following hold:

(i)

The function
\mathsf{G}
is locally bounded on an open and dense set of
M\times M
. In particular for any non-empty open set
\mathcal{O}
it holds

\mathfrak{m}(\{y\in M\;:\;|\mathsf{G}(x,y)|<+\infty\}\cap\mathcal{O})>0,\qquad\forall\,x\in M.

(7.25)

(ii)

If
(M,\mathsf{d})
satisfies the minimizing Sard property, then for any
x\in M
the function
\mathsf{G}(x,\cdot)
is locally bounded almost everywhere. In particular it holds

\mathfrak{m}(\{y\in M\;:\;|\mathsf{G}(x,y)|=+\infty\})=0,\qquad\forall\,x\in M.

(7.26)

(iii)

If
(M,\mathsf{d})
has step
\leq 2
, then
\mathsf{G}
is locally bounded, that is for every bounded subset
\mathcal{O}
there exists
C>0
such that

|\mathsf{G}(x,y)|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,x,y\in\mathcal{O}.

(7.27)

(iv)

If
\mathsf{d}
is locally Lipschitz in charts outside of the diagonal
\Delta\subset M\times M
, then
\mathsf{G}
is locally bounded away from the diagonal, that is for all
\varepsilon>0
and bounded subset
\mathcal{O}
there exists
C>0
such that

|\mathsf{G}(x,y)|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,x,y\in\mathcal{O},\;\mathsf{d}(x,y)\geq\varepsilon.

(7.28)

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the statements for
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
.

Proof of
(i)
.
Fix
x\in M
and
y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)
. Then
\mathsf{d}
is smooth in a neighborhood of
(x,y)
, and
\mathsf{D}(x,y)=\|\nabla^{R}_{x}\mathsf{d}(\cdot,y)\|_{R}<+\infty
. The set
M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(x)
is open and dense by Theorem
7.12
, and since
\mathfrak{m}
is smooth the statement follows.

Proof of
(ii)
.
By the previous argument, if
\mathsf{D}(x,y)=+\infty
we must have
y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)
. If the Sard property holds, then
\mathrm{Cut}(x)
has zero measure.

Proof of
(iii)
.
The step of
(M,\mathsf{d})
is
\leq 2
if and only if
\mathsf{d}
is locally Lipschitz in charts
[
4
, Cor. 6.2]
. Since
\mathsf{D}
is the asymptotic Lipschitz constant of
\mathsf{d}
with respect to a Riemannian metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
, then
\mathsf{D}
must be locally bounded.

Proof of
(iv)
.
Obvious.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

Item
(i)
yields the non-triviality condition (
4.41
) for natural gauge functions. Item
(ii)
yields the finiteness condition (
4.54
) required in the generalized Bishop-Gromov Theorem
4.25
.

Remark 7.26
.

Item
(iv)
holds for example when
(M,\mathsf{d})
is ideal, and more generally if there are no non-trivial Goh paths, see
[
4
, Thm. 5.5]
for details. This is true, for example, if the underlying distribution is medium-fat
[
46
, Sec. 4.5]
,
[
12
]
.

Remark 7.27
.

The step
2
assumption in
(iii)
cannot be removed. Notice that, by the Ball-Box theorem
[
53
,
31
]
,
\mathsf{d}(o,\cdot)
cannot be Lipschitz in charts in a neighborhood of any point
o
where the step
s(o)\geq 3
, so that
\mathsf{D}(o,o)=+\infty
. One can show that, in general,
\mathsf{D}
may not be essentially bounded in any neighborhood of the diagonal around such points.

To illustrate this fact, let us discuss the case of Carnot groups
(\mathbb{G},\mathsf{d})
. These are left-invariant structures on a nilpotent, connected and simply connected Lie group
\mathbb{G}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We denote points as
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
in exponential coordinates, with
o=(0,\dots,0)
the identity of the group. Carnot groups are equipped with metric dilations
\delta_{\lambda}
,
\lambda>0
, that in these coordinates read
\delta_{\lambda}(x)=(\lambda^{w_{1}}x_{1},\dots,\lambda^{w_{n}}x_{n})
, where
w_{i}
denotes the weight of the
i
-th coordinate. Furthermore, we may choose a Riemannian reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
, left-invariant by the group action of
\mathbb{G}
. Notice that, for any bounded neighborhood
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there exists a constant
C=C_{\mathcal{O}}>0
such that it holds

\frac{1}{C}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}|\leq\mathsf{d}_{R}(x,x^{\prime})\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}|,\qquad\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}.

(7.29)

Furthermore, using dilations, one checks that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\frac{1}{C^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i}|^{1/w_{i}}\leq\mathsf{d}(x,x^{\prime})\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}-x^{\prime}_{i}|^{1/w_{i}},\qquad\forall\,x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(7.30)

Let now
\mathcal{O}
be a bounded neighborhood of the origin
o\in\mathbb{G}
. Up to restricting it, we may assume that
p\in\mathcal{O}\Leftrightarrow p^{-1}\in\mathcal{O}
(recall that, in exponential coordinates, group inversion corresponds to the map
x\mapsto-x
). We have, for all
x\in\mathcal{O}

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(o,x^{-1})=\mathsf{D}(x,o)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{z,w\to x}\frac{\mathsf{d}(z,o)^{2}-\mathsf{d}(w,o)^{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(z,w)}

(7.31)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\to 1}\frac{\mathsf{d}(\delta_{\lambda}(x),o)^{2}-\mathsf{d}(\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}}(x),o)^{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(\delta_{\lambda}(x),\delta_{\lambda^{\prime}}(x))}

(7.32)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\limsup_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\to 1}\frac{|\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{{}^{\prime}2}|\mathsf{d}(x,o)^{2}}{|\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}|C^{2}\mathsf{d}_{R}(x,o)}

(7.33)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{C^{2}}\frac{\mathsf{d}(x,o)^{2}}{\mathsf{d}_{R}(x,o)}\geq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{2/w_{i}}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|},

(7.34)

where in the first equality we used the left-invariance of
\mathsf{D}
(which follows from the left-invariance of
\mathsf{d}
and
\mathsf{d}_{R}
), and we note that the constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
depends only on
\mathcal{O}
. It follows that, if there is one
w_{i}\geq 3
(that is, when the step is
\geq 3
), then it holds

\mathfrak{m}\shortminus\esssup_{x\in\mathcal{O},x\neq o}\mathsf{G}(o,x)=+\infty,

(7.35)

where
\mathfrak{m}
is any smooth measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(this includes the Haar measures of
\mathbb{G}
). Notice that in the last estimate,
\mathcal{O}
can be any bounded neighborhood of
o
. It follows by left-invariance that for any neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathbb{G}
we have

\diam_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathcal{O})=+\infty.

(7.36)

Thus, for sub-Riemannian structures of step
3
, natural gauge functions do not satisfy, in general, the
\mathsf{G}
-boundedness assumptions of the stability Theorems
5.6
and
5.14
.

The diagram in Figure
3
illustrates most of the properties for natural gauge functions we proved in this section, on sub-Riemannian structures.

)

)

of

(Def.

)

-minimizing Sard

(Def.

)

)

of

(Def.

Figure 3:
Sub-Riemannian properties (left), and of natural gauge functions
\mathsf{G}
(right). When
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{d}
all properties are trivially verified with no assumptions.

7.4
Expression of
\mathsf{D}
out of the cut locus

In this final section we show how
\mathsf{D}
is related with more familiar objects in sub-Riemannian geometry. It will also serve the purpose to better motivate the expression
\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}}
that appears in the list of natural gauge functions in Section
7.2
.

Proposition 7.28
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a sub-Riemannian metric space, equipped with a reference Riemannian metric
\mathsf{d}_{R}
. Let
\mathsf{D}:M\times M\to[0,+\infty]
be as in Definition
7.4
. For all
x\in M
, let
T_{x}M=\mathscr{D}_{x}\oplus\mathscr{V}_{x}
, where
\mathscr{D}_{x}
is the horizontal subspace at
x
and
\mathscr{D}_{x}\perp\mathscr{V}_{x}
, and denote with
\pi_{x}^{\mathscr{V}},\pi_{x}^{\mathscr{D}}
the corresponding projections. Let also
\nabla^{R}
and
\|\cdot\|_{R}
denote the gradient and the norm of the Riemannian metric. Then it holds:

\mathsf{D}(x,y)=\|\nabla_{x}^{R}c(\cdot,y)\|_{R},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M).

(7.37)

If, in addition, the reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is an extension of
\mathsf{d}
(cf. Definition
7.1
), then it holds

\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}=\mathsf{d}(x,y)^{2}+\|\pi^{\mathscr{V}}_{x}\nabla_{x}^{R}c(\cdot,y)\|_{R}^{2},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M).

(7.38)

Equivalently, identifying
T_{x}M\simeq T_{x}^{*}M
by means of the Riemannian structure, it holds

\|\pi_{x}^{\mathscr{V}}\lambda^{x,y}\|^{2}=\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}-\mathsf{d}(x,y)^{2},\qquad(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M),

(7.39)

where
\lambda^{x,y}\in T_{x}^{*}M
is the initial covector of the unique geodesic joining
x
with
y
.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.12
, on
M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(y)
the function
c(\cdot,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}(\cdot,y)^{2}
is smooth. Thus, out of
\mathrm{Cut}(y)
, the asymptotic Lipschitz constant with respect to
\mathsf{d}_{R}
coincides with the Riemannian norm of the Riemannian gradient, thus proving (
7.37
).

We then prove that, if
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is a Riemannian extension induced by the Riemannian metric
g_{R}
, and
g
denotes the sub-Riemannian metric, for all
x\in X
and
v_{x}\in\mathscr{D}_{x}
it holds
\|v_{x}\|_{g_{R}}=\|v_{x}\|_{g}
. In fact, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{L}
be a generating family for the sub-Riemannian structure. There exist
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
v_{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}X_{i}(x)
, then let
V:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}X_{i}
. Let also
\gamma_{t}=e^{tV}(x)
be the flow of
V
, for
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
. This is by construction a horizontal curve with
\dot{\gamma}_{t}=V(\gamma_{t})
. Furthermore, it holds (cf.
[
2
, Ex. 3.51, 3.52]
):

L_{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma|_{[0,t]})=\int_{0}^{t}\|V(\gamma_{s})\|_{g}\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\|v_{x}\|_{g}=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}L_{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma|_{[0,t]}).

(7.40)

Since
\mathsf{d}_{R}
is an extension,
\gamma_{t}
is rectifiable also for
\mathsf{d}_{R}
, and it holds
L_{\mathsf{d}}(\gamma|_{[0,t]})=L_{\mathsf{d}_{R}}(\gamma|_{[0,t]})
. We deduce that
\|v_{x}\|_{g_{R}}=\|v_{x}\|_{g}
, which proves (
7.38
).

As a consequence, letting
k(x)=\dim\mathscr{D}_{x}
, we can find a
g_{R}
-orthonormal basis
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in T_{x}M
such that
v_{1},\dots,v_{k(x)}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathscr{D}_{x}
with respect to the sub-Riemannian metric at the point
x
. Therefore, if
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M)
, it holds

\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}=\|\nabla^{R}_{x}c(\cdot,y)\|_{g_{R}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k(x)}\langle\mathrm{d}_{x}c(\cdot,y),v_{i}\rangle^{2}+\sum_{i=k(x)+1}^{n}\langle\mathrm{d}_{x}c(\cdot,y),v_{i}\rangle^{2},

(7.41)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:T_{x}^{*}M\times T_{x}M\to\mathbb{R}
denotes the dual action of covectors on vectors. Notice that
\mathrm{d}_{x}c(\cdot,y)=\lambda^{x,y}
is the initial covector of the unique geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
joining
x
with
y
: this follows by the characterization of sub-Riemannian geodesics via the Lagrange multipliers rule, cf. for example
[
3
, Prop. 4.3, Lemma 2.20]
. The first term in the r.h.s. of (
7.41
) is
2H(\lambda^{x,y})=\mathsf{d}(x,y)^{2}
, concluding the proof of (
7.39
).
∎

8
Sub-Riemannian comparison theory

We recall from
[
25
]
a general comparison theory for lower Ricci curvature bounds in the sub-Riemannian setting. We remind first what comparison models are in this setting.

8.1
LQ optimal control problems

Comparison models are a special class of dynamical systems, called
linear quadratic optimal control problems
(LQ in the following), and are a classical topic in optimal control theory. They arise as variational problems in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with a quadratic cost and linear dynamics. We recall their general features, and we refer to
[
7
, Ch. 16]
,
[
43
, Ch. 1]
and
[
56
, Ch. 7]
for further details.

Let
A,B
be
\ell\times\ell
real matrices, with
B
symmetric and
B\geq 0
. Letting
k\leq\ell
be the rank of
B
, there exist
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, unique up to orthogonal transformations, such that
B=\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}b_{i}^{*}
. Let also
Q
be a symmetric
\ell\times\ell
real matrix, and
T>0
. We are interested in
admissible trajectories
, namely curves
q:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
for which there exists a
control

u\in L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k})
such that

\dot{q}=Aq+\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}b_{i}.

(8.1)

Thus, we look for admissible trajectories with fixed endpoints
q(0)=x
,
q(T)=y
, that minimize the quadratic functional
C_{T}:L^{2}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}

C_{T}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\left(u^{*}u-q^{*}Qq\right)dt.

(8.2)

Admissible trajectories minimizing (
8.2
) are called
minimizers
. The vector
Aq
represents the
drift
, while
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}
are the
controllable directions
. The matrix
Q
is the
potential
of the LQ problem.

We only deal with
controllable
systems, i.e. for which there exists
s>0
such that

\rank(B,AB,\ldots,A^{s-1}B)=\ell.

(8.3)

Condition (
8.3
) is known as
Kalman condition
in control theory. It is equivalent to the fact that, for any choice of
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
T>0
, there is a non-empty set of admissible trajectories
q:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
joining
x
with
y
.

It is well-known that the admissible trajectories minimizing (
8.2
) are projections
(p,q)\mapsto q
of the solutions of the Hamilton equations

\dot{p}=-\partial_{q}H,\qquad\dot{q}=\partial_{p}H,\qquad(p,q)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{\ell}=\mathbb{R}^{2\ell},

(8.4)

where the Hamiltonian function
H:\mathbb{R}^{2\ell}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

H(p,q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(p^{*}Bp+2p^{*}Aq+q^{*}Qq\right).

(8.5)

We remark that the Hamiltonian flow is defined for all times, since
H
is quadratic.

Definition 8.1

(Conjugate time)
.

We say that
t_{*}>0
is a
conjugate time
if there exists a non-trivial solution of the Hamilton equations (
8.4
) such that
q(0)=q(t_{*})=0
.

The first conjugate time
t_{c}=\inf\{t_{*}>0\mid t_{*}\text{ is a conjugate time}\}\in(0,+\infty]
determines existence and the uniqueness of solutions of minimizers, as specified by the following proposition (see
[
7
, Sec. 16.4]
).

Proposition 8.2

(Conjugate times and existence of minimizers)
.

Let
t_{c}
be the first conjugate time of the LQ problem (
8.1
)-(
8.2
). Then, for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,

•

if
T<t_{c}
there exists a unique minimizer between
x
with
y
;

•

if
T>t_{c}
there exist no minimizers between
x
with
y
;

•

if
T=t_{c}
existence of minimizers depends on
x
,
y
.

The minimization of the functional (
8.2
) with fixed endpoints and
T>0
does not define a metric on
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, in general. Nevertheless, one can still define a distortion coefficient.

Definition 8.3

(LQ distortion coefficient)
.

Consider the LQ problem (
8.1
)-(
8.2
), with
T=1
. For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
and
t\in[0,1]
, define

Z_{t}(x,y)=\{q(t)\mid q:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\text{ is a minimizer s.t. }q(0)=x,\,q(1)=y\}.

(8.6)

The
distortion coefficient
of the LQ problem is

\beta_{t}^{A,B,Q}(x,y)=\limsup_{r\to 0}\frac{|Z_{t}(x,B_{r}(y))|}{|B_{r}(y)|},\qquad t\in[0,1],

(8.7)

where
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
B_{r}(y)
denotes the Euclidean ball with center
y
and radius
r>0
, and
|\cdot|
denotes the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.

The next proposition, proven in
[
25
, Prop. 27]
, links the distortion coefficient with the Hamilton equations of the LQ problem.

Proposition 8.4
.

Consider the LQ problem (
8.1
)-(
8.2
), with
T=1
, and assume that
t_{c}>1
. Its distortion coefficient does not depend on the choice of
x,y
, and satisfies

\beta_{t}^{A,B,Q}=\frac{\det N(t)}{\det N(1)}>0,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(8.8)

where
M(t),N(t):[0,+\infty)\to\mathrm{Mat}(\ell\times\ell)
are the solutions of the Hamiltonian system

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}M\\
N\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-A^{*}&-Q\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}M\\
N\end{pmatrix},\qquad\begin{pmatrix}M(0)\\
N(0)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{1}\\
\mathbb{0}\end{pmatrix}.

(8.9)

Equivalently, we have

\beta_{t}^{A,B,Q}=\exp\left(-\int_{t}^{1}\tr(BV(s)+A)\mathrm{d}s\right)>0,\qquad\forall\,t\in(0,1],

(8.10)

where
V:(0,t_{c})\to\mathrm{Sym}(\ell\times\ell)
is the maximal solution of the matrix Riccati equation

\dot{V}+A^{*}V+VA+VBV+Q=\mathbb{0},\qquad\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}V^{-1}(t)=\mathbb{0}.

(8.11)

Notice that, by the Kalman condition (
8.3
), the Cauchy problem with limit initial datum (
8.11
) is well-posed, its solution is well-defined on the maximal interval
(0,t_{c})
, where
t_{c}
is the first conjugate time of the corresponding LQ problem. In particular, the standing assumption
t_{c}>1
makes (
8.10
) well-defined (see
[
22
, Appendix A]
).

8.2
Constant curvature models

A LQ problem depends on data
A,B,Q
. In this section we explain how we choose these, yielding a class of constant curvature models. The matrices
A
and
B
are determined by a Young diagram. The general case can be reduced to diagrams with one row, which can be seen as basic building blocks. Consider thus the following Young diagram:

Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\cdots$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule width=0.0pt,height=15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\kern 0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\kern 0.39993pt}\nullfont &\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$\ell$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 1035.4pt}}$}\,,

(8.12)

of length
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. The case
\ell=1
must be thought of as the “Riemannian case”. We associate the matrices
A,B
as follows:

A=\Gamma_{1}^{*}(Y),\qquad B=\Gamma_{2}(Y),

(8.13)

(the transpose is a convention to agree with the one of
[
22
]
), where

\Gamma_{1}(Y):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{1}_{\ell-1}\\
0&\mathbb{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\Gamma_{2}(Y):=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{0}\\
\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{0}_{\ell-1}\end{pmatrix}.

(8.14)

The matrices
A=\Gamma_{1}^{*}(Y)
and
B=\Gamma_{2}(Y)
satisfy the Kalman condition (
8.3
). The potential
Q
is a diagonal matrix, whose entries represent the Ricci curvature bounds:

Q=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell}),\qquad\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}.

(8.15)

Notation.

Following the above prescriptions, a basic LQ model is uniquely specified by the choice of
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. We denote the corresponding distortion coefficient by

\beta_{t}^{\kappa}:=\beta_{t}^{A,B,Q},\qquad\text{$A$, $B$, $Q$ as in \eqref{eq:AandB}-\eqref{eq:QQ}}.

(8.16)

These LQ problems have constant curvature in the sense discussed in Appendix
B
. More precisely, one can show that any Jacobi curve of the corresponding Hamiltonian flow has Young diagram
Y
of one row of length
\ell
, constant canonical curvature
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}=Q
, and constant canonical Ricci curvatures
\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda_{t}}^{i}=\kappa_{i}
, where
i=1,\dots,\ell
. This can be proved by observing that the restrictions to any extremal of
E_{i}=\partial_{p_{i}}
and
F_{i}=\partial_{q_{i}}
, for
i=1,\dots,\ell
is a canonical frame along that extremal (see Section
B.2.2
).

Proposition 8.5

(Basic models)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. Consider the LQ problem (
8.1
)-(
8.2
) on
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, with
A
,
B
as in (
8.13
) and
Q
as in (
8.15
). Then there exist a computable real-analytic function
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, and
t_{\kappa}\in(0,+\infty]
, such that:

1.

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}
is strictly positive on
(0,t_{\kappa})
, and vanishes at the endpoints;

2.

the first conjugate time of the LQ problem is
t_{\kappa}
;

3.

there exists
c>0
depending only on
\ell
(and not on
\kappa
) and
N=\ell^{2}
such that

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)\sim ct^{N},\qquad t\to 0;

4.

assuming that
t_{\kappa}>1
, then the distortion coefficient of the LQ problem is

\beta_{t}^{\kappa}=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)}{\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(1)},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1];

(8.17)

5.

for
\lambda\geq 0
, let
\lambda\odot\kappa:=(\kappa_{1}\lambda^{2},\dots,\kappa_{\ell}\lambda^{2\ell})
. Then it holds:

t_{\lambda\odot\kappa}=\frac{t_{\kappa}}{\lambda},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{s}_{\lambda\odot\kappa}(t)=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t\lambda)}{\lambda^{\ell^{2}}},\qquad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R},

(8.18)

with the convention that, if
\lambda=0
, then
t_{0}=+\infty
and
\mathsf{s}_{0}(t)=t^{\ell^{2}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
;

6.

let
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and assume that
\bar{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
where each
\bar{\kappa}_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is a homogeneous function of degree
2i
, for
i=1,\dots,\ell
. Let
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta):=\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(|\theta|),\qquad\forall\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+},

(8.19)

with the convention
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(0)=0
.

The function
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}
is radially real-analytic, satisfies
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta)=c|\theta|^{N}+o(|\theta|^{N})
for some
c>0
and
N=\ell^{2}
. Its positivity domain, that is the largest open star-shaped subset
{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
such that
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}>0
on
{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}\setminus\{0\}
, is characterized as

{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\mid 1<t_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}\}=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\mid|\theta|<t_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}\},

(8.20)

and for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}
it holds

\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}=\begin{cases}t^{N}&|\theta|=0,\\
\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta)}&|\theta|\neq 0\text{ and }\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}},\end{cases}

(8.21)

with
N=\ell^{2}
.

Remark 8.6

(Link with CD theory)
.

Proposition
8.5
, and in particular item
6
, is the bridge between the curvature-dimension theory of Section
3
and the comparison theory of Section
8
. It establishes that model distortion coefficients of LQ problems coincide with model distortion coefficients in the sense of (
3.14
) (for the scalar-valued case) and (
6.4
) (for the vector-valued case).

Remark 8.7

(Scalar case)
.

Item
6
is stated in the general case
m\in\mathbb{N}
, in order to describe model coefficients for vector-valued gauge functions. The scalar case corresponds to
m=1
. In this case, we identify
\bar{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
with
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
by the identity
\bar{\kappa}_{i}(\theta)=\kappa_{i}\theta^{2i}
for all
i=1,\dots,\ell
. Our notation is consistent since it holds

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(t\theta)=\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t\theta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}=[0,t_{\kappa}).

(8.22)

In particular, there is no need to introduce the
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}
functions, and the positivity domain is just a segment. For general
m\in\mathbb{N}
, however

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}

(8.23)

act on different domains. The link between them is provided by (
8.19
).

Remark 8.8

(Convexity)
.

If
\ell=m=1
then
(0,t_{\kappa})\ni\theta\mapsto\log\beta_{t}^{\kappa}(\sqrt{\theta})
is convex and monotone for fixed
t\in[0,1]
(the case of Riemannian coefficients, see Section
8.2.1
). Convexity, in particular, is important for the deduction of the standard form of the
\mathsf{CD}(K,N)
inequality. Both properties fail, in general, if
\ell>1
. Take for example
\kappa=(2,-1)
, and compute the solution of the Hamiltonian system (
8.9
) (the values are chosen so that the system has a simple Jordan form, with eigenvalues
\pm i
).

Proof.

Using the notation of Proposition
8.4
, we set

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t):=\det N(t).

(8.24)

By definition, the first conjugate time of the LQ problem is

t_{\kappa}:=\inf\{t>0\mid\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)=0\}.

(8.25)

The Kalman’s condition implies that
\det N(t)\neq 0
for small
t>0
so that
t_{\kappa}>0
. Furthermore one can check, by studying the asymptotic behaviour of the solutions of (
8.9
), that
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)>0
for small
t
. Thus
1
,
2
and
4
follow immediately.

To prove
3
, we first observe that
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(\cdot)
is real-analytic, and thus there exist
3
3

3

There should be no confusion between
N\in\mathbb{N}
and the family of matrices
N(t)
.

N\in\mathbb{N}
and
c\neq 0
such that
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)\sim ct^{N}
as
t\to 0
. To prove that
N=\ell^{2}
, we claim that the following asymptotic formula holds:

\lim_{t\to 0}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log\det N(t)-\frac{\ell^{2}}{t}\right)=0.

(8.26)

To prove it, observe that the first term on the left hand side is

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log\det N(t)=\tr(\dot{N}(t)N(t)^{-1})=\tr(\Gamma_{2}M(t)N(t)^{-1}+\Gamma_{1}^{*}).

(8.27)

The asymptotics of
M(t)N(t)^{-1}
as
t\to 0
is obtained in
[
3
, Thm. 7.4 and Cor. 7.5]
, yielding (
8.26
). We do not repeat the details here. We remark that (
8.26
) can also be proved by deducing asymptotic formulas for the solutions
M(t),N(t)
of the Hamiltonian system in Proposition
8.4
, using the properties of
A,B
in (
8.13
). Such a proof also shows that the constant
c
depends only on
\ell
and not on
Q
(i.e. not on
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
), and
c>0
.

To prove
5
, for all
\lambda>0
let

Q=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{\ell}),\qquad Q_{\lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{1}\lambda^{2},\dots,\kappa_{\ell}\lambda^{2\ell}),

(8.28)

be the potentials corresponding to
\kappa
and
\lambda\odot\kappa
, respectively. We remark that

Q_{\lambda}=\Omega_{\lambda}Q\Omega_{\lambda},\qquad\Omega_{\lambda}:=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda,\dots,\lambda^{\ell}).

(8.29)

For the specific choice
A=\Gamma_{1}^{*}(Y)
and
B=\Gamma_{2}(Y)
of the statement, see (
8.13
), we denote by
M(t),N(t)
(resp.
M_{\lambda}(t),N_{\lambda}(t)
) the solution of the Hamiltonian system (
8.9
) for potential
Q
(resp.
Q_{\lambda}
). We observe from the explicit form (
8.14
) that

\Omega_{\lambda}A=\lambda A\Omega_{\lambda},\qquad B\Omega_{\lambda}=\Omega_{\lambda}B=\lambda B.

(8.30)

Using these properties and (
8.9
) we immediately see that

M_{\lambda}(t)=\Omega_{\lambda}M(t\lambda)\Omega_{\lambda}^{-1},\qquad N_{\lambda}(t)=\lambda\Omega_{\lambda}^{-1}N(t\lambda)\Omega_{\lambda}^{-1},\qquad\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R}.

(8.31)

It follows that, for conjugate times
t_{\lambda\odot\kappa}=t_{\kappa}/\lambda
, and furthermore

\mathsf{s}_{\lambda\odot\kappa}(t)=\det N_{\lambda}(t)=\lambda^{-\ell^{2}}\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t\lambda),\qquad\forall\,\lambda>0.

(8.32)

The above rescaling formula yields
5
in the case
\lambda>0
. The case
\lambda=0
can be seen as a limit of the previous one, or also proved directly by solving (
8.9
); in this case
Q=0
and thus
M(t)_{ij}=(-1)^{j-i}t^{j-i}/(j-i)!
for
j\geq i
, and zero otherwise, while
N(t)_{ij}=(-1)^{j+1}t^{i+j-1}/(i+j-1)!
for all
i,j=1,\dots,\ell
.

The proof of
6
is a straightforward consequence of all previous items, unraveling the notation. The fact that
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}
is radially real-analytic follows from the fact that
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}
is real-analytic for
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. Notice that if the
\bar{\kappa}_{i}
are homogeneous of degree
2i
, then for
\theta\neq 0
we have
\bar{\kappa}(\theta)=|\theta|\odot\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)
. Therefore it holds

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta)=\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(|\theta|)\sim c|\theta|^{\ell^{2}},\qquad|\theta|\to 0,

(8.33)

where we used
3
, and the fact that the constant
c>0
in
3
does not depend on
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. The characterization of the positivity domain follows from the homogeneity property
5
. Finally, by definition, and using
5
, it holds

\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}(t)}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}(1)}=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{|\theta|\odot\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(t)}{\mathsf{s}_{|\theta|\odot\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(1)}=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(t|\theta|)}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}(\theta/|\theta|)}(|\theta|)}=\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(t\theta)}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta)},

(8.34)

for all
\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}\setminus\{0\}
and all
t\in(0,1)
. Finally if
|\theta|=0
, we have
\bar{\kappa}(0)=0
and we proceed in a similar fashion reminding that
\mathsf{s}_{0}(t)=t^{\ell^{2}}
by item
5
. The cases
t=0
and
t=1
are trivially verified.
∎

The proof of Proposition
8.5
is constructive and the distortion coefficients are obtained by solving the Hamiltonian system (
8.9
) associated with corresponding LQ problem. In the next three sections we give explicit examples, recovering all typical comparison functions that have arisen in the recent literature in sub-Riemannian geometry.

8.2.1
The Riemannian case

Let
\ell=1
, so that
Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}
. According to (
8.13
), we have
A=0
,
B=1
, and we set
Q=\kappa
for some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. The Hamiltonian of the LQ problem is

H(p,q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(p^{2}+\kappa q^{2}\right),

(8.35)

which is the Hamiltonian of a one-dimensional harmonic oscillator. From Proposition
8.5
we obtain easily that

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(t)=\begin{cases}\sin(\sqrt{\kappa}t)&\kappa>0,\\
\sinh(\sqrt{-\kappa}t)&\kappa\leq 0,\end{cases}\qquad t_{\kappa}=\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa}}&\kappa>0,\\
+\infty&\kappa\leq 0.\end{cases}

(8.36)

The typical right hand side of Ricci curvature lower bounds has the form
\bar{\kappa}(\mathsf{d})=\kappa\mathsf{d}^{2}
. Thus, according to Proposition
8.5
, we obtain that for
\theta\in[0,+\infty)

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\theta)=\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}(\theta),\qquad{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}=[0,t_{\kappa}).

(8.37)

Here we stress that
m=1
. The corresponding distortion coefficient is:

\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}(\theta)}=\beta_{t}^{\kappa\theta^{2}}=\begin{cases}\frac{\sin(\sqrt{\kappa}\theta t)}{\sin(\sqrt{\kappa}\theta)}&\kappa>0,\\
t&\kappa=0,\\
\frac{\sinh(\sqrt{|\kappa|}\theta t)}{\sinh(\sqrt{|\kappa|}\theta)}&\kappa<0,\end{cases}\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\quad\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}=[0,t_{\kappa}).

(8.38)

The condition
\theta\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}
corresponds to
\bar{\kappa}(\theta)=\kappa\theta^{2}<\pi^{2}
, which is reminiscent of the Bonnet-Myers bound.

Relation with classical coefficients.

This is the building block for the
construction of Riemannian distortion coefficients. Consider a space form of dimension

N
and (sectional) curvature equal to
K/(N-1)
, and thus Ricci curvature equal to

K\in\mathbb{R}
. The potential “felt” in the directions orthogonal to the one of the motion,
along a geodesic of length
\mathsf{d}
, is
K\mathsf{d}^{2}/(N-1)
. There are
N-1

orthogonal directions, and one direction where there is no curvature (the direction of the
motion). Therefore, taking the product of all these factors we obtain

\beta_{t}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}\beta_{t}^{K\mathsf{d}^{2}/(N-1)}=\begin{cases}t\left(\frac{\sin(t\mathsf{d}\sqrt{K/(N-1)})}{\sin(\mathsf{d}\sqrt{K/(N-1)})}\right)^{N-1}&K>0,\\
t^{N}&K=0,\\
t\left(\frac{\sinh(t\mathsf{d}\sqrt{|K|/(N-1)})}{\sinh(\mathsf{d}\sqrt{|K|/(N-1)})}\right)^{N-1}&K<0,\end{cases}\quad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\ \mathsf{d}<t_{K/(N-1)}.

(8.39)

This is the usual distortion coefficient for a space form of Ricci curvature
K
and dimension
N
. Compare with Section
3.3.1
.

8.2.2
The Sasakian case

In this section we describe the typical coefficients arising in the description of three-dimensional Sasakian space forms, which were the first sub-Riemannian structures where an effective comparison theory was established
[
52
,
5
,
62
]
.

Let
\ell=2
, so that
Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
. We have
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and we set
Q=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{1},0)
for some
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
(formally,
\kappa=(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
with
\kappa_{2}=0
, which greatly simplifies this case). Proposition
8.4
can be used to obtain

\mathsf{\mathsf{s}}_{\kappa_{1},0}(t)=\frac{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)-\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\sin\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)}{\kappa_{1}^{2}},\qquad t_{\kappa_{1},0}=\begin{cases}\tfrac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}}&\kappa_{1}>0,\\
+\infty&\kappa_{1}\leq 0,\end{cases}

(8.40)

For brevity, instead of writing explicitly all the cases depending on the sign of
\kappa_{1}
as done in Section
8.2.1
, here
\mathsf{\mathsf{s}}_{\kappa_{1},0}(t)
is understood as real-analytic function of
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
, to interpret it for
\kappa_{1}\leq 0
. Furthermore we obtain

\beta_{t}^{\kappa_{1},0}=\frac{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)-\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\sin\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)}{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}\right)-\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}\sin\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}\right)}.

(8.41)

On a three-dimensional Sasakian manifold with Tanaka-Webster curvature equal to
K\in\mathbb{R}
, the sharp distortion coefficient along a geodesic
\gamma
is the product of two factors. One flat factor
t
, arising from a one-dimensional subspace (the direction of the motion), and one factor
\beta_{t}^{\kappa_{1},0}
as the one we just described, with

\kappa_{1}=\bar{\kappa}_{1}(|h_{0}|,\mathsf{d}):=|h_{0}|^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2},

(8.42)

where
\mathsf{d}
is the length of the geodesic and
h_{0}
is the Reeb component of its initial covector. In particular
\bar{\kappa}_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is homogeneous of degree
2
and the distortion coefficient thus obtained is suitable for a theory with vector-valued gauge function (the gauge function being
\mathsf{G}=(\mathsf{G}_{1},\mathsf{G}_{2})
with
\mathsf{G}_{1}=|h_{0}|
and
\mathsf{G}_{2}=\mathsf{d}
).

We will describe more precisely this case in Section
10.5
.

8.2.3
The two columns case

This example generalizes the previous one. Let again
\ell=2
so that
Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to8.4pt{\vss\hbox to8.00006pt{\hss$\scriptstyle$\hss}\vss}\kern-8.4pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\vrule height=8.4pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-8.4pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 8.00006pt\hrule width=8.79993pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
, and let
Q=\mathrm{diag}(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2})
, with
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. The difference with respect to Section
8.2.2
is that now
\kappa_{2}
may be non-zero. The Hamiltonian of the corresponding LQ problem is

H(p,q)=\frac{1}{2}\left(p_{1}^{2}+2p_{2}q_{1}+\kappa_{1}q_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}q_{2}^{2}\right).

(8.43)

We can compute the distortion coefficient using Proposition
8.4
. By reduction to Jordan normal form of the Hamiltonian system (
8.9
) (see details in
[
79
, Prop. 28]
), one obtains

\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}(t)=\frac{\xi_{-}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{+}t\right)-\xi_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{-}t\right)}{4\xi_{-}^{2}\xi_{+}^{2}(\xi_{-}^{2}-\xi_{+}^{2})},

(8.44)

where we have set
\kappa_{1}=2(\xi_{+}^{2}+\xi_{-}^{2})
and
\kappa_{2}=-(\xi_{+}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})^{2}
. In other words, choosing the principal branch of the square root:

\xi_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(\sqrt{x+y}\pm\sqrt{x-y}),\qquad\text{with}\qquad x=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2},\quad y=\frac{\sqrt{4\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{1}^{2}}}{2}.

(8.45)

It seems not trivial to deduce an explicit expression for
t_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}
from the above expression. Abstract necessary and sufficient conditions in terms of
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}
for the finiteness of
t_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}
are obtained via the results in
[
6
]
. Estimates for
t_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}
are found in
[
79
, Prop. 1]
.

Thus, assuming
t_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}>1
, the distortion coefficient is

\beta_{t}^{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}=\frac{\xi_{-}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{+}t\right)-\xi_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{-}t\right)}{\xi_{-}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{+}\right)-\xi_{+}^{2}\sin^{2}\left(\xi_{-}\right)},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(8.46)

understood with the usual conventions as a real-analytic function of
\xi_{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}
.

8.3
Main comparison result

Recall from Definition
4.6
the distortion coefficient of a metric measure space. For a sub-Riemannian m.m.s
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, it can always be written as

\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)=t\beta_{t}^{\perp}(x,y),\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M).

(8.47)

The factor
t
corresponds to the distortion felt in the direction of the geodesic between
x
and
y
, while the factor
\beta_{t}^{\perp}
represents the distortion felt in the perpendicular directions.

The following result is a reformulation of
[
25
, Thm. 16]
, with
N=n
(cf. Rmks. 17 and 12 there), with an explicit dependence on a possibly vector-valued gauge function
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
, combined with Proposition
8.5
6
. We will state directly the general vector-valued case, the scalar-valued one is obtained by setting
m=1
, in which case the statement greatly simplifies, see in particular Remark
8.7
.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with
n=\dim M
, and let
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
be a finite Borel function,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M)
and
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
be a geodesic joining them, with initial covector
\lambda
, and reduced Young diagram
Y
. Assume that there exists a function
\bar{C}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
, homogeneous of degree
1
, such that

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}\leq\bar{C}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)).

(8.48)

Denote with
\Upsilon
the set of levels of
Y
. Assume that for any level
\alpha\in\Upsilon
, with size
r_{\alpha}
and length
\ell_{\alpha}
, there exist
\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\alpha}}
such that each component
\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha_{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is homogeneous of degree
2i
, for
i=1,\dots,\ell_{\alpha}
, and such that for any superbox
\alpha_{i}
of that level it holds

\frac{1}{r_{\alpha}}\mathfrak{Ric}^{\alpha_{i}}_{\lambda}\geq\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha_{i}}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),\qquad i=1,\dots,\ell_{\alpha},

(8.49)

with the convention that if
\alpha
is the level of length
\ell_{\alpha}=1
then
r_{\alpha}
is replaced by
r_{\alpha}-1
; and if
r_{\alpha}=0
after this replacement, then that level is omitted. Then:

•

denoting with
{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
the positivity domain of
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
as in Proposition
8.5
, it holds

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}},

(8.50)

so that the distortion coefficients
\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}
are well-defined for all
\alpha\in\Upsilon
as in Proposition
8.5
;

•

with the same convention as above, we have that

\frac{\beta_{t}^{\perp}(x,y)}{\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}\left(\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}\right)^{r_{\alpha}}}e^{-t\bar{C}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}\quad\text{is a non-increasing function of $t\in(0,1]$},

(8.51)

and in particular, the following holds

\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq te^{(t-1)\bar{C}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}\prod_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}\left(\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}\right)^{r_{\alpha}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1].

(8.52)

Remark 8.10
.

We recall from Proposition
8.5
that, for
\bar{\kappa}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, and
t\in[0,1]

\beta_{t}^{\bar{\kappa}(\mathsf{G})}=\begin{cases}t^{N}&|\mathsf{G}|=0,\\
\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(t\mathsf{G})}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}(\mathsf{G})}&|\mathsf{G}|\neq 0\text{ and }\mathsf{G}\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}},\end{cases}

(8.53)

with
N=\ell^{2}
, is the distortion coefficient of the LQ model with a Young diagram of one row and
\ell
columns, with potential
Q=\mathrm{diag}(\bar{\kappa}_{1}(\mathsf{G}),\dots,\bar{\kappa}_{\ell}(\mathsf{G}))
. In particular the functions
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
depend only on the
\ell
homogeneous functions
\bar{\kappa}_{1},\dots,\bar{\kappa}_{\ell}
.

Theorem
8.9
yields a comparison result for distortion along a single geodesic. If its assumptions hold globally, then we obtain the following result, establishing the compatibility of our
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
theory with the one of sub-Riemannian Ricci bounds. Once again, in the scalar-valued case
m=1
what follows greatly simplifies, see Remark
8.7
.

Definition 8.11

(Sub-Riemannian m.m.s. with Ricci curvature bounded from below)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with
n=\dim M
, equipped with a finite gauge function
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
has
Ricci curvatures bounded from below
if there exist:

•

a Young diagram
Y
,

•

functions
\bar{C}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
homogeneous of degree
1
,

•

functions
\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\alpha}}
such that each component
\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha_{i}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
is homogeneous of degree
2i
, for
i=1,\dots,\ell_{\alpha}
for any level
\alpha
of
Y
, with length
\ell_{\alpha}
,

such that for all
x\in M
and
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
y
there exists a unique geodesic
\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(M)
joining
x
with
y
, with Young diagram
Y
, initial covector
\lambda\in T_{x}^{*}M
, for which the following hold:

•

bound on the geodesic volume derivative:

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}\leq\bar{C}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),

(8.54)

•

bound on the Ricci curvatures: for all levels
\alpha
of the Young diagram
Y

\frac{1}{r_{\alpha}}\mathfrak{Ric}^{\alpha_{i}}_{\lambda}\geq\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha_{i}}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),\qquad i=1,\dots,\ell_{\alpha},

(8.55)

with the convention that if
\alpha
is the level of length
\ell_{\alpha}=1
then
r_{\alpha}
is replaced by
r_{\alpha}-1
; and if
r_{\alpha}=0
after this replacement, then that level is omitted.

The above data determine a function
\mathsf{s}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\mathsf{s}(\theta):=|\theta|\cdot e^{\bar{C}(\theta)}\cdot\prod_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}}(\theta)^{r_{\alpha}},\qquad\forall\,\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+},

(8.56)

where
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
are as in Proposition
8.5
6
,
\Upsilon
denotes the set of levels of
Y
, and with the convention that if
\alpha
is the level of length
\ell_{\alpha}=1
then
r_{\alpha}
is replaced by
r_{\alpha}-1
; and if
r_{\alpha}=0
after this replacement, then that level is omitted from the product in (
8.56
). Finally, let
\beta
be the corresponding distortion coefficient defined as in (
6.4
).

We note that the positivity domain
{\rm{DOM}}
of
\mathsf{s}
(see Section
6
) is

{\rm{DOM}}=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}},

(8.57)

and that
\mathsf{s}
satisfies the asymptotic relation
\mathsf{s}(\theta)=c|\theta|^{N}+o(|\theta|^{N})
for some
c>0
and

N=\sum_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}r_{\alpha}\ell_{\alpha}^{2}.

(8.58)

Theorem 8.12

(Ideal sub-Riemannian structures with Ricci bounded below are
\mathsf{CD}
)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be an ideal sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with
n=\dim M
, equipped with a finite gauge function
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
, with Ricci curvatures bounded from below in the sense of Definition
8.11
, and let
\beta
be the corresponding distortion coefficient. Then
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition.

Proof.

Step 1: comparison out of the cut locus.
We use the Ricci curvature bounds assumption of Definition
8.11
, paired with Theorem
8.9
, to deduce that for all
x\in M
and
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
y\in M

\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in{\rm{DOM}}=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Upsilon}{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{\alpha}},

(8.59)

where
{\rm{DOM}}
is the positivity domain of
\mathsf{s}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
in (
8.56
), and furthermore

\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathsf{G}(x,y)),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(8.60)

where
\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
in the right hand side is the distortion coefficient built out of
\mathsf{s}
according to the construction in Section
3
(Section
6
for the vector case). See Remark
8.10
.

Step 2: interpolation inequalities for densities.
By
[
24
, Thm. 4]
, for any
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
there exists a unique
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
, associated with
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
such that
\mu_{t}\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
t\in(0,1]
, and letting
\rho_{t}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}
be the corresponding density, it holds for all
t\in(0,1)
:

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/n}}\geq\frac{\beta_{1-t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})^{1/n}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/n}}+\frac{\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{1/n}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/n}},\quad\nu\text{-a.e.}\ \gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(M),

(8.61)

with the understanding that the first term in the right hand side of (
8.61
) is omitted if
\mu_{0}\notin\mathcal{P}_{ac}(M,\mathfrak{m})
. We also remark that
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, the corresponding optimal plan
\pi
, and the corresponding
W_{2}
-geodesic
\mu_{t}
are unique, and they are induced by an optimal transport map
T:M\to M
such that
T_{\sharp}\mu_{0}=\mu_{1}
.

Furthermore, by
[
24
, Cor. 3.8]
, optimal transport on ideal structures almost surely is either static or avoids the cut locus. More precisely:

\mu_{1}\left(M\setminus\{x\in M\mid T(x)=x\}\cup\{x\in M\mid T(x)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(x)\}\right)=0.

(8.62)

Recall that the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
inequality (
3.15
) must be tested only for measures such that
\supp\mu_{0}\cap\supp\mu_{1}=\emptyset
. In this case,
\nu
is concentrated on a set of geodesics with endpoints out of the cut locus.

Thus, using (
8.60
), we have that (
8.61
) holds for
\nu
-a.e. geodesic
\gamma\in\mathrm{Geo}(M)
, with the replacements:

\beta_{1-t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})\mapsto\beta_{1-t}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})),\qquad\beta_{t}^{(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\mapsto\beta_{t}^{\mathrm{mod}}(\mathsf{G}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})).

(8.63)

Integrating the aforementioned inequality with respect to
\nu
, and using Jensen’s inequality, we obtain the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition (
3.15
) for
\beta=\beta^{\mathrm{mod}}
.
∎

9
Curvature estimates for fat sub-Riemannian structures

In this section, we establish new Ricci curvature lower bounds for fat sub-Riemannian structures. We address the reader to Appendix
B
for a self-contained summary on the canonical curvature in sub-Riemannian geometry that we use extensively.

We provide here an informal explanation of the basic concepts of Young diagram and Ricci curvatures. To the generic
4
4

4

More precisely, for an open and dense set of points
x\in M
there exists a non-empty Zariski open (and thus dense) set
A_{x}\subseteq T_{x}^{*}M
of initial covectors for which the associated geodesic has a well-defined Young diagram. See
[
91
, Sec. 5]
and
[
3
, Sec. 5.2]
.
geodesic of a sub-Riemannian manifold we can associate a Young diagram. Each Young diagram must be thought of as an ordered collection of
n=\dim M
boxes, each one corresponding to a one-dimensional subspace of the tangent space along the given geodesic. The position of the box in the diagram is determined by the number of derivations (aka Lie brackets in the direction of the geodesic) that one must perform to access to that direction starting with the original distribution. According to the general theory, a sub-Riemannian canonical curvature operator can be defined on the tangent space along the geodesic. It behaves in a different way when restricted to each one of these one-dimensional subspaces. It is natural then to group up the one-dimensional subspaces having homogeneous behaviour. We obtain in this way a so-called
reduced
Young diagram, whose boxes are called superboxes. Each superbox of a reduced Young diagram represents a subspace of the tangent space along the geodesic where the curvature behaves in a homogeneous way, and thus it makes sense to trace the curvature operator in these subspaces. This gives rise to Ricci curvatures, one for each superbox. Note that, on Riemannian manifolds, the reduced Young diagram is the same for any geodesic, it is formed by only one superbox, and a large part of the canonical curvature theory explained in Appendix
B
trivializes.

9.1
Fat curvature estimates

A distribution
\mathscr{D}
on a smooth manifold
M
of dimension
n\geq 3
is called
fat
if it is
strong bracket-generating

[
82
,
71
]
: for any smooth vector field
X
on
M
it holds

X|_{x}\neq 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathscr{D}_{x}+[X,\mathscr{D}]_{x}=T_{x}M,\qquad\forall\,x\in M.

(9.1)

Let
g
be a sub-Riemannian metric on
\mathscr{D}
. We call
(\mathscr{D},g)
a
fat sub-Riemannian structure
on
M
. Letting
\mathsf{d}
be the corresponding Carnot-Carathéodory distance.

Remark 9.1
.

We recall that the following popular classes of sub-Riemannian structures are fat: the Heisenberg groups, contact structures, all H-type groups in the sense of Kaplan
[
57
]
, and more generally H-type foliations
[
30
]
.

For a fat structure, any non-trivial geodesic has the same Young diagram with two columns. We label the superboxes of the reduced Young diagram as follows:

b

a

c

The sizes of the superboxes are
\mathrm{size}(a)=\mathrm{size}(b)=n-k
and
\mathrm{size}(c)=2k-n
. To each superbox, we associate the canonical Ricci curvatures
\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}
,
\mathfrak{Ric}^{b}
,
\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}
respectively.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
g
is the restriction to
\mathscr{D}
of a Riemannian metric
g_{R}
. Recall from Definition
7.4
the function
\mathsf{D}:M\times M\to[0,+\infty]
associated with the Riemannian extension
\mathsf{d}_{R}=\mathsf{d}_{g_{R}}
. By Proposition
7.28
, it holds

\mathsf{D}(x,y)=\|\nabla^{R}_{x}c(\cdot,y)\|_{R},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M),

(9.2)

where
c=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}^{2}
. To fix the ideas, in the Heisenberg group, and choosing the canonical Riemannian extension, for
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M)
it holds

\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}=\mathsf{d}(x,y)^{2}+h_{0}(\lambda^{x,y})^{2},

(9.3)

where
h_{0}(\lambda^{x,y})
is the vertical component of the unique initial covector
\lambda^{x,y}
of the geodesic between
x
and
y
.

The next result will be pivotal to show that fat sub-Riemannian structures on a compact
n
-dimensional manifold are
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
spaces, for suitable
\beta
(cf. Theorem
9.23
).

Theorem 9.2

(Fat curvature estimates)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a sub-Riemannian metric space with dimension
n
and with fat distribution of rank
k<n
. Then, for any compact set
K\subset M
there exists a constant
\kappa>0
such that for any
x,y\in K\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
and for the unique geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
joining them, with initial covector
\lambda\in T_{x}^{*}M
, it holds

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\geq-\kappa\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{4},

(9.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ric}^{b}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\geq-\kappa\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2},

(9.5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\geq-\kappa\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}.

(9.6)

Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{m}
is a smooth measure on
M
, there exists
C>0
such that for any
x,y\in K\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
and for the unique geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
joining them, with initial covector
\lambda\in T_{x}^{*}M
, it holds:

|\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}|\leq C\mathsf{D}(x,y),

(9.7)

where
\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}
is the geodesic volume derivative (see Section
B.3
).

Remark 9.3
.

If the last block is one-dimensional, i.e.
\mathrm{size}(c)=2k-n=1
, then the lower bound for
\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}
is redundant as
\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}_{\lambda}=0
.

Remark 9.4
.

The constants in Theorem
9.2
do depend on the choice of
g_{R}
.

Recall that the canonical frame and curvature, seen as functions of the initial covector
\lambda\in T^{*}M
, are not defined when the associated geodesic is the trivial (abnormal) one, corresponding to
\lambda\in\mathscr{D}^{0}
(the annihilator bundle of
\mathscr{D}
). From an analytic viewpoint, this translates into a singularity as
\lambda\to\mathscr{D}^{0}
of the canonical frame. This does not happen in Riemannian geometry, where the canonical frame is defined even for the zero covector.

In this section we will prove that even if the canonical frame is singular, the canonical curvature is not. More precisely, we will establish a stronger result on the structure of the canonical frame in a neighborhood of the annihilator bundle, which is the main result of this section (see Theorem
9.22
). The latter yields Theorem
9.2
as a consequence.

9.2
Preliminary setup

We start by setting up some notation. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be a local Riemannian orthonormal frame defined on a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}\subset M
, such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{k}
are horizontal (i.e., smooth sections of
\mathscr{D}
). Define linear-on-fibers functions
h_{i}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
via
h_{i}(\lambda)=\langle\lambda,X_{i}\rangle
, for
i=1,\dots,n
. These functions define a local trivialization
T^{*}\mathcal{O}\simeq\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let also
c_{ij}^{k}:\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
be the smooth functions defined by

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k},\qquad i,j=1,\dots,n.

(9.8)

We routinely use symplectic calculus (cf.
[
2
, Ch. 4]
). We recall some basic concepts here. To any
a\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\mathcal{O})
we associate the vector field
\vec{a}
on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}

\vec{a}(f)=\{a,f\},\qquad\forall\,f\in C^{\infty}(T^{*}\mathcal{O}),

(9.9)

where
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
denotes the Poisson bracket induced by the symplectic structure on
T^{*}M
. Thus, on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, we define a frame for
T(T^{*}\mathcal{O})
given by

\vec{h}_{1},\dots,\vec{h}_{n},\partial_{h_{1}},\dots,\partial_{h_{n}}.

(9.10)

Notice that it holds
\pi_{*}\vec{h}_{i}=X_{i}
, where
\pi:T^{*}M\to M
is the bundle projection. Let also
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{n}
be the dual frame of one-forms on
\mathcal{O}
such that
\langle\nu_{i},X_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
for
i,j=1,\dots,n
. In terms of this frame, the tautological one-form is
\tau=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\ell}\pi^{*}\nu_{\ell}
on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
and the symplectic form
\sigma=d\tau
is

\sigma=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}dh_{\ell}\wedge\pi^{*}\nu_{\ell}+h_{\ell}\pi^{*}d\nu_{\ell}.

(9.11)

In terms of the frame (
9.10
) we have, on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
and for
i,j=1,\dots,n
:

\sigma(\partial_{h_{i}},\partial_{h_{j}})=0,\qquad\sigma(\partial_{h_{i}},\vec{h}_{j})=\delta_{ij},\qquad\sigma(\vec{h}_{i},\vec{h}_{j})=\sum_{\mu=1}^{n}h_{\mu}c_{ij}^{\mu}.

(9.12)

Let
H:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
be the sub-Riemannian Hamiltonian and denote with
\vec{H}
the corresponding Hamiltonian vector field, which we assume to be complete, and denote with
e^{t\vec{H}}
its flow. The curves
\lambda:[0,1]\to T^{*}M
given by

\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M,

(9.13)

are called normal extremals, and the projections
\gamma=\pi\circ\gamma
are locally length-minimizing curves parametrized with constant speed
\|\dot{\gamma}_{t}\|^{2}=2H(\lambda)
, for all
t\in[0,1]
. In particular if
\gamma
is a geodesic it holds
\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{2}=2H(\lambda)
.

For any tensor field on
T^{*}M
, and without risk of confusion, the dot denotes the derivation with respect to
\vec{H}
. For example if
V\in\Gamma(T(T^{*}M))
:

\dot{V}|_{\lambda}=\left.\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\right|_{\varepsilon=0}e^{-\varepsilon\vec{H}}_{*}V|_{e^{\varepsilon\vec{H}}(\lambda)}=[\vec{H},V]|_{\lambda},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M,

(9.14)

and for functions
a:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
, we have
\dot{a}=\vec{H}(a)
.

The frame (
9.10
) will serve as a reference for the computation of the canonical frame. It is convenient to adopt the following notation to distinguish between “horizontal” and “vertical” coordinates. We let
u_{i}=h_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
and
v_{j}=h_{j}
for
j=k+1,\dots,n
. In particular
u,v
denote tuples
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
,
v=(u_{k+1},\dots,u_{n})
, and thus
h=(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})=(u,v)
. In this notation we have the following trivialization of
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
:

T^{*}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k},

(9.15)

and we denote a point
\lambda\in T^{*}\mathcal{O}
as
\lambda=(x;h)=(x;u,v)
. On
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, the sub-Riemannian and Riemannian Hamiltonians
H,H_{R}:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
, respectively, read:

H=\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad H_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\left(|u|^{2}+|v|^{2}\right).

(9.16)

For any set
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
, we adopt the notation (recall that
\mathscr{D}^{0}
is the annihilator of
\mathscr{D}
)

\Lambda_{\neq 0}:=\Lambda\setminus\mathscr{D}^{0}=\{\lambda\in\Lambda\mid H(\lambda)>0\},

(9.17)

so that, if
\Lambda\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, in the given trivialization we have

\Lambda_{\neq 0}=\{(x;u,v)\in\Lambda\mid u\neq 0\}.

(9.18)

9.2.1
Fundamental computations

The following lemma collects computations and notations that will be used throughout the section. The proofs are routine computation, and are omitted.

Lemma 9.5

(Fundamental computations)
.

The following formulas hold on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
:

\displaystyle\dot{\partial}_{v_{\mu}}

\displaystyle=-u_{\ell}c_{\ell A}^{\mu}\partial_{h_{A}},

(9.19)

\displaystyle\dot{\partial}_{u_{i}}

\displaystyle=-\vec{u}_{i}-u_{\ell}c_{\ell A}^{i}\partial_{h_{A}},

(9.20)

\displaystyle\dot{{\vec{h}}}_{A}

\displaystyle=-h_{B}c_{A\ell}^{B}\vec{u}_{\ell}+u_{\ell}c_{\ell A}^{B}\vec{h}_{B}+u_{\ell}h_{B}f_{\ell AC}^{B}\partial_{h_{C}},

(9.21)

where for lower-case latin indices
i,j,\ell=1,\dots,k
, for greek ones
\mu,\nu,\sigma=k+1,\dots,n
, for upper-case latin ones
A,B,C=1,\dots,n
, repeated indices are understood as summed over their range, and we defined the following smooth functions on
\mathcal{O}
:

f_{\ell AC}^{B}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{O}),\qquad f_{\ell AC}^{B}:=X_{\ell}(c_{AC}^{B})-X_{A}(c_{\ell C}^{B})-c_{\ell A}^{D}c_{DC}^{B}+c_{\ell D}^{B}c_{AC}^{D}-c_{AD}^{B}c_{\ell C}^{D}.

(9.22)

We rewrite these equations in a compact notation as follows:

\displaystyle\dot{\partial}_{v}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{A}\cdot\partial_{u}+\mathbb{B}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.23)

\displaystyle\dot{\partial}_{u}

\displaystyle=-\vec{u}+\mathbb{C}\cdot\partial_{u}+\mathbb{D}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.24)

\displaystyle\dot{\vec{u}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\cdot\vec{u}-\mathbb{A}^{*}\cdot\vec{v}+\mathbb{F}\cdot\partial_{u}+\mathbb{G}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.25)

\displaystyle\dot{\vec{v}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{H}\cdot\vec{u}-\mathbb{B}^{*}\cdot\vec{v}+\mathbb{I}\cdot\partial_{u}+\mathbb{L}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.26)

where the dot denotes multiplication of tuples
5
5

5

Here and in the following, the notation
V=O\cdot W
, where
O
is an
n\times m
matrix,
V=(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})
is a
n
-tuple of vector fields, and similarly
W=(W_{1},\dots,W_{m})
, with
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, means that
V_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}O_{ij}W_{j}
.
, and where we defined the following smooth matrix-valued maps:

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\mu i}

\displaystyle:=-u_{\ell}c_{\ell i}^{\mu},

(9.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,n-k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=-u_{\ell}c_{\ell\nu}^{\mu},

(9.28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=-u_{\ell}c_{\ell j}^{i},

(9.29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(k,n-k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{D}_{i\mu}

\displaystyle:=-u_{\ell}c_{\ell\mu}^{i},

(9.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=-h_{B}c_{ij}^{B}+u_{\ell}c_{\ell i}^{j},

(9.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=u_{\ell}h_{B}f_{\ell ij}^{B},

(9.32)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(k,n-k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}_{i\mu}

\displaystyle:=u_{\ell}h_{B}f_{\ell i\mu}^{B},

(9.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{\mu i}

\displaystyle:=-h_{B}c^{B}_{\mu i}+u_{\ell}c_{\ell\mu}^{i},

(9.34)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mu i}

\displaystyle:=u_{\ell}h_{B}f_{\ell\mu i}^{B},

(9.35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}

\displaystyle:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,n-k),

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=u_{\ell}h_{B}f_{\ell\mu\nu}^{B}.

(9.36)

9.2.2
Partial dilations

Recall that we have fixed a Riemannian extension so that
TM=\mathscr{D}\oplus\mathscr{V}
, with
\mathscr{V}\perp\mathscr{D}
, and thus
T^{*}M=\mathscr{V}^{0}\oplus\mathscr{D}^{0}
. This induces partial dilations on the fibers of the cotangent bundle. For simplicity we only provide local definitions in the trivialization
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, even though these maps are defined on the whole
T^{*}M
.

Definition 9.6

(
u
-dilation)
.

For
a>0
, we define the
u
-dilation
\delta_{a}^{hor}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to T^{*}\mathcal{O}
by

\delta_{a}^{hor}(x;u,v):=(x;au,v),\qquad(x;u,v)\in T^{*}\mathcal{O}.

(9.37)

Definition 9.7

(
u
-homogeneous functions)
.

Let
V
be a vector space. A map
f:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to V
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
d
if

f(\delta_{a}^{hor}(\lambda))=a^{d}f(\lambda),\qquad\forall\,a>0,\;\lambda\in T^{*}\mathcal{O}.

(9.38)

Definition 9.8

(
u
-star shaped set)
.

A set
\Lambda\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
is
u
-star-shaped
if

\Lambda=\bigcup_{a\in[0,1]}\delta_{a}^{hor}(\Lambda).

(9.39)

Similar definitions can be given for
v
-dilations,
v
-homogeneous functions,
v
-star-shaped sets but they will not be used, so they are omitted.

9.2.3
Pseudo-homogeneous maps

The class of
u
-homogeneous maps is not closed with respect to
\vec{H}
-derivations. Thus, we introduce a larger class of maps with this property. We only give a local definition.

Definition 9.9

(
u
-pseudo-homogeneous maps)
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let
\Lambda\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
be an open set. We say that
f:\Lambda\to V
is
u
-pseudo-homogeneous
of degree
d\in\mathbb{N}
if there exist

•

a finite set of indices
I
;

•

smooth functions
g_{i},c_{i}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{R}
,
i\in I
(defined on the whole
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
);

such that it holds

f(x;u,v)=|u|^{d}\sum_{i\in I}c_{i}(x;u,v)g_{i}(x;u/|u|,v),\qquad\forall\,(x;u,v)\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.40)

The following simple observation will be crucial.

Lemma 9.10
.

If
f:\Lambda\to V
is
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
d
, then
\vec{H}(f)
is also
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
d
. In particular, if
\Lambda\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
is relatively compact domain,
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C=C_{j,\Lambda}
such that

\|\vec{H}^{(j)}(f)(\lambda)\|\leq CH(\lambda)^{d/2},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},

(9.41)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes a fixed arbitrary norm on
V
.

Proof.

Notice that
H=\tfrac{1}{2}|u|^{2}
so that
\vec{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}\vec{u}_{i}
, and it holds
\vec{H}(|u|)=0
on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
. In particular
\vec{H}
is a derivation that acts only on
x,v
and the spherical part of
u
. The statement follows easily from (
9.40
).
∎

Remark 9.11
.

All
u
-homogeneous functions of degree
d
are
u
-pseudo-homogeneous. In this case (
9.40
) is satisfied for
I=\{1\}
,
c_{1}=1
, and
g_{1}
is any everywhere-smooth function such that
g_{1}(x;u,v)=f(x;u,v)
for
u\in\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
.

9.3
Regularity of the canonical frame

In the next series of lemmas we prove that one can choose in a smooth and locally bounded fashion a canonical frame as a function of the initial covector. The key feature is that the sets
\Lambda
in the statements are
u
-star-shaped so that the estimates hold arbitrarily close to the singularity at
\mathscr{D}^{0}
, corresponding to
2H=0
or, equivalently,
u=0
.

Recall that, for a fat structure, all non-trivial normal extremals have the same Young diagram, with two columns. The elements of the frame are labeled according to the boxes of the (reduced) Young diagram:

Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$c$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \mathrm{size}(a)&=\mathrm{size}(b)=n-k,\\
\mathrm{size}(c)&=2k-n.\end{aligned}

(9.42)

We organize a canonical frame along an extremal
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
as a collection of tuples

\{E_{a},E_{b},E_{c},F_{a},F_{b},F_{c}\},

(9.43)

where, for
\boxdot=a,b,c
, each
E_{\boxdot}
,
F_{\boxdot}
in this list is a tuple of smooth vector fields along
\lambda_{t}
, of size equal to the one of the corresponding superbox
\mathrm{size}(\boxdot)
.

Remark 9.12
.

In Lemmas
9.13
,
9.14
,
\Lambda
is a neighborhood of any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M
. Furthermore, the
u
-star-shaped property of
\Lambda
is a triviality since, as it is clear from the proof,
\Lambda
can actually be any relatively bounded domain contained in a local trivialization
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
. Starting from Lemma
9.15
onward, however,
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
, and furthermore the
u
-star-shaped property is not trivial and must be proved in the construction.

Lemma 9.13
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
of
\bar{\lambda}
,
T>0
, and a smooth map
\mathbb{P}:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})
, such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
the tuple

E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},\qquad t\in[0,T],

(9.44)

is (a choice of) the
E_{a}
-component of the canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

Furthermore, the map
\mathbb{P}
and all its time-derivatives are uniformly bounded, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},

(9.45)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes a fixed matrix norm.

Proof.

Let
\bar{x}=\pi(\bar{\lambda})
and let
\mathcal{O}\subseteq M
be an open neighborhood of
\bar{x}
equipped with a local orthonormal frame, as explained in Section
9.2
, so that we have the trivialization

T^{*}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k},\qquad\lambda=(x;u,v).

(9.46)

Let
\Lambda\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
be any
u
-star-shaped and relatively compact neighborhood of
\bar{\lambda}
. Let
\Lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\Lambda)
. Indeed, the union of all
\Lambda_{t}
, for
t\in[0,T]
, is relatively compact. Furthermore,
\pi(\Lambda_{t})
is contained in the union of all the sub-Riemannian balls with centers in the bounded set
\pi(\Lambda)
and radius
t\rho
, where
\rho=\sup\{|u|\mid(x;u,v)\in\Lambda\}
. If
T
is sufficiently small, then
\Lambda_{t}\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
for all
t\leq T
. Hence, in the following, all extremals
\lambda_{t}
with
\lambda_{0}=\lambda\in\Lambda
, are contained in a common compact subset of
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
.

Now fix
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
. The tuple
E_{a}
is determined (up to a constant orthogonal transformation) by the following conditions along
\lambda_{t}
:

(i)

E_{a}
is vertical (i.e.
\pi_{*}E_{a}=0
);

(ii)

\dot{E}_{a}
is vertical;

(iii)

normalization condition:
\mathbb{1}=\sigma(\ddot{E}_{a},\dot{E}_{a})
;

(iv)

Darboux frame condition:
\mathbb{0}=\sigma(\ddot{E}_{a},\ddot{E}_{a})
.

By Lemma
9.5
, points (i) and (ii),
E_{a}
must have the form

E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\theta(t)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],

(9.47)

for some smooth
t\mapsto\theta(t)\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})
.

Using Lemma
9.5
, we obtain

\ddot{\partial}_{v}=(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})\cdot\partial_{u}+(\dot{\mathbb{B}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{D}+\mathbb{B}^{2})\cdot\partial_{v}-\mathbb{A}\cdot\vec{u}.

(9.48)

Here
\dot{\mathbb{A}}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k\times k)
is the smooth map given by
\dot{\mathbb{A}}(\lambda)=(\vec{H}\mathbb{A})(\lambda)
, where the action of
\vec{H}
is meant component-wise. The notation is consistent as
\dot{\mathbb{A}}(\lambda_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t})
. Similarly for
\dot{\mathbb{B}}
.

Using (
9.48
) and (
9.23
) in the ansatz (
9.47
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{a}

\displaystyle=\theta\mathbb{A}\cdot\partial_{u}+(\dot{\theta}+\theta\mathbb{B})\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.49)

\displaystyle\ddot{E}_{a}

\displaystyle=[2\dot{\theta}\mathbb{A}+\theta(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})]\cdot\partial_{u}+[\ddot{\theta}+2\dot{\theta}\mathbb{B}+\theta(\dot{\mathbb{B}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{D}+\mathbb{B}^{2})]\cdot\partial_{v}-\theta\mathbb{A}\cdot\vec{u},

(9.50)

where we omitted the explicit evaluation along
\lambda_{t}
.

We now impose (iii). It yields:

\mathbb{1}=-\sigma(\dot{E}_{a},\ddot{E}_{a})=\sigma(\theta\mathbb{A}\cdot\partial_{u},\theta\mathbb{A}\cdot\vec{u})=(\theta\mathbb{A})\sigma(\partial_{u},\vec{u})(\theta\mathbb{A})^{*}=\theta\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}\theta^{*},

(9.51)

where we used (
9.12
) for
\sigma(\partial_{u},\vec{u})=\mathbb{1}
, and the star denotes the transpose.

Notice that
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,n-k)
is given by

\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}(x;u,v)_{\mu\nu}=-\sum_{i,j,\ell=1}^{k}u_{\ell}u_{j}c_{\ell i}^{\mu}(x)c_{ji}^{\nu}(x),\qquad\mu,\nu=k+1,\dots,n.

(9.52)

In particular
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
2
. The fat assumption means that
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(x;u,v)
has non-trivial kernel if and only if
u=0
, or equivalently that
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}>0
on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
, and thus the restriction

\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}|_{T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k}),

(9.53)

is a smooth map taking values in the space of scalar products.

By the Gram-Schmidt process applied to the columns of
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}|_{T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}}
, and the fact that the latter is smooth, we deduce the existence of a smooth map

\mathbb{S}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k}),

(9.54)

which is
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
, and such that

\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}(x;u,v)=\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}^{*}(x;u,v),\qquad\forall\,(x;u,v)\in T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}.

(9.55)

Notice that
\mathbb{A}
is a rectangular matrix, while
\mathbb{S}
is an invertible square matrix.

We define then the smooth map
t\mapsto\phi(t)\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})
by

\phi(t):=\theta(t)\mathbb{S}(\lambda_{t}),\qquad t\in[0,T].

(9.56)

Condition (iii) in (
9.51
), expressed in terms of
\phi
, is equivalent to

\phi\phi^{*}=\theta\mathbb{S}\mathbb{S}^{*}\theta^{*}=\theta\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}\theta^{*}=\mathbb{1},

(9.57)

omitting evaluation along
\lambda_{t}
. Since
\phi(t)
is invertible we obtain that

\text{condition (iii)}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad\phi(t)\in\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k}),\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T].

(9.58)

So that we can refine (
9.47
) as:

E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\phi(t)\mathbb{S}(\lambda_{t})^{-1}\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],

(9.59)

for a smooth map
t\mapsto\phi(t)
taking values now in
\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})
.

We now impose condition (iv), namely
\mathbb{0}=\sigma(\ddot{E}_{a},\ddot{E}_{a})
. This yields an ODE which will characterize
t\mapsto\phi(t)
. Using (
9.50
) and (
9.12
), we obtain

\mathbb{0}=[2\dot{\theta}\mathbb{A}+\theta(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})](\theta\mathbb{A})^{*}-\theta\mathbb{A}[2\dot{\theta}\mathbb{A}+\theta(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})]^{*}.

(9.60)

We re-express this condition in terms of
\phi
. To do this, we use again the shorthand
\dot{\mathbb{S}}=\vec{H}(\mathbb{S}):T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})
, so that
\dot{\mathbb{S}}(\lambda_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{S}(\lambda_{t})
, consistently with the previous notation. Omitting
t
and evaluation along
\lambda_{t}
, and denoting with
\mathcal{A}(M)=\tfrac{1}{2}(M-M^{*})
the skew-symmetric part of a matrix
M
, we obtain the following ODE for
\phi
:

\displaystyle 2\dot{\phi}

\displaystyle=\phi\mathbb{S}^{-1}\mathcal{A}\left(2\dot{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}-(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})\mathbb{A}^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\sigma(\vec{u},\vec{u})\mathbb{A}^{*}\right)\mathbb{S}^{-1},

(9.61)

where we used
\phi\in\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k})\Rightarrow\dot{\phi}\phi^{*}=-\phi\dot{\phi}^{*}
. We can rewrite the above ODE as
\dot{\phi}(t)=\phi(t)\Xi(\lambda_{t})
, where the smooth map

\Xi:T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k\times n-k),

(9.62)

takes values in skew-symmetric matrices and is defined by

\Xi:=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{S}^{-1}\mathcal{A}\left[2\dot{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}-(\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})\mathbb{A}^{*}+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{A}\sigma(\vec{u},\vec{u})\mathbb{A}^{*}\right]\mathbb{S}^{-1}.

(9.63)

The terms
\mathbb{A},\dot{\mathbb{A}},\mathbb{B},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{D},\sigma(\vec{u},\vec{u})
are smoothly defined on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, on the other hand
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}^{-1}
are well-defined and smooth only on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
, and they blow-up as
H(\lambda)\to 0
.

We claim that
\Xi
and all its
\vec{H}
-derivatives
\vec{H}^{(j)}(\Xi)
remain uniformly bounded on
\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. To prove this claim, observe that
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{C}
,
\sigma(\vec{u},\vec{u})
,
\mathbb{S}
appearing in (
9.63
) are
u
-homogeneous of degree one, in particular they are
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
1
. By Lemma
9.10
, the
\vec{H}
-derivatives of these maps are
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
1
. In particular this is the case for
\dot{\mathbb{A}}
and
\dot{\mathbb{S}}
appearing in (
9.63
). Taking into account the form (
9.40
) of
u
-pseudo-homogeneous maps all possible singularities at
u=0
in (
9.63
) cancel out, and
\Xi
and its
\vec{H}
-derivatives are
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
d=0
. Another application of Lemma
9.10
proves the claim.

We choose
t\mapsto\phi(t)
in (
9.59
) as the solution of the Cauchy problem

\dot{\phi}(t)=\phi(t)\Xi(\lambda_{t}),\qquad\phi(0)=\mathbb{1}.

(9.64)

Of course one might choose as the initial condition
\phi(0)
