any orthogonal matrix, and this freedom in the choice of the initial condition is expected as the canonical frame is uniquely defined only up to a constant orthogonal transformation.

By the construction of
\Lambda
and
T
all extremals
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
, for
t\leq T
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
, are contained in a common relatively compact subset of
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
. Furthermore,
\Xi
and its
\vec{H}
-derivatives are uniformly bounded for all
t\in[0,T]
,
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. In particular the solution to the ODE (
9.64
) is well-defined on
[0,T]
. Define the map

\Phi:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{n-k}),

(9.65)

such that
\Phi(\cdot,\lambda)
is the solution of (
9.64
) with
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
. Since
\Xi
is skew-symmetric then
\Phi
is orthogonal for
t\in[0,T]
.

Thanks to the aforementioned claim of uniform boundedness property of
\Xi
, we can apply Gronwall’s lemma to (
9.64
), and we obtain that for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C=C(j,\Lambda,T)>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\Phi(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.66)

In order to recover the statement of the lemma, we set

\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda):=\Phi(t,\lambda)\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}\mathbb{S}(\lambda_{t})^{-1},\qquad\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda),\qquad\forall\,(t,\lambda)\in[0,1]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.67)

By construction, the map

(t,\lambda)\mapsto E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

(9.68)

is smooth for
(t,\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
, and
E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}
is the
E_{a}
-component of a canonical frame along the extremal
\lambda_{t}
with initial covector
\lambda
.

It remains to prove that the map
\mathbb{P}
and its time-derivatives
\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{P}
are uniformly bounded as required on
[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. Notice that
\Phi(t,\lambda)
and its time-derivatives already satisfy the required property by (
9.66
), while
\lambda\mapsto\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}\mathbb{S}(\lambda)^{-1}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
0
and smooth on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
, so that we can apply Lemma
9.10
for
d=0
, so that also
\sqrt{2H(\lambda_{t})}\mathbb{S}(\lambda_{t})^{-1}
and all its time-derivatives are uniformly bounded as required.
∎

Lemma
9.13
for the
E_{a}
-component of the frame is the starting point for analogous statement for all the other tuples.

Lemma 9.14
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
of
\bar{\lambda}
,
T>0
, and smooth maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(n_{i},m_{i})
, with
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i=1,\dots,5
, such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
the tuples

\displaystyle E_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{1}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Q}_{2}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

\displaystyle t\in[0,T],

(9.69)

\displaystyle F_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{3}(t,\lambda)\cdot\vec{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\mathbb{Q}_{4}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Q}_{5}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

\displaystyle t\in[0,T],

(9.70)

are (a choice of) the
E_{b}
and
F_{b}
-components of the canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

Furthermore, the maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
and all their time-derivatives are bounded, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{Q}_{i}(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},\;i=1,\dots,5,

(9.71)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes a fixed matrix norm.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
and
T>0
as in Lemma
9.13
(cf. first paragraph of its proof). Let
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. By the structural equations
E_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\dot{E}_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}
. Using Lemma
9.5
, we obtain

E_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\left[\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t})\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+(\dot{\mathbb{P}}(t,\lambda)+\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\mathbb{B}(\lambda_{t}))\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}}\right],

(9.72)

where
\dot{\mathbb{P}}(t,\lambda)=\partial_{t}\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)
. We set hence:

\mathbb{Q}_{1}(t,\lambda):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t}),\qquad\mathbb{Q}_{2}(t,\lambda):=\dot{\mathbb{P}}(t,\lambda)+\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\mathbb{B}(\lambda_{t}).

(9.73)

Notice that
\mathbb{P}
and all its time-derivatives are uniformly bounded by the estimate in Lemma
9.13
. Since
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
are
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
1
, an application of Lemma
9.10
yields the required bounds on
\mathbb{Q}_{1}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{2}
and their time-derivatives.

Likewise,
F_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}=-\dot{E}_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}
, so that, using Lemma
9.5
, we obtain:

F_{b}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\left[\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\cdot\vec{u}-(\dot{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{P}\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})\cdot\partial_{u}\right.\\
\left.-(\ddot{\mathbb{P}}+2\dot{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{P}\dot{\mathbb{B}}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{D}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{B}^{2})\cdot\partial_{v}\right],

(9.74)

omitting evaluation along
\lambda_{t}
from
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{D}
, and evaluation at
(t,\lambda)
for
\mathbb{P}
. To obtain the statement for
F_{b}
we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{3}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A},

(9.75)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{4}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}(\dot{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{A}+\mathbb{P}\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A}),

(9.76)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{5}

\displaystyle:=-(\ddot{\mathbb{P}}+2\dot{\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{B}+\mathbb{P}\dot{\mathbb{B}}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{D}+\mathbb{P}\mathbb{B}^{2}),

(9.77)

and argue as above using Lemma
9.10
to prove the required estimates on the
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
’s.
∎

Lemma 9.15
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
of
\bar{\lambda}
,
T>0
, and smooth maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(n_{i},m_{i})
, with
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i=6,\dots,10
, such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
the tuples

\displaystyle E_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{6}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Q}_{7}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

\displaystyle t\in[0,T],

(9.78)

\displaystyle F_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{8}(t,\lambda)\cdot\vec{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\mathbb{Q}_{9}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Q}_{10}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

\displaystyle t\in[0,T],

(9.79)

are (a choice of) the
E_{c}
and
F_{c}
-components of the canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

Furthermore, the maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
and all their time-derivatives are bounded, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{Q}_{i}(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},\;i=6,\dots,10,

(9.80)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes a fixed matrix norm.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
and
T>0
as in Lemma
9.13
(cf. first paragraph of its proof). Let
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
, for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
. The
(2k-n)
-tuple
E_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}
is determined, up to a constant orthogonal transformation, by the following conditions along
\lambda_{t}
:

(i)

E_{c}
is vertical (i.e.
\pi_{*}E_{c}=0
);

(ii)

Darboux frame conditions:
\mathbb{0}=\sigma(\dot{E}_{b},E_{c})
and
\mathbb{0}=\sigma(\ddot{E}_{b},E_{c})
;

(iii)

normalization condition:
\mathbb{1}=\sigma(\dot{E}_{c},E_{c})
;

(iv)

isotropic condition:
\mathbb{0}=\sigma(\dot{E}_{c},\dot{E}_{c})
.

We observe that if
\bar{E}_{c}
is a tuple which verifies (i), (ii) and (iii) along
\lambda_{t}
, then all these conditions will be also verified by

E_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}:=\psi(t)\cdot\bar{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}},\qquad t\in[0,T],

(9.81)

for any smooth map
t\mapsto\psi(t)\in\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{2k-n})
. Then, we find first smooth tuple
\bar{E}_{c}
such that (i), (ii) and (iii) are verified, and subsequently we will fix
\psi
via (iv).

By (i), we must have

\bar{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}=U(t)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+V(t)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},\qquad t\in[0,T],

(9.82)

for some smooth
t\mapsto U(t)\in\mathrm{M}(2k-n,k)
and
t\mapsto V(t)\in\mathrm{M}(2k-n,n-k)
. From this, and Lemma
9.5
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}

\displaystyle=-U\cdot\vec{u}+(\dot{U}+U\mathbb{C}+V\mathbb{A})\cdot\partial_{u}+(\dot{V}+U\mathbb{D}+V\mathbb{B})\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.83)

\displaystyle\ddot{\bar{E}}_{c}

\displaystyle=-(2\dot{U}+U\mathbb{E}+U\mathbb{C}+V\mathbb{A})\cdot\vec{u}+U\mathbb{A}^{*}\cdot\vec{v}+\mathrm{span}\{\partial_{u},\partial_{v}\},

(9.84)

Similarly from (
9.72
) in the proof of Lemma
9.14
, and Lemma
9.5
we obtain:

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\cdot\vec{u}+\mathrm{span}\{\partial_{u},\partial_{v}\},

(9.85)

\displaystyle\ddot{E}_{b}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{T}\cdot\vec{u}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}\cdot\vec{v}+\mathrm{span}\{\partial_{u},\partial_{v}\},

(9.86)

where, for brevity of notation, we set
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda)
, as

\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda):=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\Big[2(\partial_{t}\mathbb{P})\mathbb{A}+3\mathbb{P}\dot{\mathbb{A}}+\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{A}\mathbb{E}+\mathbb{A}\mathbb{C}+\mathbb{B}\mathbb{A})\Big],

(9.87)

where the matrix maps
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{B}
,
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{E}
in the r.h.s. are evaluated along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
,
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)
, for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
. Notice that
\mathbb{A}
is
u
-pseudo-homogeneous of degree
1
, together with
\dot{\mathbb{A}}
, by Lemma
9.10
. Therefore we obtain that
\mathbb{T}
is uniformly bounded with all its time-derivatives, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C=C_{j}
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda)\|\leq C_{j},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.88)

Using relations (
9.12
), and the fact that
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*}
are invertible on
\Lambda_{\neq 0}
(cf. proof of Lemma
9.13
), conditions (ii) are equivalent to:

\mathbb{A}U^{*}=0,\qquad\text{and}\qquad V^{*}=\sqrt{2H}(\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*})^{-1}\mathbb{T}U^{*},

(9.89)

along
\lambda_{t}
and where
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)
and
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda)
.
The second equation of (
9.89
) determines
V(t)
in terms of
U(t)
. The first one is an orthogonality relation between the rows of
\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t})
and those of
U(t)
. Recall from Lemma
9.5
that
\mathbb{A}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,k)
has constant rank equal to
n-k
on
T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
, while
U:[0,T]\to\mathrm{M}(2k-n,k)
must have rank
2k-n
. By applying the Gram-Schmidt process to a local orthogonal complement to the rows of
\mathbb{A}(\bar{\lambda})
we can find a neighborhood
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset T^{*}\mathcal{O}
of
\bar{\lambda}
and a smooth map

\mathbb{A}^{\perp}:\Lambda^{\prime}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(2k-n,k).

(9.90)

such that

\mathbb{A}^{\perp}\mathbb{A}^{*}=\mathbb{0},\qquad\mathbb{A}^{\perp}\mathbb{A}^{\perp*}=|u|^{2}\mathbb{1}.

(9.91)

Furthermore, since
\mathbb{A}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree one, we can take
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
to be also
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
to be
u
-star-shaped. The
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda
of
\bar{\lambda}
built at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
9.13
was arbitrary. Up to restriction of
\Lambda
to a smaller
u
-star-shaped set, and up to taking a smaller
T
(and since the flow of
\vec{H}
is smooth and preserves
|u|
), we can assume that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
, the extremals
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
take value in a common bounded neighborhood contained in
\Lambda^{\prime}_{\neq 0}
, so that
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}(\lambda_{t})
is well-defined. We then set:

\displaystyle U(t):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{A}^{\perp},\qquad V(t):=-\mathbb{A}^{\perp}\mathbb{T}^{*}(\mathbb{P}\mathbb{A}\mathbb{A}^{*})^{-1*},

(9.92)

where the matrix maps
\mathbb{A}
,
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
in the r.h.s. are evaluated along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
and
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)
,
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
. Since
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
are
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
,
\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)
and
\mathbb{T}(t,\lambda)
are uniformly bounded with all time-derivatives and
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
, and using Lemma
9.10
, we observe that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
C=C_{j}>0
independent on
\lambda
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}U(t)\|\leq C,\qquad\|\partial_{t}V(t)\|\leq CH(\lambda)^{-1/2},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.93)

Estimates (
9.93
) will be used later.

With the definition (
9.92
), conditions (i) and (ii) are fully verified, while (iii) corresponds to the following normalization:

\mathbb{1}=UU^{*}=\frac{1}{2H}\mathbb{A}^{\perp}\mathbb{A}^{\perp*},

(9.94)

along
\lambda_{t}
, which is verified by our choice in the normalization of
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
in (
9.91
). This concludes the construction of the tuple
\bar{E}_{c}
.

Set hence
E_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\psi(t)\cdot\bar{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}
, for
\psi(t)\in\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{2k-n})
. Condition (iv) yields an ODE that determines
\psi
, once the initial condition is fixed, for example
\phi(0)=\mathbb{1}
. We obtain

\dot{\psi}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\psi(t)\sigma(\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}},\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}),\qquad\psi(0)=\mathbb{1}.

(9.95)

Since
(t,\lambda)\mapsto\sigma(\bar{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}},\bar{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}})
is skew-symmetric and smooth, and
\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{2k-n})
is compact, (
9.95
) will have a unique solution in
\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{2k-n})
for all
t\in[0,T]
, and for any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. We define then the smooth map

\Psi:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{O}(\mathbb{R}^{2k-n}),

(9.96)

such that
t\mapsto\Psi(t,\lambda)
is the solution of (
9.95
) on
[0,T]
corresponding to
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

The matrix in the r.h.s. of (
9.95
) is given more explicitly by

\displaystyle\sigma(\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}},\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}})

\displaystyle=U\sigma(\vec{u},\vec{u})U^{*}-2\mathcal{A}\left[(\dot{U}+U\mathbb{C}+V\mathbb{A})U^{*}\right].

(9.97)

where
\mathcal{A}(M)=\tfrac{1}{2}(M-M^{*})
denotes the skew-symmetric part of a matrix
M
, and
U
,
V
are defined in (
9.92
). By the uniform estimates for
U(t)
and
V(t)
in (
9.93
), and since
\mathbb{A}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
, we see that
\sigma(\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}},\dot{\bar{E}}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}})
and all its time-derivatives remain uniformly bounded for all extremals
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
for
t\in[0,T]
and
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. By Gronwall’s lemma we deduce an analogous uniform boundedness property of
\Psi
and its time-derivatives, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\Psi(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T]\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.98)

Finally, to obtain the statement of the lemma, we set then

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{6}(t,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\Psi(t,\lambda)\mathbb{A}^{\perp}(\lambda_{t}),

(9.99)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{7}(t,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=-\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}\Psi(t,\lambda)\mathbb{A}^{\perp}(\lambda_{t})\mathbb{T}^{*}(t,\lambda)\left[\mathbb{P}(t,\lambda)\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t})\mathbb{A}(\lambda_{t})^{*}\right]^{-1*}.

(9.100)

Using the fact that
\mathbb{A}^{\perp}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree one, that
\Psi,\mathbb{T},\mathbb{P}
are uniformly bounded on
[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
with all their time-derivatives, and applying Lemma
9.10
, we obtain the desired uniform bounds on
\mathbb{Q}_{6},\mathbb{Q}_{7}
.

To prove the analogous statement for the tuple
F_{c}
, we recall that by the structural equations

F_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}=-\dot{E}_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}
. Then, making use of the Leibniz rule, we obtain the corresponding statement of the lemma, for suitably defined matrix-valued maps
\mathbb{Q}_{8},\mathbb{Q}_{9},\mathbb{Q}_{10}
defined on
[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
, satisfying the required uniform bounds.
∎

We recall first, that for a general reduced Young diagram
Y
and for two superboxes
\boxtimes,\boxplus
of
Y
, the corresponding curvature map can be computed as

R_{t}(\boxtimes,\boxplus)=\sigma(\dot{F}_{\boxtimes}|_{\lambda_{t}},F_{\boxplus}|_{\lambda_{t}}).

(9.101)

Hence a first series of estimates for some of the curvature maps comes as a consequence of the previous ones for the canonical frame.

Remark 9.16
.

Notice that
R_{t}(\boxtimes,\boxplus)
is a one-parameter family of
\mathrm{size}(\boxtimes)\times\mathrm{size}(\boxplus)
matrices representing the canonical curvature operator for the given choice of the canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}
. By the uniqueness part of Theorem
B.2
, and since the Hilbert-Schmidt norm
\|M\|^{2}=\tr(MM^{*})
is invariant under this action of the orthogonal group, the quantities
\|\partial_{t}^{j}R_{t}(\boxtimes,\boxplus)\|
do not depend on choice of the canonical frame and are thus well-defined estimates for the corresponding canonical curvature operator
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}
and their time-derivatives.

Lemma 9.17

(Curvature estimates I)
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
of
\bar{\lambda}
and
T>0
such that, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that for the canonical curvature map
R_{t}
along the extremal
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
it holds:

\|\partial_{t}^{j}R_{t}(\boxtimes,\boxplus)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\,\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},

(9.102)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and
\boxtimes,\boxplus=a,b,c
, with the exclusion of the pairs
(\boxtimes,\boxplus)=(a,a)
,
(a,c)
and
(c,a)
.

Proof.

Let
T>0
and
\Lambda
as in all previous lemmas (
9.13
,
9.14
,
9.15
). Take
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
,
t\in[0,T]
. We routinely omit the evaluation along the extremal
\lambda_{t}
for vector fields, and on
(t,\lambda)
for maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
. Recall from the previous lemma that

\displaystyle F_{b}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{3}\cdot\vec{u}+\mathbb{Q}_{4}\cdot\partial_{u}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{Q}_{5}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.103)

\displaystyle F_{c}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{8}\cdot\vec{u}+\mathbb{Q}_{9}\cdot\partial_{u}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{Q}_{10}\cdot\partial_{v},

(9.104)

where
\mathbb{Q}_{i}=\mathbb{Q}_{i}(t,\lambda)
are uniformly bounded with all their time-derivatives for
(t,\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. Thanks to these estimates, the only possible singularities as
H\to 0
can arise from the
\partial_{v}
-term in the above expressions. Recalling the symplectic product in the static basis
\partial_{u},\partial_{v},\vec{u},\vec{v}
in (
9.12
), and using Leibniz rule we see that, for example

\displaystyle R(b,b)=\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},F_{b})=\text{regular part}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{3}\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\partial_{v})\mathbb{Q}_{5}^{*}+\mathbb{Q}_{5}\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\vec{u})\mathbb{Q}_{3}^{*}\right),

(9.105)

where the regular part is the multiplication of a finite number of matrix maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
and their time-derivatives, evaluated at
(t,\lambda)
, and the smooth matrix maps
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B},\dots
from Lemma
9.5
evaluated along
\lambda_{t}
. In particular the regular part will be bounded with all its time-derivatives, uniformly for
(t,\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
.

For what concerns the possibly singular part, recall from Lemma
9.5
that
\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\partial_{v})=-\mathbb{A}^{*}
, and
\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\vec{u})=\mathbb{A}
. Since
\mathbb{A}:T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(n-k,n-k)
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
, by Lemma
9.10
the term

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\left(\mathbb{Q}_{3}\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\partial_{v})\mathbb{Q}_{5}^{*}+\mathbb{Q}_{5}\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\vec{u})\mathbb{Q}_{3}^{*}\right),

(9.106)

when evaluated along the extremal
\lambda_{t}
, is bounded with all its time-derivatives, uniformly for
(t,\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
.

Therefore
R(b,b)
is uniformly bounded with all its time-derivatives as required. The same argument proves the similar estimates for
R(c,c)
,
R(c,b)
and
R(b,c)=R(c,b)^{*}
.

The estimate of
R(a,b)
is more delicate. A direct estimate requires the knowledge of
F_{a}
, which are not in position to estimate yet. However, using the normal condition in the construction of the canonical frame, one can determine
R(a,b)
only using the estimates for
F_{b}
appearing in Lemma
9.14
. Indeed, recall that for a Young diagram with two columns, the normal condition is (cf. Appendix
B
):

R(b,a)=-R(b,a)^{*}.

(9.107)

Therefore, using (
9.115
), and the fact that the canonical frame is Darboux, we obtain

\displaystyle R(b,a)

\displaystyle=\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},F_{a})=-\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},\dot{F}_{b})+\sum_{\mu=a,b,c}\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},E_{\mu})R(b,\mu)^{*}

(9.108)

\displaystyle=-\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},\dot{F}_{b})+R(b,a)^{*}=-\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},\dot{F}_{b})-R(b,a),

(9.109)

from which it follows that

R(b,a)=-\frac{1}{2}\sigma(\dot{F}_{b},\dot{F}_{b}).

(9.110)

Using the explicit estimates for
F_{b}
and its derivative, one can now estimate
R(b,a)
. Using Leibniz rule and arguing as above we obtain that

R(b,a)=\text{regular part}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\left[\mathbb{Q}_{3}\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\partial_{v})\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{5}^{*}+\mathbb{Q}_{3}\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\dot{\partial_{v}})\mathbb{Q}_{5}^{*}+\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{3}\sigma(\vec{u},\dot{\partial_{v}})\mathbb{Q}_{5}^{*}\right.\\
\left.+\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{5}\sigma(\partial_{v},\dot{\vec{u}})\mathbb{Q}_{3}^{*}+\mathbb{Q}_{5}\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\dot{\vec{u}})\mathbb{Q}_{3}^{*}+\mathbb{Q}_{5}\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\vec{u})\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{3}^{*}\right],

(9.111)

where
\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{i}=\partial_{t}\mathbb{Q}_{i}(t,\lambda)
. Using Lemma
9.5
we observe that

\displaystyle\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\partial_{v})=-\sigma(\partial_{v},\dot{\vec{u}})^{*}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{A}^{*},

(9.112)

\displaystyle\sigma(\dot{\vec{u}},\dot{\partial_{v}})=-\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\dot{\vec{u}})^{*}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\mathbb{A}^{*}+\mathbb{A}^{*}\mathbb{B}^{*},

(9.113)

\displaystyle\sigma(\vec{u},\dot{\partial_{v}})=-\sigma(\dot{\partial_{v}},\vec{u})^{*}

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{A}^{*}.

(9.114)

Using again the fact that
\mathbb{A}
is
u
-homogeneous of degree
1
, and applying Lemma
9.10
to the possibly singular part in (
9.111
), we see that all possible singularities cancel out and also
R(b,a)
remains uniformly bounded with all derivatives along
\lambda_{t}
, as claimed.

The estimates from the missing pairs of superboxes in the statement of the lemma follow by the global symmetry of the curvature maps
R(\boxtimes,\boxplus)=R(\boxplus,\boxtimes)^{*}
.
∎

We can now prove the estimate for the last element of the canonical frame, which is the
n-k
-tuple
F_{a}
. Using the structural equations, we have the general formula:

F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=-\dot{F}_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\sum_{\boxtimes=a,b,c}R_{t}(b,\boxtimes)\cdot E_{\boxtimes}|_{\lambda_{t}}.

(9.115)

The curvatures
R_{t}(b,\boxtimes)
in (
9.115
) are precisely those estimated in Lemma
9.17
.

Lemma 9.18
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M
of
\bar{\lambda}
,
T>0
, and smooth maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}:[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}\to\mathrm{M}(n_{i},m_{i})
, with
n_{i},m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
,
i=11,\dots,14
, such that for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
the tuple

F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}=\mathbb{Q}_{11}(t,\lambda)\cdot\vec{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}\mathbb{Q}_{12}(t,\lambda)\cdot\vec{v}|_{\lambda_{t}}\\
+\mathbb{Q}_{13}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{u}|_{\lambda_{t}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}}\mathbb{Q}_{14}(t,\lambda)\cdot\partial_{v}|_{\lambda_{t}},

(9.116)

for
t\in[0,T]
, is (a choice of) the
F_{a}
-component of the canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

Furthermore, the maps
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
and all their time-derivatives are bounded, that is for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathbb{Q}_{i}(t,\lambda)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},\;i=11,\dots,14,

(9.117)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes a fixed matrix norm.

Proof.

By the structural equations we have, along any given extremal
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
:

F_{a}=-\dot{F}_{b}+R(b,b)\cdot E_{b}+R(b,a)\cdot E_{a}+R(b,c)\cdot E_{c}.

(9.118)

The claim is now a consequence of lemmas
9.13
,
9.14
,
9.15
for the previous parts of the canonical frame and Lemma
9.17
for estimate on the curvature maps appearing in (
9.118
). Using Leibniz rule and omitting as usual evaluation along
\lambda_{t}
and
(t,\lambda)
we obtain

F_{a}=\left(\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{3}+\mathbb{Q}_{3}\mathbb{E}-\mathbb{Q}_{4}\right)\cdot\vec{u}-\mathbb{Q}_{3}\mathbb{A}^{*}\cdot\vec{v}+\bigg(\mathbb{Q}_{3}\mathbb{F}+\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{4}+\mathbb{Q}_{4}\mathbb{C}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{Q}_{5}\mathbb{A}\\
+R(b,b)\mathbb{Q}_{1}+R(b,c)\mathbb{Q}_{6}\bigg)\cdot\partial_{u}+\bigg(\mathbb{Q}_{3}\mathbb{G}+\mathbb{Q}_{4}\mathbb{D}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\dot{\mathbb{Q}}_{5}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}\mathbb{Q}_{5}\mathbb{B}\\
+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}R(b,b)\mathbb{Q}_{2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}R(b,a)\mathbb{P}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2H}}R(b,c)\mathbb{Q}_{7}\bigg)\cdot\partial_{v}.

(9.119)

Using the
u
-homogeneity of
\mathbb{A}
coupled with Lemma
9.10
, we obtain the required uniform estimates, for
(t,\lambda)\in[0,T]\times\Lambda_{\neq 0}
, where
T
and
\Lambda
are as in the previous lemmas.
∎

We are ready to complete the estimates on the canonical curvature maps.

Lemma 9.19

(Curvature estimates II)
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
of
\bar{\lambda}
, and
T>0
such that, for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that for the canonical curvature maps
R_{t}
along the extremal
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
it holds:

\|\partial_{t}^{j}R_{t}(\boxtimes,\boxplus)\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0},

(9.120)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and
\boxtimes,\boxplus=a,b,c
.

Proof.

We only need to prove the statement for the pairs
\boxtimes,\boxplus
excluded in Lemma
9.17
, that is the pairs
(a,a)
,
(a,c)
and
(c,a)
. We sketch the proof since the argument is analogous to the one in Lemma
9.17
for the first curvature estimates, coupled with the new piece of information on
F_{a}
coming from Lemma
9.18
.

Let
T>0
and
\Lambda
so that all previous lemmas and estimates about the canonical frame hold. Then we decomposing, for example

R(a,a)=\sigma(\dot{F}_{a},F_{a})=\text{regular part}+\text{possibly singular part},

(9.121)

where the possibly singular part contains all terms with a factor containing a positive power of
H^{-1/2}
. Then, using the estimates for
F_{a}
, Lemma
9.5
and Lemma
9.10
, one can prove that all singularities cancel out. We stress in particular that the presence of the factor
\sqrt{2H}
in the
\vec{v}
-component of
F_{a}
of Lemma
9.18
is necessary for these cancellations. One then argues similarly for
R(c,a)=\sigma(\dot{F}_{c},F_{a})
and
R(a,c)=\sigma(\dot{F}_{a},F_{c})
.
∎

We now estimate the geodesic volume derivative. Recall that the fat sub-Riemannian manifold
M
comes equipped with a Riemannian extension. We denote with
\mathrm{vol}
the associated Riemannian density, while
M
is equipped with a smooth reference volume
\mathfrak{m}=e^{-V}\mathrm{vol}
, where
V:M\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function. The geodesic volume derivative with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
is the smooth function
\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}:T^{*}M_{\neq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}:=\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\log\mathfrak{m}(\pi_{*}F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}},\pi_{*}F_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}},\pi_{*}F_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}),\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0},

(9.122)

for any choice of canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
, see Section
B.3
.

Lemma 9.20

(Volume derivative estimates)
.

For any
\bar{\lambda}\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
there exist a
u
-star-shaped neighborhood
\Lambda\subset T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
of
\bar{\lambda}
such that, for all
j\in N
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}>0
such that for the geodesic volume derivative
\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}
it holds:

\vec{H}^{(j)}\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}\leq C,\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}.

(9.123)

Proof.

Let
\Lambda
and
T>0
as in the previous lemmas. By construction, for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
the extremal
\lambda_{t}
remains for all
t\in[0,T]
in a common bounded subset of a trivial neighborhood
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
, and the corresponding geodesic
\gamma_{t}=\pi(\lambda_{t})
remains in
\mathcal{O}
. Recall that from Lemmas
9.14
,
9.15
and
9.18
we have

\displaystyle\pi_{*}F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{11}(t,\lambda)X|_{\gamma_{t}}+\sqrt{2H(\lambda)}\mathbb{Q}_{12}(t,\lambda)Z_{\gamma_{t}},

(9.124)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}F_{b}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{3}(t,\lambda)X|_{\gamma_{t}},

(9.125)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}F_{c}|_{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Q}_{8}(t,\lambda)X|_{\gamma_{t}},

(9.126)

for all
t\in[0,T]
, and where
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})
,
Z=(X_{k+1},\dots,X_{n})
are tuples forming an adapted orthonormal frame for
g
on
\mathcal{O}
. In particular since
\mathfrak{m}(X,Z)=1
, we have

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda_{t}}=-\vec{H}(V)(\lambda_{t})+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\log\left\lvert\det\mathbb{Q}_{12}(t,\lambda)\det\begin{matrix}\mathbb{Q}_{3}(t,\lambda)\\
\mathbb{Q}_{8}(t,\lambda)\end{matrix}\right\rvert,

(9.127)

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
and
t\in[0,T]
. Notice that by construction
\mathbb{Q}_{12}
is a
n-k\times n-k
matrix of full rank, while
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\mathbb{Q}_{3}\\
\mathbb{Q}_{8}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is a
k\times k
matrix of full rank. To prove the statement, observe that
\vec{H}^{(j)}\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}=\partial_{t}^{j}\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda_{t}}|_{t=0}
, so that one has only to bound the time-derivatives of (
9.127
) at
t=0
and
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\neq 0}
. The first term
\vec{H}(V)(\lambda_{t})
is uniformly bounded with all time-derivatives since all curves
\lambda_{t}
remain in a common bounded subset of the trivial neighborhood
T^{*}\mathcal{O}
by construction, and
V
is smooth. The second term in (
9.127
) is also uniformly bounded with all time-derivatives, by the properties of the
\mathbb{Q}_{i}
’s.
∎

9.4
Regularity of the canonical curvature maps

We collect the local estimates from Lemmas
9.17
–
9.19
in a unified statement for the canonical curvature operators defined as in Section
B.2
.

Remark 9.21
.

The fact that the neighborhoods
\Lambda
of Lemmas from
9.13
to
9.19
are
u
-star-shaped will be used now.

Theorem 9.22

(Fat curvature estimates)
.

Let
(\mathscr{D},g)
be a fat sub-Riemannian structure on
M
. Then for any compact set
B\subset M
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C=C_{j}(B)>0
such that, for all superboxes
\boxtimes,\boxplus=a,b,c
of the Young diagram
Y
it holds

\|\vec{H}^{(j)}\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}\|\leq CH_{R}(\lambda)^{\tfrac{j+\mathrm{col}(\boxtimes)+\mathrm{col}(\boxplus)}{2}},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}B_{\neq 0},

(9.128)

where
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and
\mathrm{col}(\boxtimes)
is the column index of the superbox. As a consequence, for some
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}_{j}(B)>0
it also holds

|\vec{H}^{(j)}\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{\boxtimes}|\leq C^{\prime}H_{R}(\lambda)^{\tfrac{j}{2}+\mathrm{col}(\boxtimes)},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}B_{\neq 0}.

(9.129)

Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{m}=e^{-V}\mathrm{vol}
, for the geodesic volume derivative it holds

|\vec{H}^{(j)}\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}|\leq CH_{R}(\lambda)^{\tfrac{j+1}{2}},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}B_{\neq 0}.

(9.130)

Proof.

With no loss of generality we assume that
B
is properly contained in a trivial neighborhood
\mathcal{O}
such that

T^{*}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k},\qquad\lambda=(x;u,v),

(9.131)

and for the sub-Riemannian and Riemannian Hamiltonians it holds:

H(x;u,v)=\frac{1}{2}|u|^{2},\qquad H_{R}(x;u,v)=\frac{1}{2}(|u|^{2}+|v|^{2}).

(9.132)

Take the following compact set
K\subseteq T^{*}B
:

K=\{(x;u,v)\in B\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\mid|u|^{2}+|v|^{2}=1\}.

(9.133)

We can find a finite number of neighborhoods
\Lambda^{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
as in Lemmas
9.17
–
9.19
, that cover
K_{\neq 0}
. This is possible, even if
K_{\neq 0}
is non-compact, since the neighborhoods
\Lambda
obtained in the previous lemmas are
u
-star-shaped (otherwise, their size in the
u
-coordinate might get smaller and smaller as
u\to 0
). We deduce that for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists
C=C_{j}>0
such that

\|\partial_{t}^{j}\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}|_{t=0}\|\leq C,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T],\;\lambda\in K_{\neq 0},

(9.134)

where
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
. Recall the homogeneity properties of the curvature from
[
23
, Thm. 4.7]
: for any
c>0
, and up to constant congruence (cf. Remark
9.16
), it holds

\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda^{c}_{t}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}\simeq c^{\mathrm{col}(\boxtimes)+\mathrm{col}(\boxplus)}\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{ct}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0},

(9.135)

where
\lambda^{c}_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(c\lambda)
. In particular taking derivatives with respect to
t
, and evaluating at
t=0
, we have

\partial_{t}\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}|_{t=0}\simeq c^{\mathrm{col}(\boxtimes)+\mathrm{col}(\boxplus)+j}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{R}_{\eta_{t}}^{\boxtimes\boxplus}|_{t=0},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0},

(9.136)

where
\eta=\lambda/c
. The statement then follows by reduction to
K_{\neq 0}
, since any point
\lambda\in T^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\neq 0}
has the form
c\eta
with
\eta\in K_{\neq 0}
and
c=\sqrt{2H_{R}(\lambda)}
.

The statement concerning the canonical Ricci curvatures follows immediately as, for any superbox
\boxtimes\in Y
and
\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
it holds by definition:
\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{\boxtimes}=\tr(\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}^{\boxtimes\boxtimes})
.

The final statement about the geodesic volume derivative follows in a similar fashion, starting from Lemma
9.20
and using the fact that the following property holds:

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda^{c}_{t}}=c\,\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda_{ct}},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0},

(9.137)

which is immediately obtained by using
[
23
, Prop. 4.9]
in (
9.122
).
∎

9.5
Proof of Theorem
9.2

We can now prove Theorem
9.2
. In fact, it is a corollary of Theorem
9.22
.

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

If
x,y\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M)
, we have by Proposition
7.28
that

\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}=\|\nabla^{R}_{x}c(\cdot,y)\|_{R}^{2}=2H_{R}(\mathrm{d}_{x}c(\cdot,y))=2H_{R}(\lambda^{x,y}),

(9.138)

where
\lambda^{x,y}=\mathrm{d}_{x}c(\cdot,y)\in T_{x}^{*}M_{\neq 0}
is the initial covector of the unique strictly normal geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
joining
x
with
y
. Notice that the first equality follows by the very definition of
\mathsf{D}
in the sub-Riemannian case and by the fact that
c
is smooth outside of
\mathrm{Cut}(M)
. The second equality is obvious by the usual duality between norm and Hamiltonian. In the third equality we used the fact that the initial covector can be recovered by the differential of the squared distance (cf. for example
[
3
, Lemma 2.20(ii)]
).

Let
K\subset M
be a compact set as in the statement of Theorem
9.2
. Let
B
be a larger compact set such that for any
x,y\in K
all geodesics joining them belong to
B
. On
B
we apply Theorem
9.22
. In particular if
\lambda\in T_{x}^{*}M
is the initial covector of the unique geodesic
\gamma
joining
x,y\in K\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)
there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

|\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{\boxdot}|\leq C^{\prime}H_{R}(\lambda)^{\mathrm{col}(\boxdot)}=\frac{C^{\prime}}{2^{\mathrm{col}(\boxdot)}}\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2\mathrm{col}(\boxdot)},

(9.139)

and where
\boxdot=a,b,c
. This (stronger) inequality in particular implies the desired lower bounds on
\mathfrak{Ric}
by recalling that
\mathrm{col}(a)=\mathrm{col}(b)=2
and
\mathrm{col}(c)=1
.

In an analogous way one proves the statement about the geodesic volume derivative starting from the corresponding estimate in Theorem
9.22
.
∎

9.6
Compact fat structures satisfy the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
condition

We combine the curvature estimates of Theorem
9.2
for fat structures, and Theorem
8.12
, to obtain the main result of this section.

Recall that
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, for
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, are the basic comparison functions of Proposition
8.5
, associated with a Young diagram of length
\ell
. In particular:

•

for
\ell=1
,
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}
is a Riemannian-type function (see Section
8.2.1
);

•

for
\ell=2
,
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}
is a two-columns-type function (see Section
8.2.3
).

In all cases, the
\mathsf{s}_{\kappa}
are real-analytic and have order
N=\ell^{2}
at zero.

Theorem 9.23
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a compact
n
-dimensional sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with fat distribution of rank
k<n
. Let
\mathsf{D}
be the natural gauge function associated with a Riemannian extension. There exist constants
C,\kappa_{a},\kappa_{b},\kappa_{c}\in\mathbb{R}
with the following property: let
\mathsf{s}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the real-analytic function

\mathsf{s}(\theta):=\theta\cdot e^{C\theta}\cdot\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{b},\kappa_{a}}(\theta)^{n-k}\cdot\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{c}}(\theta)^{2k-n-1},

(9.140)

and let
\beta
be defined accordingly as in (
3.14
). Then
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{D})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.

Since
\mathsf{s}
is real-analytic, using Proposition
3.4
(vii)
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 9.24
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a compact
n
-dimensional sub-Riemannian metric measure space, with fat distribution of rank
k<n
. Then there exists
N^{\prime}\geq 3n-2k
such that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,N^{\prime})
.

Remark 9.25
.

Both these results can be seen as a sub-Riemannian counterpart of the fact that any compact Riemannian manifold has Ricci curvature bounded from below.

Remark 9.26
.

Corollary
9.24
removes the real-analytic assumption of the analogous result proved in
[
18
, Thm. 1.3]
, in the case when the underlying distribution is fat.

Remark 9.27
.

Fat sub-Riemannian structures are ideal and their step is
2
. In particular
\mathsf{D}
is locally bounded,
\mathsf{D}
is meek, and the regularity conditions for the stability of the
\mathsf{CD}
and
\mathsf{MCP}
hold. See Figure
3
.

Proof of Theorem
9.23
.

For a fat structure, all normal extremals corresponding to non-trivial geodesics have the same Young diagram, with two columns. It has two levels, denoted by
I
and
II
, the first one with length
\ell_{I}=2
and size
r_{I}=n-k
, while the second one with length
\ell_{II}=1
and size
r_{II}=2k-n
. We label the superboxes as follows:

Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$c$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}.

(9.141)

By Theorem
9.2
, and since
M
is compact, we find
\kappa_{a},\kappa_{b},\kappa_{c},C\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for any
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M)
and the corresponding geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
joining them with initial covector
\lambda\in T^{*}_{x}M
, it holds

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n-k}\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{a}\geq\kappa_{a}\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{4},\qquad\frac{1}{n-k}\mathfrak{Ric}^{b}_{\lambda}\geq\kappa_{b}\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2},

(9.142)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2k-n-1}\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{c}\geq\kappa_{c}\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2},\qquad\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}\leq C\mathsf{D}(x,y),

(9.143)

the bound on
\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{c}
being omitted if
2k-n=1
. Thus it follows that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
, equipped with the gauge function
\mathsf{D}
, has Ricci curvatures bounded from below according to Definition
8.11
, with the above Young diagram and

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{I}

\displaystyle:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{I}(\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle:=(\kappa_{b}\mathsf{D}^{2},\kappa_{a}\mathsf{D}^{4}),

(9.144)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{II}

\displaystyle:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{II}(\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle:=\kappa_{c}\,\mathsf{D}^{2},

(9.145)

\displaystyle\bar{C}

\displaystyle:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\bar{C}(\mathsf{D})

\displaystyle:=C\mathsf{D}.

(9.146)

We conclude by applying Theorem
8.12
obtaining that
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m},\mathsf{D})
is a
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
space. The distortion coefficient
\beta
is induced by the function
\mathsf{s}:\mathbb{R}^{m}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
of (
8.56
). To describe the latter explicitly, we recall that we are in the scalar case
m=1
, and thus by Remark
8.7
the function
\mathsf{s}:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is given by

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=\theta\cdot e^{C\theta}\cdot\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{b},\kappa_{a}}(\theta)^{n-k}\cdot\mathsf{s}_{\kappa_{c}}(\theta)^{2k-n-1},\qquad\theta\in[0,+\infty).

(9.147)

The positivity domain of
\mathsf{s}
is given by

{\rm{DOM}}={\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}\cap{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{II}}=[0,\min\{t_{\kappa_{c}},t_{\kappa_{b},\kappa_{a}}\}],

(9.148)

and
\mathsf{s}
has order
N=1+(n-k)\ell_{I}^{2}+(2k-n-1)\ell_{II}^{2}=3n-2k
as
\theta\to 0
.
∎

10
Examples and applications

10.1
Heisenberg group

In this section we describe the results already presented in Section
3.3.2
, in the special three-dimensional case, in order to make a bridge with the comparison theory developed in the previous sections.

The first Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
is the non-commutative group structure on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by the law

(x,y,z)\star(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},z^{\prime})=\left(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime},z+z^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}(xy^{\prime}-yx^{\prime})\right).

(10.1)

Consider the left-invariant vector fields

X=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}-\frac{y}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\qquad Y=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}+\frac{x}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},

(10.2)

and the left-invariant metric making
\{X,Y\}
a global orthonormal frame. We equip
\mathbb{H}^{1}
with the associated sub-Riemannian (Carnot-Carathéodory) distance, denoted by
\mathsf{d}_{0}
(this notation is slightly different from the one used in Section
3.3.2
, but it is more convenient in what follows). We also equip
\mathbb{H}^{1}
with the Lebesgue measure
\mathscr{L}^{3}
, which is a Haar measure. We set

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=\theta\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\left[\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right],\qquad\theta\in[0,+\infty).

(10.3)

Notice that
\mathsf{s}(\theta)
has finite order equal to
N=5
as
\theta\to 0
. Moreover, it is clear that

\mathcal{D}=\inf\{\theta>0\mid\mathsf{s}(\theta)=0\}=2\pi.

(10.4)

The corresponding model distortion coefficient defined as in (
3.14
) is then

[0,1]\times[0,\infty]\ni(t,\theta)\mapsto\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{t}(\theta)=\begin{cases}t^{5}\quad&\theta=0,\\
t\dfrac{\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\left(\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{t\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\right)}{\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\left(\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right)}&0<\theta<2\pi,\\
+\infty&\theta\geq 2\pi,t\neq 0,\\
0&\theta\geq 2\pi,t=0.\\
\end{cases}

(10.5)

The main result in
[
19
, Thm. 1.1]
is a Jacobian determinant inequality, see
[
19
, Eq. (3.17), p. 61]
, which yields by standard manipulations an interpolation inequality for optimal transport. More precisely, for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3})
, there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
associated with a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
\mu_{t}\ll\mathscr{L}^{3}
for all
t\in(0,1]
, for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma
it holds
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
for all
t\in(0,1]
, and

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/3}}\geq\frac{\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{1-t}(\theta^{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0}})^{1/3}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/3}}+\frac{\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{t}(\theta^{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}})^{1/3}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/3}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(10.6)

where
\rho_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{L}^{3}}
, and
\theta^{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}}
is the vertical norm of the covector associated with the unique geodesic with joining
\gamma_{0}
with
\gamma_{1}
. In (
10.6
) we use the convention that if
\mu_{0}
is not absolutely continuous then the first term in the right hand side is omitted.

Notice that the argument in the r.h.s. of (
10.6
) does not depend on the distance. It is then natural to set as a gauge function any map
\mathsf{G}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to[0,+\infty]
such that

\mathsf{G}(q,q^{\prime})=\theta^{q,q^{\prime}},\qquad\forall\,(q,q^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{1}).

(10.7)

We remark that
\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
has zero
({\rm e}_{0},{\rm e}_{1})_{\sharp}\nu
-measure for all optimal dynamical plans
\nu
involved, so that the values of
\mathsf{G}
on that set are irrelevant. By standard arguments (cf. again Section
3.3.2
), the following holds.

Theorem 10.1
.

The gauge m.m.s.
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3},\mathsf{G})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},3)
condition.

One can check that
\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{5}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
. Thus, by Proposition
3.10
(iii)
,
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},3)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CD}(t^{5},3)
. In turn, the latter implies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
.

10.1.1
Application of the comparison theory to the Heisenberg group

To make the link with the curvature computations of Sections
8
-
9
, notice that for every pair
(q,q^{\prime})\in\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{H}^{1})
and for the geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{H}^{1}
joining them, the (reduced) Young diagram
Y
along
\gamma
has two columns which can be labeled as follows

Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$c$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

with all sizes of the superboxes equal to 1. The sub-Riemannian Ricci curvatures have been computed in
[
5
]
, and are:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}_{\lambda}=0,\qquad\mathfrak{Ric}^{b}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\theta^{2},

(10.8)

where

\theta=\theta^{q,q^{\prime}}=|\langle\lambda^{q,q^{\prime}},\partial_{z}\rangle|,

(10.9)

is the absolute value of the
z
-component of the unique initial covector
\lambda^{q,q^{\prime}}
of the geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{H}^{1}
joining
q
and
q^{\prime}
. We stress that, for such geodesics,
\theta<2\pi
.
We have (cf. Section
8.2.2
)

\mathsf{\mathsf{s}}_{\kappa_{1},0}(t)=\frac{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)-\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\sin\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)}{\kappa_{1}^{2}},\qquad t_{\kappa_{1},0}=\begin{cases}\tfrac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}}&\kappa_{1}>0,\\
+\infty&\kappa_{1}\leq 0.\end{cases}

(10.10)

From Theorem
8.9
, one obtains the following comparison theorem

\displaystyle\beta_{t}^{(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathcal{L}^{3})}(q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq t\frac{2-2\cos\left(t\theta\right)-t\theta\sin\left(t\theta\right)}{2-2\cos\left(\theta\right)-\theta\sin\left(\theta\right)}

(10.11)

\displaystyle=t\frac{\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\left[\sin\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{t\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{t\theta}{2}\right)\right]}{\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\left[\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)-\frac{\theta}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\right]}

(10.12)

\displaystyle=\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{t}(\theta),

(10.13)

where
\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
is the expression given in Section
3.3.2
.

10.2
The Grushin plane

The Grushin plane is a quotient of the Heisenberg group. More precisely, there is a dilation-invariant (and thus non-compact) subgroup
H
of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
and a sub-Riemannian structure on the space of
right
cosets
H\backslash\mathbb{H}^{1}
such that
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{1}\to H\backslash\mathbb{H}^{1}
is a submetry, and
H\backslash\mathbb{H}^{1}
is isometric to the Grushin plane, see
[
31
, Sec. 5]
. It is well-known that the classical synthetic theory of Ricci curvature bounds descends to suitable quotients
[
48
]
. We expect that a similar theory can be developed for gauge metric measure spaces, but this is out of the scope of the present work.
6
6

6

One should be aware, however, that the Grushin plane is not a homogeneous space in the classical sense: the Heisenberg group acts transitively (from the right) on
\mathbb{G}_{2}
, but this action is not by isometries, see the discussion in
[
31
, p. 53]
.

In this section we illustrate how the Grushin plane, equipped with a suitable gauge function, satisfies the same curvature-dimension inequalities of the Heisenberg group.

To this purpose, we introduce a presentation of the Grushin plane. The Grushin plane
\mathbb{G}_{2}
is the sub-Riemannian structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by the global generating frame

X_{1}=\partial_{x},\qquad X_{2}=x\partial_{y}.

(10.14)

We equip the Grushin plane with the corresponding sub-Riemannian distance
\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}
and the Lebesgue measure
\mathfrak{m}=\mathscr{L}^{2}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, making it a metric measure space
(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}},\mathcal{L}^{2})
.

Fix
q=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
q^{\prime}\notin\mathrm{Cut}(q)
. Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the geodesic from
q
to
q^{\prime}
. Let
\lambda=u\,dx+v\,dy\in T^{*}_{(x,y)}\mathbb{R}^{2}
be its initial covector. We set
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}:\mathbb{G}_{2}\times\mathbb{G}_{2}\to[0,\infty)
:

\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}(q,q^{\prime})=v,\qquad\forall(q,q^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{G}_{2}),

(10.15)

arbitrarily extending it to
0
at the cut-locus. It is well-known that
|v|<\pi
, and conversely if
|v|<\pi
it holds
\exp_{(x,y)}(u\,dx+v\,dy)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(q)
, see
[
2
, Ch. 13]
. See Figure
4
.

Figure 4:
Geodesics and cut-locus (in red) of the Grushin plane starting from the origin and from
q=(1,0)
. Displayed geodesics have initial covector
\lambda=u\,dx+v\,dy
, with
v=\pm\pi
and different values of
u
.

It is easy to see that (
10.15
) is a meek gauge function. The Grushin plane is an ideal structure, and hence by
[
24
]
it supports interpolation inequalities for densities (with dimensional parameter
n=2
). More precisely, for all
\mu_{0},\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d},\mathscr{L}^{2})
, there exists
\nu\in{\rm OptGeo}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
associated with a
W_{2}
-geodesic
(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
\mu_{t}\ll\mathscr{L}^{2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and for
\nu
-a.e.
\gamma
it holds
(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{G}_{2})
and

\frac{1}{\rho_{t}(\gamma_{t})^{1/2}}\geq\frac{\beta^{(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d},\mathscr{L}^{2})}_{1-t}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{0})^{1/2}}{\rho_{0}(\gamma_{0})^{1/2}}+\frac{\beta^{(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d},\mathscr{L}^{2})}_{t}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})^{1/2}}{\rho_{1}(\gamma_{1})^{1/2}},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(10.16)

where
\rho_{t}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}}{\mathrm{d}\mathscr{L}^{2}}
, and
\beta^{(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d},\mathscr{L}^{2})}
is the “true” distortion coefficient of the Grushin plane, see Definition
4.6
. The latter was computed in
[
24
, Sec. 7.3]
, and it is given by

\beta_{t}^{(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d},\mathscr{L}^{2})}(q,q^{\prime})=t\frac{(u^{2}+tuv^{2}x+v^{2}x^{2})\sin(tv)-tu^{2}v\cos(tv)}{(u^{2}+uv^{2}x+v^{2}x^{2})\sin(v)-u^{2}v\cos(v)},\qquad\forall t\in[0,1],

(10.17)

where
\lambda=u\,dx+v\,dy\in T_{(x,y)}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the initial covector of the geodesic joining
q
with
q^{\prime}
.

Lemma 10.2
.

For all
(q,q^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{G}_{2})
it holds

\beta_{t}^{(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}},\mathscr{L}^{2})}(q,q^{\prime})\geq\beta_{t}^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}(\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}(q,q^{\prime})),

where we recall, by definition,
\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}}(q,q^{\prime})=v
in the above notation.

Proof.

This can be proven by direct computation, passing to inequalities between logarithmic derivative similarly to
[
24
, Sec. 7.3]
.
∎

Replacing the inequality of Lemma
10.2
in (
10.16
), using the definition of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
, the convexity of the map
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,y)\mapsto\log(e^{x}+e^{y})
and Jensen’s inequality, one establishes the validity of the corresponding curvature-dimension inequality.

Theorem 10.3
.

The gauge m.m.s.
(\mathbb{G}_{2},\mathsf{d}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}},\mathscr{L}^{2},\mathsf{G}_{\mathbb{G}_{2}})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},2)
condition.

Remark 10.4
.

We stress that the Heisenberg group satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},3)
, while Grushin correctly satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},2)
. The correct dimensional parameter appears through the interpolation inequalities for densities, namely (
10.16
).

As we already remarked,
\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}}_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{5}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
. Thus, by Proposition
3.10
(iii)
,
\mathsf{CD}(\beta^{\mathbb{H}^{1}},3)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CD}(t^{5},3)
. In turn, the latter implies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
. Thus Theorem
10.3
can be seen as a strengthening in the
\mathsf{CD}
sense of the fact that the Grushin plane satisfies the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
.

10.3
Canonical variation

In this section we consider what is called the
canonical variation
of the Heisenberg group, namely the one-parameter family of left-invariant Riemannian distances
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{H}^{1}
, which are those associated with a left-invariant Riemannian metric for which

\{X,Y,\varepsilon Z\},\qquad\varepsilon>0,

(10.18)

is orthonormal, where

X=\partial_{x}-\frac{y}{2}\partial_{z},\qquad Y=\partial_{y}+\frac{x}{2}\partial_{z},\qquad Z=\partial_{z}.

(10.19)

The terminology canonical variation is used in the context of Riemannian submersions
[
32
]
. Only for this subsection, it will be handy to denote by
\mathsf{d}_{0}
the Carnot-Carathéodory metric, so that we study the family of metric measure spaces
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
.

We remark that the Riemannian Ricci curvature of
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon})
is unbounded as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Therefore it is convenient to study the distortion coefficients of the corresponding metric measure spaces by direct methods. To this purpose, let
\beta_{t}^{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to[0,+\infty]
be the distortion coefficient of
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})
, for
\varepsilon\geq 0
. It can be computed explicitly, out of the cut locus, denoted by
\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon\geq 0
.

This was done, for example, in
[
75
, Sec. 4.2]
: letting
\exp_{0}^{\varepsilon}:T^{*}_{0}\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{H}^{1}
be the exponential map for the metric
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}
starting from
0\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
, we use cylindrical coordinates on
T^{*}_{0}\mathbb{H}^{1}
so that
\lambda=(\theta,\rho,p_{z})
. It holds that, for a general point
q\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
:

q=\exp_{0}(\theta,\rho,p_{z}),\qquad q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0)\Rightarrow|p_{z}|<2\pi,

(10.20)

and furthermore for all
q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0)
:

\beta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0,q)=t^{2}\frac{h(tp_{z}/2)\left[\varepsilon^{2}tp_{z}^{3}h(tp_{z}/2)+2\rho^{2}k(tp_{z}/2)\right]}{h(p_{z}/2)\left[\varepsilon^{2}p_{z}^{3}h(p_{z}/2)+2\rho^{2}k(p_{z}/2)\right]},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],

(10.21)

with the convention that, if
p_{z}=0
, then we take the limit in the above formula so that
\beta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0,q)=t^{5}
. In (
10.21
), we have set

h(\varsigma):=\frac{\sin(\varsigma)}{\varsigma},\qquad k(\varsigma):=\sin(\varsigma)-\varsigma\cos(\varsigma)\qquad\varsigma\in(0,\pi).

(10.22)

The fact that
q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0)
, and thus
|p_{z}|<2\pi
, implies that the right hand side of (
10.21
) is well-defined, for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
. One can check via elementary methods that for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
:

\beta_{t}^{\varepsilon}(0,q)\geq\beta_{t}^{0}(0,q)=t\frac{2-2\cos(p_{z}t)-p_{z}t\sin(p_{z}t)}{2-2\cos(p_{z})-p_{z}\sin(p_{z})},\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,1],\,q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0).

(10.23)

The initial covector
\lambda
of the unique
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}
-geodesic joining the origin with
q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0)
is given by
\lambda=d_{0}\left[\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)^{2}\right]
, where
d_{0}
denotes the differential at zero, and thus
p_{z}=\lambda(\partial_{z})
. We define the left-invariant gauge function
\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\times\mathbb{H}^{1}\to[0,2\pi]
such that:

\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}(0,q):=\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{2}\left|\left.\tfrac{\partial}{\partial z}\right|_{0}\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,q)^{2}\right|&q\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0),\\
0&q\in\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}(0),\end{cases}\qquad\forall\,\varepsilon\geq 0.

(10.24)

We remark that
|\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}|<2\pi
by (
10.20
). We also observe that the prescribed value on the cut locus in (
10.24
) is arbitrary and unimportant.

Since all structures
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})
support interpolation inequalities for densities (with dimensional parameter
n=3
, see Definition
4.5
), with an argument identical to step
2
in the proof of Theorem
9.23
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 10.5

(Uniform
\mathsf{CD}
for canonical variation)
.

Let
\beta
be defined as in (
3.14
), with

\mathsf{s}(\theta)=\theta(2-2\cos(\theta)-\theta\sin(\theta)),\qquad\theta\in[0,+\infty).

(10.25)

Then, for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
, the gauge m.m.s.
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3},\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon})
satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,3)
condition.

Remark 10.6
.

As a matter of fact, the
\beta
in the previous result satisfies
\beta_{t}(\theta)\geq t^{5}
for all
t\in[0,1]
and
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
. It follows that this
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,3)
implies the
\mathsf{CD}(t^{5},3)
or, equivalently, the
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
. The above result can be then seen as a strengthening of the known fact, proved in
[
75
]
, that the canonical variation of the Heisenberg group satisfies uniformly the classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,5)
. See also
[
61
]
.

The sub-Riemannian limit.

As
\varepsilon\to 0
, it holds
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{d}_{0}
uniformly on compact sets, and a fortiori
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})\to(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3})
in the pmGH-sense. Furthermore,
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{d}_{0}
also in the
C^{\infty}
sense on compact subsets of
\mathbb{H}^{1}\setminus\mathrm{Cut}_{0}
(cf.
[
29
, Prop. 2.8]
, where this fact is proved for general canonical variations). It follows that:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}(q,q^{\prime})=\mathsf{G}_{0}(q,q^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\,(q,q^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{Cut}_{0}.

(10.26)

The unique optimal plan
\pi
between
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3})
,
\mu_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{bs}^{*}(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3})
, with disjoint supports, is concentrated on
\mathbb{H}^{1}\setminus\mathrm{Cut}_{0}
. It follows that
\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{G}_{0}

\pi
-a.e. as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We also recall that
|\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}|<2\pi
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}
is continuous (actually smooth) out of
\mathrm{Cut}_{\varepsilon}
. Using all these facts, it follows that:

•

the conditions of Definition
5.3
are satisfied. Namely
\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{G}_{0}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense, and the regularity condition for
\mathsf{G}_{0}
holds.

•

the condition of Definition
5.12
are satisfied. Namely
\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{G}_{0}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense over plans, and the regularity condition for
\mathsf{G}_{0}
over plans is verified.

In particular, Theorems
5.6
and
5.14
apply. This is an explicit example of stability of the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
and
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,3)
, for the sequence of gauge metric measure spaces
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3},\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon})
, pmGH converging to the Heisenberg group
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\mathsf{d}_{0},\mathscr{L}^{3},\mathsf{G}_{0})
.

The adiabatic limit.

The terminology “adiabatic limit” can be traced back to
[
90
]
. Let
\pi:\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the projection, so that
\pi
is a Riemannian submersion from
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon})
onto the Euclidean
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathsf{d}_{e})
. As
\varepsilon\to+\infty
, it holds
\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathsf{d}_{e}\circ\pi
uniformly on compact sets, and a fortiori
(\mathbb{H}^{1},\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathscr{L}^{3})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathsf{d}_{e},\mathscr{L}^{2})
in the pmGH-sense.
One can prove that:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to+\infty}\mathsf{G}_{\varepsilon}(q,q^{\prime})=0,\qquad\forall\,q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{H}^{1},

(10.27)

and as a consequence all assumptions of Theorems
5.6
and
5.14
are met by setting
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}=0
. Similarly as in the previous case, one can take the limit for
\varepsilon\to+\infty
, obtaining that the Euclidean space
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathsf{d}_{e},\mathscr{L}^{2})
, equipped with the trivial gauge function
\mathsf{G}_{\infty}\equiv 0
, satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,3)
. Notice that
\beta_{t}(\mathsf{G}_{\infty})=\beta_{t}(0)=t^{5}
, so that in this specific case
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,3)
is actually equivalent to the
\mathsf{CD}(t^{5},3)
. In contrast with the sub-Riemannian limit, in the adiabatic limit we obtain a non-sharp result.

10.3.1
Sasakian foliations

A version of Theorem
10.5
holds more in general for the canonical variation associated with Sasakian foliations with non-negative Tanaka-Webster curvature, and more generally for H-type foliations satisfying suitable assumptions in the sense of
[
29
]
. The proof makes use of more technical comparison tools, which are a sharpened version of the ones appearing in
[
29
]
(see also
[
28
]
). These techniques are out of the scope of this paper, and thus we do not delve into details here.

We only mention that, as a corollary of these generalizations one can show that the canonical variation
(M,\mathsf{d}_{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{m})
of a
2d+1
-dimensional Sasakian foliation with non-negative Tanaka-Webster curvature satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(0,2d+3)
for all
\varepsilon\geq 0
, obtained in
[
61
]
with somewhat weaker curvature assumptions. See also
[
28
, Thm. 3.11]
.

10.4
Convergence to the tangent cone

Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a smooth sub-Riemannian metric space of topological dimension
n
.
It is well-known that for any
p\in M
, the tangent cone at
p
exists and it is isometric to a homogeneous space for a Carnot group. We describe this convergence explicitly, following
[
31
]
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{L}
, be a generating family for the sub-Riemannian structure, defined in a neighborhood of
p
. For any multi-index
I\in\{1,\dots,L\}^{\times i}
we denote
|I|:=i
and
X_{I}:=[X_{I_{1}},[\dots,[X_{I_{i-1}},X_{I_{i}}]]]
. Set

\mathscr{D}_{p}^{i}:=\spn\{X_{I}(p)\,:\,|I|\leq i\},\qquad i\in\mathbb{N}.

(10.28)

Let
k_{i}=k_{i}(p):=\dim\mathscr{D}_{p}^{i}
. These subspaces yield a filtration

\{0\}=:\mathscr{D}_{p}^{0}\subset\mathscr{D}_{p}^{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathscr{D}_{p}^{s}=T_{p}M,

(10.29)

where
s=s(p)\in\mathbb{N}
is the step of the sub-Riemannian structure at
p
. A diffeomorphism
z:U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, from a neighborhood
U\subset M
of
p
, yields local coordinates that are said:

•

linearly adapted
at
p
, if they are centered at
p
, i.e.
z(p)=0
, and
\partial_{z_{1}}|_{0},\dots,\partial_{z_{k_{i}}}|_{0}
form a basis for
\mathscr{D}_{p}^{i}
in these coordinates, for all
i=1,\dots,s
. We say that the coordinate
z_{i}
has
weight

w_{i}=j
if
\partial_{z_{i}}|_{0}
belongs to
\mathscr{D}_{p}^{j}\setminus\mathscr{D}_{p}^{j-1}
;

•

privileged at
p
, if they are adapted at
p
and
z_{i}(q)=O(\mathsf{d}(p,q)^{w_{i}})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Privileged coordinates exist in a neighborhood of any point. From now on we fix a system of privileged coordinates and we identify its domain
U\subseteq M
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
p
with the origin
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Similarly, vector fields defined on
U
are identified with vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and the restriction of the sub-Riemannian distance
\mathsf{d}
to
U
is identified with a distance function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define
dilations
, for
\lambda>0
:

\delta_{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad\delta_{\lambda}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n}):=(\lambda^{w_{1}}z_{1},\dots,\lambda^{w_{n}}z_{n}).

(10.30)

Dilations induce a concept of
homogeneity
of degree
d\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

for a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
f(\delta_{\lambda}(q))=\lambda^{d}f(q)
for all
\lambda>0
and
q\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
;

•

for a one-form
\omega
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
\delta_{\lambda}^{*}\omega=\lambda^{d}\omega
for all
\lambda>0
;

•

for a vector field
X
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
\delta_{\lambda*}X=\lambda^{-d}X
for all
\lambda>0
.

The
principal part
of the generating family
\mathscr{F}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{L}\}
is given by

\hat{X}_{i}:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\delta_{1/\varepsilon*}X_{i},\qquad i=1,\dots,L.

(10.31)

The family
\hat{\mathscr{F}}=\{\hat{X}_{1},\dots,\hat{X}_{L}\}
is a set of complete vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, homogeneous of degree
-1
, with polynomial coefficients. They generate a nilpotent Lie algebra of nilpotency step
s=s(p)
, and they satisfy the bracket-generating condition at any point of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. They define a sub-Riemannian structure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, called the
nilpotent approximation
at
p
, and denoted with
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
. An essential property of
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
is that it is homogeneous of degree
1
, that is
\hat{\mathsf{d}}(\delta_{\lambda}(q),\delta_{\lambda}(q^{\prime}))=\lambda\hat{\mathsf{d}}(q,q^{\prime})
for all
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\lambda>0
.

10.4.1
Description of convergence to the tangent cone

Fix
p\in M
. The following fundamental estimate holds
[
31
, Thm. 7.32]
7
7

7

Our statement is a amended version of
[
31
, Thm. 7.32]
, which contains a typo.
.

Theorem 10.7
.

There exist
\varepsilon_{p}>0
and
C_{p}>0
such that for all
q,q^{\prime}\in B_{\varepsilon_{p}}(p)
it holds

-C_{p}\,M_{p}(q,q^{\prime})\,\mathsf{d}(q,q^{\prime})^{1/s(p)}\leq\mathsf{d}(q,q^{\prime})-\hat{\mathsf{d}}(q,q^{\prime})\leq C_{p}\,M_{p}(q,q^{\prime})\,\hat{\mathsf{d}}(q,q^{\prime})^{1/s(p)},

(10.32)

where
M_{p}(q,q^{\prime}):=\max\{\hat{\mathsf{d}}(0,q),\hat{\mathsf{d}}(0,q^{\prime})\}
and
s(p)
is the step of the structure at
p
.

Recall that the tangent cone is the pGH limit for
k\to\infty
, when it exists, of
(M,k\mathsf{d},p)
. Letting
\mathsf{d}_{k}:=k\mathsf{d}
, the set
B_{R/k}(p)
coincides with the ball of radius
R
and center
p
, with respect with the rescaled metric
\mathsf{d}_{k}
. For
R>0
, we define approximating maps:

f_{k}:B_{R/k}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad f_{k}(q):=\delta_{k}(q),

(10.33)

where
k
is taken so large that
B_{R/k}(p)
is contained in the domain of privileged coordinates, thus
f_{k}
is well-defined. Note that
f_{k}(p)=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

By Theorem
10.7
, for all
R>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
k_{0}=k_{0}(R,\varepsilon)
such that for all
k\geq k_{0}
it holds:

\hat{B}_{R(1-\varepsilon)}(0)\subseteq f_{k}(B_{R/k}(p))\subseteq\hat{B}_{R(1+\varepsilon)}(0),

(10.34)

where we denoted with
\hat{B}
the ball with respect to
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
. Furthermore, it holds

|\mathsf{d}_{k}(q,q^{\prime})-\hat{\mathsf{d}}(f_{k}(q),f_{k}(q^{\prime}))|\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\forall\,q,q^{\prime}\in B_{R/k}(p).

(10.35)

Finally, if in the given set of privileged coordinates
\mathfrak{m}=\varphi\,\mathscr{L}^{n}
for some smooth positive function
\varphi
, we set

\mathfrak{m}_{k}:=k^{Q(p)}\mathfrak{m},\qquad\text{where}\qquad Q(p):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}jw_{j}(p).

(10.36)

Then for all
R>0
it holds
(f_{k})_{\sharp}(\mathfrak{m}_{k}|_{B_{R/k}})\rightharpoonup\varphi(0)\mathscr{L}^{n}|_{\hat{B}_{R}(0)}
weakly as
k\to\infty
. We can assume, up to modification of the privileged coordinates, that
\varphi(0)=1
. Thus

\text{pmGH}-\lim_{k\to\infty}(M,k\mathsf{d},k^{Q}\mathfrak{m},p)=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{L}^{n},0),

(10.37)

with approximating maps (
10.33
). Hence
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{L}^{n})
is isomorphic to the measured tangent cone at
p
of
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
. Different choices of privileged coordinates and different sequences of rescalings yield isomorphic copies of the same m.m.s.

10.4.2
Convergence of natural gauge functions to the tangent cone

For structures of step
\leq 2
, natural gauge functions induce a limit gauge function on the metric cone, with an explicit expression in privileged coordinates. In the following,
\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(x)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
denotes the cut locus from a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
, and
\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{(x,y)\mid y\in\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(x)\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 10.8

(Gauge functions induced on the tangent cone)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian m.m.s. Let
p\in M
,
\mathsf{d}_{k}=k\mathsf{d}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{k}=k^{Q(p)}\mathfrak{m}
, so that
(M,\mathsf{d}_{k},\mathfrak{m}_{k},p)
converges in the pmGH sense to the tangent cone
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\mathscr{L}^{n},0)
, with approximating maps (
10.33
) in a set of privileged coordinates.

Let
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to[0,\infty]
be a natural gauge function as in Definition
7.6
. Assume that the sub-Riemannian distribution has step
s(p)\leq 2
at
p
. Then:

(i)

the sequence
\mathsf{G}_{k}:=\mathsf{G}
converges in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense of Definition
5.3
to a gauge function
\hat{\mathsf{G}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to[0,+\infty]
;

(ii)

\hat{\mathsf{G}}
satisfies the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
;

(iii)

\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
;

(iv)

\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is homogeneous of degree
0
with respect to dilations, that is

\hat{\mathsf{G}}(\delta_{\lambda}(x),\delta_{\lambda}(y))=\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y),\qquad\forall\,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\forall\,\lambda>0;

(10.38)

(v)

\hat{\mathsf{G}}
admits the following explicit description. For
(x,y)\in\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y)=0
. For
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, let
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}\in T_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the initial covector of the unique geodesic in
\mathrm{Geo}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
joining
x
with
y
:

\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{x,y}\mathrm{d}z_{j}|_{x}.

(10.39)

Let
g=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}(z)\mathrm{d}z_{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}z_{j}
be the metric tensor of the Riemannian reference
\mathsf{d}_{R}
, and let
f
be the function appearing in Definition
7.6
of
\mathsf{G}
. Then there exists a constant
f(0,1)\in[0,+\infty]
such that:

\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y)=f(0,1)\sqrt{\sum_{i,j\mid w_{i}=w_{j}=2}g^{ij}(0)\hat{\lambda}_{i}^{x,y}\hat{\lambda}_{j}^{x,y}},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(10.40)

In other words,
\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y)
is the norm of the homogeneous component of degree
2
of
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
, with respect to a suitable metric on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

If
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
is vector-valued, an analogous statement holds, with
\hat{\mathsf{G}}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
and, in item
(v)
,
f(0,1)\in\mathbb{RP}^{m}_{+}
.

Proof.

We remind the following basic facts in sub-Riemannian geometry, that will be used throughout the proof (see Appendix
A
). Let
(M,\mathsf{d})
be a sub-Riemannian manifold induced by a family of smooth vector fields
\mathscr{F}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{L}\}
. For any
u\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
, and point
o\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the following ODE:

\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{L}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(\gamma(t)),\qquad\gamma(0)=o.

(10.41)

Solutions
\gamma
of (
10.41
) are called horizontal trajectories, and
u
is called a control for
\gamma
. Let
\mathcal{U}_{o}^{\mathscr{F}}\subset L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
be the open subset such that the solution to (
10.41
) is well-defined up to time
1
. The endpoint map for the family
\mathscr{F}
, with base point
o\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, is the map
\End_{o}^{\mathscr{F}}:\mathcal{U}_{o}^{\mathscr{F}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
which associates to
u
the value
\gamma(1)
of the solution of (
10.41
).

For any
q,q^{\prime}\in M
, let
\gamma^{q,q^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\mathsf{d})
be a geodesic from
q
to
q^{\prime}
. There exists a non-zero Lagrange multiplier
(\lambda^{q,q^{\prime}}_{1},\nu)\in T_{q^{\prime}}^{*}M\times\{0,1\}
, and a control
u\in\mathcal{U}_{q}^{\mathscr{F}}
with
|u(t)|=\mathsf{d}(q,q^{\prime})
, such that
\gamma^{q,q^{\prime}}
satisfies (
10.41
) and furthermore

\langle\lambda^{q,q^{\prime}}_{1},D_{u}\End_{q}^{\mathscr{F}}(v)\rangle=\nu(u,v)_{L^{2}},\qquad\forall\,v\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L}),

(10.42)

where
D
denotes the Fréchet differential. Geodesics satisfying (
10.42
) with
\nu=0
are called
abnormal
, and they correspond to controls that are critical point of the endpoint map. Geodesics satisfying (
10.42
) with
\nu=1
are called normal. In this latter case,
\gamma
can be seen as the projection of an integral curve
\lambda:[0,1]\to T^{*}M
of a Hamiltonian flow on
T^{*}M
, with Hamiltonian
H:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
given by
H(\eta)=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\langle\eta,X_{i}\rangle^{2}
. For such a lift, it holds
\lambda(1)=\lambda_{1}^{q,q^{\prime}}
.

We now proceed with the proof assuming
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
(cf. Definition
7.4
).

Proof of
(i)
and
(v)
. Fix
R>0
and let
k
be so large that
B_{2R/k}(p)
is in the domain of privileged coordinates. Thus, without loss of generality, we assume
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and the structure
(M,\mathsf{d})
is defined by a family
\mathscr{F}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{L}\}
of smooth vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Let
u_{k}
be the control of a geodesic
\gamma_{k}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\mathsf{d})
joining
\delta_{1/k}(x)
with
\delta_{1/k}(y)
such that
\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=\mathsf{d}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))
. For now
\gamma_{k}
is not necessarily unique even though we will see that this is the case for large
k
.

Define also the family
\mathscr{F}_{k}:=\{\tfrac{1}{k}\delta_{k*}X_{1},\dots,\tfrac{1}{k}\delta_{k*}X_{L}\}
of vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. It is easy to show that

\delta_{k}(\End_{\delta_{1/k}(x)}^{\mathscr{F}}(u))=\End_{x}^{\mathscr{F}_{k}}(ku),\qquad\forall\,u\in\mathcal{U}_{\delta_{1/k}(x)}^{\mathscr{F}}.

(10.43)

Thus, there exist sequences of Lagrange multipliers
(\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)},\nu_{k})\in T_{\delta_{1/k}(y)}^{*}M\times\{0,1\}
, controls
u_{k}\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
with
\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=\mathsf{d}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))
, and corresponding geodesics
\gamma_{k}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\mathsf{d})
between
\delta_{1/k}(x)
and
\delta_{1/k}(y)
such that

k^{2}\langle\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k},\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1},D_{ku_{k}}E_{x}^{\mathscr{F}_{k}}(v)\rangle=\nu_{k}(ku_{k},v)_{L^{2}},\qquad\forall\,v\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L}).

(10.44)

Our goal is to take the limit in (
10.44
) for
k\to+\infty
.

Firstly, by construction
ku_{k}
is a control associated with the curve
\delta_{k}\circ\gamma_{k}
, joining
x
with
y
. Furthermore, by Theorem
10.7
,
\|ku_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=k\mathsf{d}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))\to\hat{\mathsf{d}}(x,y)
. In particular
\{ku_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is weakly precompact in
L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
. Let
\hat{u}\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
be one of its weak limits. Weak convergence of controls together with local uniform convergence of
\tfrac{1}{k}\delta_{k*}X_{i}\to\hat{X}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,L
, implies that the sequence of corresponding curves
\delta_{k}\circ\gamma_{k}
has a subsequence converging to a limit one
\hat{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with control
\hat{u}
with respect to the family
\hat{\mathscr{F}}=\{\hat{X}_{1},\dots,\hat{X}_{L}\}
. In particular
\hat{\gamma}
is the solution of

\dot{\hat{\gamma}}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{L}\hat{u}_{i}(t)\hat{X}_{i}(\hat{\gamma}(t)),\qquad\hat{\gamma}(0)=x,\quad\hat{\gamma}(1)=y.

(10.45)

By definition of
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
, and the weak semi-continuity of the norm we have

\hat{\mathsf{d}}(x,y)\leq\|\hat{u}\|_{L^{2}}\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\|ku_{k}\|_{L^{2}}=\hat{\mathsf{d}}(x,y).

(10.46)

Thus
\hat{\gamma}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\hat{\mathsf{d}})
, with control
\hat{u}
