such that
\|\hat{u}\|=\hat{\mathsf{d}}(x,y)
. Now we use the fact that
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In this case there is a unique such geodesic
\hat{\gamma}
, and it follows that for the original sequence
\delta_{k}\circ\gamma_{k}\to\hat{\gamma}
and
ku_{k}\rightharpoonup\hat{u}
, respectively.

Secondly, since
ku_{k}\rightharpoonup\hat{u}
, then
D_{ku_{k}}\End_{x}^{\mathscr{F}_{k}}\to D_{\hat{u}}\End_{x}^{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}
in the operator topology (by the same argument in
[
85
, Prop. 3.7]
or
[
33
, Appendix, Thm. 23]
).

Lastly,
\nu_{k}
must be definitely equal to
1
, otherwise
\hat{u}
would be a critical point of
\End_{x}^{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}
, and thus
\hat{\gamma}
would be an abnormal geodesic, which is not possible since
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By the same reason,
\End^{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}_{x}
is a submersion at
\hat{u}
, thus for any
V\in T_{y}\mathbb{R}^{n}
there exist
\hat{v}\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
such that
D_{\hat{u}}\End_{x}^{\hat{\mathscr{F}}}(\hat{v})=V
.

We are now ready to take the limit in (
10.44
), taking
v=\hat{v}
, we obtain that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\langle k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1},V\rangle=(\hat{u},\hat{v}).

(10.47)

Since
V\in T_{y}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
was arbitrary, we have proved that the sequence
k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1}
is convergent, and its limit is the Lagrange multiplier for
\hat{\gamma}
. We must then have

\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1}=\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}_{1},

(10.48)

where
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}_{1}\in T_{y}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the normal Lagrange multiplier of
\hat{\gamma}\in\mathrm{Geo}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
.

We want to translate (
10.48
) into a statement for
initial
covectors. To do it, let
H,\hat{H}:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be the Hamiltonians of
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathsf{d})
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
, respectively:

H(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\langle\eta,X_{i}\rangle^{2},\qquad\hat{H}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\langle\eta,\hat{X}_{i}\rangle^{2},\qquad\forall\eta\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(10.49)

We observe that
\tfrac{1}{k^{2}}H(\delta_{k}^{*}\eta)\to\hat{H}(\eta)
as
k\to+\infty
. It follows that, for the Hamiltonian vector fields (the one encoding Hamilton’s equations), it holds

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\frac{1}{k^{2}}(\delta_{1/k}^{*})_{*}\vec{H}=\vec{\hat{H}},

(10.50)

where
(\delta_{1/k}^{*})_{*}
is the push-forward of
\delta_{1/k}^{*}:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Furthermore, using also the fact that the Hamiltonian is quadratic, namely
H(k\eta)=k^{2}H(\eta)
, for flows it holds:

\lim_{k\to+\infty}k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}e^{t\vec{H}}\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\delta_{k}^{*}\eta\right)=e^{t\vec{\hat{H}}}(\eta),\qquad\forall\,\eta\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n},

(10.51)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
(recall that
\vec{\hat{H}}
is complete). For the initial covector
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
corresponding to the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda_{1}^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
, using (
10.48
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to+\infty}k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to+\infty}k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}e^{-\vec{H}}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1}

(10.52)

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to+\infty}\underbrace{k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}e^{-\vec{H}}\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\delta_{k}^{*}\right.}_{\to e^{-\vec{\hat{H}}}}\underbrace{\left.k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{1}\right)}_{\to\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}_{1}}=\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}.

(10.53)

In other words, (
10.48
) holds also for the corresponding initial covectors.

Furthermore, let
\exp_{q}:T_{q}^{*}M\to M
and
\widehat{\exp}_{x}:T_{o}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the exponential maps with base point
q\in M
and
o\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
(M,\mathsf{d})
and
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
, respectively. That is

\exp_{q}=\pi\circ e^{\vec{H}}|_{T_{q}^{*}M},\qquad\widehat{\exp}_{o}=\pi\circ e^{\vec{\hat{H}}}|_{T_{o}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

(10.54)

Since
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
is not a critical point for
\widehat{\exp}_{x}
, we claim that for all sufficiently large
k
, the covectors
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
, and thus the corresponding geodesic
\gamma_{k}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\mathsf{d})
, are uniquely determined by the choice of the endpoints
x
and
y
, and
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
is not a critical point for
\exp_{\delta_{1/k}(x)}
.

To prove the claim, let
F:[0,\varepsilon_{0})\times T_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the map

F_{\varepsilon}(\eta):=\begin{cases}\pi\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}e^{\vec{H}}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\delta_{1/\varepsilon}^{*}\eta\right)\right)&\varepsilon>0,\\
\pi\left(e^{\vec{{\hat{H}}}}(\eta)\right)&\varepsilon=0.\end{cases}

(10.55)

The map
F
is smooth. We know that
F_{0}=\widehat{\exp}_{x}:T_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a diffeomorphism in a neighborhood of
\lambda_{0}:=\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
, and that
F_{0}(\lambda_{0})=y
. We already know that any family
\gamma_{1/\varepsilon}
of geodesics from
\delta_{\varepsilon}(x)
and
\delta_{\varepsilon}(y)
are not abnormal for small
\varepsilon
. Let then
\lambda^{\delta_{\varepsilon}(x),\delta_{\varepsilon}(y)}
be a corresponding family of initial covectors. We also know from (
10.52
) that the sequence
\lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{\varepsilon}(x),\delta_{\varepsilon}(y)}
is such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\to\lambda_{0}
, and by construction
F_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{\varepsilon})=y
for all
\varepsilon
. It follows that for sufficiently small
\varepsilon
there is a unique such
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
and thus
\lambda^{\delta_{\varepsilon}(x),\delta_{\varepsilon}(y)}
and
\gamma_{1/\varepsilon}
are uniquely determined by
x
and
y
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is regular for
F_{0}
, and since
\varepsilon\mapsto dF_{\varepsilon}
is continuous, then
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
is a regular point of
F_{\varepsilon}
for small
\varepsilon
. Unraveling the notation, this means that
\lambda^{\delta_{\varepsilon}(x),\delta_{\varepsilon}(y)}
is a regular point for
\exp_{x}
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. This concludes the proof of the claim.

Summing up, so far we proved that for any
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
and sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
it holds:

•

(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M,\mathsf{d})
;

•

the initial covectors
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
of the unique geodesics
\gamma_{k}\in\mathrm{Geo}(M,\mathsf{d})
from
\delta_{1/k}(x)
to
\delta_{1/k}(y)
satisfy

\lim_{k\to+\infty}k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}=\hat{\lambda}^{x,y},

(10.56)

where
\hat{\lambda}
is the initial covector of the unique
\hat{\gamma}
in
\mathrm{Geo}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
joining
x
with
y
.

Let us now discuss more precisely the action of dilations on covectors in our system of privileged coordinates. For all
q\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, it holds:

\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}\mathrm{d}z^{i}|_{\delta_{1/k}(q)}\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k^{2-w_{i}}\eta_{i}\mathrm{d}z^{i}|_{q}.

(10.57)

Thus, the map
k^{2}\delta_{1/k}^{*}
dilates by a factor
k^{2-j}
the homogeneous component of degree
j
of
\eta
. We stress the following consequences of (
10.52
) and (
10.57
):

•

the homogeneous component of degree
1
of
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
converges to zero;

•

the homogeneous component of degree
2
of
\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}
converges to the homogeneous component of degree
2
of
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
;

•

the homogeneous components of degree
j>2
have growth
O(k^{j-2})
.

To conclude, recall from Proposition
7.28
that if
(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))\notin\mathrm{Cut}(M,\mathsf{d})
, then

\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))=\sqrt{\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}(\delta_{1/k}(x))\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{i}\lambda^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}_{j}}.

(10.58)

Thus if the step
s(p)=2
at
p
, we must have at all
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))=\sqrt{\sum_{w_{i}=w_{j}=2}g_{ij}(0)\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}_{i}\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}_{j}}=:\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y).

(10.59)

We extend
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
to zero at all cut points. A little care in the argument proves also that the convergence in (
10.59
) is uniform in any sufficiently small neighborhood of a point
(x_{o},y_{o})\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Also
(x,y)\mapsto\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
is continuous out of the cut locus. It follows that if
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
x_{k}\to x
,
y_{k}\to y
, we have

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x_{k}),\delta_{1/k}(y_{k}))=\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y).

(10.60)

We now show how (
10.60
) implies the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of
\mathsf{D}
to
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
as in Definition
5.3
. Fix
\varepsilon,R>0
. Recall that by (
10.34
),
\delta_{k}(B_{R/k}(p))\subseteq\hat{B}_{R(1+\varepsilon)}(0)
for large
k
. Let
q_{k}\in B_{R/k}(p)
form a sequence such that
\delta_{k}(q_{k})=x_{k}
is convergent in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For sufficiently large
k
, then
q_{k}=\delta_{1/k}(x_{k})
where
x_{k}
is a convergent sequence in
B_{R(1+\varepsilon)}(0)
. Then

\int_{B_{R/k}(p)}|\mathsf{D}(q_{k},z)-\hat{\mathsf{G}}(\delta_{k}(q_{k}),\delta_{k}(z))|k^{Q}\varphi(z)\,\mathscr{L}^{n}(\mathrm{d}z)\\
=\int_{\delta_{k}(B_{R/k}(p))}|\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x_{k}),\delta_{1/k}(z))-\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x_{k},z)|\varphi(\delta_{1/k}(z))\,\mathscr{L}^{n}(\mathrm{d}z)\\
\leq\int_{\hat{B}_{R(1+\varepsilon)}(0)}|\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x_{k}),\delta_{1/k}(z))-\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x_{k},z)|\varphi(\delta_{1/k}(z))\,\mathscr{L}^{n}(\mathrm{d}z).

(10.61)

Remove from the domain of integration the set
Z:=\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\bar{x})
, given by the cut locus of
\bar{x}:=\lim x_{k}
with respect to the metric
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
. It is well-known that, in the step
2
setting,
Z
has zero Lebesgue measure. At any
z\in\hat{B}_{R(1+\varepsilon)}(0)\setminus Z
, we have that:

\lim_{k\to+\infty}\mathsf{D}(\delta_{1/k}(x_{k}),\delta_{1/k}(z))=\hat{\mathsf{G}}(\bar{x},z),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{k\to+\infty}\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x_{k},z)=\hat{\mathsf{G}}(\bar{x},z).

(10.62)

The first limit is (
10.60
), while the second one follows from the fact that
(x,z)\mapsto\hat{\lambda}^{x,z}
is continuous out of the cut locus. Furthermore, the integrand of (
10.61
) is locally bounded thanks to the step
2
assumption. In fact,
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is locally bounded by item
(iii)
. On the other hand, since
s(p)\leq 2
, then the step is
\leq 2
in a neighborhood of
p
, and thus
\mathsf{D}
is also locally bounded close to
p
(cf. Theorem
7.24
). Thus, by the dominated convergence theorem, (
10.61
) tends to zero as
k\to\infty
, proving the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence of Definition
5.3
.

Proof of
(ii)
.
The map
(x,y)\mapsto\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
is continuous out of
\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the set
\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(x)
has zero Lebesgue measure, and thus
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
satisfies the regularity condition of Definition
5.3
.

Proof of
(iii)
.
The metric tangent
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
has constant step equal to the one of the original structure
s(p)\leq 2
. Thus,
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
is locally Lipschitz in charts
[
4
, Cor. 6.2]
. For
(x,y)\in\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we have set
\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,x)=0
. For
(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}_{x}\hat{\mathsf{d}}^{2}(\cdot,y)
. Thus
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is locally bounded. By item
(iv)
it is also globally bounded.

Proof of
(iv)
.
Observe that dilations map
\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to itself. Furthermore, homogeneity of
\hat{\mathsf{d}}
and the fact that
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}_{x}\hat{\mathsf{d}}^{2}(\cdot,y)\in T_{x}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}
yield

\hat{\lambda}^{\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y)}=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\delta_{k}^{*}\hat{\lambda}^{x,y},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\widehat{\mathrm{Cut}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(10.63)

Thanks to the explicit formula for
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
of item
(v)
,
\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y)
depends only on the homogeneous part of degree
2
of
\hat{\lambda}^{x,y}
, and this is invariant by (
10.57
) by the action of
\frac{1}{k^{2}}\delta_{k}^{*}
. In other words
\hat{\mathsf{G}}(\delta_{k}(x),\delta_{k}(y))=\hat{\mathsf{G}}(x,y)
out of the cut locus, and also at the cut locus since there we set
\hat{\mathsf{G}}\equiv 0
.

This concludes the proof in the case
\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{D}
. Assume that, instead of
\mathsf{D}
, one had chosen a general natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}:M\times M\to[0,+\infty]
, induced by a
1
-homogeneous function
f:\Omega\to[0,+\infty]
as in Definition
7.6
. Notice that
\mathsf{d}(\delta_{1/k}(x),\delta_{1/k}(y))\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Furthermore,
\mathsf{D}
is locally bounded in the step
2
case, and thus
f
is also bounded and smooth on its domain. It follows that
\mathsf{G}
converges in the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
sense to
f(0,\hat{\mathsf{G}}_{0})
, where
\hat{\mathsf{G}}_{0}
is the
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
limit of
\mathsf{D}
that we have described in the previous case. Using the
1
-homogeneity of
f
we deduce that
\hat{\mathsf{G}}=f(0,1)\hat{\mathsf{G}}_{0}
. This concludes the proof for scalar valued gauge functions.

The proof is unchanged in the vector-valued case, with obvious modifications.
∎

We record one main consequence of Theorem
10.8
.

Theorem 10.9

(
\mathsf{MCP}
on the tangent)
.

Let
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})
be a sub-Riemannian metric measure space equipped with a natural gauge function
\mathsf{G}
. Let
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
be a tangent cone of
(M,\mathsf{d})
at
p
in a system of privileged coordinates, where
n=\dim M
. Equip it with:

•

the measure
\hat{\mathfrak{m}}=\lim_{k\to 0}(\delta_{k})_{\sharp}\mathfrak{m}
, proportional to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
;

•

the limit gauge function
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
of Theorem
10.8
, assuming that the step is
s(p)\leq 2
.

Let
\beta
as in (
3.14
), and assume that
\beta_{t}:[0,\mathcal{D})\to\mathbb{R}
is locally Lipschitz. If
(M,\mathsf{d},\mathsf{G},\mathfrak{m})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
then also
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\hat{\mathsf{G}},\hat{\mathfrak{m}})
satisfies the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
.

Corollary 10.10

(
\mathsf{CD}
on the tangent)
.

In Theorem
10.9
, assume moreover that
(M,\mathsf{d})
is fat. Then
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\hat{\mathsf{G}},\hat{\mathfrak{m}})
, which is an ideal Carnot group, satisfies the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.

Proof of Theorem
10.9
and Corollary
10.10
.

Using the explicit description of the convergence to the tangent cone at the beginning of Section
10.4.1
, we apply the stability Theorem
5.6
. To apply it, we first remark that, since
s(p)\leq 2
, then the step is
\leq 2
in a neighborhood of
p
so that any natural gauge function such as
\mathsf{G}
must be locally bounded (cf. Theorem
7.24
). The remaining hypotheses of Theorem
5.6
are satisfied thanks to Theorem
10.8
. This proves Theorem
10.9
.

We now prove Corollary
10.10
. If
(M,\mathsf{d})
is fat, then also
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}})
is fat. We note in passing that fat structures have constant growth vector, so that the structure is equiregular and in particular the tangent cone has the structure of a Carnot group
[
31
]
.

Furthermore, fat structures do not admit non-trivial abnormal geodesics, so they are ideal. By the results in
[
24
]
, ideal structures support interpolation inequalities with dimensional parameter
n
as in Definition
4.5
, and the corresponding
\nu
appearing in Definition
4.5
is concentrated on a set of geodesics that avoid the cut locus (out of which,
\hat{\mathsf{G}}
is continuous). Notice that the parameter
N
in the definition of
\beta
in (
3.14
) must be necessarily
N\geq n
, as a consequence of Theorem
4.20
.

Thus we can apply Theorem
4.7
to
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\hat{\mathsf{d}},\hat{\mathsf{G}},\hat{\mathfrak{m}})
, improving the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
of Theorem
10.9
to the
\mathsf{CD}(\beta,n)
.
∎

Remark 10.11
.

We can appreciate here an important difference with respect to the Riemannian case. In the Riemannian case, any system of coordinates is privileged, all coordinates have weight one, and thus by Theorem
10.8
(v)
any natural gauge function induces, in the limit, the trivial one
\hat{\mathsf{G}}=0
. Since for general distortion coefficients
\beta_{t}(\hat{\mathsf{G}})=\beta_{t}(0)=t^{N}
, Theorem
10.9
can be used to prove that the metric measure tangent of Riemannian manifolds must satisfy the
\mathsf{MCP}(0,N)
,
without using the explicit knowledge of the tangent
. However, in sub-Riemannian geometry, where weight
2
coordinates are available, one can have non-trivial gauge functions in the limit, and thus the
\mathsf{MCP}(\beta)
property on the tangent does not imply, a priori, a classical
\mathsf{MCP}(0,N)
.

10.5
Vector-valued gauge functions on three-dimensional structures

In this section we illustrate through an example our theory in the case of vector-valued gauge functions.

We consider here left-invariant fat sub-Riemannian structures on three-dimensional Lie groups, sometimes referred to as three-dimensional model spaces (even if with a different meaning than in Section
8
): the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{1}
, the special unitary group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and the special linear group
\mathrm{SL}(2)
.

For a unified description, let us consider
G
a simply connected three-dimensional Lie group endowed with a contact
sub-Riemannian structure whose distribution
\mathscr{D}
is generated by two left-invariant vector fields
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
which are orthonormal with respect to the corresponding sub-Riemannian metric. We assume the existence of a killing vector field
X_{0}
transverse to
\mathscr{D}
. Up to isometries and dilations, thanks to the classification in
[
1
,
45
]
, we can assume that the following Lie bracket relations hold for some
K\in\mathbb{R}
:

[X_{1},X_{2}]=X_{0},\qquad[X_{0},X_{1}]=KX_{2},\qquad[X_{0},X_{2}]=-KX_{1}.

(10.64)

In this description the Heisenberg group corresponds to
K=0
, the group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
for
K=1
, while
\mathrm{SL}(2)
corresponds to the choice
K=-1
. Here
K
coincides with the Tanaka-Webster curvature of the CR structure.

If we endow these sub-Riemannian structures with the Popp volume
\mathfrak{m}
(see
[
21
]
), then the corresponding volume distortion
\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}
(cf. Appendix
B.3
and Section
9
) along non-trivial geodesics is always identically zero
[
10
]
.

Following the notation of Sections
8
-
9
, given
x,y\in G\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(G)
and for the geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to G
joining them,
\gamma
has (reduced) Young diagram
Y
with two columns which can be labeled as follows:

Y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$c$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}

with all sizes of the superboxes equal to
1
. The Young diagram has two levels, denoted by
I
and
II
, the first one with length
\ell_{I}=2
, while the second one with length
\ell_{II}=1
, both with size
1
. The sub-Riemannian Ricci curvatures have been computed in
[
5
]
:

\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}_{\lambda}=\mathfrak{Ric}^{c}_{\lambda}=0,\qquad\mathfrak{Ric}^{b}_{\lambda}=h_{0}(\lambda)^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}(x,y),

(10.65)

where
\lambda=\lambda^{x,y}\in T_{x}^{*}M
is the initial covector of the geodesic
\gamma
and

h_{0}(\lambda):=\langle\lambda^{x,y},X_{0}\rangle,

(10.66)

is its component in the direction of
X_{0}
.

Notice that, out of the cut locus, by Proposition
7.28
it holds:

|h_{0}(\lambda^{x,y})|=\sqrt{\mathsf{D}(x,y)^{2}-\mathsf{d}(x,y)^{2}},\qquad\forall\,(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(G),

(10.67)

where
\mathsf{D}
denotes the natural gauge associated with the Riemannian extension such that
X_{1},X_{2},X_{0}
is an orthonormal frame (cf. Definition
7.4
).

Therefore, on the basis of the curvature bounds (
10.65
), we are led to define on
G
the vector-valued gauge function
\mathsf{G}:G\times G\to\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
as follows

\mathsf{G}=(\mathsf{G}_{1},\mathsf{G}_{2}):=(\sqrt{\mathsf{D}^{2}-\mathsf{d}^{2}},\mathsf{d}).

(10.68)

Since contact structures have step
2
, then
\mathsf{G}
is locally bounded by Theorem
7.24
(iii)
.

The assumptions of the main comparison Theorem
8.9
are satisfied by setting

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{I}

\displaystyle:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{I}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

\displaystyle:=(\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2},0),

(10.69)

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{II}

\displaystyle:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}_{II}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})

\displaystyle:=0.

(10.70)

To make the formulation of Theorem
8.9
explicit, we display the functions occurring in its statement. For what concerns the level
I
, following the construction described in Section
8.2
, we have for
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
:

\mathsf{\mathsf{s}}_{\kappa_{1},0}(t)=\frac{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)-\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\sin\left(\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}t\right)}{\kappa_{1}^{2}},\qquad t_{\kappa_{1},0}=\begin{cases}\tfrac{2\pi}{\sqrt{\kappa_{1}}}&\kappa_{1}>0,\\
+\infty&\kappa_{1}\leq 0,\end{cases}

(10.71)

with usual interpretation when
\kappa_{1}\leq 0
. Using (
8.19
), we have

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}\left({\theta_{1}}/{|\theta|},{\theta_{1}}/{|\theta|}\right)}(|\theta|),

and more explicitly

\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=|\theta|^{2}\left(\frac{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}}\right)-\sqrt{\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}}\sin\left(\sqrt{\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}}\right)}{\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}}\right),

(10.72)

where
|\theta|=\sqrt{\theta_{1}^{2}+\theta_{2}^{2}}
. The positivity domain of
\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}
is

\mathrm{DOM}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}=\{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\mid\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}<2\pi\},

(10.73)

which is indeed an open and star-shaped set, whose shape depends on the sign of
K
, as shown in Figure
5
.

The level
II
is omitted according to the prescriptions of Theorem
8.9
, so we do not need to compute the corresponding comparison functions.

Figure 5:
Picture of
{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}}
for
\bar{\kappa}(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})=\theta_{1}^{2}+K\theta_{2}^{2}
. Here,
\theta_{1}=|h_{0}|
and
\theta_{2}=\mathsf{d}
.

Thus, Theorem
8.9
yields that for all
(x,y)\notin\mathrm{Cut}(G)
we have
\mathsf{G}(x,y)\in{\rm{DOM}}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}
, and

\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq t\frac{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}(t\mathsf{G}(x,y))}{\mathsf{s}_{\bar{\kappa}_{I}}(\mathsf{G}(x,y))}.

(10.74)

The above inequality can be explicitly rewritten thanks to (
10.68
) in the form:

\beta_{t}^{(X,\mathsf{d},\mathfrak{m})}(x,y)\geq t\frac{2-2\cos\left(t\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\right)-t\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\sin\left(t\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\right)}{2-2\cos\left(\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\right)-\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\sin\left(\sqrt{h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}}\right)},

(10.75)

where
h_{0}=h_{0}(\lambda^{x,y})
and
\mathsf{d}=\mathsf{d}(x,y)
.

Remark 10.12
.

One may wonder whether one could apply our framework with the
scalar
gauge function
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{scal}}:=h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}
, instead of the vector-valued one
\mathsf{G}=(|h_{0}|,\mathsf{d})
. This approach has two main drawbacks:

•

scalar gauge functions are required to be non-negative, hence the choice of
\mathsf{G}_{\mathrm{scal}}=h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}
as a gauge would be possible only in the case
K\geq 0
, failing the purpose of including all three-dimensional structures on Lie groups in a unified framework;

•

our guiding principle is that the gauge function should be a reference function with respect to which curvature bounds can be quantified. Gauge functions
should not
contain the numerical parameters that quantify the extent of curvature bounds. The three-dimensional examples in this section nicely illustrate this idea: the only non-zero curvature is
\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}^{b}\geq h_{0}^{2}+K\mathsf{d}^{2}
, the non-negative
functions

|h_{0}|
and
\mathsf{d}
are the gauge to measure the extent of the curvature bound, which is then
quantified
by the parameter
K\in\mathbb{R}
.

Appendix A
Sub-Riemannian geometry

In this appendix we collect basic facts and notations in sub-Riemannian geometry used in this paper. For a comprehensive introduction, we refer to
[
2
,
76
,
71
]
.

•

A sub-Riemannian structure on a smooth, connected
n
-dimensional manifold
M
, where
n\geq 2
, is defined by a set of
m\geq 2
global smooth vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{L}
, called a
generating family
.

•

We assume the
bracket-generating
condition, i.e., the vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{L}
and their iterated Lie brackets at
x
generate the tangent space
T_{x}M
, for all
x\in M
.

•

The (generalized)
distribution
is the disjoint union
\mathscr{D}=\bigsqcup_{x\in M}\mathscr{D}_{x}
, where

\mathscr{D}_{x}:=\mathrm{span}\{X_{1}(x),\ldots,X_{L}(x)\}\subseteq T_{x}M,\qquad\forall\,x\in M.

(A.1)

Notice that
\mathscr{D}
is a vector bundle if and only if
\dim\mathscr{D}_{x}
does not depend on
x
.

•

The generating family induces an inner product
g_{x}
on
\mathscr{D}_{x}
given by:

g_{x}(v,v):=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{L}u_{i}^{2}\mid v=\sum_{i=1}^{L}u_{i}X_{i}(x)\right\},\qquad\forall\,v\in\mathscr{D}_{x}.

(A.2)

•

For
i\geq 1
, we define the
iterated distributions

\mathscr{D}^{i}=\bigsqcup_{x\in M}\mathscr{D}^{i}_{x}
, where:

\mathscr{D}^{i}_{x}:=\mathrm{span}\{[X_{i_{1}},[\ldots,[X_{i_{j-1}},X_{i_{j}}]]\mid i_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,L\},\;j\leq i\}.

The
step
of the distribution at
x
is the minimal
s=s(x)
such that
\mathscr{D}^{s}_{x}=T_{x}M
.

•

A
horizontal curve
is an absolutely continuous (in charts) map
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
such that there exists
u\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
, called
control
, satisfying

\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{L}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(\gamma(t)),\qquad\mathrm{a.e.}\;t\in[0,1].

(A.3)

The class of horizontal curves depends on the family
\mathscr{F}=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{L}\}
only through the
C^{\infty}(M)
-module of vector fields generated by
\mathscr{F}
.

•

We define the
length
of a horizontal curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
as follows:

\ell(\gamma):=\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{g(\dot{\gamma}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))}\mathrm{d}t.

(A.4)

The length
\ell
is invariant by suitable reparametrizations. Every horizontal curve is the reparametrization of a suitable constant-speed one.

•

The
sub-Riemannian (or Carnot-Carathéodory) distance
is defined by:

\mathsf{d}(x,y)=\inf\{\ell(\gamma)\mid\gamma(0)=x,\,\gamma(1)=y,\,\gamma\text{ horizontal}\}.

(A.5)

The bracket-generating condition implies that
\mathsf{d}
is finite and continuous. If
(M,\mathsf{d})
is complete as metric space, then for any
x,y\in M
the infimum in (
A.5
) is attained.

•

On the space of horizontal curves defined on
[0,1]
and with fixed endpoints, the minimizers of
\ell
, parametrized with constant speed, coincide with the minimizers of the
energy functional

J(\gamma):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}g(\dot{\gamma}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t.

(A.6)

•

The
end-point map
associated with the generating family
\mathscr{F}=\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{L}\}
and with base point
x
is the Fréchet-smooth map
\End^{\mathscr{F}}_{x}:\mathcal{U}\to M
, which sends
u
to
\gamma_{u}(1)
, where
\gamma_{u}
is the solution of

\dot{\gamma}_{u}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{L}u_{i}(t)X_{i}(\gamma_{u}(t)),\qquad\gamma_{u}(0)=x,

(A.7)

for every
u\in\mathcal{U}\subset L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L})
for which the solution
\gamma_{u}
is defined on
[0,1]
.

•

Sub-Riemannian geodesics
are admissible trajectories associated with
minimizing controls
, namely the ones that solve the constrained minimum problem

\min\{J(u)\mid u\in\mathcal{U},\quad\End^{\mathscr{F}}_{x}(u)=y\},\qquad x,y\in M.

(A.8)

Sub-Riemannian geodesics are precisely those curves
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
such that
\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{t},\gamma_{s})=|t-s|\mathsf{d}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})
.

•

If
u
is a minimizing control with
\End^{\mathscr{F}}_{x}(u)=y
, then there exists a non-trivial pair
(\lambda_{1},\nu)\in T_{y}^{*}M\times\{0,1\}
, called Lagrange multiplier, such that

\lambda_{1}\circ D_{u}\End^{\mathscr{F}}_{x}(v)=\nu(u,v)_{L^{2}},\qquad\forall v\in T_{u}\mathcal{U}\simeq L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{L}),

(A.9)

where
\circ
denotes the composition of linear maps,
D
the (Fréchet) differential. Non-trivial means that
(\lambda_{1},\nu)\neq(0,0)
.

•

The multiplier
(\lambda_{1},\nu)
and the associated curve
\gamma_{u}
are called
normal
if
\nu=1
and
abnormal
if
\nu=0
. A minimizing control
u
may admit different multipliers so that
\gamma_{u}
might be both normal
and
abnormal. In particular we observe that
\gamma_{u}
is abnormal if and only if
u
is a critical point of
\End^{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
.

•

If
a:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function, we denote by
\vec{a}
the corresponding Hamiltonian vector field, i.e., the vector field on
T^{*}M
satisfying
\sigma(\cdot,\vec{a})=da
, where
\sigma
is the canonical symplectic form of
T^{*}M
.

•

If
\gamma_{u}:[0,1]\to M
is a normal geodesic, with Lagrange multiplier
\lambda_{1}
, then it admits a lift
\lambda:[0,1]\to T^{*}M
satisfying the differential equation

\dot{\lambda}(t)=\vec{H}(\lambda(t)),\qquad\lambda(1)=\lambda_{1},

(A.10)

where
H:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
is the
sub-Riemannian Hamiltonian
:

H(\lambda):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{L}\langle\lambda,X_{i}\rangle^{2},\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in T^{*}M,

(A.11)

and
\vec{H}
denotes the corresponding Hamiltonian vector field.

•

The
exponential map
at
x\in M
is the map
\exp_{x}:T_{x}^{*}M\to M
, which assigns to
\lambda_{0}\in T_{x}^{*}M
the final point
\pi(\lambda(1))
of the corresponding solution of

\dot{\lambda}(t)=\vec{H}(\lambda(t)),\qquad\lambda(0)=\lambda_{0}.

The covector
\lambda_{0}
is called
initial covector
of the trajectory. The curve
\gamma_{u}(t)=\pi(\lambda(t))
,
t\in[0,1]
, has control
u_{i}(t)=\langle\lambda(t),X_{i}(\gamma_{u}(t))\rangle
for
i=1,\dots,L
, and satisfies the normal Lagrange multiplier rule with Lagrange multiplier
\lambda_{1}=e^{\vec{H}}(\lambda_{0})
.

•

Given a normal geodesic
\gamma(t)=\exp_{x}(t\lambda_{0})
with initial covector
\lambda_{0}\in T_{x}^{*}M

we say that
y=\exp_{x}(\bar{t}\lambda)
is a
conjugate point
to
x
along
\gamma
if
\bar{t}\lambda
is a critical point for
\exp_{x}
. Also we say that
\gamma(s)
and
\gamma(t)
are
conjugate
if
\gamma(t)
is conjugate to
\gamma(s)
along
\gamma|_{[s,t]}
.

•

A normal geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M

contains no non-trivial abnormal segments
if for every
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,1]
with
0<|s-s^{\prime}|<1
, the restriction
\gamma|_{[s,s^{\prime}]}
is not abnormal.

•

If a geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
contains no non-trivial abnormal segments, then
\gamma(s)
is not conjugate to
\gamma(s^{\prime})
for every
s,s^{\prime}\in[0,1]
with
0<|s-s^{\prime}|<1
.

•

A sub-Riemannian structure is
ideal
if the corresponding metric
\mathsf{d}
is complete and there exist no non-trivial abnormal geodesics.

•

We say that
y\in M
is a
smooth point
, with respect to
x\in M
, if there exists a unique geodesic joining
x
with
y
, which is not abnormal, and with non-conjugate endpoints.

•

The
cut locus

\mathrm{Cut}(x)
is the complement of the set of smooth points with respect to
x
. The
global cut locus
of
M
is

\mathrm{Cut}(M):=\{(x,y)\in M\times M\mid y\in\mathrm{Cut}(x)\}.

(A.12)

The set of smooth points is open and dense in
M
, and the squared sub-Riemannian distance is smooth on
M\times M\setminus\mathrm{Cut}(M)

[
9
,
77
]
.

•

The
abnormal set

\mathrm{Abn}(x)
is the set of points
y
such that there exists an abnormal geodesic joining
x
and
y
. It holds
\mathrm{Abn}(x)\subseteq\mathrm{Cut}(x)
.

Appendix B
Canonical curvature

This is a self-contained account of the results in
[
91
]
concerning the construction of the canonical frame and canonical curvature, adapted to our settings. This approach to the construction of curvature-type invariants and its relation with the geometry of curves in the Lagrange Grassmannian has its roots in the seminal papers
[
8
,
11
]
. See also
[
2
, Appendix]
. We will use basic concepts on the geometry of the Lagrange Grassmannian, for which we refer to
[
2
, Ch. 14]
.

B.1
Curves in the Lagrange Grassmannian

With any curve
\Lambda(\cdot)
in a Lagrange Grassmannian
G_{k}(W)
of
n
-dimensional Lagrangian subspaces of a
2n
-dimensional symplectic space
(W,\sigma)
one can associate a curve of flags of subspaces in
W
. Denote:

\Lambda^{(i)}(t):=\spn\left\{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{j}}{\mathrm{d}t^{j}}\xi(t)\mid\xi(\cdot)\text{ is a smooth curve with $\xi(\tau)\in\Lambda(\tau)$ for all $\tau$},\;0\leq j\leq i\right\}.

(B.1)

Recall that the tangent space
T_{\Lambda}G_{k}(W)
to a point
\Lambda\in G_{k}(W)
is identified with the space
Q(\Lambda)
of quadratic forms on
\Lambda
via the symplectic form. Explicitly, this is done by identifying the tangent vector
\dot{\Lambda}
at a point
\Lambda
with the quadratic form
\Lambda\ni\xi\mapsto\sigma(\xi,\dot{\xi}_{0})
, where
\tau\mapsto\xi_{\tau}
is a smooth curve in
W
such that
\xi_{\tau}\in\Lambda(\tau)
for all
\tau
and
\xi_{0}=\xi
. The rank of
\Lambda(\cdot)
at
t
is the rank of
\dot{\Lambda}(t)
as a quadratic form.

We define then a flag of families of subspaces

\Lambda(\tau)=:\Lambda^{(0)}(\tau)\subseteq\Lambda^{(1)}(\tau)\subseteq\dots.

(B.2)

We make the following assumptions on the curve
\Lambda(\cdot)
:

1.

it is
equiregular
, that is
k_{i}(t):=\dim\Lambda^{(i)}(t)
does not depend on
t
, for all
i\geq 0
;

2.

it is
ample
, that is there exists
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\Lambda^{s}(t)=W
for all
t
;

3.

it is
monotonically non-decreasing
:
\dot{\Lambda}(t)\leq 0
for all
t
;

As a consequence of
1
, the sequence of numbers

d_{i}:=\dim\Lambda^{(i+1)}-\dim\Lambda^{(i)},\qquad i\geq 0.

(B.3)

is non-increasing.
We assign a Young diagram to the curve
\Lambda(\cdot)
as follows: the number of boxes in the
i
-th column of the diagram is equal to
\dim\Lambda^{(i)}-\dim\Lambda^{(i-1)}
. In particular the number of boxes in the first column coincides with the rank of the curve:
d_{1}=\rank\dot{\Lambda}
. By
2
, the Young diagram contains
n=\dim(W)
boxes. See Figure
6
.

Figure 6:
Young diagram.

B.1.1
Reduced Young diagram

Heuristically, a row of length
\ell
in the Young diagram corresponds to the existence of a curve
\xi(\cdot)\in\Lambda(\cdot)
such that
\dot{\xi}(\cdot),\dots,\xi^{(\ell)}(\cdot)
are all independent as elements of
W
. It is reasonable then to identify subspaces that have the same behaviour with respect to these derivations. This motivates the following procedure.

For any given column of the Young diagram, we merge in a single box all boxes located in the rows with the same length. This procedure yields a
reduced Young diagram
from the original one, whose boxes are called
superboxes
, and whose rows are called
levels
. All superboxes of a given level have the same
size

r
, that is the number of boxes merged together in the superbox, see Figure
7
.

Figure 7:
Superboxes
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell}
of a level
\alpha
.

By construction, the reduced Young diagram contains
d\geq 1
levels, and the sequence of lengths of the levels is strictly decreasing:

\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}>\dots>\ell_{d}.

(B.4)

We call
Y
the reduced Young diagram, which formally is identified with the set of its superboxes. Generic superboxes are denoted with the letters
a
,
b
,
\dots
. We are going to define curvature-type invariants of
\Lambda(\cdot)
in terms of matrix-valued mappings. For this reason we need to introduce some terminology. A mapping

R:Y\times Y\to\mathrm{Mat},

(B.5)

where
\mathrm{Mat}
denotes the set of all matrices, is called:

•

compatible with the Young diagram

Y
if to any pair
(a,b)
of superboxes of sizes
r_{a}
and
r_{b}
, the matrix
R(a,b)
has size
r_{a}\times r_{b}
;

•

symmetric
if
R(b,a)=R(a,b)^{*}
for any pair
(a,b)
of superboxes.

B.1.2
Normal mappings

Curvature mappings will be required to satisfy algebraic conditions, formulated in terms of vanishing of some blocks
R(a,b)
, for suitable superboxes
a,b\in Y
. We refer to
[
91
, Def. 1, Def. 2]
for the general formulation, which we describe here only for the cases in which
Y
has no more than two columns (and hence at most two levels). In this case, a symmetric and compatible mapping
R:Y\times Y\to\mathrm{Mat}
is called
normal
if:

•

R(a,b)
is skew-symmetric, that is
R(a,b)=-R(a,b)^{*}
, for all pairs of distinct superboxes
a,b
that belong to the first level of
Y
.

Of course this condition is vacuous if
Y
has only one column (and hence only one superbox, as it happens for Jacobi curves of a Riemannian or Finsler structures), so that a symmetric compatible mapping in this latter case is just any
n\times n
symmetric matrix.

B.1.3
Normal moving frames

A normal moving frame will be a smooth one-parameter family of bases of
W
, attached to a given curve
\Lambda(\cdot)
. It will be convenient to label the elements of a moving frame according to the superboxes of a reduced Young diagram
Y
. In particular, if
a
is a superbox of
Y
of size
r
, then

E_{a}(t)=(E_{a_{1}}(t),\dots,E_{a_{r}}(t)),\qquad F_{a}(t)=(F_{a_{1}}(t),\dots,F_{a_{r}}(t)),

(B.6)

denote two tuples of smooth families of vector fields in
W
, which are independent at all times. The collection
\{E_{a}(t),F_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
will then be a basis for
W
for all times. It is natural to require that the frame is Darboux, that is

\sigma(E_{a},E_{b})=\sigma(F_{a},F_{b})=\sigma(E_{a},F_{b})-\delta_{ab}=0,\qquad\forall\,a,b\in Y,

(B.7)

where for tuples
V,V^{\prime}
of vectors in
W
we denote by
\sigma(V,V^{\prime})
the matrix
\sigma(V_{i},V^{\prime}_{j})
and
\delta
is a Kronecker delta, that is
\delta_{ab}=\mathbb{0}
if
a\neq b
and
\delta_{ab}=\mathbb{1}
if
a=b
(here,
\mathbb{0}
and
\mathbb{1}
are
r_{a}\times r_{b}
matrices).

Definition B.1

(Normal moving frames)
.

The moving Darboux frame
\{E_{a}(t),F_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
is called a
normal moving frame
of a non-decreasing curve
\Lambda(t)
with reduced Young diagram
Y
if for all
t
:

\Lambda(t)=\spn\{E_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y},

(B.8)

and there exists a one-parameter family of symmetric and compatible normal mappings
R_{t}:Y\times Y\to\mathrm{Mat}
such that for any superbox
a\in Y

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=E_{l(a)}(t),

\displaystyle\qquad a\notin\mathrm{first}(Y),

(B.9)

\displaystyle\dot{E}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=-F_{a}(t),

\displaystyle\qquad a\in\mathrm{first}(Y),

(B.10)

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in Y}R_{t}(a,b)\cdot E_{b}(t)-F_{r(a)}(t),

\displaystyle\quad a\notin\mathrm{last}(Y),

(B.11)

\displaystyle\dot{F}_{a}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in Y}R_{t}(a,b)\cdot F_{b}(t),

\displaystyle\qquad a\in\mathrm{last}(Y),

(B.12)

where
\mathrm{first}(Y)
and
\mathrm{last}(Y)
denote the set of superboxes in the first (resp. last) column of each level of
Y
, while
l:Y\setminus\mathrm{first}(Y)\to Y
and
r:Y\setminus\mathrm{last}(Y)\to Y
denote the left and right shift of superboxes on
Y
.

Theorem B.2

(Existence and uniqueness of normal moving frames
[
91
]
)
.

For any ample, equiregular, monotonically non-increasing curve
\Lambda(\cdot)
in the Lagrange Grassmannian, with reduced Young diagram
Y
, there exists a normal moving frame
\{E_{a}(t),F_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
.

Furthermore
\{\tilde{E}_{a}(t),\tilde{F}_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
is another normal moving frame if and only if for any level
1\leq i\leq d
of
Y
with size
r_{i}
there exists a constant orthogonal
r_{i}\times r_{i}
matrix
U_{i}
such that for all superboxes
a
in the
i
-th level it holds

\tilde{E}_{a}(t)=U_{i}\cdot E_{a}(t),\qquad\tilde{F}_{a}=U_{i}\cdot F_{a}(t),\qquad\forall\,t.

(B.13)

B.1.4
Canonical splittings and curvatures

Theorem
B.2
yields a rich structure attached to
\Lambda(\cdot)
. Firstly, the family
\Lambda(t)
is equipped with a
canonical Euclidean structure
, given by the scalar product that makes the elements of the tuples
\{E_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
orthonormal. Secondly, we have the splitting

\Lambda(t)=\bigoplus_{a\in Y}V_{a}(t),\qquad\text{where}\qquad V_{a}(t):=\spn\{E_{a}(t)\}.

(B.14)

Similarly one can define a Lagrangian complement
\Lambda^{\mathrm{trans}}(t)
such that
W=\Lambda(t)\oplus\Lambda^{\mathrm{trans}}(t)
for all times, given by

\Lambda^{\mathrm{trans}}(t):=\bigoplus_{a\in Y}H_{a}(t),\qquad\text{where}\qquad H_{a}:=\spn\{F_{a}(t)\}.

(B.15)

The matrix-valued maps
R_{t}(a,b)
appearing in Theorem
B.2
correspond to well-defined operators. In fact, if
R_{t},\tilde{R}_{t}:Y\times Y\to\mathrm{Map}
are two compatible, symmetric normal mappings associated with two canonical moving frames
\{E_{a}(t),F_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
and
\{\tilde{E}_{a}(t),\tilde{F}_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
, then it follows from Theorem
B.2
that:

\tilde{R}_{t}(a,b)=U_{i}^{*}R_{t}(a,b)U_{j},\qquad\forall\,t,

(B.16)

where
a
and
b
belong to the
i
-th and
j
-th levels of
Y
, respectively, and
U_{i},U_{j}
are orthogonal constant matrices appearing in Theorem
B.2
. We can then give the following formal definition.

Definition B.3

(Canonical curvatures)
.

Let
\Lambda(\cdot)
be an ample, equiregular, monotonically non-increasing curve in the Lagrange Grassmannian, with reduced Young diagram
Y
. For any
a,b\in Y
, we define the
(a,b)
-canonical curvature map

\mathfrak{R}^{ab}(t):V_{a}(t)\times V_{b}(t)\to\mathbb{R},

(B.17)

as the one-parameter family of linear maps whose representative matrix correspond to the matrix
R_{t}(a,b)
with respect to the bases
E_{a}
and
E_{b}
of
V_{a}
and
V_{b}
. The
canonical curvature map
is the one-parameter family of symmetric linear maps
\mathfrak{R}(t):\Lambda(t)\times\Lambda(t)\to\mathbb{R}
such that its restriction on
V_{a}(t)\times V_{b}(t)
coincides with
\mathfrak{R}^{ab}(t)
for all
a,b\in Y
. Finally, for each
a\in Y
, the
canonical Ricci curvature
is the one-parameter family obtained by taking the trace on superboxes of
\mathfrak{R}
with respect to the canonical Euclidean structure:

\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}(t):=\tr(\mathfrak{R}^{aa}(t)),\qquad\forall\,a\in Y,

(B.18)

that is the trace of the matrix
R_{t}(a,a)
for any choice of normal moving frame.

B.2
Jacobi curves

An important case to which the previous theory applies is that of Jacobi curves
J_{\lambda}(\cdot)
associated with an extremal (i.e., an integral curve of a Hamiltonian flow) that we now introduce. Let
M
be a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold, and
H:T^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
be a function, generating the Hamiltonian flow
e^{t\vec{H}}
. For simplicity we assume it to be defined for all times
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Letting
T^{*}M_{\neq 0}=\{\lambda\in T^{*}M\mid H(\lambda)\neq 0\}
, we require the following:

(H1)

H
is smooth on
T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
;

(H2)

the Hessian of the restriction
H_{x}:=H|_{T_{x}^{*}M}
at any point
\lambda\in T^{*}_{x}M_{\neq 0}
, denoted by
\mathrm{d}^{2}_{\lambda}H_{x}:T_{x}^{*}M\to\mathbb{R}
, is a non-negative and possibly degenerate quadratic form.

These assumptions are verified for the Hamiltonian function of general (sub-)Riemannian and (sub-)Finsler structures, or for the Hamiltonian of a general LQ optimal control problem on
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see Section
8.1
). Notice that in the (sub-)Riemannian or in the LQ case, since
H_{x}
is a quadratic form, it holds
\mathrm{d}^{2}_{\lambda}H_{x}=2H_{x}
.

Definition B.4

(Jacobi curve)
.

Let
\lambda\in T^{*}M_{\neq 0}
be the initial covector of
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
. The
Jacobi curve
at
\lambda
is the following curve of Lagrangian subspaces of
T_{\lambda}(T^{*}M)
:

J_{\lambda}(t):=e^{-t\vec{H}}_{*}T_{\lambda_{t}}(T^{*}_{\gamma_{t}}M),

(B.19)

where
\gamma_{t}=\pi(\lambda_{t})
.

We have the following properties for all
t,s
where the statements make sense:

(P1)

J_{\lambda}(t+s)=e^{-t\vec{H}}_{*}J_{\lambda_{t}}(s)
;

(P2)

\dot{J}_{\lambda}(0)=-\mathrm{d}^{2}_{\lambda}H_{x}
as quadratic forms on
J_{\lambda}(0)=T_{\lambda}(T^{*}_{x}M)\simeq T^{*}_{x}M
, where
x=\pi(\lambda)
.

See
[
2
, Prop. 15.2]
for a proof in the (sub-)Riemannian case.

It follows from
(P2)
that
\dot{J}_{\lambda}(t)\leq 0
for all times, so that
J_{\lambda}(t)
is monotonically non-increasing curve. When the curve is also ample and equiregular, we can apply Theorem
B.2
. The corresponding moving frames and curvature operators can be defined in three equivalent ways. For clarity, we describe them all here.

B.2.1
Curves in the Lagrange Grassmannian point of view

If
J_{\lambda}(\cdot)
is an ample and equiregular Jacobi curve, with Young diagram
Y
, Theorem
B.2
yields the existence of:

•

a normal moving frame
\{E_{a}(t),F_{a}(t)\}_{a\in Y}
for
J_{\lambda}(t)
;

•

the subspaces
V_{a}(t)=\spn\{E_{a}(t)\}
and
H_{a}(t)=\spn\{F_{a}(t)\}
of
T_{\lambda}(T^{*}M)
;

•

the
(a,b)
-curvature maps
\mathfrak{R}^{ab}(t):V_{a}(t)\times V_{b}(t)\to\mathbb{R}
, represented by the curvature matrices
R_{t}(a,b)
;

•

the curvature map
\mathfrak{R}(t):J_{\lambda}(t)\to J_{\lambda}(t)
, extending all the
(a,b)
-curvature maps;

•

the Ricci curvatures
\mathfrak{Ric}^{a}(t)=\tr\mathfrak{R}^{aa}(t)
.

B.2.2
Canonical frame along the extremal point of view

Notice that
e^{t\vec{H}}_{*}
maps
T_{\lambda}(T^{*}M)
diffeomorphically onto
T_{\lambda_{t}}(T^{*}M)
, sending in particular
J_{\lambda}(t)
to the vertical space
V_{\lambda_{t}}=\ker\mathrm{d}_{\lambda_{t}}\pi
. Therefore, a normal moving frame can be identified with a family of Darboux bases along
\lambda_{t}
, by setting

E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}:=e^{t\vec{H}}_{*}E_{a}(t),\qquad F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}:=e^{t\vec{H}}_{*}F_{a}(t),\qquad\forall\,a\in Y.

(B.20)

Definition
B.1
for a normal moving frame can be reformulated in an equivalent way in terms of the frame (
B.20
). Firstly, equation (
B.8
) becomes the condition

V_{\lambda_{t}}:=\ker\mathrm{d}_{\lambda_{t}}\pi=\spn\{E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}\}_{a\in Y}.

(B.21)

Secondly, the structural equations in Definition
B.1
are expressed in terms of (
B.20
) replacing the time-derivative with the derivative in the direction of
\vec{H}
.

With this approach, to any extremal
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
associated with an ample and equiregular Jacobi curve with Young diagram
Y
, Theorem
B.2
yields the existence of:

•

a normal moving frame
\{E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}},F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}\}_{a\in Y}
along
T_{\lambda_{t}}(T^{*}M)
, also called
canonical frame
;

•

the subspaces
V_{a|\lambda_{t}}=\spn\{E_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}\}
and
H_{a|\lambda_{t}}=\spn\{F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}\}
of
T_{\lambda_{t}}(T^{*}M)
;

•

the
(a,b)
-canonical curvature maps
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}^{ab}:V_{a|\lambda_{t}}\times V_{b|\lambda_{t}}\to\mathbb{R}
, represented by the curvature matrices
R_{t}(a,b)
;

•

the canonical curvature map
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}:V_{\lambda_{t}}\times V_{\lambda_{t}}\to\mathbb{R}
, extending all the
(a,b)
-canonical curvature maps;

•

the Ricci curvatures
\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda_{t}}^{a}=\tr\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}^{aa}
.

This notation is consistent, in the sense that if
\lambda_{s}
, for some
s\in\mathbb{R}
, is used as initial covector to produce the extremal
(\lambda_{s})_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda_{s})
, then it holds

\mathfrak{R}_{(\lambda_{s})_{t}}=\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t+s}},\qquad\forall\,t,s\in\mathbb{R}.

(B.22)

This property is a consequence of
(P1)
. It follows that
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda_{t}}
is the restriction to
\lambda_{t}
of a well-defined operator-valued map
\lambda\mapsto\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}
, also called canonical curvature map, defined at all those points
\lambda
associated with an ample and equiregular Jacobi curve.

B.2.3
Smooth families of operators point of view

Assuming that there exists a neighborhood
U\subseteq T^{*}M
of covectors with ample and equiregular Jacobi curve with the same reduced Young diagram
Y
, the maps
\lambda\mapsto\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}^{ab}
are smooth on
U
8
8

8

This is a consequence of the proofs
[
91
]
, as there one only uses inversion of matrices, solutions of ODES, all of them depending smoothly on the initial covector
\lambda
.
. This is the point of view adopted in Section
9
, in order to study the singularity of
\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}
as
\lambda\to\partial U
for fat sub-Riemannian structures (in that case
U=\{\lambda\in T^{*}M\mid H(\lambda)=0\}
).

B.3
Geodesic volume derivative

Let
(\mathscr{D},g)
be a sub-Riemannian structure on a smooth manifold
M
, equipped with a smooth measure
\mathfrak{m}
. Let
U\subseteq T^{*}M
be an open subset where any
\lambda\in U
is associated with an ample and equiregular Jacobi curve, so that the canonical frame is well-defined.

Definition B.5

(Geodesic volume derivative)
.

The
geodesic volume derivative
with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
is the smooth function
\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}:U\to\mathbb{R}
, defined by

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\log\mathfrak{m}\left(\bigwedge_{a\in Y}\pi_{*}F_{a}|_{\lambda_{t}}\right),\qquad\forall\,\lambda\in U,

(B.23)

for any choice of canonical frame along
\lambda_{t}=e^{t\vec{H}}(\lambda)
.

The definition is well-posed, that is it does not depend on the choice of canonical frame, thanks to the uniqueness part of Theorem
B.2
.

B.4
The Riemannian case

In the Riemannian case the Jacobi curve at any
\lambda\in T^{*}M
is ample and equiregular with the same reduced Young diagram made of one superbox (so we can omit it from the notation). It is proven in
[
22
, Lemma 15]
that:

\mathfrak{R}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{R}(\cdot,\lambda,\lambda,\cdot),\qquad\mathfrak{Ric}_{\lambda}=\mathrm{Ric}(\lambda,\lambda),

(B.24)

where we identify
T_{\lambda}(T^{*}_{x}M)=T^{*}_{x}M\simeq T_{x}M
via the Riemannian structure, while
\mathrm{R}
and
\mathrm{Ric}
denote the Riemann and Ricci curvature tensors, respectively.

Concerning the geodesic volume derivative, one can also prove that, in the Riemannian case, the canonical frame projects onto an orthonormal frame. If
M
is equipped with a smooth reference volume
\mathfrak{m}=e^{-V}\mathrm{vol}
, where
V:M\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function and
\mathrm{vol}
is the Riemannian density, then the geodesic volume derivative reduces to

\rho_{\mathfrak{m},\lambda}:=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\right|_{t=0}\log e^{-V(\gamma(t))}=-g(\nabla_{\gamma_{0}}V,\dot{\gamma}_{0}),

(B.25)

where
\gamma_{t}=\pi(\lambda_{t})
.
